The repetition rule. The same three sentences, in the same words, every time the same situation recurs. Toddlers learn from pattern, not from explanation, and the parent who varies the wording is teaching the child that the limit is negotiable.

When the formula does not work. If the child escalates regardless, the limit is held with the body and with silence: stay, do not argue, do not leave, and wait. The episode ends. The parent's task is to survive it without adding a humiliation, which is what the formula is for.

4.2b The tantrum, anatomised

Tantrums are the second year's signature event, and the literature on them is more useful clinically than the advice most families receive.

What a tantrum is. A loss of the child's regulatory capacity under a combination of frustration, fatigue, hunger and an audience. It is not manipulation — manipulation requires a theory of the other's mind and a capacity to inhibit an impulse, both of which are under construction — and it is not a failure of parenting. It is the developmental task colliding with an immature nervous system.

The four phases, and what each needs. The trigger: a want blocked, a transition, a hunger, a tiredness, an over-stimulation. What it needs: anticipation, and fewer transitions when the child is tired. The escalation: shouting, going rigid, throwing. What it needs: safety, and the adult's calm — a co-regulation problem, not a discipline problem. The peak: the child cannot hear anything. What it needs: presence and very few words; this is the phase in which talking makes it worse. The recovery: the child returns, often clingy. What it needs: the repair — a cuddle, a naming of what happened, and no lecture.

The three errors at each phase. At the trigger: begging, bargaining, or giving in, all of which teach the child that escalation works. At the peak: reasoning, threatening, or shaming, all of which add a second distress to the first. At the recovery: a lecture, a punishment, or a demand for an apology, all of which lose the repair.

The one useful sentence at the peak. "I am here. I will wait." Spoken quietly, repeated, with no argument attached. The sentence is not a technique for stopping the tantrum; it is a technique for surviving it without damage, and the tantrum ends when the nervous system is finished, which is usually within ten minutes.

The household's contribution. Tantrums are more frequent in households with inconsistent rules, more transitions, more caregivers with different responses, and more adult stress. The clinical implication is the week's: the frequency is a property of the household's coordination as much as of the child. That is not a criticism of the parents; it is the reason the intervention is a meeting about five rules rather than a lecture about the child.

What to tell the parents, in three sentences. "This is the year his feelings are bigger than his ability to manage them, and he needs you to be the calm one. When it peaks, talk less and stay. Afterwards, when he comes back to you, that is the moment that matters, not the tantrum."

4.3 Language: the assessment

The most common developmental concern in clinics, and the one with the clearest assessment pathway.

Step one — hearing. Always first, always. A child with glue ear or a sensorineural loss will present as a late talker, and no amount of language stimulation will change it. The referral is to audiology, and it is made in the first session in which language is the concern.

Step two — the language history. Which languages, spoken by whom, how much, and in what register? Does the child hear sustained talk — conversation, narration, questions — or mostly commands? Who reads to the child, and in what language? Is there a family history of late talking?

Step three — comprehension before production. "Show me the one that is bigger." "Give the ball to the grandmother." Comprehension is assessed by instruction, not by vocabulary, and it is the variable that most changes the differential: poor production with good comprehension is a different case from poor comprehension.

Step four — the play observation. Ten minutes of child-led play (Week 5's skill, used early): does the child use symbols, take turns, follow the adult's point, share attention on an object? The observation answers questions the vocabulary count cannot.

Step five — the settings. Does the child speak at nursery and not at home, or the reverse? A child who is fluent in one setting and silent in another is presenting a relationship or anxiety issue, not a language disorder — and the bilingual child who is silent at nursery is the commonest example.

Step six — the referral decision. Four outcomes: no concern (review in three months); monitor with specific advice (more talk, less command, one language of instruction where the household can manage it); refer to speech and language therapy; or refer to paediatrics or child development for a broader assessment. The decision is recorded with its reasons.

What the therapist says to the parents. Not "he is a late talker" and not "he may be autistic", but a statement of what is known and what will be done: "his hearing needs checking first; in the meantime, the most useful thing you can do is talk with him rather than to him — narrate what you are doing, wait for his sound, and answer it. We will look again in eight weeks."

The advice that works. Five practices, all supported by the language-development literature: follow the child's attention rather than directing it; narrate and expand ("yes, a big bus"); pause and wait for a turn; reduce questions and increase comments; and read the same book repeatedly. The fifth is the most enjoyable for families and the most effective per minute.

The advice that does not work. Flashcards, screens as language instruction, correcting the child's speech, and telling a bilingual household to abandon one language. Each is common, and each is either ineffective or harmful.

4.3a Ten minutes of child-led play, in the second year

The practice that appears again in Week 5, used early because it is the fastest way to change a toddler's language and a caregiver's relationship with the child at the same time.

The instructions to the caregiver. "Ten minutes, once a day, at a time when you are not rushed. He chooses what to play. You do four things and nothing else: you watch, you describe what he is doing, you copy what he does, and you say back what he says. You do not ask questions, you do not teach, you do not correct, and you do not use your phone."

Why each instruction is there. Watching teaches the caregiver to see what the child is actually doing, which is the skill the household lacks. Describing gives the child language attached to his own attention, which is the mechanism of vocabulary growth. Copying gives the child the experience of being followed, which is the antidote to a week of commands. And reflecting his speech back is the single most effective language intervention available to a non-specialist.

The prohibitions, and their reasons. Questions ("what colour is that?") test rather than converse, and the second year is not the age for testing. Teaching ("no, that goes there") converts play into instruction. Correcting ("say 'truck', not 'tuck'") interrupts the attempt at speech. And the phone, which is the most common failure of the practice in this decade, breaks the contingency the practice exists to build.

What the caregiver notices, in the first week. Three things, reported with surprise: that the child does more when not directed; that the child looks at them more; and that ten minutes is longer than they expected. The third observation is the one that changes behaviour, because it shows the caregiver that attention is a practice rather than a feeling.

What the therapist does with the reports. Asks for one specific thing the child did and one thing the caregiver did, so that the account is behavioural rather than evaluative. Then asks the question that generalises the practice: "what would it take to talk to him like that at other times of the day?" — which is where the language advice becomes a household habit rather than an exercise.

The contraindication. In households where the child's play is destructive or unsafe, or where the caregiver's own state makes ten minutes of undivided attention impossible, the practice is postponed and the caregiver's circumstances are the intervention. A parent who is being asked to do something they cannot do learns that they are failing, which is precisely what the second year's stage warns against.

4.4 Case: the grandmother who "cannot manage him"

The week's case, worked in full, because it is the presentation the course's own stub describes and the one that appears most often in clinics.

The referral. Tunde (2 years, 4 months), referred by a community nurse at the request of his grandmother, who says he "does not listen" and "shouts at me". His parents work: the mother (Yemi, 27) runs a stall six days a week; the father (Kola, 31) drives a taxi on shifts. The grandmother (58) has cared for Tunde since he was eight months old. The household also contains the father's younger brother (19).

The history. Walking at 15 months; first words at 14; two-word combinations at 27 months — at the lower end of range, in a household where three languages are spoken. Hearing has not been tested. Tunde attends a nursery three mornings a week, where he is described as "settled and quiet".

The three-setting comparison. At nursery: follows routines, speaks little, plays alone but near others. With his mother on Sundays: clingy, and speaks more, mostly in Yoruba. With the grandmother on weekdays: shouts, refuses, hits the furniture. Three settings, three children.

The rules map, which is the assessment's centre. The grandmother's rules: no playing outside, eat at the table, do not touch the radio, sleep at one. The mother's rules: outside is allowed in the yard, eating happens when he is hungry, the radio is not a problem, sleep at two if he is tired. The uncle's rules: none, and he gives sweets. The nursery's rules: a routine, a mat for stories, a rule about sharing.

The formulation, in three parts. First, Tunde is living in a household with four rule systems, two of which (the grandmother's and the mother's) contradict each other on almost every point. Second, the grandmother is doing the largest share of the care with the least authority: the mother's rules prevail in principle and the grandmother's in practice, and neither woman can say so to the other. Third, the shouting is the most effective strategy available to a two-year-old in this arrangement: it produces attention, and it produces a change of plan.

The language finding. Comprehension in Yoruba is good; production is delayed at the lower end of normal, in a trilingual environment. The referral for hearing is made in the first session. The language advice is not "speak one language" but "each caregiver speaks their strongest language to him, and everyone talks with him rather than at him" — which preserves the multilingual household and increases the child's exposure to sustained talk.

The intervention, in four parts. One: a family meeting with the three adults (grandmother, mother, father) in which the rules map is drawn and five rules are agreed — and only five, because five is a number a household can keep. Two: the authority question settled explicitly — the grandmother decides the weekday routine, the mother decides the weekend, and neither contradicts the other in front of the child. Three: the limit formula, taught to the grandmother first, because she is doing the work, and rehearsed with her in her own words. Four: ten minutes a day of child-led play, by whoever is with him, because it is the fastest way to increase his language and his experience of being followed rather than directed.

What the parents' absence means. The parents are not the caregivers during the week, and a plan that requires them to change their weekday behaviour will fail. The plan therefore works through the grandmother, and the parents' role is to endorse her authority in the child's hearing — one sentence, said in front of Tunde, once a week: "you do what Grandma says."

The outcome at eight weeks. The shouting reduced substantially; the grandmother reported that "he listens better", which in practice meant that she had stopped repeating herself and started using the formula; the hearing test was normal; the language was at age level in Yoruba and improving in English at nursery. The mother and the grandmother had one argument, in the third week, about the rules, and settled it without the therapist.

What the case teaches. That "he does not listen" is usually a statement about a household rather than a child; that the caregiver with the least authority is often doing the most work; that the intervention's site is the coordination between adults, not the child's behaviour; and that the language advice for a multilingual household is not subtraction.

4.5 Annotated transcript: the family meeting about five rules

Twelve turns from the family meeting in the case above, in which the therapist builds an agreement between three adults who have been contradicting each other for eighteen months.

T1 Therapist: I want to make a list of what actually happens now, not what should happen. Grandma, what are your rules for him in the day?
T2 Grandmother: No going outside. He eats at the table. He sleeps at one.
T3 Therapist: (writing) And Yemi, when you are here on Sunday?
T4 Mother: He can play in the yard. And he eats when he is hungry, I do not force him.
T5 Therapist: (writing, then turning the sheet) So there are two sets, and both of you are doing what you think is right. Who decides when they disagree?
T6 Grandmother: (pause) She is the mother.
T7 Mother: (pause) But I am not here.
T8 Therapist: So the person who is here has the rules of the person who is not here. That is very hard for both of you, and it is also very confusing for a two-year-old.
T9 Grandmother: (nods) He knows. He goes to her when I say no.
T10 Therapist: That is exactly it. He has learned that there are two answers. Can we agree five rules that are the same whoever is with him, and let everything else be each person's own business?
T11 Mother: (to grandmother) Which five?
T12 Therapist: (quietly) That is the question. You two decide, and I will write them down, and we will give the fifth to Kola when he comes.

Annotations. T1 asks for the actual, not the ideal, and addresses the caregiver with the most practice rather than the parents with the most authority. The choice is deliberate: it credits the work before it asks for change.

T2–T4 produce the two rule systems without conflict, because the therapist is writing rather than judging. The sheet is visible to both, which means neither woman is being asked to hold her rules in her head against the other.

T5 is the question the household has avoided for eighteen months, and it is asked as a question about the household, not about either woman's competence. T6–T7 are the answer, and the sequence is the finding: the grandmother concedes authority and the mother concedes presence, and neither had said so aloud.

T8 is the reformulation, and it is the session's turning point. It names the difficulty for both women, and it introduces the child's experience as the reason for change — which converts a power negotiation into a shared problem.

T9 is the grandmother's own evidence, offered freely once the conflict has been named, and it is better evidence than anything the therapist could have produced. The child "goes to her when I say no" is the two-year-old's solution to the household's problem, described by the person who has watched it.

T10 is the intervention: five rules, agreed, with everything else left as each person's business. The "five" is chosen deliberately — small enough to keep, large enough to cover safety — and the "everything else is your own business" is what makes agreement possible.

T11–T12 hand the authorship to the two women, and the therapist's last sentence adds the father without making him the absent problem. The fifth rule is "given to Kola", which enrols him and does not blame him.

What the trainee should notice. The therapist made no behavioural recommendation, gave no parenting advice, and did not name the grandmother's methods as wrong. She built a structure in which the household could agree, and the agreement — not the technique — is what changed the shouting.

4.5b The nine words a caregiver needs

A short section on the vocabulary of the second year, because the words are the intervention.

"You may not. You may be angry." The pair that separates the behaviour from the feeling, and the earliest lesson the child receives that both can be true.

"I will wait." The sentence for the peak of a tantrum: presence without negotiation.

"You choose." The smallest autonomy, offered where it is real: which cup, which book, which way to the car.

"Show me." The instruction that replaces "use your words" for a child who does not yet have them, and that teaches the caregiver to look for the message rather than the misbehaviour.

"That was hard, and you did it." The sentence that builds the child's account of himself as someone who manages difficulty, which is the second year's real prize.

"I am sorry I shouted." The sentence that models repair, and that most caregivers have never heard from an adult themselves. Its effect on a two-year-old is small and its effect on a household is large, because it teaches everyone in the room that repair is possible.

"What does he need?" The question the caregiver asks themselves instead of "why is he doing this?", and the one the therapist is trying to install.

"Who else says something different?" The therapist's question, not the caregiver's, and the one that turns a child's behaviour into a household's coordination problem, which is the difference between a week of advice and a change in the household's arrangements.

"We will do it the same way." The household's sentence, said by each caregiver to the child, and the smallest possible statement of an agreed structure.

How the nine are taught. Not as a list to be memorised, but one at a time, in the family's language, rehearsed in the session, and written on the page the family takes home. Three of the nine, chosen by the family, are enough for a fortnight; the rest come later, and some never do.

4.6 Laboratory: the formula and the rules map

Sixty minutes, in threes.

Task one — the formula under pressure (twenty minutes). Three scenarios, each played twice: first as a tired parent at the end of a day (the therapist plays the parent badly, on purpose), then using the formula. The observer records the three parts, the tone, and whether the parent explained.

Task two — the rules map (twenty-five minutes). Each student maps a household they know: who is present, what each person's rules are, where the rules contradict, and who decides when they do. Then write the question that would open the conversation, in the family's language.

Task three — the five rules (fifteen minutes). For the mapped household, draft five rules that all caregivers could keep, and mark which one is the safety rule. The test: could a grandmother, a mother and a nursery all keep them?

Marking rubric.

CriterionWeightFull marks
The formula30%Feeling named, limit held without argument, alternative offered; quiet and repeated
Tone and restraint20%No lecture, no bargain, no threat, no shaming
The rules map25%Every caregiver covered; contradictions named; the deciding question identified
Five rules15%Keepable by all caregivers; one is a safety rule; the child could understand each
Language advice10%Multilingual household supported rather than simplified; comprehension assessed first

The debrief question. "Which part of the formula did you drop when the child escalated?" Almost everyone drops the alternative, and the discussion of why — because it feels like a reward, because the parent is angry — is the week's most useful ten minutes.

4.6a When the household cannot agree

The case in 4.4 went well; most do not, and the failures are instructive.

The household that cannot agree because the disagreement is about something else. A couple who argue about whether their three-year-old may sleep in their bed. The rules map shows the disagreement is not about the child: the mother's own mother slept beside her until she was six, and the father's family regards a child in the marital bed as a boundary violation. The therapist who helps them agree about the child without naming the difference in their own histories will produce a rule that fails in a fortnight. The intervention is the history question, asked of each parent separately: "where did you learn what a family does at night?"

The household where the person with authority is not present. A father who works abroad and who telephones every evening with instructions. The mother's rules are superseded nightly, and the child has learned that the telephone is the real authority. The intervention is a single conversation with the father, scheduled, in which the five rules are agreed and his role is redefined: not the nightly auditor, but the endorser of the mother's authority, in one sentence said to the child. Fathers who agree to this change the household more than any other single intervention in this week's material.

The household where the caregiver is exhausted. A grandmother caring for two grandchildren under four while her daughter works, with no help and a bad knee. The five rules are agreed in ten minutes and abandoned by the second week, not because the household is uncoordinated but because the caregiver has nothing left. The intervention is not the rules; it is the relief — one afternoon a week, a nursery place, a neighbour — and the rules only work afterwards.

The household where the disagreement is being conducted through the child. Each parent has a rule, and the child is the site of the argument; the toddler's "he lets me" and "she is strict" is the household's conflict in a two-year-old's vocabulary. The intervention is the same as in the first case: the adults' disagreement is named, the child is removed from the argument, and the five rules are agreed as a truce rather than as a philosophy.

The common lesson. In every failure the technique was taught and the structure was not built. The formula and the five rules are instruments; they require a household that can agree, a caregiver who has capacity, and an authority structure in which the person doing the work has the standing to do it. A therapist who teaches the instrument without the structure has taught a technique the family will fail to use, and the family will conclude that they have failed.

4.7 Six errors in toddler work

  1. The advice avalanche. Five techniques offered in one session, none of them practised. Fix: one formula, one rehearsal, one week.
  2. The child as the problem. The assessment focused on the toddler's behaviour and the household left unexamined. Fix: the rules map, before any behavioural plan.
  3. The authority vacuum. A plan that requires the parents to enforce rules they are not present to enforce. Fix: work through the caregiver who is there, and have the parents endorse that authority aloud.
  4. The missed hearing test. A language delay assessed and managed without audiology. Fix: hearing first, every time.
  5. The subtractive language advice. Telling a multilingual household to speak one language. Fix: each caregiver speaks their strongest language; increase sustained talk; assess comprehension.
  6. The shaming correction. Any intervention that produces shame in the child or the caregiver. Fix: the limit without humiliation, applied to the adults too.

4.8 Culture, faith and the second year

Where the week's material travels well. The developmental facts about autonomy, language and limits are robust, and the clinical claim — that a household's coordination matters more than any single technique — is more obviously true outside the Western nuclear family than within it, because more households are multi-caregiver.

Where it strains, three ways.

The autonomy value. Erikson's stage names autonomy as the outcome, and many households name obedience. A child who is taught to comply first and choose later is not delayed; the family's curriculum is different. The therapist's task is to distinguish a curriculum from a problem: a household that teaches obedience and also allows the child to be a person is doing both, and a household that punishes every assertion is producing the shame the stage warns about. The distinction is behavioural, not cultural.

The physical punishment question. Many families use physical punishment, and the evidence on its effects is negative and consistent. The therapist's obligation is to state the evidence plainly, to be clear about the legal position where it applies, and to offer an alternative that the caregiver can actually use — which is the limit formula, taught with respect for the caregiver's intention. Moralising does not change behaviour; a workable alternative sometimes does.

The multi-caregiver household as the norm. The literature's advice assumes a parent who is present and has authority. In households where a grandmother, an aunt or a paid helper does the care, the advice must be addressed to them, and the parents' role must be defined rather than assumed. This is the week's central cultural point, and it is the reason the case is written the way it is.

Faith and the second year. Naming, dedication, first prayer, first fast, and the child's introduction to the community's rules are all part of the second and third years in religious households. The therapist should ask what the household expects of a two-year-old in religious terms, because the answer often contains a standard the parents are failing by and a resource they have not used — a grandparent, a community, a routine.

DEBATE — "Physical punishment is a cultural practice and a therapist should not impose a view about it." Argue both sides, and say what the therapist is professionally obliged to do.

4.9 Evidence note: robust development, contested practice

Well established. The sequence of language milestones, with wide ranges; the importance of comprehension as against production in the differential; the effectiveness of hearing assessment as the first step; the association between sustained, contingent talk and language development; the association between consistent, non-humiliating limit-setting and later self-regulation; and the negative association between physical punishment and child outcomes, which holds across cultural contexts in the research, though the effect sizes vary.

Contested. Whether the vocabulary count or the comprehension assessment is the better screen; the optimal amount of screen exposure in the second year, where the evidence is clearer about displacement of interaction than about the screens themselves; the effectiveness of parenting programmes delivered in groups versus individually; and the extent to which findings on physical punishment are confounded by the family contexts in which it occurs, which is the strongest version of the sceptical case and does not overturn the association.

What the popular literature gets wrong. That the "terrible twos" are a period to be survived rather than a developmental achievement; that a child who does not speak by two is "just lazy"; that bilingual households should simplify; and that consistency means rigidity. Each is common in the advice families receive, and each requires correction.

What the practitioner may claim. "The second year is the beginning of the self, and the household's task is to hold limits without humiliating the child; language delay is assessed by hearing, comprehension and play, not by word counts; and the intervention that works is an agreement between the adults who actually provide the care." Each claim is defensible.

4.4a The same household, six months later: what changed and what did not

A follow-up section, because the course's cases should show the ordinary unevenness of clinical change.

What changed. The shouting at the grandmother reduced from "several times a day" to "maybe twice a week". The five rules were in use: the grandmother could recite them, the mother could, and Kola could recite three of them. Tunde's language was at age level in Yoruba. The grandmother and the mother had stopped contradicting each other in front of the child, mostly.

What did not change. The parents were still absent during the week. The uncle still gave sweets, which nobody had included in the five rules, and which produced one predictable argument every Sunday. The grandmother still believed that the mother was "too soft", and said so to the therapist, once, when the mother was not in the room. And the household's crowding had worsened, with a cousin moving in.

What the therapist did with the things that did not change. Three decisions, each recorded. The uncle's sweets were left alone: they were not a safety issue, and the household had chosen five rules that did not include them, which is what "everything else is each person's own business" means in practice. The grandmother's view of the mother was noted in supervision as a likely source of future difficulty, and not raised with her, because raising it would have required the therapist to take a side. And the cousin's arrival was added to the map, with one question to the grandmother: "what will be different for Tunde now that there is one more person in the room?"

Why this section exists. Students reading case studies learn that therapy produces change in the areas it addresses and leaves the rest, and that a therapist who tries to change everything produces a family who comply in the room and revert at home. The discipline is to choose the smallest change that matters, get it into the household's routine, and leave the rest to the household.

4.4b Two toddlers, two very different assessments

A comparison, to make the differential concrete.

Toddler one. Twenty-eight months. Twenty words. Understands everything: fetches named objects, follows two-step instructions, points to pictures on request. Uses gesture and vocalisation richly. Plays symbolically, feeding a doll. Hearing untested; family history of late talking (his father spoke at three).

Assessment: expressive language delay, with good comprehension and symbolic play. Action: hearing test (normal); language stimulation advice; speech and language therapy referral; review in three months. Prognosis: good, and the family should be told so plainly.

Toddler two. Twenty-eight months. Twenty words. Does not follow instructions unless they are accompanied by a gesture; does not point to show, only to obtain; does not bring objects to share; plays repetitively with one toy; distressed by a change of route to the nursery.

Assessment: the language delay is accompanied by differences in social communication and flexibility, and the differential now includes a broader developmental assessment. Action: audiology, paediatric referral, and a speech and language assessment; the family work continues alongside, with attention to the parents' own distress and to the household's routines.

What distinguishes them. Not the word count — both have twenty — but comprehension, gesture, shared attention, symbolic play and flexibility. The comparison is the reason the week teaches an assessment sequence rather than a vocabulary target.

The conversation with the parents of toddler two. The hardest conversation in this week's material. What to do: name what is observed without naming a diagnosis; state what will be assessed and why; say what will not change, which is the parents' relationship with the child; give one thing to do this week that is useful whatever the outcome (a daily ten minutes of following the child's lead); and set the next appointment before they leave. What not to do: reassure falsely ("he will catch up"), speculate about causes, or deliver a diagnosis in a corridor.

4.5a The language history, asked as a conversation

Six questions, with what each produces, because the history is where the differential is decided.

One — "who speaks to him, in what language, and how much of the day?" Produces: the exposure map, and the frequent finding that the child hears commands in three languages and conversation in none.

Two — "when he makes a sound, what happens?" Produces: the contingency of the response. A household that does not answer the child's sounds is not depriving him; it is not yet in conversation with him, and that is changeable in a week.

Three — "what does he understand that he does not say?" Produces: the comprehension-production gap, which is the single most useful piece of information in the differential.

Four — "does he point, and does he look at you when he points?" Produces: shared attention, the marker that distinguishes a language delay from a broader developmental difference.

Five — "is he different at nursery?" Produces: the setting comparison, which identifies the child who is silent in one place and fluent in another.

Six — "what did you notice first, and when?" Produces: the onset and the parents' own chronology, which is often more accurate than the referral.

How the six are used. In a single conversation of fifteen minutes, in the family's language, with the child present so that the answers can be checked against what the therapist sees. The written summary goes in the record with the hearing status, the comprehension judgement, and the referral decision — because those three are what the next clinician will need.

4.10 Key terms

TermDefinition
Autonomy versus shame and doubtErikson's second stage: the self that can act, or the self that is judged
Developmental function of "no"The word establishes separateness and the first experience of agency
Language explosionThe growth from about fifty words to three hundred and to two-word combinations in the second year
Comprehension–production gapUnderstanding more than one can say; the key variable in the language differential
Late talkerNormal variation with good comprehension and often a family history
Shared attentionFollowing and directing another's attention to an object; a marker distinguishing types of delay
Symbolic playUsing one object for another; the foundation of imagination and narrative
Limit formulaName the feeling, hold the limit, offer the alternative
Rules mapA drawing of every caregiver's rules and the points of contradiction
Five rulesA deliberately small agreed set that a multi-caregiver household can keep
Subtractive adviceTelling a multilingual household to abandon a language; not supported by evidence
CoordinationThe household's agreement about who decides and what the rules are

4.11 Self-test

  1. What is the developmental function of the word "no", and what happens in a household that cannot tolerate it?
  2. Give the three parts of the limit formula and explain why the order matters.
  3. Name the five-way differential for language delay and the assessment that decides it.
  4. Why is comprehension more useful than production in the assessment?
  5. What is the "rules map" and what does it reveal?
  6. Why is the multi-caregiver household the commonest presentation in toddler work?
  7. What does the evidence say about physical punishment, and what is the therapist obliged to do?
  8. What distinguishes a language delay from a broader developmental difference?

Answers. 1. It establishes separateness and agency; a household that cannot tolerate it produces either a compliant child or an escalating one, and both present in clinics. 2. Name the feeling, hold the limit, offer the alternative; the order matters because a distressed child cannot process a limit until understood, a limit without the feeling teaches that feelings are inadmissible, and an alternative gives the refusal somewhere to go. 3. Late talker; multilingual; hearing loss; global delay; autism spectrum presentation — decided by audiology, comprehension assessment, play observation and the language history. 4. Because it separates a child who cannot produce from a child who cannot understand, which are different clinical situations and different prognoses. 5. A drawing of every caregiver's rules and their contradictions; it reveals that "he does not listen" is usually a household's uncoordinated rules. 6. Because most households with toddlers have more than two caregivers, and the literature's advice assumes a parent present with authority. 7. The association with negative outcomes is consistent across contexts; the therapist is obliged to state the evidence, be clear about the legal position, and offer a workable alternative rather than moralise. 8. Shared attention, gesture, symbolic play and flexibility — not the vocabulary count.

4.12 Reflective task

Write down five rules that were in force in the household where you grew up. Then write the five rules you would want in a household with a two-year-old. Compare them, and write three sentences on what the comparison tells you about your own developmental curriculum — the assumptions you will bring into a family's living room without noticing. Bring it to the seminar. The exercise is the week's cultural material applied to the practitioner, and most students discover at least one rule they have been enforcing in other people's houses without knowing where it came from.

4.13 Readings for this week

  • Erikson (1963), Childhood and Society, the second stage, read alongside the critique of the stages as culturally located.
  • Lieven (1994) or Tomasello (2003), Constructing a Language, for the language development this week relies on.
  • Rescorla (2011), on late talkers, for the evidence about prognosis and the case for early referral.
  • Webster-Stratton (2005), on parenting programmes for young children, for the evidence behind the limit formula.
  • Gershoff (2002, 2016), the meta-analyses on physical punishment, read carefully, including the critique.
  • Rogoff (2003), again, for the multi-caregiver household.

Read Rogoff before the parenting literature; the corrective first. For the case analysis, take the language history of a real child (anonymised) and defend your differential in three sentences. The reading-log question this week is: "whose rules was I raised by, and how do they appear in my clinical advice?"

Next: Week 5 — Early childhood: play, theory of mind, initiative

Week 5 — Early childhood: play, theory of mind, initiative

Developmental tasks. Initiative versus guilt (Erikson, roughly three to six); the flowering of pretend play; theory of mind (the understanding that another person can hold a belief that is different from the truth, typically consolidating between four and five); moral reasoning that begins as consequence-based and slowly becomes intention-based; and school readiness, which is less about letters than about the capacity to hold a plan, wait, and recover from frustration.

Play is the work. Play develops language, self-regulation, perspective-taking, and the capacity to tolerate frustration. It is also the child's first research method: a child who pretends is running experiments on what other minds contain. Children who have experienced adversity often play less symbolically and more repetitively — a clinical observation that underpins play therapy (MFT 727) and that a family therapist must be able to read, because the playroom is often the first place a four-year-old tells the truth.

The clinical skill: observing play without hijacking it. Describe what the child does; do not direct it, and do not interpret it prematurely ("that must be your father"). Interpretation before trust produces a performance, not a communication. The therapist who says "I wonder if the lion is angry because someone left" has stopped watching and started theorising, and the child will now either agree with the adult or leave.

Why this week sits in a family therapy course. The referring complaint is almost never about play. It is "he is aggressive at school", "she will not separate from her mother", "he does not listen to anyone", "she has started lying". Each of those is a developmental question wearing a behavioural costume, and the assessment that answers it is the same one: what can this child do in play, and who is in the room when she does it?

— A mother brings her four-year-old because the nursery reports that she "plays the same thing every day" and "does not join the others". The mother has recently returned to full-time work after a year at home, and the child's grandmother has moved in. Assessment finds: the child's play is rich but narrow, restricted to one script about a mother leaving and returning; her theory-of-mind performance is age-appropriate; her language is above age; and she separates from her mother without distress but does not greet her on reunion. The problem is not the child's development. It is the family's story about the separation, which the child has been asked to carry alone.

5.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–10The fourth yearWhat changes between three and six, and why the presenting complaint is never about playList the complaints that brought children to their last placement
10–22Initiative versus guiltErikson's pair; the difference between a child who starts things and one who asks permissionRewrite four adult sentences that produce guilt
22–38Play as developmentThe six levels of play, from sensorimotor to cooperative dramaCode ninety seconds of video against the six levels
38–52Theory of mindThe false-belief task, demonstrated live, with a volunteerPredict a four-year-old's answer and explain the error
52–64Moral reasoningConsequence-based to intention-based; the broken-cup storiesInterview a child about a small transgression and code the reasoning
64–76Observing without hijackingThe four skills, demonstrated with a puppet, then with a volunteerPractise ten minutes of child-led play; count their own questions
76–86Reading the playRepetition, constriction, and the differential between adversity, autism, and ordinary developmentWrite one paragraph on what the play does not show
86–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Play is the part of this week that students most want to do, and the part they most often do badly, because doing it well means doing very little. The lecture therefore has to make boredom respectable. A therapist who can sit through ten minutes of a child putting the same bear in the same box and say only "the bear is in the box" has acquired a clinical skill that takes longer to learn than any technique in this book. The demonstration should include the lecturer failing — asking a question, making a suggestion — and then naming the impulse that produced it.

The sentence the lecture must land. "A child's play is not a message to be decoded by the adult; it is a conversation the child is having with herself, and your job is to be a good listener rather than a clever one."

5.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Initiative versus guilt. Erikson's third stage. The child now has a body that can do things, a language that can propose things, and a will that can insist on things. The developmental gain is the capacity to start — a game, a plan, a joke, an argument. The developmental risk is guilt: the conviction that wanting to start something is itself a fault. The family's contribution is decisive and it is made in small sentences. "Not now" repeated a hundred times produces a child who stops proposing. "Tell me your plan and we will see" produces a child who proposes.

The clinical translation is precise: when a five-year-old cannot initiate, the assessment is not only of the child but of the last two years of responses to initiation. Ask the parents what happens when the child asks for something. The answer is usually a rule about the household's exhaustion rather than about the child.

2. Pretend play as the engine of self-regulation. In pretend play the child holds two things in mind at once — the real situation and the imagined one — and this dual representation is the same capacity that later allows a child to hold a rule in mind while being provoked. Vygotsky's observation that play is "the leading source of development" rests on this. The implication for assessment is that the quality of a child's pretend play predicts school adjustment better than the child's vocabulary.

3. Theory of mind. Between roughly three and five the child moves from a "copy" theory of mind (everyone sees what I see, knows what I know) to a representational one (people act on their beliefs, and beliefs can be false). This is measured by the false-belief task, and it is the developmental foundation of every clinical skill the child will ever need: empathy, deception, tact, negotiation, and the understanding that a parent can be angry and loving at once.

4. Moral reasoning begins as consequence-based. Piaget's paired stories: a child who breaks one cup while helping versus a child who breaks fifteen while stealing jam. Children under about seven judge the first child as naughtier, because the outcome is larger. They are not being immoral; they are reasoning with the only tool they have. The clinical consequence: do not ask a five-year-old "was that kind?", ask "what happened next?" The first question is beyond the stage; the second builds the stage.

5. School readiness is regulation, not letters. The strongest predictors of a good first year are the capacity to follow a two-step instruction, to wait, to recover from a small frustration, and to seek help from an adult. A child who arrives at school able to do those four things will learn to read. A child who arrives knowing the alphabet but not able to wait will not. For the family therapist this reframes the "school readiness" conversation from coaching to structure: bedtime, breakfast, a predictable goodbye, and an adult who is reliably there at the gate.

5.2a Theory of mind: five tasks and what failure means clinically

Theory of mind is not a single achievement. It is a set of understandings that appear in a rough order, and knowing which one a child has tells the therapist what kind of conversation is possible with her.

Task 1 — Desire (18–24 months). Two bowls, one with biscuits, one with broccoli. The adult says she wants the broccoli and asks for the bowl. The child who hands over the broccoli has understood that the adult's desire differs from her own. Failure here is not yet diagnostic of anything except age.

Task 2 — Visual perspective (2–3 years). A card with a cat on one side and a dog on the other, held between two people. "What do you see?" "What do I see?" The child who answers "a cat" to both has not yet grasped that seeing is situated. This is the basis of the clinician's later ability to notice that two family members are describing the same event from different positions.

Task 3 — False belief (4–5 years). The classic: a doll puts chocolate in a cupboard and leaves; a second doll moves it. "Where will the first doll look?" The child who says "in the new place" has answered from her own knowledge. This is the task that most reliably indexes the shift.

Task 4 — Appearance versus reality (4–5 years). A sponge painted to look like a rock. "What does it look like? What is it really?" The child who cannot hold both at once will also struggle with the clinical insight that a family looks one way and is another.

Task 5 — Second-order belief (6–8 years). "She thinks that he thinks…" This is the machinery of social strategy, and its arrival is why middle childhood is the age of secrets, clubs, and exclusion.

What failure means, clinically. A child who fails the false-belief task at four is unremarkable. The same child at six, with good language, is a reason to look further — and the look is a developmental assessment, not a diagnosis from the therapy room. Three cautions.

  • Language. Every one of these tasks is delivered in language. A child with a language delay will fail tasks she may understand. Test in the child's stronger language, and if the therapist cannot, bring an interpreter rather than a simplified script.
  • Culture. The tasks assume a particular conversational convention — that an adult asking a question to which they know the answer is testing, not conversing. Children who have been taught that it is rude to contradict an adult may answer "the cupboard" because the adult is looking at it.
  • Trauma and vigilance. A child who has had to track a volatile adult's mood may outperform on second-order tasks and underperform on first-order ones, because vigilance trains attention to intention rather than to belief. This is not a deficit; it is an adaptation that costs something elsewhere.

The clinical use of the tasks. Not to score the child, but to calibrate the session. A four-year-old who fails false belief cannot be asked to "think how your mother felt". She can be asked to "show me with the dolls". The therapist who knows where the child is on this ladder stops asking questions the child cannot answer, which is the commonest way well-meaning adults make small children feel stupid.

5.2b Six levels of play and what each one tells you

Play has a developmental ladder, and each rung is a piece of assessment data. The ladder below is adapted from the classic social-participation scheme, with the clinical read added, because the read is what a therapist needs.

LevelWhat it looks likeAge it is typicalWhat it tells the clinician
1. Sensorimotor / practiceRepetitive movement: banging, filling, pouring, spinning0–18 monthsStill present at five only if development is disrupted or the child is regulating
2. SolitaryPlaying alone, absorbed, not attending to others2–3 yearsAt six, in a busy room, this is information about the child's state, not her sociability
3. OnlookerWatching others play, asking questions, not joining2–4 yearsAt six, suggests the child has been excluded or has learned that joining is unsafe
4. ParallelPlaying beside another child with the same materials, without exchange3–4 yearsA stable level at six suggests the child cannot yet negotiate
5. AssociativeSharing materials, borrowing, no shared goal4–5 yearsThe level at which most nursery complaints are actually generated
6. Cooperative / dramaticShared goal, assigned roles, rules, a story that survives conflict5–7 yearsThe best single predictor of school adjustment in this age band

Reading the ladder rather than a score. The question is never "what level is this child at?" but "what does this child do when the level is too hard?" A child who retreats to level 1 under stress is regulating. A child who attacks is also regulating, badly. A child who goes blank has stopped regulating, and that is the observation that should change the therapist's next move.

Three patterns that matter clinically.

Constriction. The child plays, richly, but inside one script. The script is repeated with minor variations, and any attempt to introduce a new element is refused. Constriction is common after a disruption the child could not narrate — a separation, a death, a move, a new baby, a hospital stay. The script is not a symptom to be removed; it is the child's only available formulation, and the clinical task is to help it become more flexible rather than to replace it.

Repetition without elaboration. The same act, many times, with no development across sessions. This is the pattern that most often indicates adversity, and it is also the pattern of autistic play when the repetition is sensory rather than narrative. The differential is not made in the playroom alone: it is made with a developmental history, an observation of communication, and a proper assessment.

Play that stops at the door. The child plays fluently at nursery and cannot play at home, or the reverse. This is almost always a fact about the household's state rather than the child's capacity, and it is the observation that turns a child referral into a family one.

What to record. Five lines, in this order: what the child chose; whether the play had a story; how the child responded to a new element; what happened when the play was interrupted; and how the child ended. Those five lines are more useful than a page of adjectives, and they can be compared across sessions by two different therapists.

5.3 Observing play without hijacking it

The method is four skills and one prohibition. It is taught here, in the second year of life course, because it is the foundation of every later child-facing technique and because it is the hardest thing in this book to do for ten consecutive minutes.

Skill 1 — Track. Sit at the child's level, at a slight angle rather than opposite, and let your eyes follow the child's hands rather than her face. Tracking communicates the thing that cannot be said: I am interested in what you are doing, not in what you will produce for me.

Skill 2 — Describe. Say what the child is doing, in the present tense, without evaluation. "You are putting the small one inside the big one." Not "good job", not "what a clever tower", not "is that a house?" Description gives the child a narrator, and the narrator's vocabulary becomes the child's.

Skill 3 — Reflect. When the child speaks, say it back, slightly expanded. Child: "Bear fall." Adult: "The bear fell down and he is stuck." This is the single most efficient language intervention available to a non-specialist, and it is also the most efficient way to show a child that she has been heard.

Skill 4 — Imitate. Copy one thing the child does, exactly, and wait. Imitation is a turn-taking offer that does not require words, and for a child who finds adults unpredictable it is the least threatening thing an adult can do.

The prohibition — do not interpret. No "I wonder if…", no "that must be your daddy", no "you seem angry". There are three reasons. The first is epistemic: at this age the therapist does not know, and a confident wrong interpretation teaches the child that adults invent meanings. The second is interactional: an interpretation recruits the child into confirming the adult's theory, which converts play into a performance. The third is developmental: interpretation requires exactly the representational capacity the child is still acquiring.

The one exception. If the child's play depicts something dangerous and the child addresses the therapist directly — "the man is coming" — the therapist may reflect the child's own words and stay alongside: "The man is coming and you are holding the door." That is still not interpretation. It is company.

— Ten minutes of child-led play with a volunteer child or a peer. Your only tools: describe, reflect, imitate, and set one limit. A partner times you and tallies your questions, suggestions, and praise. Aim for fewer than three questions, fewer than two suggestions, and — the hardest target — no praise at all. Debrief on the impulse behind each one.

Why "no praise" is in the exercise. Praise evaluates, and evaluation moves the child's attention from the activity to the adult's face. A child who is praised for a drawing learns to check whether the drawing is acceptable. A child who is described learns to look at the drawing. The exercise is not a claim that praise is harmful; it is a demonstration of how much of an adult's ordinary talk is evaluation, and how quickly a child adapts to it.

5.3a Setting a limit in the playroom

Sooner or later the child will do something that cannot continue: hit, throw at the window, climb the shelf, hurt herself. The limit is part of the method, not a failure of it, and it is set in four moves.

Move 1 — Stop the action, not the child. A hand on the toy, not on the child. "I am going to hold the block."

Move 2 — Name the limit as a rule of the room. "In this room we do not throw blocks at people." The rule belongs to the room, not to the therapist's mood, and it will be the same next week.

Move 3 — Name the feeling and offer the alternative in one breath. "You wanted me to look and I was writing. You can throw the block at the floor, or you can say 'look'." Two options, both acceptable, one of them the one you want.

Move 4 — Stay. Do not leave, do not lecture, do not withdraw warmth. The child's learning is that the relationship survives the limit. This is the same lesson as Week 4's limit formula, at a different age and with a different vocabulary, and the continuity is the point: the family that learned "you may be angry" at two is being asked to say the same thing at four.

What not to do. Do not ask "why did you do that?" — a four-year-old cannot answer and will invent. Do not remove the toy as punishment unless the rule requires it. Do not say "I am not angry" if you are, because children read faces more accurately than words at this age, and the mismatch teaches them to distrust their reading.

5.4 Three procedures for the clinic

Procedure 1 — The five-minute play observation, with the parents watching. Before any history, ask the parents to sit at the side of the room and watch the therapist play with the child for five minutes. Then ask one question: "what did you notice?" The parents' answer is the assessment. Some will describe the child's cleverness; some will describe the child's problems; some will describe nothing and ask what they were supposed to see. Each answer tells the therapist where the family's attention lives, and the next move is to give them one thing to look for next time.

Procedure 2 — The false-belief demonstration, used as a conversation. With a child of four or five, use two dolls and a small box in front of the parents. Run the task once. Then say to the parents, in front of the child and without scoring it, "she answered from what she knows; most children her age do, and it changes in the next year or so". The purpose is not diagnosis. It is to give the parents a developmental fact that reframes a behaviour they have been treating as defiance — usually the child's insistence that her account of an event is the only account.

Procedure 3 — The interrupted-play observation. Ask the parent to play with the child for five minutes, then to stop and say "we have to go now". Watch what happens in the next ninety seconds. Three things are being observed: whether the child can use the parent's face to regulate, whether the parent offers a bridge ("one more turn, then shoes"), and how quickly the pair recover. This ninety-second window is more informative than an hour of interview, and it takes no equipment.

5.5 The first session with a four-year-old

Structure the hour so that the child is present for the part that concerns her and absent for the part that does not.

Phase 1 — The room (5 minutes). Child and parents enter together. Name the rules of the room in the child's language: "we stay in this room, we do not hurt, and you choose what we play". The rules are for the child; the parents are also listening, and they are learning what a rule sounds like when it is not a threat.

Phase 2 — Play with the child, parents watching (15 minutes). Therapist plays, using the four skills. Parents sit where the child can see them. This is simultaneously assessment, demonstration, and — for many parents — the first time they have watched an adult be interested in their child without correcting her.

Phase 3 — The child leaves (5 minutes). Do not sneak her out. Say what is happening: "you are going to wait with Auntie Bisi while I talk to your mum and dad." A child who is told what is happening learns that adults can be trusted with information.

Phase 4 — Debrief with the parents (25 minutes). Three questions, in order. What did you notice? What does this look like at home? What have you already tried? Then one piece of developmental information, given plainly, that bears on the referral.

Phase 5 — One task (10 minutes). Not a programme. One thing: usually ten minutes of child-led play a day, or one change to the goodbye, or one sentence to use instead of the sentence they are using. Write it down. Ask them to notice what happens, not to succeed.

Why the order cannot be reversed. If the parents are interviewed first, they arrive at the play phase having already told the therapist what is wrong, and the therapist will see it. The five minutes of uncontaminated observation is the most valuable data in the session, and it is lost the moment the therapist has a theory.

5.6 Case: the child who plays the same thing every day

Referral. Zainab, four years and two months, brought by her mother, Nkechi, 33, a bank officer, on the recommendation of the nursery. The nursery's written note says: "Zainab plays the same game every day. She does not join group activities. She is very bright but very alone."

Household. Mother Nkechi, father Emeka, 37, an engineer who travels two weeks in four. Paternal grandmother, 61, moved in eight months ago after a fall. A baby brother, six months old. A house help, 19, who does the school run. Languages: Igbo at home, English at nursery; Zainab's English is fluent and her Igbo comprehension is good.

History. Pregnancy and birth unremarkable; milestones on time; no illnesses; hearing tested at three, normal. The change the mother can date precisely: eight months ago, when the grandmother moved in and the mother returned to work within the same month. "Before that she was the one who talked to everybody. Now she talks to nobody."

The play observation. Zainab chooses the doll's house. She puts the mother doll in the car and drives it to the edge of the rug. She says, quietly, "bye". She waits. She drives back. She says, "I'm home". She does this eleven times in ten minutes, with the same two sentences, at the same pace. The therapist describes, reflects, imitates the driving once. On the twelfth repetition the therapist drives her own small car to the other side of the rug and parks it. Zainab looks at it, does not touch it, and continues.

At minute nine the therapist says only: "the mama keeps coming back." Zainab stops, looks at the therapist for perhaps two seconds, and says: "she always comes back." Then she resumes.

What the play shows. A child with intact symbolic capacity, a coherent narrative, age-appropriate language, and a single theme: departure and return. The theme is not a symptom. It is a formulation — the child's working model of the thing that changed, expressed in the only medium available to her. The constriction is the finding: eleven repetitions, no elaboration, no new element tolerated.

What the play does not show. It does not show autism. Zainab's communication is reciprocal, her language is above age, her play is narrative rather than sensory, and her eye contact — brief, but present and socially timed — is not what autism looks like. It does not show attachment disorder. She separated without distress and greeted her mother on reunion, which is the pattern of a secure child managing a hard change. It does not show trauma in the sense of a single event; it shows a disruption of arrangement, which is a different clinical object.

The differential, written out. Four candidates, and the evidence for and against each.

CandidateForAgainstVerdict
Normal adjustment to changeDated onset; coherent theme; good development otherwiseEight months is long for uncomplicated adjustment; no elaboration at allPlausible but incomplete
Autism spectrumRepetitive play; social isolation at nurseryReciprocal communication; fluent imaginative language; narrative (not sensory) repetitionNot supported
Attachment difficultyReunion greeting is muted; theme of departureNo indiscriminate behaviour; no freezing; separates comfortablyNot supported as a disorder
Family-system disruptionOnset coincides with return to work and a new adult in the house and a new baby; father absent half of each month—Primary formulation

The formulation. Zainab is not ill and does not need a child therapy referral. She has been asked, at four, to hold a change that nobody explained to her, in a household where the adults themselves have not agreed what the change means. Her play is the most articulate statement of the family's situation in the room. The intervention is with the adults, plus a small, precise piece of work with Zainab about goodbyes.

The session with the parents. The therapist shows them nothing of the play — she does not narrate it back as evidence, which would make the child's private material into an exhibit. Instead she asks three questions.

  1. "Who told Zainab that you were going back to work?" (Answer: nobody; it was decided in a conversation the child was not in.)
  2. "What happens at the door in the morning?" (Answer: the house help takes her; the mother leaves before she wakes "because it is easier".)
  3. "When she says 'Mummy, don't go', what do you say?" (Answer: "I have to work, so you can have things." Followed, the mother admits, by leaving the room.)

The intervention, in four parts.

Part 1 — A goodbye with a shape. The mother wakes the child fifteen minutes earlier and does the handover herself. The goodbye has four elements, always in the same order: a warning ("in two minutes I am going"), a physical routine (a hug, then a handover to the grandmother, not to the house help), a sentence ("I always come back, and I will be here before your bath"), and a departure that is not hurried. The mother's objection is predictable and worth answering: "she will cry." She will. The alternative is a child who learns that departures happen without warning, which is exactly what she has learned.

Part 2 — The return is the intervention. The evidence that matters to a four-year-old is not the promise but the arrival. The mother commits to being home before the bath three evenings a week for a month, and to the first five minutes after arriving being with Zainab and nobody else — no phone, no report from the grandmother, no baby in her arms.

Part 3 — The grandmother's authority, said aloud. The therapist asks the mother and the grandmother to agree, in the session, on the two things the grandmother decides without appeal (bedtime, and safety) and the two things the mother decides without appeal (school, and what Zainab is allowed to watch). Then each says to the child, in front of the other, "Grandmother is in charge of bedtime, and I am in charge of school." The sentence is for the adults' benefit as much as the child's: a four-year-old who is caught between two authorities will stop proposing anything, which is the definition of guilt in Erikson's terms.

Part 4 — A play prescription, of a peculiar kind. The mother is asked to play the doll's house game with Zainab, once, for ten minutes, and to let Zainab direct it. She is not asked to add anything. She is asked to notice what her daughter does with the mother doll, and to report one observation next session. This is not play therapy; it is the mother being given access to her child's account of the change without anyone having to interpret it.

5.6a Six weeks later

The mother reports three changes, in her own order: Zainab now cries at the door in the morning, which the mother finds harder and the therapist regards as progress, because a child who protests a departure has decided that the departure is worth protesting; she greets her mother at the door in the evening and takes her hand; and the doll's house game has acquired a new element — a grandmother doll, which Zainab puts in the kitchen and moves, once, to the front door.

The nursery reports she has joined a group of three girls in a game with assigned roles. The repetition has not disappeared. It has become a game the child plays with other children, which is what elaboration looks like at four.

The father, who was on a trip for the first three sessions, attends the fourth by telephone and the fifth in person. He says very little and cries once. The therapist does not interpret the crying. She asks him what he would like his daughter to know about why he travels, and he says, "that I am coming back." The therapist suggests he says it to Zainab, at the door, every time he leaves, and he does.

What would have changed the plan. Three findings would have redirected this case, and the student should be able to name them. If Zainab had failed language comprehension tasks in Igbo as well as English, the referral would have gone to audiology and speech therapy before any family work. If her play had been sensory rather than narrative, with no social referencing, the referral would have gone for a developmental assessment. If the reunion greeting had been absent altogether, with freezing or indiscriminate approach to the house help, the formulation would have shifted toward caregiving disruption and the safeguarding question would have been asked explicitly.

5.7 Transcript: ten minutes of play, annotated

The transcript below is the observation described above, with the therapist's internal reasoning annotated. Read the annotations as the discipline the exercise is trying to install: every line of therapist speech is classified by which of the four skills it uses, and every impulse that was resisted is named.

T1 (Zainab puts the mother doll in the car; says nothing.) Therapist: "The mama is getting into the car." — Description. No question. Note the temptation: "where is she going?" would have converted the child's play into an answer for the adult. T2 (Zainab drives the car to the edge of the rug.) Therapist: "She is driving away." — Description, present tense, short. T3 (Zainab, quietly: "bye".) Therapist: "Bye." — Reflection, at the same volume. Matching volume is a skill; a therapist who says "BYE!" has taken the play away from the child. T4 (Pause of about fifteen seconds. Zainab does not move.) Therapist: (says nothing) — The hardest line in the transcript. The impulse to fill the silence is the therapist's, not the child's. T5 (Zainab drives back. "I'm home.") Therapist: "She came home." — Reflection with a grammatical expansion. The child hears her own sentence completed. T6 (Repetition three.) Therapist: "She went, and she came home." — Description that holds the whole cycle in one sentence, which the child has not yet done aloud. T7 (Repetition seven. The therapist's impulse: "does she always come home?" — resisted.) — A question would have asked the child to state a belief about the future, which is precisely the capacity under construction. Do not test what is being built. T8 (Repetition ten. The therapist drives a second car to the other side of the rug and parks it.) — A non-verbal offer. It changes the scene without requiring the child to accept a suggestion and without claiming a role. If she ignores it, nothing is lost. T9 (Zainab looks at the second car, does not touch it, continues.) Therapist: (nothing) — The child declined. The offer was made correctly and its refusal cost nothing. T10 (Minute nine. "The mama keeps coming back.") — The only interpretive-adjacent statement in the session, and it is a description of the play's pattern, not of the child's feelings. It contains no "I wonder", no "you feel", and no named person from the child's family. T11 (Zainab stops. Looks at the therapist. "She always comes back.") — The child has made a statement about the world. This is the moment the therapist has been working toward for nine minutes, and the correct response is the smallest possible one. T12 Therapist: "She always comes back." — Reflection. Nothing added. No "and mummy always comes back too." The child made a general statement; the therapist does not force it to be about her mother. If the child wants to make that link, she will, in her own time, and probably not in this session.

5.8 Laboratory: the ten-minute observation, marked

Aim. To produce a written observation of a child's play that another clinician could use, and to demonstrate command of the four skills under supervision.

Procedure. In pairs, with a volunteer child (or, where a child is not available, with a peer instructed to play at the level of a four-year-old — a surprisingly demanding role). One student plays for ten minutes using only the four skills and one limit. The second student times the play, tallies questions, suggestions, praise, and interpretations, and writes the five-line record. Roles swap. Then each pair writes a single paragraph answering: "what did this child's play tell me that an interview would not have?"

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
RestraintMore than six questions or three suggestionsThree to six questions, one or two suggestionsFewer than three questions, no suggestions, no praise
DescriptionEvaluative or absentPresent but intermittent, with judgement wordsContinuous, present tense, non-evaluative, matched to the child's attention
Reading the playDescribes the child's personalityDescribes actions onlyNames level, theme, elaboration, and response to a new element
The limitNot set, or set with threat or withdrawalSet, but with explanation and a lectureSet in four moves, relationship intact, play resumed
The recordAdjectives and impressionsActions without structureFive lines in the required order, usable by a second clinician

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The difference between "she was anxious and withdrawn" (an inference with no evidence) and "she played the same two-sentence script eleven times and did not touch the second car" (an observation). Students reliably believe the first is more clinically sophisticated. It is the second that can be checked, taught, and compared.

5.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Interpreting before the child has told you anything. "I wonder if the lion is your father." The child either agrees, which teaches her that adults supply meanings, or stops playing. The error is not that the interpretation is wrong. It is that it is a question disguised as an observation and it recruits the child into the adult's theory.

Error 2 — Turning play into a lesson. Asking the colour of the block, counting the blocks, naming the animal. This is not play and it is not therapy; it is a test administered by someone who has run out of things to say. The child's developmental level is not improved by being quizzed at it.

Error 3 — Using praise as the default response. "Good job!" every thirty seconds trains the child to look at the adult after each action. The child who is watched learns to play; the child who is praised learns to perform. The distinction matters most for children whose households already require performance.

Error 4 — Reading repetition as pathology. Repetition is the mechanism by which this age group consolidates. The question is not whether the play repeats but whether it elaborates across sessions. A therapist who treats the eleventh repetition as a symptom will miss the twelfth, in which the child adds a grandmother doll.

Error 5 — Doing the child work and calling it family work. A weekly play session that never touches the household will produce a calm child who returns to the same arrangement. This is the characteristic error of clinicians trained only in child modalities, and it is why this course teaches play observation inside a family therapy programme: the observation is the door, and the family is the room.

Error 6 — Diagnosing in the playroom. Autism, attachment disorder, ADHD, and "trauma" are not diagnosable from a ten-minute observation, and a therapist who says "this looks like autism" to a parent has made a claim that will govern the family's next two years. Describe what you saw, name what you cannot tell, and refer for the assessment that can.

5.10 Culture, faith and the child who plays

Play is not culturally neutral. The materials in a therapy room — a doll's house, a doctor's kit, a family of small figures — encode a household. A child who has never seen a doll's house will not necessarily be less competent; she will be less fluent with the props. The assessment must therefore include an observation with materials the child knows. In the Nigerian clinic the most useful additions are usually a market stall, cooking pots, a bus, and a church or mosque set, because those are the scripts most children have.

Who plays with whom. In many households the answer is "the other children", and a four-year-old who plays fluently with cousins and neighbours but not with adults is not delayed. The clinical question is whether the child has any setting in which she plays richly. If she does, the household has a resource the therapist can build on rather than a deficit to correct.

Faith and the child's explanations. By five, children in observant households have absorbed explanatory frameworks — prayer, protection, spirits, the will of God — and will use them in play. A child who stages a deliverance scene is not necessarily expressing distress; she may be rehearsing something her household does weekly. Ask the parents what she has been part of before deciding what her play means.

Discipline and initiative. This is the week's sharpest cultural tension. Erikson's "initiative" is cultivated in households that reward a child's proposals and tolerated in households that treat a child's proposal as insubordination. A therapist who measures initiative against a single cultural standard will misread a well-regulated child as inhibited, and will recommend a change the parents experience as an attack on their authority. The clinical question is not "does this child initiate?" but "in what domains is this child permitted to initiate, and what happens to her when she does?"

DEBATE — "The child's play belongs to the child, and the therapist has no right to describe it to the parents." Argue for: the playroom is the one place a child's material is not reported, and showing it to parents converts it into evidence in a case the child did not consent to. Argue against: the parents are the intervention, a therapist who learns something decisive and withholds it is practising on the family, and a child's private play is not confidential in the way an adult's disclosure is. Then take a position, and state the rule you would follow: what, precisely, would you never say to a parent about a child's play, and why?

5.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The association between the quality of pretend play in the preschool years and later social competence and school adjustment is well established in longitudinal work, with effect sizes that are modest but consistent after controlling for language and family income. The false-belief literature is one of the most replicated bodies of findings in developmental psychology, with the four-to-five transition robust across dozens of cultures — though the timing shifts with schooling, sibling number, and the language of testing.

What is weaker. The claim that play causes these gains is much harder to establish than the claim that it correlates with them. Interventions that add play opportunities produce smaller effects than the correlational literature would predict, and the most defensible reading is that play is a context in which existing capacities are consolidated and displayed rather than a training programme that installs them. The clinical implication is modest and useful: observe play because it shows you what the child has, not because ten minutes of it will make her have more.

What is not supported.
