The strong form of the play-therapy claim — that non-directive play sessions alone reliably resolve identified clinical disorders in preschoolers — is not supported by meta-analytic evidence, which finds small effects that shrink when the comparison is an active control. Non-directive play remains a valuable engagement method and a window on the child's representational world. It is not a substitute for changing the household, and this week's case is the argument.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. Constriction in play at four, in a stable household, resolves in most children within a year. The same constriction, in a household that is itself unresolved, tends to persist and to generalise — which is the argument for treating the arrangement rather than the symptom.

5.12 Key terms

Initiative versus guilt — Erikson's third psychosocial stage; the capacity to start things, balanced against the conviction that wanting is a fault.

Pretend (symbolic) play — play in which an object or action stands for something else; the developmental engine of dual representation and self-regulation.

False-belief task — the standard test of whether a child understands that another person can act on a belief that is untrue.

Representational theory of mind — the understanding that people act on beliefs rather than on the world directly.

Constriction — play that is rich but confined to a single, repeated script, with new elements refused.

Elaboration — the developmental marker that matters: the addition of new elements, roles, or resolutions across sessions.

Scaffolding — adult support calibrated to the child's current level, withdrawn as competence grows.

Dual representation — holding the real and the imagined situation in mind at once; the mechanism by which play builds self-regulation.

School readiness — the capacity to follow instructions, wait, recover from frustration, and seek help; a stronger predictor of first-year success than early literacy.

Social referencing — the child's checking of a caregiver's face to decide how to feel about an ambiguous event; the observation that most reliably distinguishes security from its absence in the second and third years.

5.13 Self-test

  1. A four-year-old answers "the cupboard" on a false-belief task even though she watched the chocolate being moved. What has she done, and what should the therapist not conclude?
  2. Give three reasons why interpreting a child's play prematurely is a technical error and not merely a matter of taste.
  3. A nursery reports that a child "will not join in". Name four observations that would distinguish exclusion, shyness, language difficulty, and a developmental difference.
  4. What is the difference between repetition that is consolidating and repetition that is constricted?
  5. Why does the exercise forbid praise, and what does praise do to a child's attention?
  6. A parent says "he knows exactly what he is doing — he is manipulating me". What developmental information changes the conversation, and how would you say it?
  7. In the case of Zainab, name the three findings that would have changed the formulation, and where each would have sent the family.
  8. What is the strongest argument against telling parents what their child did in the playroom?

Answers. (1) She has answered from her own knowledge — she has not yet represented the other's belief as different from the truth, which is normal at four. The therapist should not conclude that she is delayed, dishonest, or unable to understand other people; the task indexes one narrow capacity, in one language, under one conversational convention. (2) It is epistemically unjustified at this age; it recruits the child into confirming the adult's theory, converting play into performance; and it demands the very representational capacity the child is still acquiring. (3) Whether she is invited and refused; whether she watches from the edge (onlooker) or does not attend at all; whether she plays richly in her own language with one other child; and whether her play is narrative or sensory and repetitive. (4) Consolidating repetition elaborates across sessions and tolerates a new element; constricted repetition repeats identically and refuses any addition. The test is across sessions, not within one. (5) Praise evaluates, and evaluation moves attention from the activity to the adult's judgement; a praised child checks the adult, a described child looks at her own work. (6) At four the capacity to hold another's belief in mind is still forming, so "manipulation" — which requires representing what the parent believes and intending to change it — is usually not available; say that plainly, and replace the word with a description of what the child actually did. (7) Failed comprehension in the home language → audiology and speech therapy; sensory rather than narrative repetition with no social referencing → developmental assessment; absent or indiscriminate reunion behaviour → caregiving disruption and the safeguarding question. (8) The child cannot consent, cannot correct the record, and will live with the parents' new knowledge of her private material; the counter-argument is that the parents are the treatment.

5.14 Reflective task

Watch a child between three and six for ten minutes and write the five-line record. Then write, in one paragraph and without naming anyone, what you found yourself wanting to do and did not. Most students find the impulse is to teach, to test, or to be liked. Name yours. Then answer: which of the three would have been hardest for the child to refuse?

5.15 Further reading

  • Piaget, J. The Moral Judgement of the Child — the paired-story method, still the clearest account of consequence-based reasoning.
  • Vygotsky, L. "The role of play in development" — the dual-representation argument, in twenty pages.
  • Wellman, H. The Child's Theory of Mind — the tasks, their order, and their limits.
  • Winnicott, D. W. Playing and Reality — the clinical essay this week's prohibition is drawn from.
  • Slade, A. "Representational play and the therapeutic process" — play as formulation rather than symptom.

Next: Week 6 — Middle childhood: competence, friendship and the school years.

Week 6 — Middle childhood: industry, peers, school

Developmental tasks. Industry versus inferiority (Erikson, roughly six to eleven); the consolidation of mastery through practice and product; the peer group as a structure with its own rules; a self-concept that becomes comparative ("I am good at maths, bad at football"); and moral reasoning that becomes reciprocal — fairness, turn-taking, and the beginning of genuine perspective exchange.

What changes in the family. The child's world widens and the parents' role changes from direct control to monitoring and scaffolding. School becomes a second system with its own rules, its own adults, and its own judgement of the child, and the family–school interface becomes a clinical site in its own right. A child now spends more waking hours with non-relatives than with relatives, which is new for the family and new for the child.

The developmental gift of this age. Middle childhood is the least dramatic period of development and the most consequential for identity, because it is when the child builds an answer to the question "what am I good at?" That answer will organise her adolescence. The clinical stake is therefore not usually crisis; it is the slow accumulation of evidence — in a classroom, on a pitch, at a kitchen table — that the child is less than.

School refusal — a developmental and systemic differential. Ask: is this anxiety, separation difficulty, bullying, a learning difficulty, a family crisis, or a bid to protect a parent? The distinction matters because the same behaviour has opposite treatment implications.

— A nine-year-old refuses school on Mondays only. Developmental enquiry: what happens on Sunday nights? The answer: the parents' weekly argument about money. The child's presence at home on Monday keeps the mother from leaving for work, and the argument stops. The symptom is a Monday-shaped solution to a marital problem — and it is also genuinely distressing to the child.

6.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The quiet ageWhy middle childhood looks uneventful and is decisiveRecall what they were good at at nine, and who told them
8–22Industry versus inferiorityThe mechanics of mastery, and the four ways adults interrupt itIdentify the four from a case vignette
22–36The comparative selfHow "I am good at X" becomes "I am better/worse than Y"Map a nine-year-old's self-description across five domains
36–48Peers and friendshipThe stages of friendship, and what a friend is for at seven, nine, elevenCode two friendship descriptions against the stages
48–62School refusalThe six-way differential, and the seven questions that decide itRun the seven questions on the case and defend a formulation
62–74Monitoring and scaffoldingThe shift from control to oversight, and the crime of over-monitoringRewrite three controlling sentences as monitoring ones
74–84The school as a systemHow to work with a school without becoming its agentDraft a letter to a head teacher
84–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Students arrive believing that middle childhood is the easy placement, because the children talk and the problems are legible. The lecture has to revalue the age: the child is now being judged continuously by strangers, and the household's job is to interpret that judgement without either denying it or amplifying it. The single most useful teaching move is to ask the class what they were told they were bad at between the ages of seven and eleven, and to notice how many of them can still recite it in the original wording, thirty years later.

The sentence the lecture must land. "At this age a child is assembling an answer to the question 'what am I good at?', and she is using other people's faces as her evidence."

6.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Industry versus inferiority. The child now wants to make things and to be competent at them — a model, a goal, a sum, a drawing, a game of football, a recitation. The developmental gain is a self sustained by skill. The risk is inferiority: the settled conviction that one's efforts do not produce acceptable products. The mechanism is comparison, and the adults who shape it are teachers, coaches, siblings, and parents — in that order of hours, though not of weight.

The clinical translation is precise. When an eight-year-old presents as "lazy" or "not trying", the assessment question is not about motivation but about whether effort has ever produced a product that was received. A child who has never had the experience of making something and having it accepted will stop making things, and the behaviour that follows — avoidance, clowning, aggression, or quiet disappearance — is a rational response to a losing game.

2. The comparative self-concept. Before about seven, children describe themselves in absolute terms ("I can run fast"). After seven they describe themselves in comparative terms ("I am the third fastest in my class"), and they begin to describe themselves in the terms others use. This is the developmental moment when a family's labels become the child's self-description, and it is why the words used about a seven- to ten-year-old are worth more clinical attention than the words used about a toddler.

3. The peer group as a structure. The peer group is not a crowd of children; it is a system with rules, hierarchies, gatekeepers, and sanctions. Belonging is a developmental need, not a preference, and exclusion at this age produces measurable distress. Two clinical errors follow. The first is to dismiss peer difficulty as trivial ("children are cruel"). The second is to treat it as a problem to be solved by the therapist rather than by the adults who control the setting.

4. Moral reasoning becomes reciprocal. The child moves from "what will happen to me?" to "what is fair?", and fairness is understood as equal shares, turn-taking, and rules that apply to everyone including adults. This development is what makes this the age of the indignant question — "why does he not have to?" — and it is a resource for family work rather than an impertinence. A nine-year-old's sense of injustice is frequently accurate, and a family that can hear it gains a member who can name what is happening.

5. Monitoring and scaffolding replace control. Parents cannot see the child's day, so they must build a system of oversight: who is where, with whom, and how the child can reach an adult. Effective monitoring is warm, predictable, and interested. Ineffective monitoring takes two forms. Under-monitoring (no questions, no knowledge of the child's world) leaves the child unprotected. Over-monitoring (interrogation, phone tracking without conversation, no privacy) teaches the child to manage the surveillance rather than to use the adult, and it reliably produces the secrecy it is trying to prevent.

6.2a The school-refusal differential: seven questions

"School refusal" is a description, not a diagnosis, and it is the single most common developmental presentation of this age band. The differential has six members, and the questions below separate them in roughly fifteen minutes of interview if asked in this order.

Question 1 — When did it start, and what happened that week? An abrupt onset with a datable event points to a specific stressor: a fight, a humiliation, an exam, a bereavement, a change of teacher. A gradual onset over months points to an accumulating process: learning difficulty, bullying, or an anxiety that has been building.

Question 2 — Which days, and which lessons? A pattern is diagnostic information. Mondays only suggests a weekend–weekday transition and often a family process. One subject only suggests a specific failure or a specific adult. Mornings only, with recovery by break, suggests separation or anticipatory anxiety.

Question 3 — What happens in the body? Headache, stomach ache, vomiting, and sleeplessness before school, resolving once the child is allowed to stay home, is the pattern of anxiety. Symptoms that do not resolve on staying home point elsewhere — a medical problem, or a family in which being ill is the only available currency.

Question 4 — Is the child behind, and in what? Ask for the last report, not a description of it. A child who cannot read at nine will find a hundred ways not to be in a room where reading happens, and none of them will be called "reading".

Question 5 — Who is unkind, and where? Ask directly, and ask about the phone. Bullying at this age has moved partly into a space the school cannot see, and a child who is refusing school is often protecting the adults from information rather than protecting herself from school.

Question 6 — What is happening at home? Not "is there anything wrong at home?" — that question produces "no". Ask what the evenings look like, who is away, who is unwell, who is arguing, and what the child does when the arguing starts.

Question 7 — What does the child's absence make possible? This is the systemic question and the one students forget. Absence has consequences: a parent does not leave, a sibling is not alone, a grandmother is not left with nobody. Ask who benefits. The answer is rarely the child, and it is never nobody.

The formulation grid. Put the answers into five cells: anxiety, separation, school-based threat, learning, and family function. Most cases load on two. The treatment follows the loadings — an anxiety-driven case needs graded return plus parental alliance; a bullying case needs the school and protection before any exposure; a family-function case needs the family work first, because graded return will fail while the child's absence is doing a job at home.

6.2b What a friend is, at seven, nine and eleven

Friendship has stages, and each stage describes a different capacity the child brings to the clinic.

At six or seven a friend is someone you play with today. Friendship is concrete, provisional, and defined by shared activity. Betrayal is "he played with someone else". A child of this age who has no friends is not necessarily lonely in the way an adult means it.

At nine a friend is someone who is loyal, keeps promises, and shares. Friendship is now a relationship with obligations, and the characteristic injury is exclusion from a group rather than absence of a partner. This is the age at which "nobody sits with me" becomes a clinical statement.

At eleven a friend is someone who knows you, who you can tell things to, and who understands you without explanation. Intimacy and self-disclosure arrive. The characteristic injuries are betrayal of a confidence and the collapse of a friendship group, both of which present to clinics as sudden, dramatic, and — to parents — disproportionate.

The clinical use. Ask the child to describe a friend and listen for the stage. A nine-year-old who describes friendship entirely in terms of shared activity is not necessarily delayed, but a nine-year-old who describes no one at all is telling you about the setting. Then ask the parents the same question about their own friendships, because the household's model of friendship is usually the child's curriculum.

6.3 The method: assessing school refusal in one session

The method below is a sequence, and the order matters because each step changes the questions in the next.

Step 1 — Get the attendance data. Dates, not adjectives. "He refuses school" becomes usable when it is "absent eleven of the last twenty school days, all Mondays and two Fridays". Bring the register printout or the school's messages. Two clinicians looking at the same pattern will usually agree; two clinicians listening to two descriptions will usually not.

Step 2 — Interview the child alone, briefly, with the parents informed. Ten minutes, at the child's level, without the parents' faces. The two questions that produce most of the information are "what is the worst part of the morning?" and "what would happen if you went?" The second question is the one adults rarely ask, and it is the one that surfaces the feared consequence — a confrontation, a test, a parent alone in the house.

Step 3 — Separate the five cells. Write the five-cell grid in front of the parents and place the evidence in it. The purpose is not to be clever; it is to show the family that the behaviour has more than one possible meaning, which is itself an intervention in households that have settled on "he is being difficult".

Step 4 — Ask the systemic question aloud. "What happens at home when he stays?" Ask it plainly, and then be quiet. In the case below, the answer arrived in nine seconds.

Step 5 — Agree the return plan before the family leaves. Not "we will work on it". A graded plan with dates, a named adult at the school gate, a named person the child can go to at break, and an agreed consequence for the first morning. The plan is written down and copied to the school the same day.

Step 6 — Protect the plan from the family's other business. This is the step that fails. A return plan collapses when the underlying family process is unaddressed, and the therapist who has spent the session on the school will find the child back at home within a fortnight.

6.3a The family–school interface

The therapist is not the school's agent and not the family's advocate. The position is consultant to the system, and it is maintained by three practices.

Practice 1 — One channel. Agree with the family who speaks to the school, how often, and about what. Families with two parents, a grandmother, and a house help who all telephone the school produce a school that stops answering, and the child learns that the adults cannot coordinate.

Practice 2 — Ask for observations, not verdicts. A school says "he is disruptive". Ask what he did, in what lesson, after what, and what the teacher did next. Schools, like parents, describe children in adjectives; the therapist's job is to convert adjectives into sequences, in both directions.

Practice 3 — Give the school one thing to do. Not a programme. One change: a named adult at the gate, seating the child next to a specific person, a signal the child can use to ask for help without speaking in front of the class. A school that is given one workable action will do it; a school that is given a system will do nothing.

The letter. The letter below is the model taught in the laboratory, because a well-written letter is a clinical act and, in the experience of most trainees, the single most useful document they will write in their first year of practice. It is short, it names one request, it does not diagnose, and it does not blame.

— Write the letter. Three paragraphs, 200 words, no jargon. Paragraph one: what the family has asked for help with, in one sentence. Paragraph two: what the clinic will do, and by when. Paragraph three: one specific request of the school, with a named person and a date. Bring it to the next session and read it aloud; the class will tell you the moment it becomes a complaint.

6.4 Three techniques for this age

Technique 1 — The week map. With the child, draw the seven days in a grid and put a mark on each day for how the morning felt, using a three-point scale the child invents. Then, in a different colour, mark the days something happened at home. The technique works because it is the child's own data, collected by the child, and it makes a pattern visible without anyone having to accuse anyone. It is also the beginning of the child's sense of agency: she is the one who noticed.

Technique 2 — The competence inventory. With the child, list five things she can do — not five things she is good at compared with others, five things she can do that she could not do a year ago. Then find one that an adult in the household does not know about, and arrange for that adult to be told. The technique addresses the mechanism of inferiority directly: the child needs at least one domain in which effort produces a product that is received.

Technique 3 — The one adult. Ask the child to name one adult at school she would go to if something happened. If she cannot name one, that is the finding, and the school intervention is to create one. This single question predicts more about a child's resilience at school than any questionnaire, and it takes eleven seconds to ask.

6.4a The digital playground

By nine, a substantial minority of children in Nigerian cities are in a group chat with their classmates, and the therapy room has to take account of it because the peer group now has a second, invisible location. Three observations, and then what the therapist asks.

The group chat is a room with no adult in it. The developmental function is the same as the playground — belonging, status, exclusion — and the developmental difference is that it is continuous, archived, and unobserved. A child can be excluded at eleven at night, in her own bed, by forty classmates, and no adult will know unless she says so.

Exclusion at this age is often technical rather than social. A child is not told she is unwelcome; she is simply not added to the group. This is harder for parents to see and easier for a child to misinterpret, and it produces a particular kind of distress: the child knows something is happening and has no evidence.

What children do with a phone at nine is mostly not what parents fear. The realistic risks in this age band are not predation — which is real but rarer than the anxiety about it — but (a) the erosion of the boredom in which imagination happens, (b) the acceleration of comparison, because a nine-year-old now sees the edited highlights of forty lives, and (c) the collapse of the parental monitoring that this week's concept describes, because the child's social world has moved out of the parents' line of sight.

What the therapist asks, in this order. First: "does she have a device of her own, and where does it sleep?" The answer to the second half is more informative than the first. Second: "who is in her group, and does she want to be?" Third: "what happens in the house between seven and nine in the evening?" — because the family's evening is the variable that determines whether the device is a tool or a substitute.

What the therapist does not do. Do not run a session on screen-time limits. The evidence for specific hour-based rules is weak and the argument consumes the session. The two interventions with better support are structural and relational: no device in the bedroom overnight, for anyone in the household including the adults; and one daily period of twenty minutes in which the child has an adult's undivided attention, which is the same prescription as Week 4's ten minutes of child-led play, at a different age and with a different content.

The sentence that reframes the parental complaint. When a parent says "he is always on his phone", the developmental translation is usually "he has found a place where he belongs and it is not here". That translation is not always correct, but it is always worth testing, because it moves the intervention from confiscation to invitation.

6.5 The first session with a nine-year-old and two parents

Phase 1 — The child first (15 minutes), parents in the room. Nine-year-olds are usually relieved to be asked directly and embarrassed by being discussed. Ask the child: what is the worst part of the morning; what would happen if you went; who do you sit with; who would you tell. The parents hear the answers at the same time as the therapist, which prevents the later reconstruction in which the child's account is softened.

Phase 2 — The child out (5 minutes), with a clear explanation. "I am going to talk to your mum and dad about the grown-up parts. You are not in trouble and nothing you said was wrong."

Phase 3 — The parents (20 minutes). The seven questions, asked in order, with the attendance data on the table. The purpose is not to interrogate but to demonstrate the differential: by question five the parents usually begin to supply the formulation themselves.

Phase 4 — The systemic question (5 minutes). "What happens at home when he stays?" Ask it once, in plain language, and let the silence work. Then: "who is at home with him?" and "what would have to change for the mornings to be ordinary?"

Phase 5 — The plan (15 minutes). Written, dated, with names. One request to the school, one change at home, one thing the child will do. The parents leave with a page, not with an understanding.

Why the child goes first. Because a nine-year-old who has been discussed for twenty minutes before being seen will arrive defensive, and because the parents' account of a child's inner life at this age is frequently a description of the parents' own anxiety. The child's own words, heard by the parents in the room, do more to loosen a fixed formulation than an hour of parental interview.

6.5a When the child will not talk

A nine-year-old who has been brought to a clinic by two adults, about a problem the adults have defined, has three good reasons not to speak: she does not know what will happen to what she says, she has been told not to discuss family business, and she has learned that saying nothing is safer than being misquoted. The following five moves are taught because the standard one — "can you tell me how you feel?" — produces a shrug from a child of this age in almost every culture.

Move 1 — Give the child a job. "I need your help with something. I am trying to understand what a Monday morning is like in your house, and only you can tell me." A task recruits the child's competence, which is exactly what this stage is about, and it changes the child's position from subject to consultant.

Move 2 — Move the question to the third person. "Some children tell me the hardest part is the morning. Others say it is the night before. Which is it for the children you know?" The third person gives the child a legitimate way to describe her own experience without owning it, and it is not a trick: it genuinely produces different and often more accurate information.

Move 3 — Draw. A week map, a house with the rooms and who sleeps where, a line with marks for the good days. Drawing occupies the part of the child that would otherwise be managing the adult's face, and it produces a document the therapist and the child can both look at instead of each other.

Move 4 — Offer a scale with an escape. "On a scale of one to ten, how much does Monday morning worry you? One is nothing, ten is the worst." A number is easier than a sentence, and it can be revised. Children who will not answer a feeling question will answer a number, and the number is comparable across sessions.

Move 5 — Say what will happen to the information. Before the first question: "I will tell your mum and dad the things that are about the family, because they need to know them to help. If you tell me something that is not safe, I will have to tell them too. Everything else stays in this room." Nine-year-olds understand this perfectly, and the children who relax most visibly after it are the ones who had assumed everything was being reported.

What to do with silence. If the child says nothing for a full minute, wait. If the minute becomes four, stop asking and do the drawing yourself, narrating: "this is the Monday box, and I do not know what goes in it." Most children will correct the drawing, and a correction is the beginning of speech: it is the first time in the session that the child has decided the therapist's version is worth amending.

6.6 Case: the Okonkwos again — Mondays

The course's recurring family returns, six months after the sessions in Week 1, with a different presenting complaint about the same nine-year-old.

The referral. Ada telephones. "The defiance has stopped, but now he will not go to school. Only Mondays. Every Monday since the beginning of term."

The household. Ebuka, 9, in Primary 4. Ada, 34, a schoolteacher. Chuka, 39, a driver on night shifts, home from about five in the morning and asleep until two. The baby, now seventeen months. Chuka's mother died in 2023. Chuka's younger brother, 24, has been staying since December, sleeping in the sitting room, looking for work.

The attendance data. Eleven of the last twenty school days missed. All five Mondays; two Fridays in the same period, both following nights when Chuka was not on shift. Non-attendance on Mondays accounts for nine of the eleven.

The parent's account. "He says his stomach hurts. Sunday night he is fine, he eats, he watches football with his father. Monday morning he is crying. By ten o'clock he is playing. It is not real."

The child's account, ten minutes alone. The worst part of the morning is "when Daddy is coming in and Mummy is getting ready". What would happen if he went? A long pause. "They would be alone." Asked who "they" are: "Mummy and Daddy." Asked what happens when he stays: "Mummy doesn't go. She stays with me and we watch films. Daddy goes to sleep."

The seven questions, answered.

QuestionAnswerCell
Onset and dateStart of term, four weeks after the uncle moved inFamily function
Which daysMondays, plus two Fridays when father was homeFamily function / separation
Body symptomsStomach ache, resolves on staying homeAnxiety (secondary)
Is the child behindReport: average in all subjects, excellent in mathematicsNo learning problem
Who is unkindNobody named; one friend, Tobi, "he is my best friend"No school-based threat
What is happening at homeMoney; father's nights; uncle in the sitting roomFamily function
What the absence makes possibleThe mother stays; the parents do not argue; the uncle leaves the roomFamily function

The Sunday-night mechanism. The interview with the parents, without the child, produces the mechanism in about four minutes. Sunday night is the only evening Chuka is reliably at home and awake. It is when the week's money is counted. For the last two months, the counting has not worked: the school fees for the term are short, the uncle has not found work, and Chuka has begun to talk about taking a second job on Sundays — which would mean he is never home in the evening at all. The conversation starts calmly and ends in an argument. Ada cries. Chuka leaves the room.

Ebuka hears all of it. He has done so since he was seven, because the flat has two rooms.

What Ebuka has constructed. A nine-year-old, at the stage where moral reasoning is reciprocal and causality is sought, has assembled a theory: the argument happens on Sunday night; when he is at home on Monday, his mother does not go to work, his father does not leave, and the house is quiet. He has therefore found a mechanism for preventing the argument, and he is operating it. He is not lying about the stomach ache; he is not fabricating it either. His body is doing what bodies do when a child has found that illness is the one currency that works.

The formulation, in one sentence. The symptom is a Monday-shaped solution to a marital and financial problem, and it is also genuinely distressing to the child — both things are true, and the therapist must hold both, because a formulation that makes the child merely strategic will produce an intervention that shames him.

Why this is not "manipulation". Manipulation requires representing another's belief and intending to change it for one's own advantage. Ebuka is not gaining anything a nine-year-old wants. He is paying: he misses his friend, he misses mathematics — the one thing he is excellent at — and he is alone in a quiet house with a sleeping father. The behaviour is a sacrifice, and the clinical error is to read a sacrifice as a con.

The intervention, in four parts.

Part 1 — Tell the child the truth, at his level. The therapist asks the parents to say three things to Ebuka, in the session, in their own words: that the money is short and it is the adults' problem; that they are not separating; and that he does not have to stay at home to keep them together. Ada cries while saying it. Chuka says the third sentence twice. Ebuka says nothing and then asks whether he can go and play. That is the correct response.

Part 2 — Move the argument. Not "stop arguing" — an instruction no couple can follow. Instead: the money conversation happens on Saturday afternoon, out of the flat, in the car, and it does not happen on Sunday night. The content does not change; the time and the place do. This is a structural intervention, and it is the one that most reliably works, because it removes the child from the audience without asking the parents to change their marriage in a fortnight.

Part 3 — Give the Monday morning a shape. Ada leaves for work at the same time whether Ebuka goes to school or not. She does not stay. The handover to the uncle is explicit and cheerful: "Uncle will walk you at seven twenty." Chuka, coming in at five, sleeps in the inner room with the door closed, and the uncle is told, in front of Ebuka, that he is in charge of the school run. The Monday morning stops being a negotiation and becomes a timetable.

Part 4 — Give the father a Monday. Chuka takes Monday afternoon, from two until six, with Ebuka, once a week — football, or mathematics, or nothing. This is not a reward for attendance; it is the removal of the reason for the absence. The child was keeping the family together with his body. The father now does the keeping, on a timetable, in daylight.

6.6a Four weeks later

Attendance: nineteen of twenty days. One Monday missed, in the week Chuka's second job was confirmed.

Ada reports that Ebuka asked, on a Sunday evening, "is it Saturday tomorrow?" — meaning, is the money conversation today. She said no, it was yesterday, and he went to bed.

The stomach aches have not disappeared. They now occur on Sunday evenings rather than Monday mornings, which is a change the therapist regards as clinically meaningful: the symptom has moved from a device that altered the household's behaviour to an expression of ordinary anticipatory worry. The distinction between a symptom that does something and a symptom that says something is one of the most useful in this book, and it is the difference between the first and fourth sessions of this case.

Chuka's second job means he is away on Sunday afternoons. The Monday afternoon with Ebuka survives, on Tuesdays, at Ebuka's request.

What would have changed the plan. If the report had shown Ebuka two years behind in reading, the plan would have begun with the school and an assessment. If the child had named a child who was hurting him, the school would have been the first call and the family work second. If the attendance data had shown a scattered pattern with no day structure, the formulation would have moved toward anxiety and the intervention to a graded return with a named adult at the gate. And if the parents had refused to move the Sunday conversation — a real possibility, and a common one — the therapist would have worked with the child alone on the sentence "the argument is not mine to stop", which is a smaller intervention with a smaller effect and is sometimes all that is available.

6.7 Transcript: telling a nine-year-old the truth

Twelve turns from the third session, with Ebuka present for the first half. The transcript is annotated for what the therapist is doing and, more importantly, for what she is not doing.

T1 Therapist: Ebuka, your mum and dad told me something and I want them to tell you, because you have been working very hard on something and nobody told you what it was. — Framing. The child is credited before anything is disclosed, and the disclosure is handed to the parents, not performed by the therapist. T2 Ada: We have been arguing about money. On Sunday nights. You have heard us. — Plain, no euphemism. Note that she does not ask "have you heard us?" — the question would invite a denial and put the child in charge of the disclosure. T3 Ebuka: (nods, looks at the floor) T4 Ada: And I am sorry. It is not your fault and it is not your job. — Two sentences, both necessary. The apology is not for the argument; it is for the child having had to manage it. T5 Therapist: Ebuka, can I ask you something about Mondays? (he nods) What do you think would happen if you went to school? — Question 2, asked again in front of the parents so that they hear the answer, which is the whole point of the session. T6 Ebuka: (long pause) They would be alone. T7 Chuka: (quietly) We are not going anywhere. — The father's sentence. It is not a reassurance the therapist supplied; it is the one thing the child needed and could not ask for. T8 Therapist: Ebuka, say what you heard. — A checking move. Not "do you understand?", which produces a yes; "say what you heard", which produces the child's version and shows whether the message arrived. T9 Ebuka: That they are not going anywhere. And the money is not mine. — The child's formulation, in his own words, is the outcome measure of the session. T10 Therapist: The money is not yours. And here is the other thing. (to the parents) Tell him about Monday. — One more piece of information, again delivered by the parents. The therapist has said almost nothing of substance in this session, which is the technique. T11 Chuka: On Monday I sleep in the back room and Uncle takes you to school. And then in the afternoon it is you and me, every Monday, from two. — A timetable, not a promise. Concrete, dated, and owned by the father. T12 Ebuka: Can we do maths? — The child negotiates the content of the repair. This is the sign that he has accepted the new arrangement: he is now planning rather than preventing.

6.8 Laboratory: the school-refusal formulation

Aim. To produce a five-cell formulation and a written return plan from a single interview, within ninety minutes, and to defend the formulation against an alternative.

Procedure. The class divides into trios: therapist, parent, observer. The trainer supplies one of four vignettes, each of which loads on two cells. The therapist has thirty minutes for the seven questions, five minutes to draw the grid, and fifteen minutes to agree a plan. The observer scores against the rubric. Then the trio must argue the case for the alternative formulation, which is the part students find hardest and learn most from.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
Data firstAccepted adjectives; no datesAsked for the pattern but not the recordObtained dates, days, lessons; used them to form hypotheses
The differentialSettled on one explanation in the first ten minutesConsidered two cellsPlaced evidence in all five, named the loadings, and said what would change the formulation
The systemic questionNot askedAsked, but answered by the therapistAsked plainly, followed by silence, and the answer used
The planGeneral adviceA plan with no dates or namesDated, named, copied to the school, with a fallback
PositionBecame the school's agent or the family's advocateHeld a position but said soHeld the consultant position and could describe how

The distinction that carries most of the marks. A formulation that cannot be falsified is not a formulation. The top band requires the student to state, in advance, what they would expect to see if they were wrong — "if this is family function, attendance should improve when the Sunday conversation moves, within three weeks; if it does not, I will re-open the anxiety cell."

6.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Believing the child. Not the child's honesty that is in question; the child's theory. A nine-year-old's account of why he cannot go to school is a genuine attempt at explanation by someone with limited information, and treating it as the formulation stops the enquiry at the first cell.

Error 2 — Disbelieving the child. The mirror error, and the commoner one in this age band. "He is manipulating me" closes the assessment. The child's stomach ache is real; the question is what it is for.

Error 3 — Making the school the client. Once the therapist's plan is "get him back to school", the family process becomes an obstacle and the therapist begins to coerce the family on the school's behalf. Attendance is an outcome measure, not the treatment.

Error 4 — Treating the symptom without moving it. A return plan imposed on an unaddressed family process produces a child who attends and deteriorates, or a child who attends for three weeks and then presents with something else. The mechanism has to be addressed, not just the behaviour.

Error 5 — Over-monitoring as a solution. Parents who respond to a child's withdrawal by interrogating, tracking the phone, and removing privacy will produce a child who is present and unreachable. The monitoring that works at this age is warm and interested, and it is built years before it is needed.

Error 6 — Ignoring the report card. A learning difficulty is the most under-detected member of the differential, because no child says "I cannot read" and no parent volunteers "he is behind". Ask for the document. Every time, and read it in the session rather than at home, because the parent's face while you read it is data you will not get twice.

6.10 Culture, faith and the child at school

Whose school is it? In many households the school is experienced as an extension of the state rather than of the family, and a child's difficulty at school is managed by the family without the school being told anything. A therapist who telephones the school without an explicit agreement will lose the family in one move. Ask first: "who in this family talks to the school, and what are they allowed to say?"

The child who is needed at home. Ebuka's case is not exotic; it is the ordinary structure of many households where a parent works nights, a relative is staying, and a nine-year-old is the most reliable person in the flat. The clinical question is never "is this child being parentified?" as an accusation, but "what is this child actually doing, and who else could do it?" The answer is frequently a specific task that can be reassigned to a named adult, and that reassignment is the intervention.

Faith and the meaning of difficulty. Households may interpret a child's trouble at school through prayer, deliverance, or a spiritual account of the family's season. A therapist who treats that account as a competitor to the assessment will be heard as an opponent. The workable position is to accept the household's framework as the language in which the difficulty is held, and to add a second language — what the child can do, what the school observes, what the report says — without asking anyone to give up the first.

The high expectations question. Many Nigerian and West African households hold a standard of academic achievement that is both a resource and a pressure. The resource is that the child's effort is noticed. The pressure is that a child who is average in a family of high achievers may conclude that she is inferior at eight. The clinical move is not to lower the standard but to widen the field: find the domain in which this child's effort produces a product, and make sure one adult receives it.

DEBATE — "A nine-year-old should be told the truth about the family's money, including the arguments." Argue for: the child is already hearing it, the silence is what produces the symptom, and the truth returns the burden to its owners. Argue against: children cannot hold adult information without cost, disclosure serves the parents' relief rather than the child's need, and "the truth" at nine is a version of the truth that someone has chosen. Then decide what you would actually say, word for word, to a nine-year-old whose parents cannot pay the fees.

6.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The association between peer acceptance in middle childhood and later adjustment is one of the more robust findings in developmental psychopathology, with prospective studies showing that peer rejection at eight to ten predicts internalising and externalising difficulties into adolescence — though the effects are moderated strongly by the presence of at least one reciprocated friendship, which is the finding that matters clinically. A child with one friend is not a child without peers.

What is weaker. The evidence on school refusal is fragmented because the samples are heterogeneous: studies mix anxious children, oppositional children, and children absent for family reasons, and then report a single treatment effect. The best-supported component is not a therapy brand but a structural one — a rapid, coordinated return to school with parental involvement and a school-based adult — which appears in almost every effective protocol regardless of the theoretical label on it.

What is not supported. The claim that early intensive academic instruction in the preschool years produces durable advantage. The evidence points the other way: gains in directly instructed literacy and numeracy in the early years tend to fade, while the regulatory and social capacities that make use of instruction possible are the ones that persist. This is the empirical basis for the previous week's argument that school readiness is regulation.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same Monday refusal, in a household with a stable income and an available father, would have resolved without treatment in most cases. In this household, at this moment, it became the mechanism by which a family stayed organised.

6.12 Key terms

Industry versus inferiority — Erikson's fourth stage; the consolidation of competence, and the risk of a settled conviction of inadequacy.

Comparative self-concept — the shift, around seven, from absolute to relative self-description, and the beginning of self-description in other people's terms.

Monitoring — parental oversight of where, with whom, and how the child can reach an adult; effective when warm and predictable.

Scaffolding — support calibrated to current competence and withdrawn as competence grows.

School refusal — non-attendance driven by emotional distress or family process, distinguished from truancy (which is concealed) and from exclusion.

Graded return — a dated, stepped plan for re-entry, with a named adult and predictable supports.

Parentification — the assignment of adult responsibilities to a child; useful as a description and harmful as an accusation.

Reciprocated friendship — a mutual, named friendship, which moderates the effect of peer rejection more strongly than acceptance by the group.

Locus of control — the child's developing sense that outcomes follow from her actions; built at this age by tasks that are hard but achievable.

6.13 Self-test

  1. A parent says "he is manipulating me". Give the definition of manipulation, and explain why it is usually the wrong word for a nine-year-old.
  2. Name the five cells of the school-refusal formulation and one piece of evidence that would fill each.
  3. Why is a symptom that does something different from a symptom that says something, and how does the distinction change treatment?
  4. What is the difference between monitoring and over-monitoring, and what does each produce?
  5. A child of nine describes friendship only as "we play football". Is this delayed? What else do you need to know?
  6. Why does the laboratory rubric award marks for stating what would prove you wrong?
  7. In the Okonkwo case, what would you have done if the parents had refused to move the Sunday conversation?
  8. Name three things a school can do that cost nothing and change a child's week.

Answers. (1) Manipulation requires representing another person's belief and intending to change it for one's own advantage; at nine the capacity is emergent and the child in this pattern is usually sacrificing something rather than gaining, so the word misreads both the mechanism and the direction of the behaviour. (2) Anxiety, separation, school-based threat, learning, family function — evidence might be: body symptoms resolving on staying home; distress at the gate but not at the classroom; a named pupil or a phone message; a report showing a two-year gap; attendance data patterned by a day on which the child's presence changes the household. (3) A symptom that does something is functional within the system and will persist until the system's need is met elsewhere; a symptom that says something is an expression and responds to being heard and to the child's own coping. The first needs structural change, the second needs the child's distress addressed. (4) Monitoring is oversight with interest and warmth; over-monitoring is surveillance without relationship. The first produces a child who uses adults; the second produces a child who manages adults. (5) Not necessarily — at nine friendship is often described in terms of shared activity; you need to know whether there is a reciprocated friendship, whether the child is invited and refuses or is not invited, and what the parents' own friendships look like. (6) Because a formulation that cannot be falsified cannot guide treatment, and because stating the disconfirming evidence is the only protection against the therapist's preferred theory. (7) Work with the child alone on the sentence "the argument is not mine to stop", teach the parents one sentence to say to him before the argument begins, and re-open the case if attendance does not improve — a smaller intervention with a smaller effect, honestly labelled. (8) A named adult at the gate; a signal a child can use to ask for help without speaking in class; seating next to a specific child; a five-minute check-in at the end of the day.

6.14 Reflective task

Write down, in the original words if you can remember them, one thing an adult told you about what you were bad at between the ages of seven and eleven. Then write down the domain in which you were competent, and who noticed. Then answer: how many years did the first sentence take to stop being true? Most students find the second question harder than the first, which is the point.

6.15 Further reading

  • Erikson, E. Childhood and Society — the industry stage, in the chapter on the school years.
  • Harter, S. The Construction of the Self — the comparative self, and its measurement.
  • Ladd, G. "Peer relationships and social competence" — rejection, friendship, and the moderating effect of one friend.
  • Kearney, C. School Refusal Behavior in Youth — the functional model that underlies this week's grid.
  • Selman, R. The Growth of Interpersonal Understanding — the friendship stages used above.

Next: Week 7 — Adolescence I: puberty, identity, autonomy.

Week 7 — Adolescence I: puberty, identity, autonomy

Developmental tasks. Identity versus role confusion (Erikson, from about twelve); pubertal change and its timing; separation-individuation without severance; peer belonging as a biological need rather than a preference; emerging sexuality; and the first genuinely ideological commitments — the adolescent's own answers to questions about religion, politics, loyalty and justice.

The neuroscience, briefly and correctly. The adolescent brain is not "broken": it is specialised. The reward system matures ahead of the regulatory system, which produces sensation-seeking and heightened sensitivity to peer approval alongside gradually improving impulse control that continues into the mid-twenties. Three things follow, and three things do not. What follows: adolescents are more responsive to peer presence than children or adults; they discount delayed outcomes more steeply; and they learn fast in conditions of high arousal and interest. What does not follow: that they cannot reason, that they are irrational, or that the findings license a lecture. On tasks with no peers present and no time pressure, adolescents reason as well as adults. The difference is in the conditions, and conditions can be designed.

The family task. To renegotiate the relationship: less control, more influence; more privacy, more accountability. Families that cannot renegotiate produce two common patterns: escalation, in which control increases as autonomy increases, and abdication, in which parents withdraw entirely and call it respect.

Why the two adolescence weeks are separate. This week is about the person — body, identity, autonomy, the developmental machinery. Next week is about risk — the specific dangers that this machinery produces in this environment, and what a therapist does about them. The split is artificial and is made for teaching: in the clinic they arrive together, and the practitioner must hold both.

— Amara, fifteen, in Senior Secondary 2. Her parents describe her as "secretive"; she describes them as "spies". The developmental reframe: privacy is a developmental need, and secrecy is what privacy becomes when there is no negotiated trust. The intervention is not more supervision and not more freedom; it is a negotiation — what will she share, what will they ask, and what happens when the agreement is broken?

7.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The problem with the questionWhy "why is my teenager like this?" is the wrong question, and what replaces itWrite the question they actually want answered
8–22PubertyThe sequence, the timing, and the two sexes' different relationships to itPlace four pubertal events on a timeline and mark the range
22–34Timing effectsEarly maturation in girls, late in boys, and the mechanismsPredict the clinical presentation for each of four cases
34–48The neuroscienceFour findings, three misuses, and the conditions under which adolescents reason wellRewrite three "teenage brain" claims as testable statements
48–62IdentityMarcia's four statuses, and how a therapist recognises each in a sessionClassify four adolescent presentations
62–74Autonomy without severanceThe renegotiation, and the two failure patternsScript a negotiation opening for a real family
74–84Privacy and accountabilityThe three-column contract, worked on the whiteboardDraft one contract for their own case
84–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Students come to adolescence with either of two inherited attitudes: that teenagers are a problem to be managed, or that they are a mystery to be respected. Both are ways of not working with them. The lecture's job is to install a third attitude — that the adolescent is a colleague in a renegotiation, with legitimate interests and real obligations — and the way to install it is to have the class run the negotiation themselves, in role, against a parent played by the lecturer who does not concede easily.

The sentence the lecture must land. "The adolescent's job is not to leave you; it is to become someone you have not met yet, in front of you, while you are still paying for the food."

7.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Identity versus role confusion. Erikson's fifth stage, and the one that gives the age its name. Identity is not a feeling; it is a set of commitments — to a vocation, a set of beliefs, a kind of relationship, a place. James Marcia's operationalisation is the most useful tool in the week, because it turns identity into something observable in a session. He crossed two dimensions: exploration (has the young person actively tried options?) and commitment (has she settled on one?).

Commitment madeNo commitment
ExplorationAchieved — "I want to do physiotherapy because I worked in a clinic for a year and I liked the problem-solving"Moratorium — "I am trying law, medicine and music; I will know by next year"
No explorationForeclosed — "I am going to be a doctor; my father is a doctor" (no examination of alternatives)Diffuse — "I do not know, I have not thought about it"

The clinical value is that the four statuses have different prognoses and different treatments. Foreclosure is not a problem while it fits, and becomes one at twenty-six when it does not. Moratorium is healthy and anxiety-provoking. Diffusion in a fifteen-year-old is common, and diffusion at twenty-two with no exploration is a clinical concern. And the therapist's question is never "what do you want to be?" but "what have you tried, and what did you find out?"

2. Puberty as a social event as much as a biological one. The biological sequence is predictable: in girls, breast development, growth spurt, menarche; in boys, testicular growth, voice change, growth spurt. The social meaning is not predictable at all, and it does most of the clinical work. A girl who menstruates at nine in a school where the others are eleven will be treated as older than she is, by adults, for years. A boy who matures at sixteen in a culture that equates physical size with status will be treated as younger than he is, and may comply.

3. Separation-individuation without severance. The psychoanalytic language is clumsy and the phenomenon is real: the adolescent must become a separate person while remaining attached. The metaphor of leaving home is wrong for most of the world, where adolescents do not leave and are not expected to. What they must separate from is not the household but the household's definition of them — and they must do it in front of an audience that keeps feeding them.

4. Peer belonging as a need, not a preference. Peer presence changes adolescent risk-taking in experiments; being excluded activates the same systems as physical pain. This is not weakness; it is the developmental mechanism by which the young person moves their attachment from the family to the wider world. The clinical consequence: an intervention that requires the adolescent to be socially expendable will fail, and an intervention that uses the peer group will succeed more often than one that fights it.

5. Ideological commitment arrives. Adolescents begin to hold positions on justice, religion, politics and loyalty that are genuinely their own, and they test those positions against the adults in the house. The household experiences this as argumentativeness. Developmentally it is the emergence of a moral agent, and it is the raw material of identity. A family that can survive being disagreed with produces an adolescent with convictions; a family that cannot produces either a secretive adolescent or a foreclosed one.

7.2a Pubertal timing: the effects that matter clinically

Timing, not just change, is a developmental variable with measurable consequences, and it is one of the few things in this book that a therapist can assess in a single question.

Early maturation in girls. Associated with higher rates of depressive symptoms, body dissatisfaction, and involvement with older peers — the last of which mediates much of the rest. The mechanism is not the hormones; it is the social response to a body that looks older than the person is. The clinical implication: a twelve-year-old who looks sixteen is being treated as sixteen by strangers, relatives, and sometimes by her own family, and she has the cognitive and emotional equipment of twelve.

Late maturation in boys. Associated with lower self-esteem and, in some cohorts, more risk behaviour in the late teens once maturation arrives — the "catch-up" pattern. The mechanism is again social: size is a currency in this age band, and a boy who does not have it is at the bottom of a visible hierarchy.

The reverse patterns are milder but real. Early-maturing boys gain social advantage; late-maturing girls generally do better on the outcomes that matter. Neither is a reason to be complacent: the advantage is contingent on the peer group's values, and it is the mismatch between the body's apparent age and the person's actual age that carries the risk.

What the therapist asks. One question, to the parent, in the child's presence or not: "when did you notice her body changing, compared with her friends?" And one to the adolescent, alone: "do people treat you as older or younger than you are?" The second answer is usually more informative than the first, and it frequently produces the material for the whole session.

The clinical presentations to expect. Early-maturing girls are over-represented in referrals for anxiety, mood, and "behaviour" that turns out to be an older peer group. Late-maturing boys are over-represented in referrals for withdrawal, "lack of confidence", and school refusal in the first years of secondary school. Both are frequently formulated as family problems before anyone asks about timing, and the reframe alone is often relieving for the adolescent, who has usually concluded that the problem is her character.

7.2b The teenage brain: four findings and three misuses

The neuroscience is useful to a family therapist only if it changes what she does in the room. It changes three things: how she explains risk to parents, how she designs tasks for adolescents, and how she thinks about the timing of consequences.

Finding 1 — The reward system is responsive, and more so in company. Mid-adolescents show heightened striatal response to reward, and this is amplified by the presence of peers. Use: a plan that gives an adolescent something to gain, in front of people whose opinion matters, will outperform a plan built on avoiding loss.

Finding 2 — Regulatory control is still developing. Performance on inhibition and planning tasks improves steadily into the twenties. Use: do not build a plan that requires the adolescent to inhibit a strong impulse in a high-arousal moment; build one that removes the moment, or that puts an adult between the impulse and the act.

Finding 3 — Adolescents reason well under cool conditions. On tasks with no audience and no time pressure, adolescent reasoning is comparable to adult reasoning. Use: the therapy room is a cool condition, and an adolescent who is articulate and thoughtful in the session and reckless on Friday night is not being a hypocrite. She is reporting accurately from two different states, and the treatment target is the state, not the report.

Finding 4 — Sleep timing shifts. Melatonin release shifts later, so the adolescent is biologically not sleepy at ten and biologically not awake at seven. Use: this is the single most useful fact in the week for parents, because it converts "laziness" into a physiological fact — and because the practical recommendation (a consistent wake time, light in the morning, no bright screens in the last hour) is one that a family can actually follow.

Misuse 1 — "Their brains are not finished, so they cannot decide." The finding is about the rate of development of one system, not about competence. Adolescents can and must decide; the finding argues for scaffolding decisions, not for removing them.

Misuse 2 — "Peer pressure explains everything." Peers matter most for risk decisions in arousing conditions. They matter much less for the ordinary choices of a Tuesday afternoon, and an explanation that uses peer pressure for everything explains nothing.

Misuse 3 — "They will grow out of it." Some things resolve; some, like a depressive episode or a developing substance pattern, worsen. The neuroscience says nothing about which is which, and using it as reassurance in the wrong case is a serious error.

7.3 The method: renegotiating the frame

The adolescent session fails, when it fails, for one of two reasons: the therapist has been recruited as the parents' agent, or the therapist has tried to be the adolescent's friend. Both are avoided by negotiating the frame in front of both parties, in the first fifteen minutes, and writing it down.

Step 1 — Say what you have been told, and say it in the room. "Your parents have told me that you are secretive and that they are worried. They have told me some specific things, which I will not repeat in front of you without asking. I want to hear your version, and I will tell you afterwards what I think." The move is radical for families in which information flows upward only, and it buys the therapist the right to ask the next question.

Step 2 — Establish the confidentiality rule explicitly. The three-part rule: what the adolescent says stays in the room; exceptions are stated in advance (danger to self, danger to others, and — in most clinics — any disclosure of abuse); and anything about the household that the parents need in order to change is discussed with the adolescent first, including how it will be said. The last part is the one that distinguishes a working adolescent practice from a performative one.

Step 3 — Ask what the adolescent wants out of this. The question is unfamiliar and usually productive. The answers cluster: "I want them to stop asking me the same question"; "I want to be allowed out"; "I want them to stop fighting"; "I want to be left alone". Each is a negotiable interest, and the therapist's next move is to convert it into a proposal.

Step 4 — Run the negotiation, not the therapy. With both parties present, three columns are filled: what she will share, what they will ask, and what happens if the agreement breaks. This is the substance of the session and it takes about twenty minutes. It works because it addresses the actual complaint — a broken information system — rather than the presenting emotion.

Step 5 — Forecast the test. The agreement will be tested within a fortnight. Say so: "someone will break this in the next two weeks. When it happens, we will look at it here rather than at home." This single sentence reduces the probability that the first breach becomes a family rupture, because it pre-frames the breach as data.

Step 6 — Give the parents a job they can do. Not "be less controlling" — unactionable. A specific behaviour: one question a day instead of six; a fixed time for the conversation; a weekly hour with no agenda. Parents who are given a specific behaviour change it; parents who are given an attitude do not.

7.3a The three-column contract, worked

The contract is a table on a sheet of paper with three columns, and it is the week's central practical.

Column 1 — What she will share, unasked. Typically: where she is, who with, when she will be back, and a message if she is late. These are safety data, not intimacy data, and separating them is the key move. An adolescent will usually agree to share safety data because it costs her nothing she values.

Column 2 — What they will ask, and how often. Typically: one question at a time, at a stated hour, and not at the moment she comes in the door. The specificity matters more than the content: "not in the first ten minutes after I get home" is a rule that can be kept, and it usually solves the complaint on both sides, because the parents' need is information and the adolescent's need is decompression.

Column 3 — What happens when it breaks. Written in advance, by both parties, and mild. A missed message means a phone call, not a grounding. A phone call means a conversation, not a punishment. The column exists because families without it escalate to the maximum sanction on the first breach, and the maximum sanction cannot be repeated, so it fails immediately.

The four things that are never in the contract. No blanket access to the phone's contents; no permission to read a diary; no requirement to report the content of friendships; and no agreement that the adolescent will "tell them everything". Each of these is a demand for intimacy that the stage does not support, and each will be signed and then evaded, which is worse than not having an agreement at all.

What to do when the parents refuse the contract. This is common, and it is information rather than an obstacle. The refusal usually means the parents' own fear is unmanageable, and the therapist's next move is to take the fear seriously: "what are you most afraid will happen if she has a door you cannot open?" The answer is frequently specific and addressable — a pregnancy, a cult, a boy, a bus — and once it is named, a narrower agreement becomes possible.

7.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The five-domain identity interview. Ask, in order, about five domains: work (what are you good at, what do you want to be doing at twenty-five, what have you tried); beliefs (what do you believe that your parents do not); relationships (who matters to you and why); leisure (what do you do when nobody is watching); and body (how do you feel about how you look). Then classify against Marcia: exploration, commitment, both, neither. Twenty minutes, and it produces a formulation of the person rather than of the symptom.

Technique 2 — The question they cannot answer at home. "If your parents understood one thing about your life that they do not understand, what would it be?" Ask it in the joint session and then ask the adolescent to say it to them, not to the therapist. The technique fails about a third of the time and when it works it does more than four sessions of individual work, because the adolescent discovers that saying it is survivable.

Technique 3 — The future letter. Ask the adolescent to write, in ten minutes, a letter from herself at twenty-five to herself now. Read it in the session only if she offers it. The technique surfaces the content of identity — what she actually wants — which is otherwise unavailable to a fifteen-year-old who is asked directly, and it gives the therapist a vocabulary to use for the rest of the work.

7.5 The first session with a fifteen-year-old and two parents

Phase 1 — Both, briefly (10 minutes). Everyone in the room. The parents state the complaint in two minutes each; the adolescent is told she will get the same time and does. The therapist's only job here is to keep the time fair, visibly, because fairness is the currency of this age.

Phase 2 — The parents out (35 minutes). The adolescent alone. The confidentiality rule stated first, then the frame questions: what do you want out of this; what do they get wrong about you; who knows you best. Then the identity interview, or as much of it as she will give.

Phase 3 — The parents back (15 minutes). The therapist states, with the adolescent present, what she has permission to say. This is the hinge of the session: it demonstrates that the frame is real, and it usually produces a visible change in the adolescent's posture, because she has never seen an adult keep that promise.

Phase 4 — The contract (20 minutes). Three columns, written, signed by nobody (signing infantilises), photographed by everyone. The parents leave with a behaviour, the adolescent leaves with a rule, and the therapist leaves with a baseline.

Phase 5 — The follow-up question (5 minutes). "What would have to happen in the next two weeks for you to think this was worth coming to?" Ask both parties. Write the answers down. They are the outcome measures.

Why the adolescent goes second and gets more time. Because the parents have had fifteen years of being listened to and the adolescent has had none, and because the therapeutic alliance in this age band is the entire treatment. A therapist who spends the session with the parents and fifteen minutes with the adolescent has built a relationship with the wrong client.

7.5a When the adolescent will not speak

The silent adolescent is the commonest difficulty in this age band, and the standard response — waiting patiently, then asking about school — produces forty-five minutes of nothing. Five moves, in order of escalation.

Move 1 — Name the situation honestly. "You have been brought here by two people who are worried about you, and you did not choose to come. I would not want to talk either." This is not a technique for building rapport; it is an accurate description, and adolescents are unusually responsive to accuracy.

Move 2 — Ask about the thing she is allowed to have opinions about. Not her feelings. Her views: what is wrong with the school, which teacher is useless, what her parents do not understand about her generation. The identity stage runs on judgement, and an adolescent who will not describe herself will happily describe the world.

Move 3 — Use a third object. A phone (theirs, not yours), a diagram, a list of statements to sort. "The things people say about teenagers are on these cards. Sort them into true and rubbish." Every card sorted is a self-description the adolescent has given without being asked for one.

Move 4 — Offer a deal with a real cost to you. "Give me ten minutes of real answers and I will spend the last twenty minutes of this session arguing your case to your parents, in front of you." This works because it is a trade between parties with interests, which is the register of the age, and because it is kept.

Move 5 — Accept the silence and change the client. If none of that works, end the individual session early and say why: "I am not going to sit here and pretend. I will work with your parents for now, and the door stays open." This protects the alliance for the session in which she does talk, which is often the fourth.

What never works. Asking "how are you feeling?" three times; interpreting the silence ("you seem angry"); producing a worksheet; or telling the adolescent that she is "resistant". The last is the most common and the most damaging, because it converts the therapist's failure into the client's defect — and the adolescent, who has been described in deficit terms by adults her whole life, will accept the description and stop coming.

7.6 Case: Amara, secretive and spied upon

Referral. The Adeyemis, whom the course first met in Week 3, return. Amara is fifteen and in Senior Secondary 2. Her mother, Funke, 42, telephones: "She has a boyfriend and she is lying to us. We found the phone. Her father is coming home on Friday and he wants this finished."

The household. Tunde, 47, an engineer on a contract in Lagos, home two weekends in four. Funke, a school administrator. Segun, twelve. A paternal aunt, 55, who lives with them and who, Funke says, "knows everything before I do".

The parents' account. Six weeks ago Funke looked through Amara's phone while she was in the bathroom. She found a message thread with a boy in SS3, and a second thread in which Amara described her mother to a friend as "the police". Since then: the phone is confiscated at nine; Amara's movements are checked by the aunt; the gate is locked at seven; and Amara has stopped speaking at dinner. Funke's summary: "she is secretive, and she is becoming somebody I do not know."

Amara's account, alone, in the second session. "They are spies. She reads my phone. Auntie reports where I go. My father comes home and shouts for two days and then leaves and it is worse." Asked what the messages were about: "He is in SS3. We talk. That is all." Asked what she wants out of this: "I want a door."

What the therapist establishes, in three questions. First: what have you tried that they did not notice? Answer: she has started walking home the long way, and she has begun attending a church youth group on Wednesdays that she did not tell them about, "because if I tell them they will come". Second: what do you want to do after school? A long pause, then: "Physiotherapy." Who decided that? "Me. I read about it." Does your father know? "He says I am doing medicine." Third: when did you last tell your mother something true? "When I was thirteen."

The three findings, in order of clinical weight.

1. The parents' surveillance is functioning as designed and producing the opposite of its aim. Phone search, aunt as informant, locked gate, no negotiated agreement. Each measure was a response to a real fear, and together they have produced an adolescent with a private life she now has a reason to hide. This is the mechanism of the week's reframe: secrecy is what privacy becomes when privacy is not available.

2. The identity work is real and is being read as defiance. She has made an occupational commitment (physiotherapy, based on exploration — she read about it, she talked to a physiotherapist at the church clinic) and a belief commitment (the youth group). Her father has foreclosed the first on her behalf: medicine, because he is a man who did not have the choice himself. The conflict at the dinner table is not about a phone. It is about whether she is allowed to be the author of her own life, and the father experiences the question as ingratitude.

3. There is nothing in the referral that constitutes a safeguarding concern. The boy is seventeen, the relationship is described as talking, there is no coercion, no age gap that triggers the clinic's protocol, and no indication of harm. The therapist records this explicitly, because the family's escalation invites a professional to escalate with it, and a clinician who over-reacts here will be used as an instrument of the surveillance.

The formulation. A fifteen-year-old at the moratorium stage, with one commitment made and one foreclosed by her father, in a household where the information system has broken and been replaced by surveillance. The presenting problem is the broken system. The underlying problem is that the father's absences have left the mother managing alone, with an aunt as a second authority and no agreed hierarchy — which is the Week 3 problem in this family, returned at a different age.

The intervention, in four parts.

Part 1 — Restore an information system, on the three-column contract. Safety data unasked (where, who, when, a message if late). Questions at one stated hour, not at the door. A breach met with a phone call, not a lockdown.
