The phone returned, with the agreement that the parents do not read it — and with the therapist saying plainly, in front of the adolescent, that she has advised this and why: because a fifteen-year-old with no private space will create one, and the created one is the one you cannot see.

Part 2 — Give the aunt a defined role, aloud. The aunt is not the problem; the undefined authority is. Funke and the aunt agree, in the session, on what the aunt decides (whether Amara goes out at all while Funke is at work, and safety) and what she reports (arrival times only, and anything unsafe — not friendships, not clothes, not moods). The sentence is said to Amara: "Auntie is in charge of whether you go and when you come back. She is not in charge of who your friends are."

Part 3 — The Wednesday. Amara tells her parents about the youth group, in the session, and the parents agree that she attends without being accompanied. This is the cheapest and most powerful intervention in the case: a piece of her life is voluntarily disclosed, in exchange for nothing, and its effect on the parents' fear is larger than any explanation the therapist could give.

Part 4 — The father's two days. Tunde's fortnightly returns are the family's structural problem. In the session with him present, the therapist asks one question: "what do you want her to remember about these two days, in ten years?" He is silent for a while and then says: "that I asked her things." The agreement that follows is specific and small: on the Saturday of each return, one hour, no agenda, no school results, no phone — and the topic is hers. The therapist does not ask him to accept physiotherapy. She asks him to ask her about it, in front of her, once, and then to say what he thinks without deciding.

7.6a Six weeks later

The contract has been broken twice. The first time, Amara came home at nine-forty without a message; the phone call was made; the conversation lasted four minutes; nothing was confiscated. The second time, Funke asked a question at the door; Amara said "not now, at eight", and the question was asked at eight.

Amara has told her father about physiotherapy. He has not agreed. He has stopped saying "you are doing medicine", which the therapist logs as the realistic outcome of a single hour, and which is more than the referral predicted.

The aunt has been given her defined role and has, according to Funke, "started telling me less", which Funke reports with ambivalence and which is the correct direction.

The most informative change is one nobody planned. Amara has started asking her mother about her own school days. Funke reports this as a curiosity; the therapist hears it as the beginning of the adolescent's interest in the parent as a person, which is the developmental signature of the move from identity to intimacy that occupies the late teens.

What would have changed the plan. If the messages had contained sexual content with an adult, the safeguarding protocol would have taken precedence over everything in this section. If Amara had described coercion, threats, or a pregnancy, the plan would have been safety first and negotiation second. If the father had refused to attend, the work would have proceeded with mother and daughter, with a specific question kept open for him. And if Amara had refused the contract entirely — a real possibility — the therapist would have worked with the parents alone on the surveillance, on the grounds that the system that is producing the secrecy does not require the adolescent's consent to be changed.

7.7 Transcript: the negotiation, with the contract on the table

Twelve turns from the third session. Tunde is present for this session, having flown in the night before. The adolescent is fifteen. The therapist has a blank three-column sheet in front of her and a pen she is not holding.

T1 Therapist: I want to do something different today. I want us to write three lists, together, and I want the lists to be the only thing we argue about. The first list is what Amara tells you without being asked. (to Amara) You start. What would you be willing to tell them without being asked? — The therapist hands the first move to the adolescent, which is the whole architecture of the intervention. Note what she does not do: she does not begin with the parents' complaint. T2 Amara: Where I am. When I am coming back. If I am late. T3 Tunde: That is not the problem. The problem is — T4 Therapist: (gently) Hold it for four minutes. I promise it gets a column. — The single most useful sentence in adolescent family work: a promise of a specific place for the objection, with a time limit. It works because it is true. T5 Tunde: (nods, sits back) T6 Therapist: (writing) Where you are, when you are coming back, a message if you are late. That is column one. Second list: what they get to ask, and when. (to the parents) What do you need to know? T7 Funke: Who she is with. And where. T8 Therapist: "Who she is with" is in column one, if she will put it there. (to Amara) Will you? — Note the move: the therapist does not grant or refuse the request; she routes it to the person who owns it. This is what "negotiation" means operationally, and it takes about eight seconds. T9 Amara: (pause) Yes. But not the names. Just "a friend from school". T10 Funke: How is that supposed to — T11 Therapist: (to Funke) That is a counter-offer, and it is the first one she has made. Take it or make one, but do not lose it. — The therapist names the offer as an offer, which changes its status from a half-victory to a move in a negotiation. Parents in these sessions consistently miss the conciliatory move because it arrives in the same tone as the defiant one. T12 Tunde: (to Amara, not to the therapist) If you tell us "a friend from school" and you are late, what happens? — The father has moved from complaint to terms. This is the turn the session was for, and the correct response is to let him run it.

7.8 Laboratory: the negotiation, marked

Aim. To conduct a three-column negotiation with an adolescent and two parents in twenty-five minutes, and to keep the frame intact when it is tested.

Procedure. Quadruples: therapist, adolescent, two parents, one observer. The trainer supplies a one-page brief to each role privately, including one instruction designed to break the frame — the adolescent is told to refuse column one entirely; one parent is told to demand the phone's contents; the other is told to leave the room if the therapist takes the adolescent's side. The therapist must run the negotiation without knowing which brief is which. Twenty-five minutes, then the observer reports which pressure arrived and how it was handled.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The frameConfidentiality not stated, or stated and then brokenStated clearly, with exceptions, but not held under pressureStated, held, and used — the therapist refuses to report content and says why
The three columnsTwo or fewer filled, or filled by the therapistThree filled, but with unactionable itemsThree filled, specific, with breach consequences written by the parties
VoiceTherapist speaks more than the familyTherapist speaks about as muchTherapist speaks least; both parties address each other
Handling refusalConcedes the frame or abandons the sessionHolds the frame, but arguesHolds the frame, names the refusal as information, and offers a narrower agreement
The closeEnds with understandingEnds with a planEnds with a plan, a forecast of the breach, and each party's own outcome question

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the student to not fill in a column themselves. A therapist who says "so you will tell them where you are" has written the agreement on the family's behalf, and the family will not keep a therapist's agreement.

7.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Becoming the parents' agent. The referral was written by the parents; the therapist reads it; the first session is with the parents. By the time the adolescent arrives she is meeting an employee. The correction is procedural: the adolescent is seen first, or at least given equal time and equal billing, in the first session.

Error 2 — Trying to be the adolescent's friend. The opposite error, and it produces a therapist the adolescent does not respect and the parents do not trust. The position is not friendship; it is advocacy with limits — the therapist is on the adolescent's side in the sense that she will not lie to her, which is a different and more durable thing.

Error 3 — Treating the phone as the problem. The phone is the site. Remove it and the site moves. The formulation is about an information system and a developmental need, and a therapist who negotiates about a device has accepted the family's definition of the problem.

Error 4 — Calling normal development a disorder. Moratorium looks like confusion; experimentation looks like risk; argument looks like opposition; a late bedtime looks like defiance. Each has a developmental reading, and the diagnostic threshold in this age band should be high — which is not the same as absent.

Error 5 — Colluding with cultural foreclosure. "In our culture children do what their parents decide" may be true as a description of a household and false as a description of a fifteen-year-old's development. The therapist's job is not to overturn the household's values but to make room for the question of what the young person actually wants, because a foreclosed identity chosen for someone else is a stable arrangement that fails at twenty-six.

Error 6 — Ignoring the parent who is absent. Tunde's two days in four are the structure of this family and no one in the referral mentioned them. The absent parent's absence is a clinical fact even when — and especially when — it is presented as a virtue.

7.9 Six errors, continued: the one that ends treatment

There is a seventh error in this age band, and it is not a technique error; it is a relationship error with a predictable end.

Error 7 — Becoming the only adult who understands her. The therapist who says, or implies, "I am the one person who gets you" has made a promise that requires the family to remain incomprehensible. It is flattering to the therapist, it is common in trainees working with adolescents, and it converts the work into a competition in which the adolescent must choose between the therapist and her parents — a choice that will be made against the therapist the moment the therapy ends.

The correction is a discipline, and it has three parts. Never criticise the parents in the adolescent's presence, even when she invites it and even when the criticism is accurate. Always attribute the adolescent's insights to her rather than to your own cleverness ("you worked that out", not "as I said last week"). And always be explicit that the aim is a better relationship at home, not a better relationship with you: "if in six weeks you and your mother can have this conversation without me, we have done the job."

The marker of the error in supervision is a therapist who reports that the parents are "the problem" and that the adolescent is "the only sensible one in the family". The marker of the correction is a therapist who can say what she likes about each member and can name, without contempt and without exception, the interest that each of them is pursuing in the room.

7.10 Culture, faith and the adolescent

Dating, courtship and the family's stake. In many households the adolescent's relationships are not private matters but family matters, because marriage is a family project and a daughter's reputation is a family asset. A therapist who frames the issue as "her right to privacy" will lose the parents in one sentence, and will have done nothing for the adolescent. The workable frame is the one this week uses: safety data belongs to the household, intimacy data belongs to the young person, and the boundary between them is written down. That frame is defensible inside the household's own values, which is why it is usable.

Faith as an identity resource. For many adolescents the first genuinely owned commitment is religious, and the youth group, the choir, or the mosque's youth programme is the setting in which identity work happens. Amara's Wednesday is the ordinary case. A therapist who hears "church group" as a symptom of parental control will misread the most developmentally healthy thing in the referral.

Gender and the double standard. Households frequently hold different rules for sons and daughters, and the adolescent will name this before the parents do. The therapist should neither endorse nor dismiss the double standard; she should make it speakable, because a rule that cannot be discussed becomes a rule that is broken. The question that tends to work: "what is this rule protecting, and is there a version of it that everyone can live with?"

The aunt, the uncle and the second authority. Extended-household caregiving is a resource and a coordination problem. The clinical rule from Week 3 applies unchanged: authority must be defined aloud, in domains, in front of the child. A household with three adults and no defined hierarchy produces an adolescent who plays the adults off against each other, and the adults will describe that as manipulation.

DEBATE — "A parent has the right to read a fifteen-year-old's messages." Argue for: the parent is legally and morally responsible for a minor's safety, the child has no expectation of privacy in a phone the parent paid for, and the risk of not knowing is severe and irreversible. Argue against: reading messages purchases information at the cost of the relationship that would have produced it, the adolescent will simply move to a second account, and the intervention makes the parent the adversary at exactly the age when the adolescent most needs an adult ally. Then state your actual rule, and say what you would do if a parent told you they had already read the messages and found something alarming.

7.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The dual-systems account of adolescent risk — a relatively mature reward system and a still-developing control system — is well supported, and the peer-amplification finding has been replicated in both laboratory and naturalistic designs. The identity-status literature is observational but consistent: exploration with subsequent commitment (achieved) is associated with better adjustment than foreclosure or diffusion, across a wide range of cultures, though the content of identity differs by setting.

What is weaker. The claim that parenting style causes adolescent outcomes is confounded by the obvious possibility that adolescents influence parenting. The longitudinal work that separates the directions finds bidirectional effects, with parental warmth and monitoring predicting somewhat better outcomes and adolescent behaviour predicting parental monitoring strongly. The practical implication: a parent who monitors more because the adolescent is in difficulty is doing something reasonable, and the question is whether the monitoring is warm and negotiated or surveillance.

What is not supported. That adolescent-parent conflict is inherently pathological, or that its absence is health. Cross-cultural work finds that moderate conflict over autonomy is normative in settings where adolescents have individualised futures, and that the families with no conflict at all are as likely to be foreclosed as harmonious.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same parental monitoring that protects a thirteen-year-old can damage a seventeen-year-old, and the therapist's judgement is about calibration rather than quantity.

7.12 Key terms

Identity versus role confusion — Erikson's fifth stage; the construction of commitments to work, belief, relationship and place.

Moratorium — active exploration without settled commitment; developmentally healthy and anxiety-provoking.

Foreclosure — commitment without exploration, usually inherited from the family.

Diffusion — neither exploration nor commitment.

Separation-individuation — becoming a separate person while remaining attached; in most of the world this happens inside the household rather than by leaving it.

Pubertal timing — the developmental effect of when maturation occurs relative to the peer group, independent of maturation itself.

Dual-systems model — the account of adolescent risk as the product of an early-maturing reward system and a later-maturing control system.

Monitoring — parental knowledge of the adolescent's world; effective when warm and negotiated.

Surveillance — information obtained without the adolescent's participation; reliably produces concealment.

Negotiated trust — an explicit, written agreement about what is shared, what is asked, and what happens when it breaks.

7.13 Self-test

  1. Give Marcia's four identity statuses and one presentation of each that a therapist would meet in a first session.
  2. A twelve-year-old girl who looks sixteen is referred for "defiance". What is your first hypothesis, and what one question tests it?
  3. State the dual-systems model in two sentences, and name one thing it does not license.
  4. Why does the therapist in the transcript promise the father four minutes, and what would have happened without the promise?
  5. Distinguish monitoring from surveillance, and give one behavioural example of each.
  6. An adolescent says nothing for the first twenty minutes. Name three moves, in order.
  7. What are the three columns of the contract, and what are the four things that must never appear in it?
  8. In the Adeyemi case, what was the single cheapest intervention, and why did it work?

Answers. (1) Achieved — "I want to do physiotherapy because I worked in a clinic and liked it"; moratorium — "I am trying law, medicine and music"; foreclosed — "I am doing medicine, my father is a doctor"; diffuse — "I do not know, I have not thought about it". (2) That the social response to an early-maturing body is doing the clinical work — she is being treated as older than she is; the question is "do people treat you as older or younger than you are?" (3) The reward system matures ahead of the control system, so sensation-seeking and peer sensitivity are high while inhibition is still developing; it does not license the claim that adolescents cannot reason or decide. (4) To give the objection a specific place, so that the father could tolerate being interrupted without concluding he had been silenced; without it he would have escalated, and the negotiation would have become a contest the therapist had to win. (5) Monitoring is knowledge acquired with the adolescent's participation and is warm; surveillance is knowledge acquired without it. Example of monitoring: a stated evening hour in which the day is discussed; of surveillance: reading the messages. (6) Name the situation honestly; ask for views rather than feelings; use a third object or a trade with a real cost to the therapist. (7) What she will share unasked; what they will ask and how often; what happens when it breaks. Never: blanket access to the phone, reading a diary, reporting the content of friendships, or a promise to tell them everything. (8) The Wednesday youth group disclosed in the session — it cost the adolescent little, gave the parents a piece of her life voluntarily offered, and reduced their fear more than any explanation could.

7.14 Reflective task

Think of one thing you decided about yourself between thirteen and sixteen that your family did not know at the time. Write it down, without naming anyone. Then answer three questions: what would have happened if they had found out? What did keeping it cost you? And what would an adult have had to do to be told? Most students find that the third answer is very specific and very small — a particular aunt, a particular drive, a particular hour of the evening — which is the point: adolescents do not withhold from adults in general, they withhold from adults who have not earned a particular door.

7.15 Further reading

  • Erikson, E. Identity: Youth and Crisis — the source text, still the clearest.
  • Marcia, J. "Identity in adolescence" — the four statuses and the interview that produces them.
  • Steinberg, L. Adolescence — the dual-systems account, with the evidence.
  • Smetana, J. "Adolescents and parents: negotiating autonomy" — the conflict literature, and why moderate conflict is normative.
  • Keddie, A. and Dinham, J. on extended-family authority — useful on the second-authority problem in households with resident relatives.

Next: Week 8 — Adolescence II: risk, peers, digital life and mental health.

Week 8 — Adolescence II: risk, peers, digital life and mental health

Risk behaviour is normative in moderation and dangerous in combination: substance use, risky driving, unsafe sex, self-harm, disordered eating. Risk is not a moral category; it is a developmental phenomenon shaped by peers, family, school and access. The purpose of this week is to replace the question "why is he doing this?" with the four questions that actually predict harm: what, how often, with whom, and what does it do for him?

Digital life. Assess function, not just quantity: what is the phone doing in this adolescent's life — connection, escape, status, self-soothing, harm? Ask about sleep, cyberbullying, sexual content, and the family's rules about devices. Banning without substituting an alternative rarely works, and the substitute is always a relationship rather than an activity.

Mental health. Half of all lifetime mental disorders begin by mid-adolescence. Screen, do not guess. Distinguish transient distress from disorder by duration, impairment and severity — and use the same developmental question as every other week: is this a normal task being struggled with, or a disorder?

What makes this week different from the last. Last week was the person. This week is the environment the person is in — the peer group, the phone, the substances that are available at the junction, and the adults who are or are not watching. The clinical skill is to hold both: an adolescent who is self-harming is a person in developmental transition and a person in an environment, and a formulation that has only one of those halves will produce an intervention that fails.

— Conduct a twelve-minute adolescent interview with a peer who has been briefed to be reticent. You may not ask more than two questions in a row without reflecting something back. The observer records the ratio of questions to reflections, and whether the adolescent elaborated. The target ratio is one reflection to every two questions.
— This week contains material on self-harm and suicide. The clinical content is included because a family therapist who cannot ask the questions directly will miss the presentations that matter most. The teaching position is that asking does not plant the idea; the evidence on that point is consistent and should be stated explicitly to every class and to every parent who raises it.

8.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8Three questions that replace oneWhy "why is he doing this?" produces nothing usableRewrite the question for two cases
8–22Risk as developmentThe four risk domains, their functions, and the combinations that matterPlot four vignettes on a function-versus-harm grid
22–34Peers and accessPeer amplification, and the local availability mapDraw an availability map for their own catchment
34–48Digital lifeAssessing function rather than hours; sleep, bullying, sexual content, and the substituteTake a digital history from a volunteer
48–62Mental healthOnset, the duration-impairment-severity rule, and the seven presentations not to missScreen two vignettes and justify the referral
62–74Self-harm and suicideWhat to ask, in what words, and what to do with the answerRehearse the four questions aloud, in pairs
74–84The family's responseEscalation, abdication, and the third optionScript a family safety plan
84–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The lecture must rehearse the suicide questions aloud, in the room, by every student, because the barrier to asking is entirely in the throat. A therapist who has never said "have you thought about killing yourself?" out loud will substitute a euphemism at the moment it matters, and the euphemism does not get answered. The rehearsal is uncomfortable and it is the most important ten minutes in the module.

The sentence the lecture must land. "Risk behaviour is a solution somebody is using. Your first job is to find out what problem it solves, and your second is to find out whether it is working."

8.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Risk is a developmental phenomenon, not a moral one. Sensation-seeking, peer sensitivity, and a steep discounting of delayed outcomes are features of the period, not defects of the person. The clinical consequence is that prevention works better than prohibition: an adolescent who is given a domain in which risk is supervised — sport, performance, competition, a demanding apprenticeship — takes less unsupervised risk, because the developmental appetite has somewhere to go.

2. Function beats frequency. The question is not how much but what for. Cannabis every weekend with a group of friends who play football is a different clinical object from cannabis alone at eleven at night. The five functions that account for most adolescent risk behaviour are: belonging (the group does it); escape (it makes the evening bearable); status (it changes how others see me); self-soothing (it quiets something); and self-punishment (it expresses what I cannot say). Each function points to a different intervention, and the therapist who has not identified the function is guessing.

3. Combination is the danger, not any single behaviour. One risk behaviour in an adolescent with a stable household and an available adult is usually a phase. The same behaviour combined with school withdrawal, sleep loss, a bereavement, and an absent parent is a different prognosis. The assessment should therefore always ask: what else is happening at the same time? The answer is the risk multiplier.

4. Digital life is a setting, not an activity. The phone is where the peer group lives, where status is measured, where sleep is lost, and where a substantial proportion of bullying and sexual content arrives. Assessing "screen time" tells you almost nothing. Assessing what the phone is doing — connection, escape, status, self-soothing, harm — tells you what to do. The single most useful structural question is where the phone sleeps.

5. Most adult mental disorders begin here. The epidemiological finding is robust: half of lifetime disorders have onset by the mid-twenties, with anxiety, mood, substance and impulse-control disorders beginning earliest. This makes adolescence the period of maximum preventive leverage and the period in which the family therapist is most likely to be the first clinician in the room. The obligation that follows is a screening obligation: a therapist who is working with an adolescent for anything must be able to ask about mood, anxiety, sleep, eating, self-harm and substances, and must know where to refer.

8.2a Seven presentations that must not be missed

The differential in this age band is wide and the presentations are quiet. The seven below are the ones that a family therapist must be able to recognise, name, and refer — not diagnose.

1. Depression. Not "sadness". The adolescent pattern is more often irritability than sadness, combined with withdrawal from things that used to matter, sleep change, appetite change, a fall in school performance, and a flatness that the family describes as "laziness". Duration of two weeks or more, with impairment, is the threshold that warrants a referral. One question screens surprisingly well: "when did you last enjoy something?"

2. Anxiety, including panic and social anxiety. Physical symptoms — racing heart, breathlessness, nausea — presenting as school refusal or as repeated visits to a clinic for a physical complaint. The distinguishing feature is anticipatory fear that leads to avoidance, and the avoidance is what maintains it.

3. Psychosis prodrome. Social withdrawal, a drop in functioning, odd ideas that are treated as eccentricity, suspiciousness, a decline in self-care, and sometimes a preoccupation with a new interest that has become the whole of the person's life. This is rare and it is the presentation where delay costs most. Any adolescent with a marked, unexplained decline in functioning plus unusual beliefs should be referred for assessment, promptly, and the referral should not be softened into "stress".

4. Substance use. Ask about what, how often, with whom, and what happens when it stops. The developmental danger signs are: use alone, use before midday, use to manage sleep or mood, and withdrawal from other activities. Do not accept "only at parties" without asking what happens at parties.

5. Disordered eating. Weight change is the least reliable sign. More useful: eating in secret, food rituals, a sudden interest in "healthy" eating that narrows to a list, exercising alone and compulsively, and a change in the family's mealtimes. The family's report of "she has become fussy" is worth three questions.

6. Self-harm. Cutting, burning, hitting, and — increasingly — the deliberate consumption of harmful content. Self-harm is not a failed suicide attempt and it is not attention-seeking; it is most often a regulation strategy, and its presence is one of the strongest single predictors of later suicide risk. It requires direct, unshocked questioning and a proper assessment.

7. The ordinary distress of a normal task. Most referrals are this. A fifteen-year-old who is miserable for three weeks after a breakup, who sleeps badly before exams, who is angry about a move, is doing developmental work. The distinction is duration, impairment, and severity — and the error is not to dismiss ordinary distress, which is real, but to treat it as a disorder, which pathologises a competent person at the moment she is learning to cope.

The rule that governs all seven. A family therapist screens and refers; she does not diagnose in the therapy room, and she does not manage a psychosis or a developing eating disorder in a family session. The screening obligation is real and the scope limit is real, and holding both is the professional position this week is teaching.

8.2b Self-harm and suicide: what to ask and what to do with it

This section is written to be used. It is deliberately concrete, because the failure mode in this age band is not a wrong intervention; it is a question that was never asked.

The four questions, in this order.

  1. "Some young people, when things are hard, hurt themselves on purpose. Have you ever done that?"
  2. "Have you ever thought that you would be better off dead, or wished you could go to sleep and not wake up?"
  3. "Have you thought about how you would do it?" — the presence of a method, and its availability, is the single strongest predictor of risk.
  4. "What has kept you going so far?" — the protective factors, and the beginning of the safety plan.

Why the wording matters. "Are you having any thoughts of self-harm?" is a professional's sentence and gets a professional's answer, which is "no". "Have you ever hurt yourself on purpose?" is a person's sentence and gets an answer. The questions must be asked plainly, without a preamble that signals alarm, and without the therapist's face changing, because the adolescent is watching the face for the verdict on whether she has just made a terrible mistake.

The myth, stated plainly. Asking about suicide does not increase risk. The evidence on this is consistent across settings and the belief that it does is the main reason clinicians do not ask. Say this to parents, in these words, because they will be frightened by the questions and will otherwise interpret them as an intervention that caused the problem.

What to do with a positive answer. In order: do not leave the adolescent alone if risk is current; involve the parents in the same session, with the adolescent's knowledge and, where possible, her agreement about how it is said; remove or restrict means — medication, blades, pesticides, firearms, and anything else available in the household; arrange a same-week or same-day referral according to local protocol; document exactly what was asked, what was answered, and what was done. Then arrange the next contact before the family leaves the building.

The safety plan, which is not a contract. A "no-harm contract" has no evidence behind it and can shift responsibility onto the adolescent. A safety plan is different: it is a written list of (a) the warning signs, (b) the things the adolescent will do first, (c) the people she will contact, in order, and (d) the means that have been removed from her reach. It is written with her, in her words, and the parents have a copy that says what they will do, not what she has promised.

The family's part. Two things a family can do that matter more than any conversation: reduce access to means, and increase supervision without surveillance. The distinction is the same as Week 7's: a parent who sits in the room is supervision; a parent who reads the diary is surveillance. The second produces concealment, which is the one outcome a safety plan cannot survive.

8.3 The method: a risk assessment a family therapist can actually do

Ten steps, thirty to forty minutes, doable in a family session with the adolescent seen alone for the middle portion. The purpose is not diagnosis. It is to answer three questions: how serious, what else, and what happens next.

Step 1 — Establish the frame first. "I am going to ask you some direct questions. Some of them will feel strange. I ask everybody. If I think you are in danger I will have to act on it, and I will tell you before I do." The last clause is what makes the questions answerable.

Step 2 — Ask about the presenting behaviour in behavioural terms. Not "why did you cut yourself?" but "where, when, how many times, what were you feeling just before, and what happened afterwards?" The afterwards question identifies the function with surprising reliability: relief points to regulation; numbness points to dissociation; punishment points to self-directed anger.

Step 3 — Ask the four questions from 8.2b. In order, plainly, without a preamble.

Step 4 — Map the combination. Sleep, appetite, school attendance, withdrawal, substances, eating, mood, anxiety. The question "what else is going on?" is asked after the disclosure, never before, because before it signals that the therapist has already decided what the problem is.

Step 5 — Ask about the environment, not just the individual. Who knows? Who would she tell? Who in the household is available at eleven at night? What is in the house that could be used? Where does the phone sleep? Is there anyone at school?

Step 6 — Establish the protective factors. What has kept her going; what she is looking forward to, however small; who she would not want to hurt; what she is good at. These are not consolation prizes. They are the material of the safety plan and the best available predictors of who comes back.

Step 7 — Bring the parents in, with the adolescent's knowledge. How it is said is negotiated first: "your mother needs to know about the cutting. Do you want to tell her, or shall I?" The choice matters more than the content, because it preserves the adolescent's standing in her own disclosure.

Step 8 — Restrict means, concretely. Ask what is in the house: medications, blades, rat poison, pesticides, a car, a rope in the yard. Then assign the task of removal to a named adult, with a date. A safety plan that does not change the household's physical environment is a conversation.

Step 9 — Decide the level of care and say it out loud. Outpatient with weekly contact; urgent referral; emergency. Say which, and say why, in front of the family, so that the decision is not a mystery. Families who are told the reasoning cooperate with the plan; families who are handed a plan comply with it until the first difficulty.

Step 10 — Write it down and book the next contact. A one-page safety plan in the adolescent's words, a copy to the parents, a date in the diary before anyone leaves the room. Then document all of it.

8.3a Digital life: the history that is worth taking

Five questions, ten minutes, and they replace the argument about hours.

Question 1 — Where does it sleep? The answer predicts sleep loss more reliably than any measure of use. An adolescent whose phone is in the bed has a device that interrupts sleep, and sleep loss is causally implicated in mood deterioration, which makes this the highest-yield structural question in the week.

Question 2 — What is it for? Connection (the group), escape (the scroll), status (the post), soothing (the same video forty times), or harm (the group that excludes, the person who threatens). Ask for a description of the last hour before sleep, which is when the function is most visible.

Question 3 — Who is in the group, and who is not? Exclusion in this age band is frequently technical: not added, muted, removed. Ask whether she is in the class group and whether she wants to be. The answer is often the whole of the presenting problem.

Question 4 — What has arrived that you did not want? This is the question that produces disclosures of bullying, sexual content, and strangers, and it is asked without the parents in the room. Then: "did you tell anyone? what did they do?" The second question audits the adult system, not the adolescent.

Question 5 — What would you do if you lost it for a week? The answer describes the phone's function better than any inventory. An adolescent who is horrified is describing a social lifeline. An adolescent who is indifferent is describing an escape that has stopped working.

What to do, in order of evidence. Protect sleep (device out of the bedroom, for everyone); protect the one relationship that matters (a named adult who is available and who does not confiscate); build one non-screen domain of competence (this is the "substitute" the stub refers to, and it is always an activity with other people in it); and then, only then, negotiate limits. Limits imposed before the substitute exists produce concealment.

8.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The function grid. With the adolescent, place each risk behaviour on a two-axis grid: what it does for me (the five functions) and what it costs me (health, money, school, relationships, freedom). Fill it in her language. The grid does more than a lecture because it is her data, and because it makes visible the behaviours that are cheap and the ones that are expensive — which is not always where the family's anxiety is concentrated.

Technique 2 — The availability map. Draw the household and the route to school, and mark where each substance or means is available: the junction, the flat upstairs, the older cousin, the chemist that sells without a prescription, the drawer in the kitchen. The map converts a moral question into a logistical one, and logistics can be changed. It also routinely reveals that the family has more control over access than it believed.

Technique 3 — The one adult at eleven at night. Ask the adolescent to name the person she would contact at eleven at night if something had happened. If the answer is "nobody", that is the intervention: find, in the household or the extended family, one adult who will agree to be reachable at that hour without asking questions first. This is the cheapest suicide-prevention intervention available to a family therapist and it is almost never written down.

8.5 The first session with a self-harming adolescent and two parents

Phase 1 — The parents' two minutes each (5 minutes). What they found, when, and what they did. The therapist listens for the sequence: discovery, reaction, and what has happened since. The reaction is usually a confiscation, a shouting, or a silence, and each of the three has a predictable consequence.

Phase 2 — The adolescent alone (25 minutes). The frame, then the ten-step assessment, compressed. The four questions are asked in this phase and never in front of the parents, because the parents' faces make the answers impossible.

Phase 3 — Negotiate the disclosure (5 minutes). What will be told, in what words, and by whom. The adolescent is given the first option to tell it herself.

Phase 4 — The joint session (20 minutes). The disclosure; the parents' reaction, which the therapist manages by slowing it down rather than by correcting it; then the means restriction, assigned by name; then the safety plan, written.

Phase 5 — The plan and the next appointment (5 minutes). Level of care, next contact, who telephones whom, and what the parents do tonight. Nothing is left as "we will see".

Why the parents are not in the room for phase 2. Not to protect the adolescent from them, but to protect the information. A sixteen-year-old will not describe the function of her cutting in front of a mother who is crying, and the function is the treatment target. The parents are brought in as soon as the information exists, and they are brought in by design rather than by accident.

8.5a The crisis hour: when the risk is immediate

Most of this week concerns assessment. This section concerns the one hour in which assessment is not the task, because the adolescent in front of you is at immediate risk and the decision cannot wait for a referral.

The four indicators of immediacy. A stated intent, rather than an idea. A plan with a time. Access to the means, in the room or in the house, tonight. And the absence of any named reason to stay alive in the next twenty-four hours. Two of the four is urgent. Three is an emergency, and the therapist's job changes from treating to transporting.

The first move is physical, not conversational. Do not leave the adolescent alone. If she is in the clinic, stay in the room and ask a colleague to attend. If she is at home on the telephone, ask her to go and sit with a named person in the house, now, while you stay on the line. The single most useful thing in the first five minutes is another human being in the same room as her.

The second move is to remove the means, by name. "Where is it? Is it in the house? Who can take it now?" Ask the adolescent first, then the parent. A blade, a bottle of pesticide, a stock of tablets, a car key. The instruction is specific: not "keep her safe" but "take the bottle to the neighbour's house tonight and tell me when it is done". Then record what was said and who confirmed it.

The third move is to tell the parents, with the adolescent's knowledge, in her hearing. "I am going to tell your mother that you have a plan and a time, because she is the person who can be with you tonight. I am not telling her because you did something wrong." This is the one situation in which the therapist does not offer a choice about disclosure, and saying so plainly is more respectful than pretending the choice exists.

The fourth move is the handover, and it has a name in it. Not "go to the hospital". A named facility, a named person if possible, a written note, and confirmation that the family has arrived. A therapist who sends a family to "casualty" has sent them to a building, and a family that arrives at a building at midnight without a name and without a person to ask for will go home again, and will wait until Monday, and the hour will have been lost in the interval.

The fifth move is the follow-up call. Within twenty-four hours, to the family and, with permission, to the adolescent. The call is not clinical; it is the demonstration that the crisis did not end the relationship. Adolescents who are admitted after an attempt and who receive no contact from the referring clinician are markedly less likely to return afterwards.

What not to do. Do not extract a promise. Do not ask the family to "watch her closely" without saying what that means in hours and in people. Do not schedule the next appointment for a fortnight. Do not tell the adolescent that she is being dramatic, and do not tell her that everything will be fine — both are statements she has heard before from adults who then did nothing.

Afterwards, the therapist's own state. A crisis hour is frightening, and the two common reactions are over-reaction (escalating every subsequent disclosure to the emergency pathway, which teaches the adolescent to stop disclosing) and under-reaction (recording the risk as lower than it was, in order to feel that the hour was manageable). Both are corrected in supervision, and the requirement to take a risk assessment to supervision within the week is not bureaucratic. It is the one reliable protection available to a lone clinician against the second error, which is the more dangerous of the two and the harder one to see from inside a hard week.

8.6 Case: the boy whose teacher saw his arm

Referral. Sadiq, sixteen, in Senior Secondary 2, was sent to the school counsellor after a teacher noticed scars on his left forearm during a practical. The counsellor telephoned his mother the same day and referred him to the clinic. The mother, Rukayat, 39, a textile trader, arrives with him. She has not told his father.

The household. Rukayat; Sadiq, the eldest of five children (16, 14, 11, 7, 3); Rukayat's mother, 66; and a younger brother of Rukayat's who helps in the shop. Sadiq's father, 52, lives in Kano with a second wife and telephones on Fridays. He pays school fees and has not visited in fourteen months. Rukayat's income is the household's income.

The discovery. Eight scars on the left forearm, five of them recent, all consistent with a blade. Two on the upper thigh, which the mother did not know about and which the therapist found by asking.

The parents' account, in the first five minutes. "He is quiet at home. He does not tell me anything. He used to be first in his class and now he is eleventh. I thought it was the phone." Then, after a pause: "His father will say I have failed."

The adolescent alone. The four questions are asked in order and answered in order.

  1. Have you ever hurt yourself on purpose? "Yes." When did it start? "Last year. After my father stopped coming."
  2. Have you ever thought you would be better off dead? A long pause. "Sometimes. Not like a plan. I think about not being here."
  3. Have you thought about how you would do it? "I have thought about the gutter on the third floor of the school. But I would not. My mother would die." — A method has been considered; a protective factor is named in the same breath. Both are recorded, and both change the plan.
  4. What has kept you going? "My sisters. And my mother. And football."

The function. Asked what happens just before: "it is after the phone call on Friday. He talks to my mother and then she is crying and she says nothing to us and I am the man of the house. I go to the bathroom." Asked what happens after: "it is quiet. For maybe ten minutes it is quiet."

The assessment. The function is regulation, with a secondary self-punishment component: the cutting follows a weekly event, it produces a brief quiet, and it is accompanied by a conviction that he should be managing better. The combination map shows: sleep onset after one in the morning, appetite reduced, school performance fallen from first to eleventh, withdrawal from football in the last two months, no substance use, no disordered eating. Duration: fourteen months, with the first scars dating to the month the father's visits stopped.

The risk formulation, written as it would be in a record. Current risk: moderate. Ideation present, intermittent, without intent or plan, with a named protective factor and a named method that has been considered and rejected. Self-harm: current, increasing in frequency over three months. Means: a razor blade kept in the bathroom, and medication in the kitchen. Protective: mother's availability, four younger siblings, football, a teacher who noticed. Aggravating: paternal absence, financial pressure, the role of "man of the house" assigned to a sixteen-year-old, sleep loss.

The formulation. A sixteen-year-old carrying an adult role he was given and not prepared for, in a household where the father's Friday telephone call is the week's emotional weather, and where nobody has asked him how he is because he is the one who is supposed to be all right. The cutting is a private regulation strategy for a public situation. It is not attention-seeking: nobody was meant to see it, and the teacher seeing it is the crisis of the case rather than its solution.

The intervention, in five parts.

Part 1 — Means restriction, tonight. The blade is removed by the mother in Sadiq's presence, with the therapist having said why. The kitchen medication moves to the mother's room. The head teacher is contacted with Sadiq's knowledge and asked to make the third-floor landing supervised at break. This is unglamorous and it is the part of the plan with the best evidence behind it.

Part 2 — The safety plan, in his words. Warning signs: "Friday night. When she is quiet after the phone." First three things: "call Tunde" (a teammate), "go and kick the ball outside even if it is dark", "play the guitar for ten minutes". People in order: mother, Tunde, the counsellor, the clinic number. Removed means: blade, kitchen medication. Written on one page, photographed by him and by his mother, and put by the clinic in the record.

Part 3 — Move the Friday. The single structural change of the case. The therapist asks Rukayat, with Sadiq present, what happens after the Friday call. She describes it: she cries in the bedroom, then cooks, then says nothing. The agreement: after the call she will sit with Sadiq and the two eldest for ten minutes and tell them what was said, in one sentence, without asking them to look after her. The content does not change. The secrecy does. A sixteen-year-old who knows what was said no longer has to infer it from his mother's face, and the inferring is what was producing the bathroom.

Part 4 — Give the role back. "Man of the house" is a role without a job description, and Sadiq has been doing it badly because it cannot be done. The therapist asks Rukayat to name, in front of him, three things that are his (school, football, his sisters' homework) and two that are not (money, her marriage, whether his father visits). The list is written. The purpose is to make the boundary visible, because an adolescent cannot refuse a role that nobody has named.

Part 5 — One hour a week with an adult who asks nothing. The counsellor at school, or the football coach, or an uncle. The instruction to the adult is the same as the week's technique: no questions about home, no advice, one hour, once a week, doing something. Sadiq chooses football with the coach on Wednesdays.

The referral. Sadiq is referred for a diagnostic assessment, which returns a picture of moderate depressive symptoms without a formal disorder at that point, and for six sessions of individual work alongside the family sessions. The therapist does not do the individual work herself; she keeps the family work, because a sixteen-year-old who is self-harming needs two clinicians less than he needs two distinct relationships that are not the same relationship.

8.6a Eight weeks later

The cutting has stopped for six weeks, which is a fact and not a cure. It has been replaced, in Sadiq's own description, by "the guitar and calling Tunde", and he says this with the flatness of someone reporting a task rather than a change.

The Friday conversation happens. Rukayat reports that it is "short and not nice", which is the realistic outcome: the content of the marriage has not changed and is not going to change in this therapy. What has changed is that Sadiq no longer receives it as atmosphere.

The grades have not recovered. The therapist records this as the case's honest limitation: a sixteen-year-old who has lost fourteen months of study does not recover them in eight weeks, and the school has agreed to a reduced subject load for one term.

The most significant change is not in the record. Rukayat has begun telling her brother, who helps in the shop, one true thing a week about the household. The therapist did not plan this. It is the ordinary consequence of a family in which information has started to move, and it is the best available sign that the change will survive the therapy.

What would have changed the plan. If the ideation had included intent, a plan, or a recent preparation, the case would have gone to emergency assessment the same day and the therapy room would have become a holding place rather than a treatment. If the mother had refused to remove the blade, the therapist would have documented the refusal and escalated within the safeguarding framework, because means restriction is not negotiable. If the father had been reachable and willing, one session with him on the telephone would have been attempted, with Sadiq's agreement — and if Sadiq had refused, the refusal would have been respected and recorded.

8.7 Transcript: asking the four questions

Twelve turns, compressed from twenty-five minutes. The transcript's purpose is to show the tone: flat, direct, unhurried, and without a single reassuring phrase in the wrong place.

T1 Therapist: I am going to ask you some direct questions. Some of them will feel strange. I ask everybody I see, and I am not going to be shocked by the answers. If I think you are in danger I will have to do something about it, and I will tell you before I do. All right? — The frame, in four sentences, before anything else. T2 Sadiq: Okay. T3 Therapist: Some young people, when things are hard, hurt themselves on purpose. Have you ever done that? — Question 1, with no preamble and no euphemism. T4 Sadiq: (pause) Yes. T5 Therapist: Where? T6 Sadiq: My arm. The inside. T7 Therapist: How many times? T8 Sadiq: I don't know. Many. T9 Therapist: (no change of expression, pen down) Have you ever thought that you would be better off dead, or wished you could go to sleep and not wake up? — Question 2, asked in the established tone. The pen going down is deliberate: writing while a person answers this question makes them a specimen. T10 Sadiq: (long pause) Sometimes. T11 Therapist: Have you thought about how you would do it? T12 Sadiq: (longer pause) The gutter. At school. The third floor. — The answer that changes the plan: a method, a place, and a school. Everything after this turn is logistics, and the therapist's next sentence is not a question about feelings.

8.8 Laboratory: the risk assessment, marked

Aim. To complete the ten-step assessment with a reticent adolescent in thirty-five minutes, obtain the disclosure, and leave with a signed safety plan and a documented level of care.

Procedure. Trios: therapist, adolescent (briefed with a disclosure that includes a method, a protective factor, and one detail she will withhold unless asked twice), observer. Thirty-five minutes, then ten minutes of feedback against the rubric. The withholding is the exercise's centre: the briefed adolescent will not volunteer the second location of the scars, and a therapist who does not ask about the body directly will miss it.

Marking rubric (25 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The frameNot stated; or stated as a warningStated, but after the first questionStated first, including the limits, and the adolescent's assent obtained
The four questionsNot asked, or asked with a euphemismAsked, but in a preamble-laden wayAsked plainly, in order, without a change of tone or expression
FunctionNot establishedAsked what happened beforeAsked before and after, and the function named in the adolescent's words
CombinationOnly the presenting behaviourTwo or three domains coveredAll seven domains screened, including sleep and eating
MeansNot discussedDiscussed generallySpecific inventory, named adult, date, and confirmation in the room
The planAdviceWritten plan without protective factorsWritten in the adolescent's words, with warning signs, sequence, means removed, and a booked next contact

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the student to ask question 3 (the method) after a positive answer to question 2, without softening. Most trainees stop at "sometimes I think about not being here", because the disclosure is distressing and because stopping feels kind. It is the moment at which the assessment is either completed or abandoned.

8.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Not asking. The largest error by volume, and the one with the clearest cause: the fear of causing harm. The evidence is unambiguous that asking does not increase risk. A therapist who works with adolescents and does not ask is not protecting them; she is protecting herself.

Error 2 — Asking in a way that can only be answered "no". "You are not thinking of doing anything silly, are you?" is a question with the answer built into its grammar. Every one of the four questions is closed, direct, and free of the answer.

Error 3 — Promising confidentiality you cannot keep. An adolescent who discloses self-harm after being told "everything stays in this room" has been misled at the moment of maximum vulnerability, and the breach of that promise is often the end of treatment. State the limits first, every time.

Error 4 — Making the family the enemy. Parents who react with confiscation, shouting, or silence are frightened, not cruel. A therapist who treats them as the problem loses the household, and the household is the safety plan.

Error 5 — The no-harm contract. A signed promise not to self-harm has no evidence behind it and shifts responsibility onto the adolescent at the moment she has least capacity to carry it. Replace it with a safety plan that describes what she will do, who she will call, and what has been removed from her reach.

Error 6 — Treating the disclosure as the endpoint. The disclosure is the beginning of the assessment. The commonest failure in supervision is a therapist who reports, with relief, "he told me everything", and who cannot say what the level of care was, what was removed from the house, or when the next contact is.

8.10 Culture, faith and adolescent risk

The household's reputation. In many settings an adolescent's self-harm or substance use is experienced by the family as a judgement on the household, and the first clinical obstacle is not the behaviour but the family's need for it not to be known. A therapist who telephones the school, calls a meeting, or writes to a relative without an explicit agreement will lose the family. The sequence is always: agree who knows, then act.

Faith, deliverance and the clinic. Families may hold that the behaviour is spiritual, and may pursue prayer, fasting or deliverance alongside or instead of clinical care. The workable position is neither collusion nor confrontation: accept the household's framework as the language of concern, ask what the prayer or the fasting is for, and negotiate one non-negotiable — that the safety plan is in place while the spiritual work continues. A family that is told to choose between its faith and the clinic will choose its faith, and the adolescent will be the one who loses.

Gender and the boy who must not be weak. Sadiq's case is a gender case before it is anything else. A sixteen-year-old boy in a household where he is "the man of the house" has been given an emotional prohibition along with the role. The clinical move is not to tell him to express his feelings; it is to give him a permitted domain — football, an uncle, a coach — in which being looked after is not a humiliation.

The second wife, the absent father, and the therapist's judgement. Polygynous arrangements, labour migration, and long paternal absences are ordinary structures in many of the households this book is written for. A therapist whose formulation requires the family to become a different kind of family will produce a plan that nobody follows. The question is always what can be changed inside the arrangement that exists — the Friday conversation, the role list, the uncle's hour — rather than what would have to change for the problem to disappear.

DEBATE — "A sixteen-year-old's self-harm should be disclosed to the parents whether or not she agrees." Argue for: the parents are the safety plan, the risk of concealment is death, and the adolescent's judgement is the thing that is compromised. Argue against: disclosure against her will ends the therapeutic relationship at the moment it is most needed, the adolescent will conceal more effectively next time, and a competent sixteen-year-old has a legitimate interest in how her own body is discussed. Then write the rule you would follow, including the case in which she refuses and the risk is moderate rather than imminent.

8.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. Means restriction is the intervention with the strongest evidence in suicide prevention at population level: reducing access to a common method reduces deaths, including in adolescents, and the effect is not merely substitution of method. Safety planning, as a structured and collaborative written intervention, has better support than no-harm contracts, which have essentially none. Sleep disturbance is a prospective predictor of adolescent mood deterioration, which is why the question about where the phone sleeps is a clinical question and not a parenting preference.

What is weaker. The evidence for digital-detox interventions is thin and the field is moving faster than the trials. The most defensible claims are structural (sleep protection, one non-screen domain of competence, one available adult) rather than quantitative (a number of hours). The evidence on family-based treatment for adolescent self-harm is promising but based on small trials with heterogeneous comparators.

What is not supported. That asking about suicide increases risk — the evidence is consistent that it does not, in clinical and school settings. That self-harm is attention-seeking — the clinical literature describes a predominantly private behaviour whose discovery is usually experienced as a crisis. And that confiscation of devices reduces harm — the observable effect is migration to unmonitored devices, which reduces the parents' knowledge without reducing the exposure.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same paternal absence produces, in one sixteen-year-old, a determination to succeed and, in another, fourteen months of silent cutting. What differs is not the input but the presence of one adult who asks, and that is what the therapist is trying to install.

8.12 Key terms

Function — what a risk behaviour does for the person; the first question of the assessment.

Combination risk — the multiplicative effect of several concurrent adversities, as against the effect of any one.

Means restriction — reducing access to the method; the best-evidenced single intervention in suicide prevention.

Safety plan — a written, collaborative list of warning signs, coping steps, contacts and removed means.

No-harm contract — a signed promise not to self-harm; not evidence-based and not a substitute for a safety plan.

Prodrome — the period of subtle change preceding the frank onset of a disorder, particularly psychosis.

Duration, impairment, severity — the three dimensions that distinguish disorder from distress.

Digital function — what a device is doing in a person's life: connection, escape, status, soothing, or harm.

Protective factor — a circumstance that reduces risk; in this age band usually a named adult and a domain of competence.

Level of care — the decided setting and intensity of treatment: outpatient, urgent, or emergency.

8.13 Self-test

  1. State the four questions of the risk assessment, in order, and say why question 3 follows question 2.
  2. A parent asks whether asking about suicide will put the idea in her daughter's head. What do you say, and what is the evidence?
  3. Name the five functions of adolescent risk behaviour and give one intervention matched to each.
  4. Why is means restriction the best-evidenced intervention in this field, and what does it require in practice?
  5. Distinguish a safety plan from a no-harm contract, and say why the difference matters clinically.
  6. Name the three dimensions that separate disorder from distress, and apply them to a fifteen-year-old who has been miserable for three weeks after a breakup.
  7. In the case of Sadiq, what was the single structural change, and what did it change?
  8. What is the "one adult at eleven at night", and why is it described as the cheapest intervention available?

Answers. (1) Self-harm; suicidal ideation; method and intent; protective factors. Question 3 follows because a positive answer to question 2 identifies ideation while question 3 identifies risk, and the method is the strongest single predictor. (2) That it does not — the evidence across clinical and school settings is consistent, and the belief that it does is the main reason clinicians fail to ask; say it plainly, because the parent's fear will otherwise be interpreted as a reason for silence. (3) Belonging — change the group or build an alternative group; escape — address what is being escaped, usually the household's evening; status — build a domain of real competence; self-soothing — teach a regulation skill and protect sleep; self-punishment — address the self-directed anger, usually with individual work. (4) Because it does not require the person in crisis to make a decision at the moment of crisis; in practice it requires a specific inventory of the household, a named adult, and a date. (5) A safety plan is collaborative and describes what the adolescent will do and what has been removed; a no-harm contract is a promise that shifts responsibility onto her. The first builds capacity, the second borrows it. (6) Duration, impairment, severity — three weeks after a breakup with no impairment in school or friendships and moderate severity is ordinary distress and should be respected as such rather than treated. (7) The Friday conversation: naming what was said, to the two eldest, in one sentence, without asking them to manage her feelings — it converted an atmosphere that had to be inferred into information that could be held. (8) A named adult reachable at that hour without interrogation first; cheapest because it requires no clinical resource, and effective because the hours after ten at night are when risk is highest and when the adolescent is least able to reach a professional.

8.14 Reflective task

Write down the last time you asked someone a direct question about their safety, or the last time you avoided one. If you avoided it, reconstruct the sentence you used instead, word for word. Then answer: what were you protecting? Most trainees find that the substitute sentence protected the therapist's comfort, and that the discomfort was located in a single word which, once said aloud in a rehearsal, is never as difficult again.

8.15 Further reading

  • Hawton, K., Saunders, K. and O'Connor, R. "Self-harm and suicide in adolescents" — the epidemiology and the evidence on means restriction.
  • Stanley, B. and Brown, G. "Safety planning intervention" — the structured alternative to no-harm contracts.
  • Steinberg, L. "Risk taking in adolescence" — the peer-amplification evidence behind this week's first concept.
  • Nock, M. "Why do adolescents hurt themselves?" — the functional account used in the case.
  • Odgers, C. and Jensen, M. "Annual research review: adolescent mental health in the digital age" — the most careful summary of a thin evidence base.

Next: Week 9 — Adult development: work, intimacy and the middle years.

Week 9 — Adult development: work, intimacy and the middle years

Developmental tasks. Intimacy versus isolation (Erikson, roughly the twenties and thirties); work and career identity, including the difference between a job, a career and a calling; generativity versus stagnation (the forties); and the "social clock" — the culturally shared timetable for marriage, children, promotion and retirement, and the distress that follows when a life runs off it.

Why this matters clinically. Most couple problems presented in clinics are adult developmental problems wearing a relational mask: a thirty-two-year-old's struggle with intimacy, a forty-five-year-old's reckoning with a career, a fifty-eight-year-old's grief for the life they did not have. The therapist who hears only the relationship will treat the mask. The therapist who hears the developmental task will treat the person, and the relationship will follow.

Life structure and the "seasons" of adulthood. Levinson's seasons, Vaillant's longitudinal cohorts, and the midlife literature are best treated as metaphors with empirical support, not as laws. The strongest finding in the whole of adult developmental research is continuity with change: people change, but in patterned ways that reflect early relationships and current context. The man who was defended at twenty-five is defended at fifty, in a different vocabulary, about different things.

What this week does not claim. It does not claim that there is a crisis everyone has at forty, that marriage has stages everyone passes through in order, or that a person who has not achieved generativity by fifty is damaged. The evidence supports tasks, timetables, and patterns. It does not support prescription, and the therapist who turns a developmental map into a standard of normal adulthood will spend the week hurting families who are simply living on a different schedule.

— A forty-five-year-old man presents with "irritability". Developmental enquiry finds that he has been passed over for a promotion he had organised his entire adult life around, that his father died eighteen months ago, that his eldest child has left for university, and that he has begun to notice that he has no friends he did not acquire through work. The irritability is at the surface. Under it are four adult developmental events arriving in the same eighteen months.

9.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The maskWhy adult developmental problems arrive as relationship complaintsTake a couple complaint and name the developmental task inside it
8–22Intimacy versus isolationWhat intimacy actually requires, and the four adult forms of not doing itScore themselves on a five-item intimacy audit, privately
22–36Work identityJob, career, calling; and the difference between a bad month and a bad decadeClassify six work presentations
36–48GenerativityThe three routes — children, work, mentoring — and stagnation as its absenceName three people who were generative towards them
48–62The social clockOn-time and off-time events, and the distress of the off-time lifePlace four life events on the clock and mark the culturally expected age
62–74The midlife reckoningHow it presents in a clinic, and the two ways therapists pathologise itRewrite two diagnostic-sounding formulations as developmental ones
74–84Continuity with changeWhat longitudinal studies actually find, and what they do notPredict a fifty-year-old from a twenty-five-year-old in a case vignette
84–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Adults present with a symptom and a story, and the story is usually chronological but not developmental: it lists events and omits tasks. The lecture's central demonstration is to take one ordinary adult complaint and place it against the developmental timetable, so that the class sees the difference between what happened and what it cost developmentally. Once students can do that, they stop treating forty-five-year-olds as thirty-year-olds with more money.

The sentence the lecture must land. "By forty-five most people are not struggling with their marriage; they are struggling with a question their marriage has been asked to answer, and the marriage is not equipped to answer it."

9.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Intimacy versus isolation. Intimacy is not affection and it is not sex. It is the capacity to be known by another person without managing the version of oneself that is known — which requires, first, that there is a self to know. This is why Erikson placed identity before intimacy, and why the sequence matters clinically: a couple in which one partner has never settled who they are will present with "communication problems" that no communication exercise will touch.

The four adult forms of not doing intimacy are worth memorising, because they present as different referrals.

  • Fusion — the person cannot tolerate separateness; difference is experienced as abandonment, and the couple's argument is always about closeness.
  • Pseudo-intimacy — the person performs closeness; the couple is highly affectionate in public and has no private conversation.
  • Isolation — the person keeps a whole domain of life outside the relationship; the complaint is "he does not let me in".
  • Serial replacement — the person changes partners rather than being known by one; the pattern is visible across relationships and invisible within any one of them.

2. Work identity: job, career, calling. A job is what you do for money. A career is a trajectory with advancement. A calling is what you would do anyway. The three are not ranked; a person with a job and a rich life outside it is not deficient. The clinical significance is in the mismatch: a person who has organised their identity around a career and loses the trajectory has lost more than income, and a person who experiences their work as a calling and finds it meaningless is in a particular kind of distress that will not respond to work-life balance advice.

3. Generativity versus stagnation. Generativity is the concern for establishing and guiding the next generation, and it has three routes: one's own children, one's work, and mentoring. The clinical value of the concept is that it provides an alternative to the two things adults in distress usually believe are their only options — more achievement, or resignation. A forty-eight-year-old man who is stagnating at work may be generative as a mentor, as a son, as an uncle, as a coach, and the therapeutic move is to find where the generative capacity can go rather than to fix the job.

4. The social clock. Every culture has a timetable: the age at which one is expected to marry, to have a first child, to be established, to be a grandparent, to retire. The clock is not usually stated, which is what makes it powerful. Distress follows off-time events far more than expected ones — a first marriage at forty-five, a first child at nineteen, a promotion at twenty-nine, a retirement at fifty-five, a bereavement at thirty. The clinical implication is that the therapist must ask not only "what happened?" but "when, and where were you supposed to be by then?"

5. Continuity with change. Longitudinal evidence supports the view that adult personality is stable in its structure and changing in its expression: the defended young adult becomes the defended middle-aged adult, with better manners and worse consequences. The clinical use is a caution against two errors: the error of expecting a person to become someone else in therapy ("he will never be warm"), and the error of assuming nothing can change ("this is just who he is at forty-five"). What changes is usually the relationship to the pattern, and the practical arrangements around it.

9.2a The social clock and the off-time life

The concept is simple and the clinical consequences are large. Five off-time situations account for most of the developmental distress seen in adult clinics.

Off-time marriage and the unmarried thirty-five-year-old. In cultures with a strong marital timetable, an unmarried adult in their mid-thirties is subject to continuous enquiry, and the enquiry is experienced as an assessment of worth. The clinical presentations are anxiety, depression, or a relationship entered under pressure and presented as a decision. The therapist's task is to separate the person's own desire from the timetable's, which is done with one question: "if nobody ever asked you again, what would you want?"

Off-time parenthood, in both directions. A first child at nineteen and a first child at forty-two produce different but comparable difficulties: in the first, the parent's own development is interrupted; in the second, the parent's peers are launching adolescents while they are managing toddlers, and the grandparent generation may be unavailable or dead.

The early promotion and the plateau. Being established "too early" produces a lifelong problem of comparison; being passed over at forty-five produces the case in this week's stub. Both are versions of the same injury: the timetable said one thing and the life delivered another, and the gap is experienced as personal failure rather than as a fact about an organisation.

Off-time bereavement. Losing a parent at twenty-five is statistically ordinary and developmentally early; losing a parent at seventy is developmentally "on time" and contains far less of the "I was not ready" quality. The distinction matters because the same event produces different clinical pictures depending on its position on the clock.

Off-time retirement. Retirement at fifty-five in a culture where sixty-five is expected produces a particular shame, especially for men whose identity is occupational. The clinical picture is often depression misdiagnosed as a physical complaint, and the question that opens it is "what were you supposed to be doing now?"

How to use the clock without imposing it. Ask about the timetable, do not supply one. "In your family, when were people supposed to marry? When were they supposed to be settled? Where are you on that?" Then listen for the gap. A therapist who tells a client that the timetable is oppressive — however true — will be heard as dismissing the family, and the client will defend a clock she has just spent an hour telling you she resents.

9.2b The four adult tasks as they present in couple therapy

Adults do not present their developmental tasks; they present their partners. The translation table below is the week's practical core, because it converts a couple complaint into a developmental hypothesis in one step.

The complaintThe developmental task underneathThe question that tests it
"He never talks to me"Intimacy — the capacity to be known, or the absence of a self to offer"What would he have to say that would frighten him?"
"She is never satisfied"Work identity — a calling unfulfilled, or a career stalled"Whose standard is she failing to meet?"
"There is no us any more, only the children"Generativity, collapsed into one route"Who were you generative towards before the children?"
"He changed after his father died"Off-time bereavement plus the collapse of a generation"Who is the eldest in his family now?"
"We have nothing to say now the children have gone"Launching — the couple's identity was outsourced"What did you talk about before them?"
"She has become obsessed with her mother's care"Generativity, reversed; the "responsible child" role"Who decided she was the responsible one?"
