The rule that governs the table. Each cell is a hypothesis, never a finding. The therapist offers it as a question, not a formulation, because a couple told that their problem is developmental will immediately begin to argue about whether it is.

9.3 The method: the adult developmental history

Eight questions, twenty to thirty minutes, taken with the individual present and the partner listening. The order is deliberate: it moves from the concrete to the evaluative, so that the person is describing their life before they are interpreting it.

Question 1 — Where were you at twenty-five, and what did you think your life would be by now? The answer produces the person's own timetable, in their own words, and it is the baseline against which everything else is measured. Write the answer down verbatim; it will be quoted back in session four.

Question 2 — What happened instead? Not "what went wrong". The phrasing allows for both gains and losses, and it prevents the interview from becoming a catalogue of failure in front of a partner who is listening.

Question 3 — What are you doing that you did not choose? This surfaces the inherited life: the family business, the profession chosen to satisfy a parent, the move to a city for a spouse's work, the second child who was not planned. The answer is frequently the first time the partner has heard it.

Question 4 — What are you doing that nobody in your family has done before? The counterweight to question three, and the source of legitimate pride. Adults in distress systematically under-report their own novelty, and the therapist's task is to make it visible in front of a witness.

Question 5 — Who depends on you, and what do they need? Generativity, mapped concretely: children, parents, siblings, staff, a congregation, a community. The answer is often the discovery that the person is highly generative and has no route to receive.

Question 6 — Who did you lose, and when? Deaths, separations, migrations, the end of a friendship, the loss of a country. The timing is asked for because off-time losses carry more weight.

Question 7 — What do you do that is not for anyone else? The question that identifies the person's own territory, which has frequently collapsed in the preceding decade. If the answer is "nothing", that is the finding, and it is often more actionable than anything in the presenting complaint.

Question 8 — What would you want your life to have been about? The integrity question, asked early because adults answer it more honestly at forty-five than at seventy, when it has become a verdict rather than a direction.

How to record the answers. Not as a narrative. As an eight-line grid with the questions as headings and the answers as the person's own words. The grid is given to the person, which is itself an intervention: most adults in a couple clinic have not seen their own life summarised on one page.

9.3a The midlife reckoning: what it is and what it is not

The popular notion of a "midlife crisis" — a sports car, a younger partner, an abandoned career — is largely a marketing story with weak empirical support. What the evidence supports is something narrower and more useful: in the forties and early fifties, adults conduct a reckoning between the life they have built and the life they expected, and the reckoning produces either a reorganisation or a set of symptoms.

The four triggers. A loss of a parent (the collapse of the generation ahead, and the arrival of one's own mortality as a fact rather than an idea). A child's departure (the loss of a daily role). A work event — promotion, plateau, redundancy, or the recognition that the ceiling has been reached. And a physical change — illness, injury, or the ordinary evidence of ageing that arrives earlier for some bodies than others.

How it presents. Not usually as a crisis. As irritability, sleep disturbance, increased drinking, withdrawal from the couple, an obsessive new interest, an affair, a sudden conversion, a resignation letter, or a somatic complaint that no physician can explain. The commonest presentation in a family clinic is irritability with no stated cause, which is why this week's case begins there.

The two ways therapists go wrong. The first is to pathologise: to diagnose depression in a person who is grieving a life, and to medicate or cognitive-restructure a reckoning that needs to be conducted. The second is to romanticise: to treat the affair, the resignation, or the abandoned family as a legitimate expression of midlife need, which converts a developmental event into a justification for harm.

The clinical position between them. Take the reckoning seriously as a developmental task, name it as such, and hold the person accountable for what they do while conducting it. The sentence that does both: "what you are describing is a real question, and it does not entitle you to do whatever answers it."

The couple's part. A midlife reckoning is conducted in front of a partner who is having their own. The clinical work is rarely with one person; it is with two people whose timetables have diverged. The question that organises it: "what did each of you expect to be doing at this age, and what has each of you given up to get here?" The answer is usually the first honest accounting the couple has done in a decade.

9.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The lifeline, drawn by the person. A horizontal line with the person's age along it, and vertical marks above the line for high points and below for low points, annotated in their own words. Twenty minutes, one sheet of paper, no interpretation while drawing. The technique works for four reasons: it makes the life visible as a shape rather than a series of complaints; it surfaces the pattern of transitions; it produces material the partner has never seen; and it gives the therapist a document to return to. The instruction that makes it work is "put the things that mattered, not the things that should have mattered".

Technique 2 — The two clocks. Two columns on a sheet. Left: "when I expected to…" Right: "when it actually happened". Marriage, children, house, promotion, the death of a parent, retirement. The gap between the columns is the developmental distress, quantified by the person themselves. The technique is particularly effective with couples, because partners routinely discover that they are running on different clocks and have never said so.

Technique 3 — The generativity audit. Three columns: "who I am responsible for", "what they need from me", "what I receive". The third column is almost always empty or filled with the same item repeated. The audit is not a complaint about ingratitude; it is a map of a system with no return path, and the intervention that follows — one hour a week that is not for anyone else — is drawn directly from it.

9.5 The first session with a forty-five-year-old and his wife

Phase 1 — Both, and the complaint in each person's words (15 minutes). The referrer has written "irritability". The therapist asks each partner to describe the last bad week, with one example each, and writes the examples down. This prevents the session from becoming an argument about character.

Phase 2 — The individual history, with the partner listening (25 minutes). The eight questions, asked in order. The partner is asked not to comment, and is told why: "I want you to hear this without having to answer it. You will get your turn." The instruction is usually obeyed, and what follows is the most valuable twenty-five minutes in the case.

Phase 3 — The partner's turn, three questions (10 minutes). What did you hear that you did not know? What did you expect your life to be at this age? What have you given up? The third question is the one that converts the session from an assessment of one person into a developmental conversation between two.

Phase 4 — The shared finding (5 minutes). The therapist names, in one or two sentences, the developmental tasks she heard, and checks them: "I heard four things arriving at once. Is that right?" The couple corrects her, and the corrected version is the formulation.

Phase 5 — One task each (5 minutes). One thing for him, one for her, and one for the pair. Not a programme. Written down, with a review date.

Why the partner listens before she speaks. Because the couple's usual conversation is duelling accounts of the same events, and the developmental history is not an account of events; it is an account of expectations. A partner who hears her husband's answer to "what did you think your life would be by now?" has received information that no amount of arguing about the dishes could have produced.

9.6 Case: the man who was passed over

Referral. Dapo, 45, a bank branch manager, referred by his general practitioner after two visits with "fatigue and stomach trouble" for which no cause was found. He attends with his wife, Kemi, 43, a pharmacist, who says in the first minute: "he is angry all the time and he will not say why."

The household. Dapo and Kemi, married nineteen years. Tolu, 18, in her first year at a university in another state. Femi, 14. Simi, 9. Dapo's mother, 71, widowed eighteen months ago, living alone in Ibadan in the house Dapo grew up in. Kemi's mother, 68, lives with Kemi's brother in Abuja. The household is comfortable and stretched.

The presenting complaint, in each person's words. Dapo: "I am tired. I do not sleep. I do not enjoy anything." Kemi: "he shouts at the children over nothing. He sits in the car outside the house for twenty minutes before he comes in. Last month he did not come to my sister's wedding and he would not say why."

The eight questions, and what they produced.

  1. At twenty-five, what did you think your life would be? "By forty-five I would be a regional director. I would have built the house in Ibadan. My father would have seen it." — Written down verbatim; quoted back in session four.
  2. What happened instead? "I got the branch in 2019 and I have been there six years. In March they gave the region to a man who is thirty-eight and who I trained." A pause. "My father died in April last year. I did not build the house."
  3. What are you doing that you did not choose? "The branch. I did not want the branch. I wanted the region." Then, after some pressing: "I did not choose to be the one who is responsible for my mother. It was decided. I am the first son."
  4. What are you doing that nobody in your family has done? Long pause. "I have never lost a job. Twenty years. My father lost three." — The first item of legitimate pride in the session, and it arrives only because the question was asked.
  5. Who depends on you? "Kemi, three children, my mother, my younger brother's school fees, and about forty staff." What do they need? "Everything. I am the roof."
  6. Who did you lose, and when? "My father, April last year. And Tolu left in September." — Two losses in the same eighteen months, one a death and one a departure, and both are collapses of a role.
  7. What do you do that is not for anyone else? A very long pause. "Nothing. I used to play squash." When did you stop? "When my father was ill."
  8. What would you want your life to have been about? "That my children did better than me. And that my father was proud." — The generativity answer and the foreclosure answer arriving in one sentence, which is the formulation of the case.

Kemi's three questions, and what they produced.

What did you hear that you did not know? "That he wanted the region. He never told me. He told me it was a good job." What did you expect your life to be at this age? "I expected to have some time. The children are older. I thought we would have a life again." What have you given up? "My mother lives with my brother. I have never said this to anyone, but I gave up my mother because I was managing his." — The sentence that reorganises the session, and it arrives from a question the therapist asked of the partner rather than of the patient.

The five findings.

1. Four developmental events in eighteen months. A work plateau (the ceiling reached at forty-five, in public, in front of a man he trained); a bereavement (his father, and with him the audience for the life project); a launching (Tolu's departure, which removed the daily evidence of his generativity); and a reversal of generativity (his mother's widowhood, which converted him from the person who receives care to the person who must provide it, permanently).

2. The social clock has failed and nobody has said so. Dapo's timetable said: regional director by forty-five, house built, father present. Every item on it has not happened, and the failure is experienced as personal rather than structural. He has not told his wife because telling her would require him to say the words, and the words are the injury.

3. The presenting symptom is a reckoning conducted badly in private. Sleeping badly, sitting in the car, drinking two beers more than he used to, shouting at a nine-year-old about shoes. None of this is a disorder and all of it is a person conducting a reckoning without a single interlocutor.

4. The marriage has become an administration. Kemi manages the household, Dapo provides; the couple's conversation is logistics and money. This is the ordinary outcome of two careers and three children, and it is also the reason the reckoning cannot be conducted at home: there is no room in the schedule for a conversation that does not have an agenda.

5. The live conflict is about his mother, and it is not about his mother. Dapo wants his mother to move in. Kemi does not. Both positions are legitimate; neither has been stated as a position. Dapo experiences Kemi's resistance as a betrayal of the family; Kemi experiences Dapo's proposal as the final expropriation of a life she has already given two decades to. The therapist's job is to make both statements sayable in the same room, not to resolve them.

The formulation, in one paragraph. A forty-five-year-old man whose life structure has been organised around a single audience — his father — and a single trajectory — the region — has lost both within eighteen months, and has no vocabulary, no interlocutor and no time in which to conduct the resulting reckoning. His irritability is the surface of a grief that has no permitted form. His marriage has become an administration, and the one live decision in front of the couple — whether his mother moves in — is being conducted as a loyalty test rather than as a household negotiation.

The intervention, in five parts.

Part 1 — Name the reckoning. In the second session the therapist says, in front of both: "four things have happened in eighteen months and each of them has taken away a role. That is not an illness. It is a lot, and it has arrived without a single conversation in which you were allowed to say it out loud." Dapo cries, briefly, and is embarrassed. Kemi takes his hand. Nothing else in the session matters as much.

Part 2 — Restore one non-instrumental hour. The generativity audit has produced an empty column. The task is one hour a week that is not for anyone — squash, restored, on Saturday mornings. The therapist is specific about the obstacle: Dapo's objection is that it is self-indulgent, and the answer is that a man who receives nothing has nothing to give, which is a statement about the household's economics rather than about his entitlements.

Part 3 — The mother, as a negotiation with four options. The therapist writes four options on the board, not two: she moves in; she stays and a paid carer is arranged; she moves to a flat nearby with support; she moves in for a trial of three months with a review. The purpose is to break the binary that has made the argument unwinnable. The couple chooses the fourth, and the review date is written into the plan.

Part 4 — The brother's school fees, named. A second loyalty obligation, never mentioned in the referral and discovered by question five. The therapist asks one question: "what was the agreement, and who made it?" The answer reveals an arrangement made by Dapo alone, presented to Kemi as a fact. Renegotiating it is not the point; naming it as a joint decision rather than a unilateral one is, because the pattern — a man deciding alone and then resenting alone — is the marriage's actual problem.

Part 5 — One conversation a week that is not logistics. Twenty minutes, Sunday evening, agenda-free, no children, no money. The rule: neither party may raise a problem in that twenty minutes. The purpose is not intimacy in the romantic sense; it is to rebuild the channel through which a reckoning could be conducted at home rather than in a car.

9.6a Five months later

The mother did not move in permanently. She moved into a flat two streets away with a carer who comes daily, and the arrangement is reviewed every three months. Dapo visits on Wednesdays and Sundays, which is more than he visited when she lived alone in Ibadan.

Squash has become twice a week, with a man Dapo knew at university and had not telephoned in nine years. The therapist did not suggest the friend; the squash did.

The region has not been offered and will not be. In session seven Dapo says, unprompted: "I am not going to be a regional director", and the sentence is flat rather than bitter. Kemi says afterwards, privately, that this was the moment she stopped being afraid of him.

The drinking has reduced without being addressed. The therapist notes this in the record as an example of a symptom that resolves when the function it was serving is met elsewhere, which is the general principle this week has been teaching.

The honest limitations. Dapo has not grieved his father, in any sense a therapist would recognise. He has stopped being angry, which is not the same thing, and the therapist says so in the final session rather than claiming a resolution. The couple's twenty minutes survives about three weeks in four. And the eldest child, Tolu, has not been part of the work at all, which the therapist records as a gap: a launching that happens to a family in difficulty usually needs the person who left to be spoken about, at least once.

What would have changed the plan. If Dapo had described persistent low mood with anhedonia for more than two months and impairment at work, the plan would have begun with a diagnostic assessment and possibly medication, alongside the developmental work. If the drinking had reached a level with withdrawal symptoms, substance work would have taken precedence. If Kemi had refused to continue after the mother's proposal, the work would have become a couple's negotiation about competing obligations to two sets of parents, which is a different treatment with a different endpoint. And if Dapo had disclosed an affair — which the therapist asked about directly, and which he denied — the reckoning would have been conducted against a background of breach, and the sequence would have been different.

9.7 Transcript: the lifeline, drawn in front of the couple

Twelve turns from the second session. The therapist has one sheet of A4 and a pen, and she has given the pen to Dapo.

T1 Therapist: I want to draw your life on this page. A line across the middle, and your age along it. Above the line, the things that were good. Below, the things that were hard. Not the things that should have mattered — the things that did. — The instruction that makes the technique work, and the only instruction given. T2 Dapo: (draws, silent, for about four minutes) T3 Therapist: (says nothing) — Four minutes of silence while a man draws his life. The impulse to comment at the first mark is strong and must be resisted; the drawing is the intervention and the therapist's commentary would take it back. T4 Dapo: (stops) There. (handing it over) T5 Therapist: (reading) 1998, graduation. 2001, marriage. 2003, Tolu. 2007, the first branch. 2011, your mother's illness. 2014, Femi. 2019, this branch. (pause) 2024, your father. (pause) What is that mark in 2016? — The therapist reads the marks back flatly, without evaluation, and then asks about one unexplained mark rather than about the obvious ones. The unexplained mark is where the person has put something they have not decided how to describe. T6 Dapo: That is when I stopped the squash. (pause) My father was ill from 2015. T7 Therapist: So the squash went first. T8 Dapo: (quietly) Yes. T9 Kemi: I did not know that. I thought you were tired. — The partner's first contribution, and it is not an argument. This is the effect the technique is designed to produce: a fact the partner did not have, delivered without accusation. T10 Therapist: (to Kemi) What do you notice about the line? — The question is given to the partner deliberately. It converts her from an audience into a reader, which is the difference between a session about a man and a session about a couple. T11 Kemi: Everything above the line is work or children. There is nothing on it that is just him. — She has found the generativity audit without being shown it. T12 Therapist: (to both, quietly) That is what I noticed too. (pause) Dapo, what would you put on it if you had an hour a week that was not for anybody? — The question is asked of him, not proposed by the therapist, and it is asked last, when the evidence for it is on the page in his own hand.

9.7a What to do when the partner weeps and the client does not

A practical note that belongs to this week and recurs in every adult case.

In the session above, Kemi weeps twice and Dapo does not. Trainees read this as evidence that Kemi is "more in touch" and Dapo is "defended", and they begin to work on Dapo's defences. The reading is usually wrong, and the intervention that follows from it usually fails.

Three alternative explanations, and how to tell them apart. First, timing: the partner may have had years to grieve the marriage in private and is now crying about something old, while the client is hearing his own life described aloud for the first time and is in the numb phase that precedes grief. Second, permission: in many households the man has never cried in front of anyone and the prohibition is stronger than the feeling. Third, role: one partner is the expressive one and the other the containing one, and the arrangement is stable rather than pathological.

The test. Ask each of them, separately and plainly: "when did you last cry, and who was there?" The answers distinguish the three explanations within a minute. A man who cries alone in a car is not defended; he is unaccompanied.

What to do in the session. Do not pursue the non-crying partner's tears. Attend to the one who is crying, briefly and without fuss — a sentence, a pause, a glass of water — and then return to the material. The non-crying partner is watching how the therapist treats the crying one, and what he learns in those thirty seconds determines whether he will ever cry in this room.

What to say afterwards, if anything. One sentence at the end of the session, addressed to him and not to the couple: "you did not have to do anything while she was crying, and you stayed." Naming the staying is more useful than naming the absence of tears, and it is accurate.

9.8 Laboratory: the developmental history with a partner present

Aim. To take the eight-question history with an individual whose partner is in the room, without losing the individual to the couple.

Procedure. Trios: therapist, client, partner. The trainer briefs the client with a life containing three off-time events and one foreclosed choice; the partner is briefed to interrupt twice — once with a correction, once with an accusation — and to be silent otherwise. Thirty minutes. The observer records every interruption the therapist allowed and every one she prevented, and how she prevented it.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
StructureQuestions asked out of order or skippedAll eight asked, some mergedAll eight asked in order, with the answers written verbatim
Protecting the individualAllowed the interruption and the session became a couple argumentStopped the interruption but lost the client's threadStopped it, named why, and returned the client to the exact question
Using the partnerPartner ignoredPartner invited at the endPartner invited at the two designed moments, and her contribution used
The off-time readingTook the events at face valueNoticed one off-time eventNamed the gap between the timetable and the life, in the client's words
The closeSummarised for the clientChecked the summaryChecked it, was corrected, and used the correction

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the therapist to write the answers verbatim, which is harder than it sounds: most trainees paraphrase into clinical language within the first question, and the paraphrase is what loses the case. "He says his father would have seen the house" is data. "He reports unresolved paternal expectations" is a summary of the therapist's own theory.

9.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Hearing the relationship and missing the life. The couple's complaint is the presenting system, not the whole person. A therapist who works only with the couple's communication will produce better arguments about the same unmet developmental needs.

Error 2 — Imposing the timetable. Telling a forty-year-old unmarried woman that the social clock is oppressive is not support; it is the substitution of the therapist's analysis for the client's dilemma. Ask about the clock; never set it.

Error 3 — Pathologising the reckoning. A man of forty-five who is grieving a life is not necessarily depressed, and the distinction matters because the treatments differ. The screening question is duration, impairment and severity, as in Week 8 — and if the criteria are met, treat the depression, because a depression will prevent the reckoning being conducted at all.

Error 4 — Romanticising the reckoning. The mirror error. "He is finding himself" is not an adequate response to an affair, an abandoned family, or a resignation that leaves a household without income. The developmental frame explains the behaviour; it does not license it.

Error 5 — Treating generativity as a virtue. Generativity becomes a trap when it is the only structure a person has: the man who is the roof for everyone and receives nothing is not a saint, he is a system with no return path, and the collapse is predictable. The audit's third column is the clinical finding.

Error 6 — Believing the timetable is the same for both partners. Couples routinely run on different clocks — different families, different expectations, sometimes different countries. The two-clock exercise is not an icebreaker; it is the assessment of a mismatch that has been conducted silently for twenty years.

9.10 Culture, faith and the adult timetable

The Nigerian adult clock. The timetable in most of the households this book is written for is explicit and densely populated: finish university by twenty-three, marry by twenty-eight, first child within a year of marriage, a house by forty, a title by fifty, a grandchild by sixty. The clock is enforced by relatives, by church and mosque, and by the ordinary conversation of a compound. An adult who is off it is not merely uncomfortable; they are the subject of a collective project of correction.

The clinical consequences are specific and worth naming. A thirty-four-year-old unmarried woman presents with anxiety that is entirely rational given her circumstances, and the therapist's first task is to distinguish between her own wish and the family's project. A forty-year-old man with no child presents with depression and is asked by everyone he meets when he will have one. A couple with one child and secondary infertility present with a marital problem that is, in fact, a public one.

The first son, the last born, and the birth-order contract. Many households operate an explicit birth-order contract: the first son is responsible for the parents, the first daughter for the younger siblings, the last born for the parents' old age. The contract is not usually written and is rarely negotiated. Dapo's case is the ordinary version, and the clinical move is not to overturn it but to make it speakable — who decided, when, and what is the limit — because an unstated contract cannot be declined and therefore cannot be renegotiated.

Faith and the meaning of a stalled life. Adults in a religious household may interpret a plateau, a childlessness, or a bereavement as a spiritual matter, and the interpretation is frequently a resource rather than an obstacle: it provides a language for endurance and a community for support. The clinical error is to treat the framework as a defence to be interpreted. The workable position is to ask what the framework is doing — sustaining, or postponing — and to negotiate only what must be negotiated, which is usually whether the person is also permitted to be angry.

Migration and the split timetable. A substantial proportion of the adults in these clinics have one foot in another country: a sibling in the United Kingdom who sends money, a spouse working in the Gulf, a child studying abroad. The developmental timetable of the household is then partly external, and the clinician must ask who is where, who is expected to return, and what the migration has cost. A couple's "distance" is sometimes a visa.

DEBATE — "A forty-five-year-old man who has done everything his family required of him and is now miserable should be encouraged to disappoint them." Argue for: the foreclosure was never his choice, the misery is the cost of a life authored by others, and the therapeutic task is to return authorship. Argue against: the obligations are real, the family is not an obstacle to be overcome, the man is likely to lose more than he gains, and encouraging rebellion is a projection of the therapist's individualism. Then state what you would actually say to Dapo if he told you he wanted to stop paying his brother's school fees.

9.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The longitudinal evidence for continuity with change is strong: personality traits are rank-order stable across adulthood while their behavioural expression shifts, and the "midlife crisis" as a universal phenomenon is not supported. Generativity in midlife is associated with better wellbeing and, in several cohorts, with better outcomes for the next generation — though the direction of causation is not settled. The association between off-time life events and distress is robust across cultures, which is the strongest single support for the social-clock concept.

What is weaker. Levinson's seasons rest on a small interview sample of American men and should be taught as a heuristic. Vaillant's cohort studies are rich and unrepresentative — educated, male, and longitudinally privileged — and their conclusions travel poorly to households with different structures. The "work as calling" literature is largely correlational and heavily confounded by occupation and income.

What is not supported. That there is a universal midlife crisis with a predictable shape. That marriage has invariant stages that all couples pass through in order. That generativity requires children. And that an off-time life is a damaged one — the evidence is about distress, not about outcome.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same promotion at thirty-eight is a triumph for one manager and a ceiling for another, and what distinguishes them is not the event but what the event was supposed to mean.

9.12 Key terms

Intimacy versus isolation — Erikson's sixth stage; the capacity to be known without managing what is known.

Fusion, pseudo-intimacy, isolation, serial replacement — the four adult forms of avoiding intimacy.

Generativity — concern for establishing and guiding the next generation; the midlife task.

Social clock — the culturally shared timetable for life events; distress follows deviation from it more than the events themselves.

Off-time event — a life event occurring earlier or later than the cultural timetable expects.

Life structure — the underlying pattern of a person's life at a given time: roles, relationships, and the commitments that organise them.

Continuity with change — the finding that personality is stable in structure and changing in expression across adulthood.

Foreclosure — a commitment adopted without exploration, typically inherited from the family.

Birth-order contract — an unstated family allocation of responsibility by ordinal position.

Two clocks — the divergence between partners' expected and actual timetables.

9.13 Self-test

  1. Name the four adult forms of avoiding intimacy and one presenting complaint for each.
  2. What is the difference between a job, a career and a calling, and why is the distinction clinically useful?
  3. Explain the social clock and give three off-time events with their typical clinical presentations.
  4. Why does the lecture insist that the reckoning be taken seriously and that the person be held accountable? Give the error that each half prevents.
  5. In the Dapo case, what were the four developmental events, and why did they arrive together?
  6. What is the third column of the generativity audit, and what does an empty column mean?
  7. Why is the partner asked what she gave up, rather than what she wants?
  8. What is the difference between pathologising and romanticising a midlife reckoning, and what is the position between them?

Answers. (1) Fusion ("he cannot bear me having a friend"); pseudo-intimacy ("we are very close" with no private conversation); isolation ("he does not let me in"); serial replacement (a pattern of relationships each of which ends when it becomes known). (2) A job is for money, a career is a trajectory, a calling is what you would do anyway; the distinction identifies what a person has actually lost when work changes, which determines whether the intervention is occupational or developmental. (3) A first child at nineteen (interrupted own development); a first marriage at forty-five (enforced singleness and pressure); retirement at fifty-five (shame and somatic presentation). (4) Taking it seriously prevents pathologising a developmental task as a disorder; holding the person accountable prevents romanticising harm as self-discovery. (5) A work plateau, his father's death, his daughter's departure, and his mother's widowhood — they arrived together because midlife concentrates losses of role, as the generation ahead dies and the generation behind leaves in the same decade. (6) What I receive; empty means a system with no return path, and the intervention is to build one. (7) Because the question produces the partner's own developmental account, which converts an assessment of one person into a conversation between two, and because "what do you want" invites a demand while "what did you give up" invites a history. (8) Pathologising treats a reckoning as depression; romanticising treats harm as growth; the position between them is to name the task and hold the person accountable for how they conduct it.

9.14 Reflective task

Write your own lifeline, on paper, with the instruction the case used: the things that mattered, not the things that should have mattered. Then write your own two clocks — when you expected to marry, to have children, to be established, to be settled — and when it actually happened or looks likely to. Then answer one question: which gap have you explained to yourself, and which have you not?

9.15 Further reading

  • Erikson, E. The Life Cycle Completed — intimacy and generativity, in the author's own late revision.
  • Levinson, D. The Seasons of a Man's Life — read as a heuristic, with the sample in mind.
  • Vaillant, G. Triumphs of Experience — the Harvard cohort, and the case for continuity.
  • McAdams, D. The Redemptive Self — how adults narrate generativity, and why the story matters.
  • Neugarten, B. "Time, age and the life cycle" — the original social-clock paper, still the clearest.

Next: Week 10 — Couple development: the relationship across time.

Week 10 — Couple development: the relationship across time

Couples develop too, and the development is not a metaphor. A relationship has tasks that arrive in a sequence, capacities that are built or not built at each stage, and a trajectory that can be described in advance well enough to be clinically useful.

PhaseCentral taskCommon failure
FormationCommitment and identity as a coupleOne partner never really chooses
Early marriageBuilding rituals and boundaries with families of originIn-law triangles
Young childrenDividing labour; protecting the couple bondCo-parenting replaces partnering
Adolescent childrenRe-negotiating autonomy, revisiting one's own adolescenceEscalation or abdication
LaunchingRe-intimacy after children leaveThe "empty nest" that was already empty
Later lifeFacing illness, retirement, loss togetherCaretaking replaces relating

What the map is for. Not prediction, and not assessment of how far along a couple "should" be. The map is a diagnostic instrument with three uses: it tells the therapist which task is live; it predicts which task is next, which is the basis of preventive work; and it locates the couple's complaint in a sequence, which is often the first time either partner has seen the complaint as a stage rather than a verdict.

What the map is not. It is not a universal sequence. Couples form at twenty and at fifty; some never have children and some begin with them; some skip the launching phase because a parent moves in instead of a child moving out; and a substantial number of the households in these clinics are not a two-person system at all but a network of three or four adults raising children together. The map is a set of tasks, not a set of stations.

Why this week follows adult development. Last week was the person's timetable. This week is the relationship's timetable, and the two are frequently out of step. The most common clinical picture in this week is a couple whose individual clocks have diverged from the couple's clock: one partner is still in the formation task ("have we really chosen each other?") while the other is deep in the young-children task ("who is doing the school run?").

— With a peer, draw a couple timeline: five high points, five low points, and one turning point each. Then ask: which developmental transition explains the shape of this line? Most students find that at least two of their five low points sit next to a transition rather than next to a conflict, which is the whole argument of the week.

10.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8Why a couple developsThe difference between a relationship that changes and one that growsName the last transition their own family passed through
8–22The six phasesThe tasks, in order, with one failure mode eachPlace four couples on the map from a one-line description
22–34Formation and the unchosen coupleThe "one partner never really chooses" pattern and how it presents ten years laterIdentify the unchosen partner in two vignettes
34–46Young childrenThe division of labour, the disappearance of the couple, and the two-year windowCalculate the couple's weekly non-logistical time
46–58Adolescence and launchingThe re-negotiation, and the couple who outsourced their identity to the childrenAsk the "what did you talk about before them?" question in role
58–70The cascade and the repair trajectoryThe behaviours that predict dissolution, and the ones that predict recoveryCode a two-minute argument for the four horsemen
70–82RitualsWhat a ritual is, why couples without them drift, and how to build oneInventory their own family's rituals
82–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Students arrive with a model of couple therapy as conflict management, and the week's job is to replace it with a model of development management. The demonstration that does this is to take a couple who argue well — who resolve every disagreement respectfully — and show that they are nonetheless in trouble, because they have not had a non-logistical conversation in four years. Conflict skill is necessary and it is not the same as a relationship.

The sentence the lecture must land. "Couples do not fail because they fight. They fail because the tasks change and nobody tells them, so they keep doing the last stage's work in this stage's conditions."

10.2 The six phases, with the clinical reading of each

1. Formation. The task is commitment: two people becoming a couple that is distinct from the two families that produced them, and each of them genuinely choosing. The clinical issue that matters most is the unchosen couple — the relationship entered because of pregnancy, family pressure, immigration status, loneliness, or the end of another relationship. Ten years later, the unchosen partner presents a resentment that has no argument attached to it, because there was never a decision to point at.

The signature in the room: one partner can describe the moment they chose, and the other cannot. The therapist's question is not "do you love him?" but "when did you decide?" — and the answer, or its absence, organises the whole treatment.

2. Early marriage. The task is building rituals and boundaries: how Sundays work, whose family comes for Christmas, who decides about money, what the couple does that is theirs. The characteristic failure is the in-law triangle — a partner in coalition with a parent against the other partner, usually without intending it.

The signature: the couple's disagreements are not about content but about loyalty, and every argument resolves into a question of whose family wins. The intervention is structural rather than communicative: the couple must have a stated position that they can deliver to both families, in the same words.

3. Young children. The task is dividing labour while protecting the couple bond. The characteristic failure is that co-parenting replaces partnering: the couple becomes an efficient organisation for the management of children and ceases to be a relationship. This is the single most common presentation in couple clinics worldwide, and it is not a failure of love but a failure of time and structure.

The signature: the couple can talk for an hour about the children and cannot name what they did together in the last month that was not for the children. The assessment question is arithmetic: how many hours a week does this couple spend alone together, awake, not discussing logistics? The answer is usually between zero and two.

4. Adolescent children. The task is re-negotiating autonomy as a family, and — unavoidably — revisiting each parent's own adolescence. The characteristic failure is the two patterns from Week 7: escalation and abdication, frequently with one parent in each.

The signature: the couple's conflict is conducted through the adolescent. One parent is the strict one, the other the lenient one, and the adolescent has learned to route every request through the lenient one. The developmental reading is that the parents are replaying their own adolescent conflicts with each other, using the child as the arena.

5. Launching. The task is re-intimacy after the children leave. The characteristic failure is the empty nest that was already empty: a couple who discover, at the point of launch, that they have not had a relationship independent of the children for twenty years.

The signature: the couple presents when the last child leaves, with a complaint that sounds sudden and is not. The question that opens it — "what did you talk about before them?" — usually produces a silence followed by an accurate and painful answer.

6. Later life. The task is facing illness, retirement and loss together. The characteristic failure is that caretaking replaces relating: one partner becomes a nurse and the other a patient, and the relationship that was between two adults becomes a service.

The signature: the healthy partner describes exhaustion and the ill partner describes loneliness, and both are describing the same loss. The intervention is to restore some domain of the relationship that is not about the illness — one hour, one activity, one subject that is not permitted to be medical.

10.2a What the therapist asks at each phase

The table below is the practical instrument. In a first session, the therapist does not need to know which phase the couple is in so much as which task is live, and the questions below identify it in about ten minutes.

PhaseThe question that identifies itWhat a positive answer means
Formation"When did you decide?"If only one can answer, the couple is unchosen
Early marriage"Whose family decides about Christmas, and who decided that?"A coalition with a family of origin is live
Young children"How many hours a week are you alone together, awake?"Below two hours, the bond is unprotected
Adolescent children"Who is the strict one?"The conflict is being routed through the child
Launching"What did you talk about before the children?"The couple's identity was outsourced
Later life"When did you last do something together that was not about the illness?"Caretaking has replaced relating

The rule for using the table. One question per session, not six. A therapist who runs the whole table in the first session produces a couple who feel assessed rather than met, and the information gained is worth less than the alliance lost. The table is for the therapist's internal map; the questions are delivered one at a time, across sessions, in the order the couple's material invites.

10.2b The cascade and the repair trajectory

Couples do not dissolve in a single event. They move along one of two trajectories, and the behaviours that distinguish them are observable in a two-minute argument.

The cascade. Four behaviours reliably predict deterioration, and they are not the loud ones. They are: criticism (an attack on the person rather than the behaviour: "you always", "you never"); contempt (mockery, sarcasm, eye-rolling, name-calling — the single strongest predictor); defensiveness (counter-attack or victim stance in response to a complaint); and stonewalling (withdrawal, silence, a phone). These are usefully taught not as sins but as stages of a losing strategy: criticism is a complaint that has been made too many times without being heard; contempt is criticism that has stopped expecting an answer; defensiveness is the response of a person who believes the accusation is a verdict; and stonewalling is what a physiologically flooded person does when they have no other option.

The repair trajectory. The distinguishing feature of couples who recover is not the absence of the four behaviours but the presence of repair attempts: a joke, a hand, a "can we start again", a break, a sentence that concedes one point. Repair attempts succeed in proportion to the couple's capacity to notice them — which is trainable — and in proportion to the positive sentiment override built up outside the argument.

The developmental reading. The cascade is what happens when a stage's task is not done and the couple keeps using the previous stage's methods. The couple in the young-children phase who are still arguing like a formation couple — about whether they chose each other — will fight about the school run and mean something else entirely. The therapist's job is not to teach them to argue better about the school run. It is to identify which task is live and to move the argument there.

What predicts recovery, in order. The couple's capacity to repair in the moment; the ratio of positive to negative interaction outside conflict; the presence of shared meaning (children, faith, a project, a place); and the absence of contempt. Note what is not on the list: the frequency of conflict, the content of the disagreement, and the couple's skill at "communicating". Many couples with excellent communication skills are in trouble, and many couples who shout are not.

10.3 The method: the couple timeline

The timeline is the assessment and the intervention at once, and it takes one session.

Step 1 — Draw it, with both present, on one sheet. A horizontal line, and the years along it from the couple's meeting to today. Above the line: five high points. Below: five low points. Both partners contribute; the therapist writes.

Step 2 — Ask for the same five moments from each partner separately, then compare. The comparison is the finding. Couples routinely produce different high points, and the difference is not a memory error; it is a difference in what each of them is living for.

Step 3 — Mark the transitions in a third colour. Marriage, first child, a move, a job change, a bereavement, a migration, a child leaving. Then look at the line. The pattern that appears in the great majority of cases is that the low points cluster adjacent to the transitions, not adjacent to the arguments.

Step 4 — Ask the turn question of each. "Which point on this line was the turning point, and what turned?" The answers are frequently surprising and always usable, and they are the material for the rest of the treatment.

Step 5 — Ask the unanswered question. "What happened on this line that is not on it?" The question produces the events the couple has agreed not to discuss: an affair, a miscarriage, a debt, a termination, a period of violence, a child who died. The therapist asks it once, and does not pursue the answer in the first session.

Step 6 — Convert the timeline into one task. Not a programme. One thing that the couple will do, or stop doing, between now and the next session, chosen because it addresses the live task rather than the loudest complaint.

Why the technique works. It externalises the relationship. A couple who have been conducting a characterological argument ("you are selfish") are suddenly looking at the same object on a page, and the object is a history rather than a defect. It also does something the partners cannot do alone: it makes visible the ratio between the good years and the bad, which is frequently more balanced than either partner believed.

10.3a The turning-point interview

The turn question from step 4 deserves a section of its own, because it is the most productive single question in couple work.

The question. "If you had to put your finger on one point where this relationship turned — for better or worse — which point would it be?"

Why it works. It asks for a moment rather than an evaluation. Couples in difficulty have an evaluation ready ("it has been bad for years") and no moment, and the request for a moment produces specificity, a date, and usually an incident that the other partner did not know was being carried.

The four kinds of answer, and what each requires.

  • An event — a job loss, a move, a death, an affair. The therapist's work is to establish what the event meant, which is usually not what happened but what was not said about it.
  • A decision — "when we decided to have the second child", "when I took the posting". The work is with the decision's authorship: who decided, and who was informed.
  • A sentence — "when he said, in front of his mother, that I was exaggerating". The work is with the moment itself, which is frequently the first time the other partner has understood its weight.
  • A silence — "I cannot think of one". This is the most informative answer of all, and it usually means that the couple's difficulty is chronic rather than eventful, which changes the treatment from a reconstruction of an injury to a construction of a relationship.

The rule for the therapist. Do not choose between the two answers. A couple will offer two turning points and the therapist's temptation is to adjudicate. The useful move is to ask each partner what the other's turning point means to them, which is the first genuinely mutual act in most of these sessions.

10.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The ritual inventory. List every recurring practice in the household: the Sunday meal, the Friday call to a parent, the way birthdays are marked, the prayer before a journey, the walk, the programme watched together, the way an argument ends. Then mark each as alive, dead, or contested. Couples in difficulty have reliably lost their rituals, not their love, and the restoration of one ritual is frequently more effective than a communication exercise.

Technique 2 — The division-of-labour audit. Two columns: everything that has to be done in a week, and who does it. Then a third column, which is the one that matters: who notices when it is not done. The third column is the measure of mental load, and it is the source of most of the resentment in the young-children phase. The audit is not a negotiation; it is a description, and the description usually produces the negotiation without the therapist having to run it.

Technique 3 — The twenty minutes. One twenty-minute period per week, at a stated time, with no children, no money, no logistics and no problem-solving. The rule that makes it work: if a problem is raised, the twenty minutes ends and the problem goes to a different slot. Couples find this harder than any other task in the book, and their difficulty is the diagnosis.

10.5 The first session with a couple in the young-children phase

Phase 1 — The complaint, in each person's words, with one example each (15 minutes). Written down verbatim. The therapist does not allow the partners to respond to each other's examples in this phase, and says why: "you will each get to answer, and I want the examples to be on the table first."

Phase 2 — The timeline (25 minutes). Drawn on one sheet, both contributing, with the transitions marked in a third colour.

Phase 3 — The live task, named and checked (5 minutes). "I think the task in front of you is not the argument about the phone. I think it is that you have not been alone together, awake and not talking about the children, for about two years. Is that right?" The couple corrects, and the corrected version is the formulation.

Phase 4 — The arithmetic (5 minutes). "How many hours a week?" The answer is written on the sheet, next to the timeline, because a number on a page is more persuasive than a number in a conversation.

Phase 5 — One task (10 minutes). The twenty minutes, scheduled now, in the session, in the diary. The therapist does not leave the scheduling to the couple, because the couples who most need it are the ones least likely to find the time.

Why the timeline precedes the task. A couple given a task before they have seen their own history experiences the task as an instruction from a stranger. A couple who have just drawn twenty years on a page experience the same task as a repair to something they can see.

10.5a When one partner will not come

The commonest structural obstacle in couple work: one partner attends and the other refuses. Three positions, and one of them is right.

The wrong position — treat the attending partner individually as if the couple were the problem. This produces a therapy that is, in effect, an alliance against an absent person, and the absent person will eventually be told what was said, accurately or otherwise.

The second wrong position — refuse to work until both attend. This abandons the person in front of you and, in practice, ends the treatment.

The workable position — work with one, on the relationship, explicitly and transparently. Say what the frame is: "I will work with you on what you can change, which is your part. I will not build a case against your husband." Then three practical moves: ask the attending partner to invite the other once, in the therapist's words, with the offer that the first session is a consultation and not a commitment; offer a single joint session with an explicit agenda that the absent partner can read in advance; and set a review date at which the situation is reassessed.

The question that changes the refusal. An absent partner usually refuses for one of three reasons: they believe the problem is the other person's; they are ashamed; or they are afraid of what will be said. The invitation that works addresses the third: "nothing will be decided about you in a room you are not in." That sentence, delivered by the attending partner, converts a substantial minority of refusals.

10.4a The negative cycle, in the couple's own words

Emotionally focused couple therapy describes a circular pattern — a pursue-withdraw or attack-defend cycle — that organises most distressed couples' arguments. The description is useful to a developmental therapist because the cycle is the mechanism by which a missed stage task turns into a stable arrangement.

How to name the cycle without jargon. Ask each partner two questions: "when you are not getting through, what do you do?" and "what do you see him doing?" The answers produce the cycle in the couple's own language, which is the only version that will be remembered. A typical formulation, in a couple's words: "when I feel alone I ask for more, and the more I ask the more he goes quiet, and the quieter he goes the more I ask." The therapist writes it down and gives it a name the couple chooses.

Why the developmental frame matters here. The cycle is not a communication defect; it is a stage-specific adaptation that has outlived its stage. In the young-children phase, pursuing is what one partner does to protect the bond from disappearing and withdrawing is what the other does to survive exhaustion. Both strategies are reasonable responses to the actual conditions. They become a cycle when each is experienced by the other as the cause of the problem, and the cycle becomes stable when neither partner can see their own half of it.

The three moves that break it, in order.

First, name the cycle as the problem, not the partner. "The thing that has been happening in your house for four years is this pattern, and it is not either of you." This is not a rhetorical softening; it is accurate, and it changes the couple's task from mutual accusation to joint observation.

Second, find the primary emotion under each position. The pursuer's anger is usually underlain by fear of being unimportant; the withdrawer's flatness by a conviction of failure. Naming these is done with tentativeness — "I may be wrong, but I wonder whether the quiet is not indifference but a kind of protection" — because a confident attribution at this point is experienced as taking the other's side.

Third, give one small enactment in the session. Ask the pursuer to say the fear directly to the withdrawer, and the withdrawer to answer without defending. The therapist keeps it to two turns and stops it before it becomes a conversation, because the first two turns are the intervention and the third is usually a relapse.

What the developmental therapist adds. The cycle is described, and then the task is addressed: the Thursday, the audit, the defined grandmother. Naming a cycle without changing the structure that produces it produces a couple who understand their pattern and continue to enact it, which is the characteristic failure of insight-oriented couple work in a household with no time.

10.5b The couple with a child with a disability

One presentation deserves separate treatment because it reorganises the couple's developmental timetable more than any other.

What changes. The young-children phase does not end when it should; the launching phase may never arrive; the division of labour becomes permanent and unequal; the couple's social world narrows to families in the same situation or disappears; and the services system becomes a third party with its own demands, appointments and forms. The couple's shared meaning is tested in a way that few other events test it.

What the evidence says. The divorce rate among parents of children with disabilities is frequently quoted as higher than the general population; the better-designed studies find a smaller and less consistent difference than the popular claim, with the strongest predictors being the child's behaviour difficulties and the family's financial and service support rather than the disability itself. What is robust is the elevation of parental stress and the reduction of couple time.

The three clinical tasks. First, protect one domain of the couple's life that is not about the child — which is the same intervention as the twenty minutes, with a higher stake. Second, define the division of labour explicitly, because the default is that one parent becomes the specialist and the other becomes the provider, and the arrangement is rarely chosen. Third, address the sibling, who is the family member most often described as "fine" and least often asked.

The question that opens the work. "What did you imagine this would be like, and what is it like?" Asked of each partner separately, then together. The gap between the two answers is the couple's grief, which is usually unmourned because the child is loved and the grief feels like a betrayal of the child. Naming that directly — "it is possible to love this child completely and to grieve the life you expected" — is frequently the most useful sentence in the treatment.

10.6 Case: Aisha and Sam, four years on

The couple first met in Week 2, where Aisha, 31, and Sam, 34, were assessed for goodness of fit with a temperamentally slow-to-warm infant. They return three years later with a second child.

The household. Aisha, 34, a lawyer, on a partnership track. Sam, 37, an architect, in a small practice he started with a colleague. Their daughter, Yusra, is four and in pre-school; their son, Bilal, is nine months. Sam's mother, 66, comes three days a week to help. Aisha's sister lives twenty minutes away and has two children of her own.

The referral. Aisha telephones. "I do not think I love him any more. I am not saying I want to leave. I am saying I cannot remember." Sam's version, in the first session: "she is never here. When she is here she is on her phone. She has decided something and she has not told me what it is."

The complaint, with examples. Aisha's example: "last Saturday I came back from the market and he had taken Bilal to his mother's without telling me, and I sat in the car outside my own house for ten minutes because I did not want to go in." Sam's example: "on Wednesday she came home at ten and I had done both bath times and she said 'you should have put him down earlier'."

The timeline. Drawn with both, and it is the session.

  • 2016 — met at a wedding. High.
  • 2018 — married. Aisha's family objected to the tribe; she married him anyway. High.
  • 2019 — Yusra born, difficult birth. Low.
  • 2020 — Sam's practice started; the money stopped for eighteen months. Low.
  • 2021 — Aisha's father died. Low, the deepest mark on the line.
  • 2022 — Aisha made senior associate. High.
  • 2023 — The move to the bigger flat. High, contested.
  • 2024 — Bilal born. High and low in the same month.
  • 2025 — Sam's practice stable; Aisha's partnership decision due. Unmarked, so far.

What the timeline shows that neither partner had said. Three findings.

First, the low points cluster at 2019 to 2021, and the couple has never discussed 2021 at all. Aisha's father died in the same year Sam's practice was failing and Yusra was two. The therapist asks the unanswered question — "what is not on this line?" — and Aisha says: "I was pregnant in 2021 and I lost it in the August, and then my father died in the October, and nobody in this family knows that because we were doing the practice." Sam puts his face in his hands. It is the first time in four years that this has been said in the same room as both of them.

Second, the high points are almost all about achievement, and none of them are about each other. 2022 is Aisha's promotion. 2023 is the flat. The couple's celebrations are external, and the therapist names this without interpretation: "there is nothing on the top of this line that is just the two of you."

Third, the word "partnership" appears twice — Sam's practice and Aisha's track — and the couple is competing in the same currency. They are not in conflict about love. They are in a symmetric struggle in which each is asking the other to accommodate a career, and neither has said so in those terms.

The live task. The young-children task: dividing labour while protecting the couple bond. The arithmetic is performed in the session: Aisha estimates that she is alone with Sam, awake, not discussing children, for "about twenty minutes a week, and that includes the argument". Sam says "less".

The three findings that produce the formulation.

  1. The couple's bond was never built as a separate structure; it was built as a joint project. Two ambitious people married in 2018 and immediately began building: a practice, a career, a flat, two children. The project is now succeeding and the relationship that was supposed to be its purpose has no independent existence. This is not a failure of love; it is a failure of construction.
  2. An unmourned loss has been silently organising the last four years. The miscarriage and the father's death in the same autumn were absorbed as a logistical problem ("we were doing the practice") and never mourned. Aisha's "I cannot remember loving him" is accurate: the years in which the memory would have been made were the years in which the household was in emergency.
  3. The conflict about the market and the bath time is not the conflict. It is the ordinary surface of a symmetric struggle about whose career accommodates whose, conducted through domestic detail because neither partner has the standing to raise the real question — which is that they have never decided whose work comes first.

The intervention, in five parts.

Part 1 — Name the mourning, once, and do not process it in this session. The therapist marks the September and October on the timeline, says "that is not a bad year, that is two deaths and a birth plan that ended", and moves on. The instruction is to notice, not to work. A couple who have avoided something for four years will not process it in the session in which it is first named, and a therapist who tries will teach them to avoid it more carefully.

Part 2 — Build one structure that is not the project. The twenty minutes, scheduled in the session, on Thursday at nine-fifteen, after Bilal's last feed. The rule: no children, no money, no problems. The couple's objection — "we have nothing to say" — is answered by predicting it: "the first two will be awkward. That is the finding, not the failure."

Part 3 — The division of labour audit, with the third column. Written in the session. The third column — who notices when it is not done — is almost entirely Aisha's, and Sam's reaction to seeing it written is the session's second significant moment. The negotiation that follows is not run by the therapist; it is run by the list.

Part 4 — The career question, named as a question. "Whose work comes first when they conflict?" is asked plainly and answered badly, which is the beginning. The couple agrees on a rule for one year: Aisha's partnership year takes precedence for childcare logistics, and in exchange Sam's practice gets the first claim on any surplus money and one weekend a month. The rule is arbitrary and it is written down, and the arbitrariness is the point: a rule they chose is different from a pattern they resent.

Part 5 — The grandmother, defined. Sam's mother comes three days a week to help. The therapist asks what she decides and what she does not. The answer reveals that she has views about Bilal's feeding which she expresses to Sam and not to Aisha, and that Sam has been carrying them. The three-domain agreement from Weeks 3 and 7 applies unchanged: what she decides, what she does not, and the sentence that says so in front of everybody.

10.6a Four months later

The Thursday twenty minutes has happened eleven times out of sixteen. Aisha reports that the first three were "terrible" and that the fourth was the first conversation in four years in which nobody mentioned a child.

The miscarriage has been spoken about twice more, both times on a Thursday, both times briefly. The therapist has not run a grief protocol and does not intend to: the couple is doing the work in the place where the relationship is being rebuilt, which is more durable than a protocol.

The rule about careers has already been broken once, in Aisha's favour, and renegotiated without a session.

Sam's mother now says her views about feeding to both of them, which is worse in the short term and better in the long term. Aisha reports this with irritation and the therapist logs it as progress.

The honest limitation. Aisha has not said she loves him. In the final session she says, "I do not want to leave", which is a different sentence and is the one available at this stage. The therapist does not convert it into a declaration, and the case is closed with a review at six months.

What would have changed the plan. If the answer to "what is not on this line?" had been an affair, the sequence would have been disclosure, then a decision about the frame, then the developmental work. If either partner had disclosed violence — which the therapist screened for in the first session, as a matter of routine — the couple work would have stopped and safety work begun. If Aisha had said "I have decided", the treatment would have become a separation-focused process, which is a different treatment with a different endpoint and a different literature.

10.7 Transcript: the unanswered question

Ten turns from the third session, at the point where the timeline has been drawn and the therapist asks what is not on it. The couple has been together nine years.

T1 Therapist: I want to ask one more question about the line, and then we will stop drawing. (pause) What happened in this relationship that is not on it? — The question is asked once, flatly, after the couple has invested twenty minutes in the drawing, which is when they have something to protect and therefore something to add. T2 Aisha: (long silence) Nothing. It is all there. T3 Therapist: (waits) — The single most important behaviour in the transcript. The silence is not a technique for producing disclosure; it is the absence of pressure, which is the only condition in which disclosure occurs. T4 Aisha: (quietly) There is one thing. (pause) I was pregnant in 2021. In the August. And then I was not. T5 Sam: (turns to look at her) What? T6 Therapist: (to Sam, very quietly) You did not know. — Not a question. A statement that gives the partner a moment to be a person rather than a defendant. T7 Sam: (shakes his head) T8 Aisha: You were at the practice. The money had gone. My father was already ill. I thought — (stops) I thought if I told you it would finish you. — The reason for the silence, which is always a protection and never a deception, and the therapist's next move depends on recognising that. T9 Therapist: (to both, after a pause) I am not going to work on this today. I want it on the line, and I want you to know that it is on the line. We will come back to it when you have had a week to know that it is known. — The refusal to process. Almost every trainee wants to work this material immediately, and immediate processing of a four-year silence teaches the couple that disclosure produces a session rather than a relationship. T10 Sam: (to Aisha, not to the therapist) I would have wanted to know.

10.8 Laboratory: the couple timeline

Aim. To draw a couple timeline in twenty-five minutes, identify the live developmental task, and convert it into one task without running a communication exercise.

Procedure. Trios: therapist, two partners. The trainer briefs the couple with a nine-year history containing three transitions and one undisclosed event which one partner will reveal only if asked the unanswered question at the right moment. Twenty-five minutes, then ten minutes of feedback.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The drawingTherapist drew it, or asked for a verbal historyCouple contributed, but unequallyBoth partners' marks on the page, in the therapist's handwriting, with transitions in a third colour
The comparisonTreated the two lists as oneNoticed a differenceUsed the difference as the finding and asked what each difference meant
The transitionsNot markedMarked, not usedMarked, and the clustering of lows named
The unanswered questionNot askedAsked, then pursued immediatelyAsked once, held, and the answer scheduled rather than processed
The taskA programmeOne task, unscheduledOne task, scheduled in the session, with a prediction of how it will fail

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the therapist to predict the failure of the task she has just set. A couple told that the first three Thursdays will be awkward is protected from concluding, on the first awkward Thursday, that the therapy is not working.

10.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Teaching communication skills to a couple whose problem is structural. A couple with no shared time, two careers and an undefined grandmother do not need to learn to listen; they need a Thursday. Skill without structure produces a couple who listen beautifully in a session they cannot schedule.

Error 2 — Treating the loudest complaint as the task. The market and the bath time are the surface. The therapist who negotiates the surface will be negotiating it again in a month, with a different example.

Error 3 — Taking sides with the more articulate partner. In every couple there is one who speaks in paragraphs and one who speaks in sentences, and the therapist's sympathy follows the paragraphs. The correction is arithmetic and it should be visible: equal turns, equal minutes, and the therapist counting.

Error 4 — Processing a disclosure immediately. The transcript above is the exemplar. A four-year silence is not resolved in the session in which it ends; it is registered, held, and returned to.

Error 5 — Believing the "empty nest". A couple who present at the point of the last child's departure have usually been in difficulty for a decade. The presentation is late, not sudden.

Error 6 — Ignoring the household beyond the couple. Three adults raising two children is not a couple with an in-law problem; it is a caregiving network with an undefined hierarchy. The unit of treatment is whatever is actually raising the children, which is frequently more than two people.

10.10 Culture, faith and the couple

Marriage as a contract between families. In much of the world the couple's formation is not a private decision but a treaty: bride price, family approval, religious ceremony, and obligations that run in both directions for decades. The Western developmental map's "formation" phase, with its emphasis on the couple choosing each other, describes one version of a universal task. In a marriage arranged with family involvement, the task is not choosing but authoring — the couple must become the authors of a relationship that others initiated, and the clinical question is whether they have ever been given the opportunity.

Polygyny and the couple unit. Where a household includes more than one wife, the couple is not the clinical unit and a therapy designed for two people will misdescribe the system. The relevant tasks are allocation (of time, money, and the husband's presence), hierarchy (whose children, whose room, whose authority), and the children's experience of the arrangement. The therapist who has a position on polygyny will be unable to work; the therapist who can map the allocation without endorsing or condemning it can.

Religion as a third party in the room. Couples frequently hold their marriage inside a religious framework that supplies obligations, rituals and an audience. This is a resource: it supplies a shared meaning, which is on the list of what predicts recovery. It is also a constraint: a couple who believe divorce is unavailable may present late, when the difficulty is chronic. Both are facts to be assessed, not positions to be taken.

The wife who earns more. A specific and increasingly common presentation in the households this book is written for: a wife whose income exceeds her husband's, in a culture where provision is part of the definition of masculinity. The clinical picture is not usually the couple's own discomfort; it is the discomfort of the wider family, conducted through remarks, and it produces a husband who withdraws from decisions and a wife who resents the withdrawal. The intervention is to name the external audience and to build the couple's own position on it, which is Week 10's early-marriage task arriving late.

DEBATE — "A couple should stay together for the children." Argue for: the evidence on children's outcomes is about conflict, not separation, and a low-conflict marriage is a better environment than a divided household in most circumstances. Argue against: a high-conflict marriage is worse for children than a clean separation, the phrase is used to trap people, and the children in question learn the relationship they are shown. Then state what you would actually say to a couple who tell you this is their reason for staying, and what you would ask them first.

10.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The observational literature on couple interaction is strong: contempt, criticism, defensiveness and stonewalling predict deterioration, and repair attempts predict recovery, with effect sizes that survive controls for initial satisfaction. The association between shared meaning and relationship stability is consistent across studies. Emotionally focused couple therapy has the best evidence base of the couple modalities, with replicated effects in randomised trials and follow-up to two years (MFT 710).

What is weaker. The stage models. The six-phase map is a teaching device assembled from family life cycle theory and clinical observation; it is not a validated developmental sequence, and couples vary enormously in the order and timing of the tasks. The claim that the couple should be the primary unit of treatment is a value as much as a finding, and in households with distributed caregiving it is frequently wrong.

What is not supported. That communication training alone improves satisfaction in distressed couples — the evidence is that skill acquisition without change in the couple's structure and emotional bond does not transfer. That the empty nest is a crisis for most couples — the average finding is an improvement in satisfaction after the last child leaves, for couples who were not already distressed.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same second child, born into a couple with a Thursday and a defined grandmother, is a manageable strain; born into a couple with no shared time and an undefined authority, it is the point at which the relationship loses its structure entirely.

10.12 Key terms

Formation — the phase in which the couple becomes a distinct unit and each partner chooses it.

The unchosen couple — a relationship entered without a genuine decision; presents later as resentment without an argument.

Ritual — a recurring, meaningful practice that marks the relationship's continuity.

Negative sentiment override — the state in which a partner's neutral behaviour is read as hostile.

Repair attempt — any move that interrupts an escalating exchange; the best single predictor of recovery.

The four horsemen — criticism, contempt, defensiveness, stonewalling.
