Mental load — the noticing and planning of household work, distinct from its execution.

Symmetric struggle — a conflict in which both partners want the same thing from each other, and neither can yield.

Shared meaning — the couple's common account of what their life is for; a predictor of stability.

Couple timeline — the drawn history of high points, low points and transitions.

10.13 Self-test

  1. Name the six phases and one failure mode for each.
  2. Why is the "unchosen couple" the most consequential formation pattern, and what is the diagnostic question?
  3. What is the arithmetic question of the young-children phase, and what does a number below two hours predict?
  4. Distinguish the cascade from the repair trajectory, and name the single strongest predictor in each direction.
  5. Why does the therapist refuse to process the miscarriage in the session in which it is disclosed?
  6. What is the third column of the division-of-labour audit, and why does it matter more than the first two?
  7. How would you work with a couple in which one partner will not attend?
  8. In the Aisha and Sam case, what were the three findings, and which one changed the treatment?

Answers. (1) Formation — one partner never chose; early marriage — in-law triangle; young children — co-parenting replaces partnering; adolescent children — escalation or abdication; launching — the nest that was already empty; later life — caretaking replaces relating. (2) Because there was never a decision to point at, so the resentment has no argument attached and cannot be negotiated; the question is "when did you decide?" (3) Hours per week alone, awake, not discussing logistics; below two predicts a bond with no maintenance and a slow loss of the couple's separate existence. (4) The cascade is criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling; the repair trajectory is the presence of repair attempts. Contempt is the strongest predictor of dissolution, repair the strongest of recovery. (5) Because a four-year silence cannot be resolved in the session in which it ends, and attempting it teaches the couple that disclosure produces a session rather than a relationship. (6) Who notices when it is not done — the measure of mental load, which is the actual source of the resentment. (7) Work with the attending partner on their part, be explicit that no case will be built against the absent partner, invite once in the therapist's words with a consultation-only offer, and set a review date. (8) The bond was built as a joint project rather than a separate structure; an unmourned loss had organised four years; and the conflict was a symmetric struggle about whose career accommodates whose. The first changed the treatment, because it moved the work from conflict to construction.

10.14 Reflective task

Draw the timeline of a couple you know well — your parents, relatives, or your own if you have one — with five high points and five low points, and mark the transitions. Then answer: which transition produced the largest low point, and what did the couple do about it? Most students find that the couple did something practical and nothing relational, which is not a failure; it is what most couples do, and it is the reason this week's techniques are so simple, and so easy to forget in a room where two people are angry

10.15 Further reading

  • Gottman, J. What Predicts Divorce? — the observational literature behind the cascade.
  • Johnson, S. Hold Me Tight — the emotionally focused account of the negative cycle, written for couples.
  • McGoldrick, M. et al. Genograms — the family life cycle chapters that this week's map draws on.
  • Carter, B. and McGoldrick, M. The Expanded Family Life Cycle — the source of the phase structure.
  • Fowers, B. "The limits of communication training" — why skills without structure do not transfer.

Next: Week 11 — Parenting across stages: talking to parents well.

Week 11 — Parenting across stages: talking to parents well

Parents do not need lectures; they need accurate, specific, hopeful information in plain language, and they need it from someone who is not judging them. That is a skill, it is teachable, and it is assessed in this course.

The four parts of the skill.

  1. Normalise first. "What you are describing is very common at this age."
  2. Give one developmental fact. "At four, the part of the brain that manages impulse is still being built, so he cannot yet do what you are asking consistently."
  3. Give one concrete action. "Try naming the feeling and offering two acceptable choices."
  4. Set a review. "If this is still happening in three weeks, or if X or Y appears, come back."

What to avoid. Technical vocabulary; predictions ("he will grow out of it"); blame; over-reassurance when the concern is real; and overwhelming a parent with five recommendations.

Why this week is the hinge of the course. Every previous week has produced information a parent needs. This week is about delivering it. A therapist with an accurate formulation and a delivery that shames the parent will produce a family that does not return, and the formulation will have been worth nothing. The skill is not a soft addition to clinical work; it is the mechanism by which clinical work reaches the people who do it.

The developmental spine of the week. The same four parts are used at every age, and the content changes with the stage. Every previous week supplies the content: Week 3 for the infant (synchrony, the still face, the meaning of a cry), Week 4 for the toddler (the limit formula, the language differential), Week 5 for the preschooler (play, pretend, the false-belief shift), Week 6 for the school-age child (industry, monitoring, the report card), Weeks 7 and 8 for the adolescent (privacy, negotiation, risk). This week is where the course becomes usable in a room with a worried parent.

(OBSERVED) — A ten-minute parent-feedback role-play using the four parts, with a peer playing a parent who is frightened and slightly defensive. The exercise is assessed (see 11.5), and the standard is not warmth; it is accuracy plus one action plus a review**.

11.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8Why advice failsThe difference between information and instruction; why parents have already had adviceList the advice they have given and been ignored
8–20The four partsThe structure, demonstrated on a toddler caseIdentify the four parts in a recorded feedback
20–34Normalising without dismissingThe two errors: "it is normal" as a brush-off, and alarm as a defaultRewrite three dismissing sentences
34–46The developmental factWhich fact to give at which age, and how to say it without jargonChoose the fact for four ages and say it in one sentence
46–58One actionWhy one, and how to choose itChoose one action for four cases and defend it against a rival
58–70The reviewSetting a review that is not a threat and not a promiseWrite three review sentences
70–82When the parent disagreesThe four reasons a parent rejects a formulation, and the move for eachRole-play a rejection and hold the frame
82–90The assessmentHow the observed role-play is markedDraft their role-play plan

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Trainees default to either reassurance or instruction, and both are ways of ending a conversation. The lecture's job is to make the conversation open — to leave the parent with something to do and a reason to come back — and the way to do that is to demonstrate the failure first: the lecturer plays a therapist who reassures a worried mother for four minutes, and the class watches the mother's face close.

The sentence the lecture must land. "A parent who leaves your room feeling stupid will not do what you suggested, and will not come back to tell you it did not work."

11.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Parenting is a developmental task that changes. The parent has a task at each stage, and the tasks conflict. The parent of a toddler must hold limits; the parent of a seven-year-old must let go of the minute-by-minute view; the parent of a fifteen-year-old must negotiate. Parents who are given information appropriate to their child's age rather than to their own experience are being given something they cannot get from their own upbringing, which is why the developmental fact is the second part of the structure.

2. Information is not advice. Advice tells a parent what to do; information tells them how something works. Advice invites compliance or resistance; information invites thinking. A parent told "you should not shout" has been advised and will feel judged. A parent told "at four, a child's braking system is still being built, which is why shouting produces a pause rather than a change" has been informed, and can then decide what to do. The clinical discipline is to give information and let the parent derive the action — and to supply the action only if they cannot.

3. Normalising is a technical act, not a kindness. "That is very common" does three things: it lowers the parent's shame, which is what prevents disclosure; it locates the child in a population, which changes the parent's attribution from character to development; and it creates permission for the parent to say the next thing. It fails when it is used to close a conversation ("all children do that") or when it is untrue.

4. One action, chosen for feasibility. Five recommendations produce none. The action must be small enough to survive a bad week, specific enough to be observable, and chosen with the parent rather than for them. The test question is: "could you do this on the worst evening of the month?" If not, it is not the action; it is the aspiration.

5. The review is part of the treatment. A review date converts a suggestion into a plan, gives the parent permission to fail, and creates the conditions for the therapist to learn whether anything worked. The review should name the exception ("if he is still doing this in three weeks, or if he starts X, come back"), because an unconditional review is heard as "come back when you have failed again".

11.2a The four parts, worked at five ages

The table gives the same structure five times, with the content drawn from the weeks that produced it. It is the single most useful page in the module and it should be learned well enough to be used without notes.

AgeNormaliseThe developmental factOne actionThe review
6 months"Waking at night is very common at six months, and it is not a sign you have done something wrong.""At this age he is learning that you still exist when he cannot see you, and that is why he calls.""Go to him, keep the room dark and boring, and put him down drowsy rather than asleep.""If it has not changed in three weeks, or if he stops feeding well, come back."
2 years"Every child this age says no; it is the age, not your parenting.""He has just discovered that he is a separate person, and 'no' is how he tests it.""Name the feeling, hold the limit, offer two acceptable choices.""Three weeks. If the hitting is worse, or if he stops talking, come back sooner."
4 years"The lying is extremely common at four and it is not a moral problem yet.""At four he cannot always tell the difference between what happened and what he wishes had happened.""Ask 'what happened next?' rather than 'why did you do it?'""Three weeks, and if the lying comes with other changes — sleep, appetite, friends — come back."
7 years"Wanting to be first is normal at seven; the comparison has just arrived.""At seven he begins to compare himself with other children, and losing becomes a fact about him rather than about the game.""Name the effort rather than the result, once a day, in a sentence he can hear.""A month. If he stops wanting to play at all, come back."
15 years"Not talking to you is common and it is not permanent.""At fifteen the private world is a developmental need, not a rejection; secrecy is what privacy becomes when there is no agreement.""One question a day, at a stated time, and not at the door.""Two weeks. If he stops seeing his friends altogether, come back at once."

How to use the table without sounding like a table. Do not deliver the four parts in order as a script. Deliver the normalising sentence, then wait, then give the fact in the parent's own vocabulary, then the action. The structure is for the therapist's head; the parent should hear a conversation.

11.2b The five questions parents are actually asking

Underneath almost every parental question is one of five concerns, and a therapist who answers the question asked without hearing the concern underneath will be asked it again in different words.

1. "Is something wrong with my child?" The question is about diagnosis and it is asked in almost every first session, in some form. The answer that works is not "no" and not "we will see", but a description of what is and is not yet known: "what you are describing is common at this age; the things that would make me worried are X, Y and Z, and he does not have them. I would be looking for them at the review."

2. "Is it my fault?" The question is about blame, and it is usually unspoken. A therapist who does not address it will find the parent either defensive or collapsed, and neither can implement an action. The sentence that works is factual rather than absolving: "children's development is affected by many things, and a parent who is asking this question is usually doing more than they think." Then move to the action, because blame is not resolved by argument but by competence.

3. "Will it get better?" The question is about the future and it is a request for a prediction. Do not predict. Offer the shape instead: "the pattern you are describing usually changes as language develops, which is in the next year. What I cannot tell you is how much, and I would rather review it in three weeks than guess."

4. "What do I do tonight?" The question is about the immediate. It is the most answerable and the most frequently unanswered, because clinicians are trained to formulate and parents need an action before bedtime. Answer it, even if the answer is provisional.

5. "Do you think I am a bad parent?" The question is about judgement, and it is asked by the parents who are most competent and most exhausted. The answer must be honest and behavioural: "I think you are the person who noticed, brought him here, and is prepared to try something different, which is what the parents I worry about do not do."

The rule. Answer the question underneath, and answer it in the first ten minutes. A parent whose underlying concern has been met can then hear information; a parent whose concern is still unanswered will hear every subsequent sentence through it.

11.3 The method: a parent feedback conversation, ten steps

Twenty minutes, in a room with one or two parents, after an assessment. The structure is fixed because improvisation is what produces the two failure modes: a lecture, or a reassurance.

Step 1 — Ask what they think is happening. Before saying anything. The parent's own formulation is the starting point, and it identifies which of the five questions is live.

Step 2 — Ask what they have already tried. Their attempts are the first resource of the plan and the first evidence of their competence. Name one aloud: "you have already done the hardest part, which is changing what you do at bedtime."

Step 3 — Give the finding in one sentence, without jargon. "He is not behind in his development. He is a boy with a lot of energy and a hearing test that has never been done." One sentence, and then stop.

Step 4 — Normalise, and be specific about what is normal. "What you are describing is very common at five, and the reason it is common is that the part of the brain that stops an impulse is still being built."

Step 5 — Give the one developmental fact they will remember. One. Not three. It must be sayable in a single sentence and repeatable to a spouse that evening.

Step 6 — Check understanding by asking them to say it back. "Say that back to me in your own words, because I want to know whether I said it clearly." This is not a test of the parent; it is a test of the therapist, and it is the step trainees most often skip.

Step 7 — Offer one action, and ask whether it is possible. "Could you do that on the worst evening of the month?" If the answer is no, the action is wrong, and the therapist adjusts until the answer is yes.

Step 8 — Write it on a page, in their words. Not in the therapist's. A page in the parent's own phrasing is followed; a page in clinical language is filed.

Step 9 — Set the review, with the exception named. A date, and the specific things that would bring them back sooner.

Step 10 — Ask the closing question. "What are you going to tell his father tonight?" Or the grandmother, or the aunt. The question surfaces the household's information system, and it frequently reveals that the plan will be contradicted within an hour of the parent getting home.

11.3a Telling a parent something difficult

Some feedback is not normalising. A developmental delay, a probable diagnosis, a safeguarding concern, a recommendation that a child be assessed by another service — these require a different structure, and the difference is that the therapist is delivering news the parent will remember for years.

Before the conversation. Decide what the one piece of news is, in one sentence, and say it aloud twice before the session. Decide what you do not yet know, and be ready to say so. Arrange for both parents to be present if possible, and for someone to be with the parent afterwards. Book a longer slot than usual, and book the follow-up before the conversation begins.

The structure, in six moves.

  1. Signal. "I want to talk about what the assessment found, and some of it is not what you were hoping for." Two seconds of warning, which is the difference between being told and being ambushed.
  2. The news, in one sentence, without hedging. "His language is about two years behind where we would expect."
  3. Stop. Silence. The parent needs time, and anything the therapist says in the next thirty seconds will not be heard.
  4. The meaning, in the parent's terms. "I know that what you are hearing is that the thing you were afraid of is true." Naming the fear accurately is more useful than reassurance.
  5. What is not changed. "He is the same boy he was this morning. He is affectionate, he is good with his hands, and he has a mother who noticed." True sentences only.
  6. What happens next, concretely. A referral, a date, a name, a telephone number, and a follow-up appointment with the therapist. Nothing is left open.

What not to do. Do not deliver the news in the last five minutes of a session. Do not use a diagnostic label without the explanation that goes with it. Do not follow the news with a programme, because the parent will not hear it. Do not say "I am sure it will be fine" — the parent will remember that sentence, and it will be the one that makes them distrust everything else you said.

Afterwards. Write to the parent, briefly, within a week: what was said, what happens next, and the telephone number. A letter after difficult news is one of the few interventions in this book with no downside, and it is almost never done.

11.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The one-sentence formulation, said to the parent's face. Write the formulation as one sentence, in plain language, and say it: "your son is not naughty; he is a five-year-old with an undiagnosed hearing problem, and he has been getting into trouble for two years for something he cannot help." The sentence does three things at once: it explains, it removes blame and it implies the action. If the sentence cannot be written in plain language, the formulation is not yet finished.

Technique 2 — The three-week review card. A card with the date, the action, and the two exceptions that would bring the parent back sooner. The card is the treatment's memory, and it survives a fortnight in which the parent is too busy to think about any of it.

Technique 3 — The household rehearsal. Ask the parent to rehearse, in the session, the sentence they will use with the other caregiver. Then ask them what that caregiver will say back, and rehearse the answer. Families do not fail at the clinic; they fail at the front door, when the grandmother says "in my time we did not do that".

11.5 The assessed role-play

The parent-feedback role-play is worth twenty per cent of the module mark. The assessment is deliberately public: each student performs in front of the group for ten minutes, and the group marks against the same rubric the examiner uses, which is the fastest way to teach a skill that is normally learned by watching someone else do it badly for years.

The set-up. A brief of no more than 150 words, containing the age of the child, the presenting concern, one thing the parent has already tried, one thing the parent believes that is wrong, and one live emotion in the room (shame, anger, exhaustion, or fear of a partner's reaction). The student has ten minutes with the parent and no notes.

What is being marked. Not warmth. Not the accuracy of the developmental content in the abstract — the examiners accept any defensible fact. What is marked is the structure in use: did the parent leave with (a) a normalising statement they could repeat, (b) one fact, (c) one action they had agreed to, and (d) a review date with an exception named.

The four ways students fail.

  • The lecture. Three facts and five recommendations in ten minutes, delivered to a parent who has not been asked a single question. The mark is low irrespective of accuracy, because nothing will be done.
  • The reassurance. "Do not worry, he will grow out of it", delivered before any assessment has been described. This fails on the rubric's accuracy criterion, and it is the commonest failure among trainees who are naturally warm.
  • The collusion. Agreeing with the parent's formulation because disagreeing feels unkind ("yes, his father is very strict"). This fails, because a therapist who agrees with one parent against the other has joined the household's conflict.
  • The hedge. Never stating a finding, so that the parent leaves with no information at all. This is common among trainees who have been taught that they must not diagnose, and the correction is that describing is not diagnosing: "his language is behind what we would expect, and that needs an assessment" is a description, and it is what the parent came for.

11.5a The parent who arrives with a diagnosis from the internet

The commonest new complication in this work, and it has a structure that can be taught in ten minutes.

What arrives. A parent who has read about a condition — attention deficit, autism, attachment disorder, sensory processing, "highly sensitive child", or a term from a social media video — and who is either relieved to have a name or frightened by it. The parent may ask the therapist to confirm the label, or may resent any suggestion that the label is not the whole picture.

What not to do. Do not dismiss the reading. A parent who has spent three months researching is a parent who is trying, and dismissing the research dismisses the effort. Do not confirm a diagnosis you have not made, which is both a scope error and a clinical one. And do not argue about the label, which converts the session into a debate about a word.

The four-step response.

First, ask what led them to it. "What did you see that made you look that up?" The answer produces the behavioural description the therapist needs, and it is usually better than the label.

Second, separate the description from the name. "The things you are describing — not sitting still, covering his ears, needing the same routine — are real and I saw two of them today. The name is a decision for an assessment, and the assessment is the way to get it right."

Third, say what would change either way. This is the step that resolves most of these conversations: "whatever the assessment says, the things we would do first are the same — protect his sleep, give him one instruction at a time, and find the thing he can do for twenty-five minutes." A parent who discovers that the immediate plan does not depend on the label stops needing the label today.

Fourth, name the referral honestly. If an assessment is indicated, say so and give a date. If it is not, say what the therapist would need to see for it to become indicated, in behavioural terms. A parent who has been told the threshold can watch for it, and watching is itself useful.

The sentence that holds the whole position. "You may be right, and I am not going to pretend to know before we have looked. What I do know is what to do this month." Parents accept uncertainty about the future far more readily than they accept being told that their observation was wrong.

11.6 Case: the mother who has been told she is too soft

Referral. Chidi, seven, in Primary 2, brought by his mother, Adaeze, 33, a tailor, on the school's recommendation. The school's note: "Chidi does not sit still, does not finish his work, and is rough with the smaller children. He is bright. The mother is not firm with him."

The household. Adaeze; Chidi; a daughter, 4; Adaeze's husband, Emeka, 38, a long-distance driver, home four days a month; and Emeka's mother, 61, who lives in the same compound and who believes Chidi is spoiled. Adaeze runs her tailoring business from the front room.

The presenting complaint, in the mother's words. "They say I am too soft. His grandmother says it. His father says it when he comes home and then he shouts at him and it is worse for a week. The teacher says I should beat him. I do not want to beat him. I do not know what to do and I am tired."

What the assessment finds. Vision normal. Hearing never tested. Language: Chidi's vocabulary is good, his sentence construction is age-appropriate, and he cannot follow a three-step instruction in a noisy room — which the therapist tests by giving one, twice, in the consulting room and in the corridor. Attention: he can sustain a preferred activity for twenty-five minutes and a non-preferred one for four. Sleep: he goes to bed at ten, shares a bed with his sister, and wakes when the compound is noisy at five. Diet: he eats once a day, in the evening, because the family eats late. School: forty-eight children in the class, and his seat is at the back.

The five findings.

  1. There is no evidence of a conduct problem and there is evidence of an unassessed hearing and attention picture. The therapist does not diagnose ADHD; she describes what she saw, names two things that must be excluded, and refers for hearing and for a developmental assessment.
  2. The child is not naughty at four in the afternoon; he is a seven-year-old who has eaten nothing since the previous evening, slept six hours, and is being asked to sit in a class of forty-eight. The environmental account is not an excuse; it is a set of variables that can be changed.
  3. The mother is being managed by three authorities and supported by none. The school blames her, the grandmother blames her, the father arrives monthly and shouts. Her "softness" is not a parenting style; it is what remains of a woman with no allies.
  4. The father's monthly arrival is the family's actual behavioural cycle. Chidi is worse for a week after each return, which the school reads as deterioration and which is better understood as a boy who has been shouted at by a man he sees four days a month.
  5. The most useful intervention in the case has nothing to do with Chidi's behaviour. It is an alliance with the mother that makes her the author of a plan rather than the object of three plans.

The feedback conversation, in order. The therapist asks what Adaeze thinks is happening. She says: "he is a good boy but he is stubborn, and I am too soft." The therapist asks what she has tried. She lists six things, including two she has invented herself, and the therapist names one: "you have already changed his bedtime once and it worked for a while. That is not a soft mother; that is a mother who experiments."

The one-sentence finding. "Chidi is a bright seven-year-old whose hearing has never been tested and who is being asked to sit still in a class of forty-eight on six hours' sleep and no breakfast, and the behaviour you are being blamed for is the predictable result."

Adaeze cries. The therapist waits. Then: "and you have been told by three people that the problem is you, which is why you are tired."

The four parts, delivered.

Normalise: "Boys of seven who cannot sit still in a hot classroom are extremely common, and it is not a sign that a mother has failed."

The fact: "At seven, a child can hold one instruction in mind but not three, especially in a noisy room. He is not ignoring you; he is losing the middle of the sentence."

The action: one. "Breakfast before he leaves the house, even if it is bread and a boiled egg, and say the instruction one step at a time: 'put your shoes on', then when that is done, 'bring your bag'." The therapist does not give the sleep change, the hearing referral and the school request as actions; those are the plan, and they are on the page, but only one thing is asked for this week.

The review: "Three weeks. If the school reports he is hurting another child, come back at once. If the hearing test comes back abnormal, we will change the whole plan."

The rest of the plan, on the page. A hearing test, booked by the clinic. A letter to the school asking for a seat at the front and one named adult. A developmental assessment referral. A conversation with the grandmother, held by Adaeze, rehearsed in the session, in which she says one sentence: "the doctor says his hearing has never been tested and we are doing that first." And a request to the father, by telephone, in which Adaeze says one sentence she has rehearsed: "when you come home, I need you to ask him about school before you tell him anything."

The grandmother, handled. The therapist does not invite the grandmother to the session, and explains why: the household has three authorities and no hierarchy, and adding a fourth voice — the therapist's — will not produce agreement. Instead Adaeze is given the sentence to say, and the authority to say it, because the grandmother's objection is not to the plan; it is to being excluded from it. The sentence makes her the person who is informed first.

11.6a Six weeks later

The hearing test is normal, which the therapist records as a genuinely useful negative: two years of "not listening" have been excluded as a hearing problem, and the school has been told so in writing.

Chidi is eating breakfast. The three-step instruction has become a two-step instruction, because Adaeze found that three was still too many, and she adjusted it herself. This is the outcome the week is aiming for: a parent who modifies a plan is a parent who owns it.

The school has moved his seat and named a teacher. The rough behaviour has reduced, not disappeared. The developmental assessment has a date in eleven weeks, and the therapist has written to say that the assessment is pending and that the school's "not firm" note should be removed from the file, which it is.

Emeka has been telephoned once by the therapist, at Adaeze's request, for four minutes. He said little. He did, on his next return, ask Chidi about school before saying anything else, and Adaeze reports that Chidi talked for eleven minutes.

The honest limitation. The grandmother's view has not changed. She now says it less in front of Chidi, which is the achievable outcome and is worth having: a child who hears one criticism instead of three is better off even when the third person's opinion is unchanged.

11.7 Transcript: the ten minutes, annotated

The transcript is the assessed role-play's model answer, compressed. The annotations name the part of the structure being used at each turn.

T1 Therapist: Before I tell you anything, I want to know what you think is happening. — Step 1. The single sentence that distinguishes this conversation from a consultation with a verdict. T2 Adaeze: He is a good boy. But he is stubborn, and I am too soft. Everybody says so. T3 Therapist: Who is everybody? — Three words. The answer identifies the household's authority structure, which is the actual clinical problem, and it takes eight seconds. T4 Adaeze: The teacher. His grandmother. His father when he comes. T5 Therapist: And what have you tried? — Step 2. The question that finds the parent's competence, which in this case has been invisible to her for two years. T6 Adaeze: (lists six things) T7 Therapist: You changed his bedtime in January and it worked for a while. That is not a soft mother. That is a mother who experiments. — Naming competence with evidence. Note that the therapist does not say "you are a good mother", which is a verdict the parent cannot use; she describes a behaviour. T8 Adaeze: (quietly) Nobody has said that to me. T9 Therapist: Here is what I found, in one sentence, and then we will talk about it. (pause) Chidi is a bright seven-year-old whose hearing has never been tested, who is being asked to sit still in a class of forty-eight, on six hours' sleep and no breakfast. The behaviour you are being blamed for is the predictable result. — Step 3, and the sentence is prepared, plain, and contains no diagnosis. It also answers the question underneath, which is "is it my fault?" T10 Adaeze: (cries) T11 Therapist: (waits, offers the box of tissues, says nothing) — Step 3b. The silence after the finding. Anything said here will be remembered instead of the finding, and the finding is what she needs to remember. T12 Therapist: (after about twenty seconds) Say that back to me, in your words, so I know whether I said it clearly. — Step 6, and the pronoun is deliberate: the fault for an unclear sentence is assigned to the therapist, which is what makes the check honest.

11.7a When the complaint comes from the school

A frequent configuration: the referral originates with a school, the parent arrives defensive, and the therapist is positioned between an institution and a family. Three practices keep the therapist from becoming the school's representative.

Practice 1 — Distinguish the observation from the verdict. "Chidi does not finish his work" is an observation. "The mother is not firm with him" is a verdict, and it is a verdict about an adult who is not in the room. The therapist works with the first and declines the second, in writing if necessary.

Practice 2 — Ask the parent what the teacher would say if asked. The answer is often accurate and sympathetic, and it reveals that the parent already knows the school's view and has been carrying it home. Naming that — "you have been told this for two years and you have kept coming back" — is frequently the moment the alliance forms.

Practice 3 — Give the parent something to give the school. A sentence, a request, a letter. A parent who arrives at the school gate with a specific request from a clinic is in a different position from a parent who arrives with an explanation, and the difference is not rhetorical: schools respond to requests with names and dates attached.

The boundary. The therapist does not become the family's advocate against the school, and does not become the school's agent inside the family. The position is with the child's development, which both institutions say they are serving, and which is the only ground on which both can be held to account.

11.8 Laboratory and the marking scheme

The laboratory runs in two parts across two weeks. Part one is rehearsal with peers, unmarked. Part two is the assessed role-play in front of the group.

Marking scheme (20 marks).

CriterionMarksWhat the top band looks like
Structure in use6All four parts present and in order, without the parent noticing a structure
Accuracy4The developmental content is correct for the age, and no diagnosis is made in the room
One action4One action, agreed rather than prescribed, tested against the worst evening of the month, written in the parent's words
The review3A date and a named exception, both written
Holding the frame3When the parent disagrees, the therapist neither capitulates nor argues; she names the disagreement and offers a review

The pass standard. A student who reassures for ten minutes and gives no action has failed, however warm the room felt. A student who gives five actions has also failed, however accurate the content.

The role-play briefs. Four, rotated. Each contains one emotion and one false belief.

Brief A — the exhausted mother of a two-year-old. She believes the problem is that he "does not respect her". The emotion is shame. The hidden question is "is it my fault?"

Brief B — the father of a nine-year-old who refuses school. He believes the boy is lazy. The emotion is anger, covering fear. The hidden question is "will he turn out like me?"

Brief C — the mother of a fifteen-year-old who has been caught lying. She believes she must search the room. The emotion is fear. The hidden question is "what do I do tonight?"

Brief D — the parents of a four-year-old with a speech delay. They believe he will talk when he is ready, as his uncle did. The emotion is resignation. The hidden question is "is something wrong with my child?"

The debrief question, asked of the group every time. "What did the parent leave with that they did not have when they came in?" The answer must be a sentence, an action and a date. If the answer is "they felt better", the role-play failed, and the group must be able to say why.

11.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Reassuring before assessing. The commonest error in the whole of paediatric practice, and the most damaging, because the reassurance is remembered and the assessment is not. Reassurance after an assessment is a finding; before one, it is a guess with a kind face.

Error 2 — Giving the parent the therapist's formulation. "He has a regulatory difficulty secondary to maternal inconsistency" is a sentence for a file. Said to a parent, it produces shame and no action.

Error 3 — Five recommendations. The parent will implement none and will not say so. One action, chosen for the worst evening of the month.

Error 4 — Agreeing with one parent against the other. "Yes, his father is very strict" feels supportive and is a coalition. The therapist's position is with the household's capacity to change, which requires both parents in the room eventually.

Error 5 — Treating the parent as the patient without saying so. Sometimes the parent is the focus, and when that happens it must be said: "the work I am proposing is with you, not with him, and I want to be clear about that." A parent who discovers halfway through that they have become the client will leave.

Error 6 — Leaving the household's other authorities out of the plan. The grandmother, the aunt, the father on the telephone. A plan that requires a parent to contradict three people alone will fail at the front door, and the therapist will conclude that the parent was not motivated.

11.9a Six sentences that do harm, and what to say instead

Small language choices do more damage in parent feedback than any amount of wrong content. The six below are the ones trainees use most.

"He is doing it for attention." True and useless, because attention is a legitimate need and the sentence implies a fault. Instead: "he has found that this is the way to get you, and we are going to give him a better way."

"He is testing the boundaries." A metaphor that makes the child sound like a strategist. Instead: "he is finding out whether the rule is the same on a bad day, which is what every child does at this age."

"You need to be consistent." An instruction to have a quality, not to do a behaviour, and it is heard as "you are failing." Instead: "pick one rule to hold this week, and let the others go."

"In my experience…" Places the therapist's experience above the parent's knowledge of their own child, and invites a comparison the therapist will win. Instead: "what does he do when you try that?"

"He will grow out of it." A prediction, usually accurate, always remembered, and it prevents the review. Instead: "this pattern usually changes as language develops. I would rather see you in three weeks than guess."

"There is nothing wrong with him." Meant kindly, heard as "your concern is imaginary". Instead: "I did not find the things that would worry me today. What I did find is X, and that is worth working on."

The exercise, in the laboratory. Students are given these six sentences on a card and asked to deliver each one to a partner in role, then to deliver the replacement, and to say which one produced a response. The difference is felt immediately and is remembered for years.

11.10 Culture, faith and talking to parents

The authority to say it. In many households a young clinician, and particularly a young woman, will not be heard on the subject of child-rearing by an older relative. This is not resistance to the content; it is a fact about standing. The workable moves are three: route the information through the person who has standing (a paediatrician, a religious leader, a respected aunt); give the parent a sentence that carries the clinic's authority ("the doctor says his hearing has never been tested"); and never require the parent to win an argument with a grandmother in order to follow the plan.

Corporal punishment. A substantial proportion of the parents in these clinics were beaten and believe that they turned out well, and many schools still recommend it. The therapist who opens with a moral position will end the conversation. The sequence that works is: ask what the parent believes and why; give the developmental fact (what beating does to the behaviour being targeted, over what timescale); give one alternative that is specific and feasible; and accept that the change may be partial. A parent who shouts less is a better outcome than a parent who leaves the clinic believing they have been judged.

Faith and the child's condition. Where a child's difficulty is understood spiritually, the parent may be pursuing prayer and may be advised by a religious authority not to accept a referral. The clinical position is to work with the framework: ask what the prayer is for, offer to speak with the religious authority if the parent wishes, and hold one non-negotiable — that a hearing test and a developmental assessment are not acts of unbelief. Framing them as seeing what God has made is not a manipulation; it is a translation, and translation is part of the job.

The father on the telephone. In households with labour migration or long-distance work, the father is present by telephone and his authority is exercised remotely. A plan that does not include him will be reversed by him. The four-minute telephone call is a clinical instrument: one sentence from the therapist, one question to him, and one agreement. It is worth more than four sessions with the mother alone.

DEBATE — "A therapist should not tell a parent what to do." Argue for: advice creates dependence, parents know their children, and the evidence on autonomy support favours collaboration over instruction. Argue against: parents come for advice, a refusal to give it is a withholding of expertise, and a parent with a seven-year-old who has not eaten breakfast needs an instruction rather than a reflective conversation. Then state your rule for when you instruct and when you ask, and give the case that would make you break it.

11.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The content of the four parts is well supported: psychoeducation about normative development reduces parental anxiety and increases the accuracy of parental attributions; specific, behavioural, positively framed instructions are followed more often than general advice; and brief, structured parenting interventions with a scheduled review produce better outcomes than open-ended support. The finding that parental attributions — whether the parent believes the behaviour is intentional — predict both parental response and treatment outcome is robust and is the empirical basis for the normalising step.

What is weaker. The evidence on how to deliver feedback is thinner than the evidence on what to deliver. The four-part structure is assembled from clinical teaching and from the psychoeducation literature rather than tested as a package. The claim that one action outperforms five is plausible, consistent with the implementation literature, and not directly tested in this form.

What is not supported. That reassurance reduces anxiety when the concern is real — the evidence suggests the opposite, because a parent whose concern is dismissed becomes more anxious and less likely to disclose. That insight alone changes parenting behaviour. And that a single feedback session is sufficient for established difficulties; the evidence favours brief structured contact with review, which is what the fourth part exists to create.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same feedback, given to a mother with three authorities against her and given to a mother with one ally, produces two different families, and the difference is not in the feedback.

11.12 Key terms

Normalising — locating the child's behaviour in a population, to reduce shame and change attribution.

Attribution — the parent's belief about why the child behaves as he does; intentional versus developmental attributions predict different parental responses.

One action — a single, feasible, observable behaviour agreed with the parent.

The exception — the specific change that would bring the family back sooner than the review date.

Household rehearsal — practising the sentence the parent will use with the other caregivers, and their likely reply.

Standing — the social authority to be heard on a given subject, which is not the same as expertise.

Psychoeducation — structured information about a condition or a developmental stage, given to reduce distress and improve management.

Coalition — an alliance between the therapist and one family member against another; the characteristic error of sympathetic practice.

11.13 Self-test

  1. Give the four parts of the feedback skill and say what each one prevents.
  2. Why is reassurance before assessment an error, and what is the evidence?
  3. Name the five questions parents are actually asking, and give one sentence that answers each.
  4. What is the test question for choosing an action, and why is it asked rather than assumed?
  5. Why does the therapist in the Chidi case not invite the grandmother to the session?
  6. What are the four ways students fail the assessed role-play, and what do all four have in common?
  7. How would you handle a parent who believes the child's difficulty is spiritual and has been advised not to accept a referral?
  8. What is the "exception" in a review, and why is an unconditional review heard as a threat?

Answers. (1) Normalising prevents shame and closes the disclosure problem; the fact prevents blame and changes attribution; the action prevents paralysis; the review prevents abandonment and creates the conditions for the therapist to learn the outcome. (2) Because it is a guess delivered with a kind face and it is remembered instead of the assessment; the evidence is that dismissing a real concern increases anxiety and reduces later disclosure. (3) Is something wrong with my child — "what you describe is common at this age, and the things that would worry me are X, Y and Z, which he does not have"; is it my fault — "a parent asking that question is usually doing more than they think"; will it get better — "this pattern usually changes as language develops; I would rather review than guess"; what do I do tonight — give the action; am I a bad parent — "you noticed and you came, which is what the parents I worry about do not do". (4) "Could you do this on the worst evening of the month?" — asked because feasibility, not correctness, determines whether an action is carried out. (5) Because the household has three authorities and no hierarchy, and adding the therapist's voice produces a fourth rather than an agreement; the sentence given to the mother makes the grandmother the person informed first, which addresses the actual objection. (6) The lecture, the reassurance, the collusion, the hedge — all four end the conversation rather than opening it. (7) Work inside the framework: ask what the prayer is for, offer to speak to the religious authority, and hold one non-negotiable — that a hearing test and a developmental assessment are not acts of unbelief, since they are ways of seeing what has been made. (8) The specific change that would bring the family back sooner; without it the review is heard as "come back when you have failed again".

11.14 Reflective task

Record yourself giving feedback to a colleague in role, and listen to it twice. On the first listening, count the number of recommendations you gave. On the second, listen for the moment your voice changed when the colleague disagreed with you. Most trainees find between three and six recommendations and a voice that either rose or softened into agreement. Both are the same failure: the therapist leaving the frame in order to keep the room.

11.15 Further reading

  • Hattie, J. and Timperley, H. "The power of feedback" — the general evidence on what makes feedback usable.
  • Sanders, M. Positive Parenting Program materials — the worked examples of specific, behavioural instructions.
  • Johnston, C. and Mash, E. "Parental attributions" — the empirical basis for the normalising step.
  • Brazelton, T. Touchpoints — the classic account of developmental information given to parents well.
  • Buckman, R. Breaking Bad News — the six-step protocol adapted in 11.3a.

Next: Week 12 — Later life: integrity, loss and caregiving.

Week 12 — Later life: integrity, loss and caregiving

Developmental tasks. Integrity versus despair (Erikson, from about sixty): the consolidation of a life into something that can be told as a whole, or the conviction that it did not add up. Adaptation to retirement, which is a role loss before it is a financial one. Bereavement, which at this stage arrives repeatedly and in sequence. Caregiving, which reverses the generational direction. Grandparenthood, which is the longest single role in most people's lives and the least theorised. And legacy — what is handed on, and to whom.

Clinical realities. Cognitive change must be distinguished from delirium, depression and dementia, and from hearing loss — which is a major and under-treated contributor to isolation and apparent confusion. In this age band more mistaken diagnoses are made by omission of a hearing test than by any other single error. Caregiving burden falls disproportionately on women and on the "responsible" child, and it frequently reopens sibling hierarchies that were settled forty years ago.

Grief. Use the dual-process model (Figure 22, MFT 712): healthy grieving oscillates between loss-oriented and restoration-oriented activity, and the oscillation is the mechanism, not a sign of avoidance. In many cultures, mourning is collective and prescribed; the clinician's task is to support the rites rather than to impose a private model of "working through". A widow who is surrounded by prescribed mourning for forty days and then alone is not in a pathological grief; she is in a transition that the rites mark and the culture does not follow through.

Why this week is difficult to teach. Because it concerns the end of the story, and because students carry their own grandparents into the room. The week is also the most practically useful in the second half of the course: families with an ageing parent make up a large share of the referrals a family therapist receives, and the work is concrete — a rota, a hearing test, a family meeting — rather than interpretive.

— Three siblings disagree about their mother's care. The eldest, a daughter, has done everything — the medical appointments, the money, the telephone calls — from another city. The son with whom the mother lives has a wife who does the daily work and who has not been consulted. The youngest son lives twenty minutes away and visits twice a year. The referral comes from the daughter-in-law, and she is the only person in the family who is not related to the patient.

12.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The last stageWhy integrity is a task and not a moodName one older person whose life they would call "integrated", and say why
8–20RetirementThe role loss, the two sexes' different experiences, and the somatic presentationPredict the presentation for three retirees
20–34The cognitive differentialDelirium, depression, dementia, hearing — the four-way table, workedSort eight observations into the four categories
34–46CaregivingBurden, the "responsible child", and the reopening of sibling hierarchiesMap a caregiving network and its unstated contract
46–58GriefThe dual-process model, and why oscillation is healthClassify six bereavement behaviours
58–70The family meetingStructure, roles, and the one thing that must be decidedRun a five-minute family meeting in role
70–82Grandparenthood and legacyWhat is handed on, and to whom, in households with distributed careInterview a peer about what they received
82–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. Students arrive with a sentimental model of old age — wisdom, grandchildren, a rocking chair — or a medical model — decline, burden, placement. Both are ways of not asking. The lecture's job is to install a practical attitude: what does this person need, who is providing it, who is not being consulted, and what would have to change this week? The demonstration is the caregiving network map, drawn in five minutes on the board, because it makes visible in one diagram the eleven people who are involved and the one who is doing the work.

The sentence the lecture must land. "The question in later life is almost never 'what does this person need?' — everybody knows that. It is 'who is going to do it, and who has been deciding without them?'"

12.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. Integrity versus despair. Integrity is the capacity to hold one's life as a whole — including its failures — and to accept it as having been worth living. It is not contentment and it is not wisdom. It is an achievement of narrative, and it is made possible or impossible by the audience available. A seventy-eight-year-old with grandchildren who ask questions has a different task from one whose contemporaries have all died and whose children telephone on Sundays.

The clinical translation: the therapist's task is not to supply meaning but to elicit the account. The life review (below) is the technique, and the evidence for it is modest but consistent: structured reminiscence reduces depressive symptoms in older adults, and its mechanism appears to be the construction of coherence rather than the discharge of emotion.

2. Retirement is a role loss before it is a financial one. The research finding that matters is that the loss of the work role predicts distress more strongly than the loss of income, and that the effect is largest for men whose identity was occupational and for people whose retirement was involuntary. The presentation in a family clinic is usually somatic — fatigue, pain, a "heart" that has been investigated — and the question that opens it is "what were you supposed to be doing now?"

3. Bereavement in later life arrives in sequence. The distinguishing feature of grief at this stage is not intensity but accumulation: the death of a spouse is frequently preceded by siblings, friends, and sometimes a child. The dual-process model is useful here because it normalises the oscillation — a widow who laughs at a funeral and weeps while sorting a wardrobe is not confused; she is moving between loss-oriented and restoration-oriented coping, and both are work.

4. Caregiving is a role with no induction. The caregiver is usually appointed by proximity, gender, or birth order, rarely by negotiation, and the appointment is rarely spoken. The clinical consequences are three: the caregiver's exhaustion is invisible because it is expected; the caregiver's resentment is unmentionable because it feels like a betrayal; and the person being cared for is frequently not consulted at all, which is the dignity injury that the family never notices.

5. Grandparenthood and legacy. In households with distributed caregiving, the grandparent is often a primary caregiver rather than a visitor, and the role runs in both directions: the grandmother raising children while the parents work, and later the adult children caring for the grandmother. The legacy question — what is handed on, and to whom — is answered by what the older person is actually asked. A grandmother whose only conversations are about medication has no route to give anything, and the giving is the developmental task.

12.2a The four-way cognitive differential

This table is the most practically important page in the week. In an older person with confusion, four conditions account for the great majority of presentations, and three of them are reversible or treatable.

FeatureDeliriumDepressionDementiaHearing loss
OnsetHours to daysWeeks to monthsMonths to yearsGradual, often years
CourseFluctuates through the dayPersistent, worse in the morningProgressive, steadyConsistent; worse in noise
AttentionImpaired, wandersIntact but poorly sustainedIntact early, impaired lateIntact
MoodFearful, agitatedLow, hopeless, anhedonicVariable; may be apatheticOften withdrawn, embarrassed
MemoryImpaired, patchyComplains of memory, performs better than complaintImpaired, especially recentAppears impaired because of mishearing
ReversibilityUsually, if the cause is treatedUsuallyNot currentlyCorrectable with aids
The question that reveals it"Was he like this yesterday?""When did you last enjoy something?""When did you last manage the money on your own?""Does he answer better when he can see your face?"

The three rules that follow.

Rule 1 — Any sudden change in an older person is delirium until proved otherwise, and it is a medical emergency. A urinary tract infection, constipation, dehydration, a new medication, and pain are the common causes, and all are treatable. A family that has been told "it is dementia" after a two-day change has been told something that may be false and that will organise the next five years.

Rule 2 — Depression in later life looks like dementia and is more treatable. The label "pseudodementia" is unhelpful; the phenomenon is real. The depressed older person answers "I do not know" to cognitive questions, while the person with dementia attempts an answer and gets it wrong. That single observation distinguishes them better than any test the therapist can administer.

Rule 3 — Test the hearing before interpreting the confusion. The commonest scene in this week's work is an older woman described as "difficult" and "not following what is said", who is simply not hearing. The therapist's test is free: sit facing her, at her level, in a quiet room, and repeat the same question with and without your face visible. A difference between the two conditions is the diagnosis.

12.2b Caregiving: the network and the unstated contract

The caregiving network map is drawn in six steps and takes about eight minutes on a whiteboard. It is the week's assessment instrument and, in most cases, the intervention as well.

Step 1 — Put the older person in the centre. Name, age, and the one sentence that describes what she can and cannot do. Not a diagnosis; a function. "She can wash and dress herself. She cannot manage her medication, leave the house, or remember what she was told an hour ago."

Step 2 — Draw everyone who does something, in a ring. Include the daughter-in-law. Include the neighbour who looks in. Include the paid carer, if there is one. The map routinely reveals that the "family" doing the care is five people, of whom two are not related.

Step 3 — Write, next to each name, what they actually do, in hours per week. The numbers are estimates and the estimates are the finding: the person who does forty hours is usually the one who was never asked, and the person who does two is usually the one whose opinion carries the most weight.

Step 4 — Mark who decides. A different marker, on the same names. The gap between who does the work and who makes the decisions is the network's fault line, and it is the source of most of the conflict that arrives in the clinic.

Step 5 — Mark who is not consulted. The person in the centre is almost always on this list, and so is the daughter-in-law. Writing "not consulted" next to the patient's own name is the single most useful intervention in the exercise, because it makes visible an omission that no one in the family has noticed.

Step 6 — Ask the network question. "If the person doing the most stopped tomorrow, what would happen?" The answer is the network's fragility, and it is usually discovered in the same session in which the family arrived complaining about something else.

The unstated contract. Every caregiving network has one, and it has four clauses: who is responsible; what counts as good care; what the carer is entitled to; and what happens if the carer refuses. None of the four is ever spoken, and each is a source of conflict when it is breached.

The clinical move is to make the contract speakable without making it an accusation. The four questions: "who decided that you would be the one? what would count as doing this well? what are you allowed to have for yourself? and what would happen if you said no?" The fourth question is the one that produces tears, because most carers have never considered that refusal is an option and have been living as though it is not.

The burden measures, used carefully. Standardised burden instruments (Zarit, and similar) are useful for research and for demonstrating severity to a family that has normalised it. In the room, the two questions that do most of the work are: "when did you last sleep through the night?" and "who asks how you are?" The second question identifies the carer's isolation more reliably than any scale.

12.2c Retirement: the presentation, and what to ask

Retirement is the developmental event most often missed in a family clinic, because it presents as something else.

The four presentations. A somatic complaint that has been investigated and explained as "nothing serious"; a sudden increase in drinking; a man who is under the family's feet and who has begun to manage the household in a way that produces marital conflict; and a withdrawal into a chair that the family describes as depression. All four are the same role loss in different costumes.

What has actually been lost. Not the money, usually. The structure of the day; the colleagues, who were the social network; the competence, which was visible and daily; and the audience, which is what an occupational identity needs. A man who was the branch manager for eleven years does not miss the money as much as he misses being the person whose judgement was sought.

The four questions.

  1. "What time did you get up before you retired, and what time do you get up now?" The answer is the structure, quantified.
  2. "Who did you talk to every day at work, and who do you talk to every day now?" The answer is the network, quantified.
  3. "What are you good at that nobody asks you about any more?" The answer is the competence, and the question is the first hinge of the intervention.
  4. "What would you have done with the time if you had been allowed to choose, at fifty?" The answer is frequently a lost project, and the lost project is often still available.

The intervention, in two moves. Restore a structure — a fixed weekly commitment with other people in it, which is what the workplace was providing — and restore a use of competence — tutoring, a committee, a trade association, a church role, coaching, a small business. The evidence on volunteering and structured activity in retirement supports the first more strongly than the second; the clinical experience is that both are necessary and that neither is achieved by advice.

The wife's experience. The retirement literature under-reports the marital consequence: a woman who has run a household for thirty years and whose husband is now present in it for all of it. The clinical presentation is a couple in their sixties who have "started arguing", which is usually a pair of people negotiating territory for the first time since 1994. The intervention is not conflict resolution; it is a division of the day, written, in the way that Week 10's division-of-labour audit is written.

12.3 The method: the family meeting about an older parent

One session, three to six participants, ninety minutes if possible. The structure below is adapted from family meetings in medical settings and is used unchanged in this book.

Step 1 — Establish who is in the room and who is not, and why. Name every absent person and the reason. Then ask whether anyone wants them present. A meeting that excludes the person who pays, or the person who does the daily work, will produce a decision that is reversed within a fortnight.

Step 2 — Ask each person, in turn, one question: "what is your greatest worry?" In turn, without interruption, and written down verbatim. This single round does more than any other part of the meeting, because it converts a group of people who have been arguing about arrangements into a group of people who have each stated a fear.

Step 3 — Put the facts on one page. What the older person can do; what the medical picture is; what the options are; what each costs, in money and in hours. Facts first, feelings second, is the order that prevents the meeting from becoming a re-run of a forty-year-old argument.

Step 4 — Name the unstated contract, out loud. "It seems that the arrangement has been that Bola manages the medical care, Funke does the daily work, and nobody has asked Mama what she wants. Is that right?" The family corrects, and the correction is the working formulation.

Step 5 — Generate four options, not two. The binary — she stays where she is, or she goes into a home — is what has made the argument unwinnable. Four options, written on the page, each with costs. The purpose is not to choose in the meeting; it is to demonstrate that there is more than one way for this to go.

Step 6 — Decide one thing, and only one. A review date, a rota for one week, a hearing test, a conversation with the older person. One decision, made in the room, with a named person and a date.

Step 7 — Say what happens if it fails. The fallback, named in advance. Families that have a fallback implement the plan; families without one abandon it at the first difficulty and conclude that the meeting was pointless.

The older person's presence. If she can participate, she participates. The most common and most damaging omission in these meetings is the exclusion of the person whose life is being arranged, on the grounds that she will be upset or confused. A hearing-impaired woman who is spoken over for ninety minutes will be described afterwards as "difficult", and the description will be accurate.

12.3a When the siblings disagree

Sibling conflict about an older parent is not usually about the parent. It is usually about a ledger that was opened in childhood, and the clinician's task is to keep the present decision from being decided by the old ledger — while acknowledging that the ledger is real.

The four patterns, and the move for each.

The responsible child and the absent ones. One sibling has done everything and is exhausted and resentful; the others have done nothing and feel judged. The absent siblings' position is usually defended with a practical argument ("I am in Lagos", "I have small children"), which is true and is not the whole truth. The move: give the absent siblings a specific, bounded, non-negotiable task, and give the responsible child a specific relief. General appeals to help more produce nothing; "Kunle will take Mama to the clinic on the second Saturday of every month" produces a decision.

The child who is not a child. A daughter-in-law, a nephew, a foster kin who has done the work and has no standing in the family's hierarchy. The move: give them standing explicitly, in the meeting, by having the family agree to their participation. A therapist who fails to do this will watch the most competent person in the room be overruled by the least involved.

The sibling who wants placement and the sibling who refuses it. Usually the difference is about money, guilt, or a promise made to the parent. The move: separate the promise from the plan. "What did she ask you to do?" is asked of each sibling, and the answers are frequently different, which is the finding.

The sibling who is not speaking to another. A silence of years, brought into the room by the parent's decline. The move: do not attempt reconciliation. Ask only whether the two of them can agree on one practical matter for six weeks, on the understanding that nothing else is being resolved. Concrete cooperation across an unresolved conflict is achievable and reconciliation is not, and a therapist who aims at the second will lose the first.

The rule that governs all four. The parent's needs are the organising question and are not the whole conversation. A therapist who insists that "this is about Mama" will be met with agreement and no change, because the siblings know it is not only about Mama, and so does the therapist.

12.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The life review, in six questions. Not a memoir. Six questions, asked across two or three sessions, recorded if the older person agrees, and given to the family as a transcript.

  1. "What is the first thing you remember?"
  2. "What did you think your life would be, and what happened instead?"
  3. "Who did you love, and who loved you?"
  4. "What are you proudest of that nobody knows about?"
  5. "What do you want the ones coming after you to know?"
  6. "What would you do differently?"

The technique works because the older person is being asked as an author rather than as a patient, and because each question has a developmental function: memory for continuity, the timetable question for integrity, the love question for relationship, the pride question for self-authorship, the legacy question for generativity, and the counterfactual for the acceptance of a life that was not perfect.

Technique 2 — The legacy recording. A telephone recording of the six answers, given to a grandchild. The instruction to the family is what makes it work: "do not listen to it while she is alive". Its function is not comfort in the present; it is the presence of a voice after the death, which is the thing that grandchildren ask for and that families almost never make.

Technique 3 — The rota, written and limited. A weekly schedule with names beside tasks: who takes her to the clinic, who calls on Tuesday, who sleeps in the house on Friday. Bounded in time — six weeks — and specific in task. The rota does three things that conversation cannot: it distributes the mental load, it makes absence visible, and it converts an obligation into a schedule that can be renegotiated.

The caregiving session, in practice. The therapist who does all three in one session will fail. The sequence that works: the map first (assessment), then one technique (intervention), then the rota (a concrete output). Sessions two and three can add the review.

12.5 The first session with three siblings and a daughter-in-law

Phase 1 — The round of worries (20 minutes). Each person, in turn, one question: "what is your greatest worry?" Written down verbatim. No interruption, no response, and the therapist explains why: "everybody will get a chance to answer the others, and I want the worries on the table first."

Phase 2 — The facts (15 minutes). What Mama can do; what the medical picture is; what the money is; what has been tried. The therapist supplies what she has and asks for the rest, and writes it on one page.

Phase 3 — The map (10 minutes). Drawn on the whiteboard, with hours and decisions marked, and "not consulted" written next to the names that belong there — including, usually, Mama's.

Phase 4 — The contract named (15 minutes). Who decided; what counts as good care; what the carer is entitled to; what happens if she refuses. The four questions, asked slowly, to the group rather than to an individual.

Phase 5 — Four options (15 minutes). On the page, with costs. The therapist's discipline is not to recommend; the family's job is to eliminate.

Phase 6 — One decision and one fallback (10 minutes). Named, dated, written, photographed.

Phase 7 — The absent person (5 minutes). "Who is not here, and who will tell them?" The question is asked at the end, because the answer determines whether the decision survives the week.

Why Mama is asked first, if she is present. Because a meeting about an older person that begins with her children describing her decline has already decided what she is. The therapist's first question to her is not about her health but about her preference: "what would you like the next year to look like?" The answer is frequently surprising, frequently practical, and it reorganises the meeting.

12.6 Case: the Baloguns and the woman in the back room

The family. This household first appeared in MFT 702, where Mr Femi Balogun (now 55) was a long-distance lorry driver away three weeks in four, his wife Funke (49) ran a food business from the house, and his mother (then 78, now 81) was living with them with a diagnosis of dementia. The family has changed since then: the eldest son has left school and is working; the second child, with sickle cell disease, is now 17 and stable; there are two younger children.

The referral. From Funke, the daughter-in-law, by telephone, in tears. "I cannot do this any more. His sister in Lagos decides everything and she is not here. His brother lives twenty minutes away and comes at Christmas. I am the one who bathes her."

The household, as mapped. Mama, 81. Femi, 55, away three weeks in four. Funke, 49, the daily carer. Bola, 58, Femi's elder sister, in Lagos, a retired nurse, who pays for medication and manages appointments by telephone. Kunle, 49, the youngest, in Ibadan, twenty minutes away, visits at Christmas and Easter. Four children in the house, aged 22, 17, 12 and 9. A neighbour, Mama Seri, who looks in on weekdays. A physician at a private hospital who has seen Mama twice in two years.

The hours, estimated in the session. Funke 38 hours a week; Bola 4 (telephone and payments); Kunle 2 (a year); Mama Seri 3; Femi 6 in the week he is home.

The decisions, marked. Bola makes the medical decisions. Femi makes the financial decisions when he is home. Funke makes every decision in between and is not consulted about any of the ones the others make. Mama makes none.

The facts. The dementia diagnosis was made two years ago after a gradual decline. Three months ago there was a sudden worsening over two days — agitation, not recognising Funke, wandering at night — and a locum doctor at the hospital said "the dementia is progressing" and gave a sedative. The worsening resolved in five days. The therapist writes this on the page and circles it, because it is the case's most important fact.

The five findings.

1. The three-month-old "progression" was probably a delirium, and it has never been assessed. A change over two days with agitation and nocturnal worsening, resolving in five days, is the signature of a delirium — most likely a urinary tract infection, constipation, dehydration, or the sedative itself. A diagnosis of progression was made on the basis of a five-day event, and the family has organised three months of decisions around it. The first action of the case is a medical review, not a family decision.

2. The hearing has never been tested and is the likely source of "difficulty". In the session Mama is spoken to from across the room and does not respond; spoken to from a metre away, facing her, she answers appropriately three times out of four. Funke reports that Mama "is better in the morning" — which is when the house is quiet. The therapist asks for a hearing assessment and, in the meantime, demonstrates the difference in the room, in front of the family. Bola, who is a retired nurse, is visibly affected.

3. Funke is doing the work, has no standing, and is not related to the patient. She is the network's most competent member and its least consulted. Every decision about Mama is made by people who are not there, and Funke's experience of the last three months is that she is a servant with clinical responsibilities. Her tears on the telephone are not a symptom of the family's difficulty; they are the most accurate description of it.

4. Bola's control is functional, not merely controlling. She is a retired nurse, she pays, and she has been managing a mother at a distance for two years with the telephone as her only instrument. Her insistence on deciding is what a frightened eldest daughter does when she cannot be present. The therapist who treats her as the villain will lose the person who pays for the medication.

5. Mama has not been asked anything in two years. Not what she wants, not how she feels, not whether she is frightened. The life review questions have never been put to her, and the family has no account of her life that is longer than the last two years.

The formulation. An eighty-one-year-old woman with a dementia diagnosis and an untested hearing loss, whose recent "progression" may have been a treatable delirium, cared for by a daughter-in-law with no standing in the family's hierarchy, managed medically by a daughter at a distance, and financially by a son who is away three weeks in four. The presenting complaint is Funke's exhaustion, and the family's problem is a caregiving network whose workload and authority are inversely distributed.

The intervention, in six parts, in order.

Part 1 — The medical review, first. A written request to the physician for a review of the three-month-old episode, with the specific question: was this a delirium, and was the sedative appropriate? The therapist does not diagnose; she puts the question on paper and asks the family to take it.

Part 2 — The hearing test, and the demonstration in the room. The demonstration has already changed Bola's position, and the test is booked for the following week.

Part 3 — Funke gets standing, explicitly, in the meeting. The therapist asks Bola and Kunle, in front of Funke, to agree that Funke is the person who knows Mama's daily condition and that decisions about the daily arrangements will be made with her. This is a single sentence and it is the intervention on which the rest depends.

Part 4 — Bola gets a defined role and a bounded one. Medical decisions and payment, with a monthly telephone call to Funke at a stated time. The therapist names the reason plainly: "she has been managing from Lagos with a telephone, which is the hardest way to do it, and the answer is not to remove her authority but to give her one that has a schedule."
