Part 5 — Kunle gets one task. The second Saturday of every month, the clinic visit, with a written report to the family group. A specific, bounded, non-negotiable task, which he accepts in the room after some resistance and which he performs for eight consecutive months.

Part 6 — The rota, for six weeks. Written, photographed, with a review date. Bathing, nights, medication, clinic, the neighbour's days.

Part 7 — Mama is asked. In a separate session, with Funke present and Bola on speakerphone, the six life review questions are begun. Mama answers three, is tired, and stops. The therapist asks whether she would like to continue next week, and she says yes. The transcript of that session is, six months later, the thing the family values most.

12.6a Six months later

The medical review found a urinary tract infection at the time of the episode and no evidence of progression beyond the expected; the sedative was stopped. Mama's functioning is close to what it was before the episode, which means that three months of family decisions were made on a false premise.

Hearing aids were prescribed and are worn, inconsistently. When they are worn, Funke reports that Mama is "a different person", which is the phrase families always use and which is a description of the difference between a woman who cannot hear and a woman who is confused.

Kunle has done eight clinic visits out of six months, which is not the plan and is more than the previous decade. Bola telephones Funke on the first Sunday of the month. The two women have begun to speak to each other as colleagues rather than as a manager and an employee, which Funke describes as "she asks me now".

The rota held for five weeks and then dissolved. The therapist notes this without alarm: a rota's function in most families is to make the distribution visible once, not to run indefinitely, and the visibility persisted after the document was lost.

The honest limitation. Mama is not going to recover, Funke is still doing thirty hours a week, and the family is still one woman's illness away from crisis. What changed is that the work is now visible, the worker has standing, and the person in the centre has been asked what she wants. Those are the achievable outcomes of this week's work, and a therapist who promises more will be lying to a family that has enough disappointment already.

What would have changed the plan. If the medical review had confirmed progression, the meeting would have become a placement discussion and the four options would have been about care settings. If Bola had refused Funke's standing, the work would have proceeded with Funke alone on the question of what she is entitled to, which is a legitimate treatment in its own right. And if Mama had been unable to participate at all, the life review would have become a review with the family about her, which is a poor substitute and better than nothing.

12.7 Transcript: asking an eighty-one-year-old what she wants

Ten turns from the session in Part 7, with Funke present and Bola on speakerphone. Mama wears a hearing aid for the first time in the therapist's presence.

T1 Therapist: (facing her, at her level, unhurried) Mama, I want to ask you something that nobody has asked you. (pause) What would you like the next year to look like? — The question is put to her first, in the first person, seated at her level and facing her. Everything about the physical arrangement is the intervention. T2 Mama: (long pause) Nobody has asked me that. T3 Therapist: I know. That is why I am asking. T4 Mama: (pause) I would like to sit outside in the morning. (pause) In the compound. With the sun. — The answer is concrete, small, and entirely outside the family's frame of reference, which is what these answers are almost always like. T5 Funke: (quietly) She cannot go outside. There are steps. — The daughter-in-law's objection is practical and true, and it is the beginning of the intervention rather than a contradiction of it. T6 Therapist: (to Funke) Then that is the thing we are going to solve. (to Mama) Mama, if someone carried the chair out for you, would you want to sit there? T7 Mama: (nodding) In the morning. Before it is hot. T8 Therapist: (to Bola, on the speakerphone) Bola, did you hear that? — The question is asked of the absent authority, deliberately, because the decision will not survive unless she has heard it in Mama's voice. T9 Bola: (after a pause) I heard. T10 Therapist: (to the room) Then the first item on the rota is not the clinic and it is not the medication. It is the chair, in the morning, before it is hot. — The therapist writes it first on the page, above the medical items, which is a statement about what this meeting is for.

12.8 Laboratory: the caregiving network map

Aim. To map a caregiving network in fifteen minutes, identify the inverse distribution of work and authority, and produce one decision that the family can make in the room.

Procedure. Groups of five: therapist, older person, primary carer, distant decision-maker, and one absent sibling represented by an empty chair (which students find unexpectedly powerful). The trainer supplies a one-page brief with hours, decisions and one unassessed medical event. Twenty-five minutes, then feedback.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The mapVerbal description onlyDrawn, but without hours or decisionsDrawn with hours, decision markers, and "not consulted" named
The medical checkAccepted the diagnosis on the fileAsked about the diagnosisIdentified the unassessed event and wrote the question for the physician
The hearingNot consideredAsked about itTested it in the room and demonstrated the difference
StandingLeft the carer without authorityAcknowledged the carerGave the carer standing explicitly, in front of the others
The decisionNoneOne decision, no dateOne decision, dated, named, with a fallback

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the student to ask the older person a question and write the answer down. Groups reliably spend twenty minutes on the network and never speak to the patient, which reproduces in the laboratory the exact error the week is about.

12.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Accepting "dementia" as an explanation for a change. Any sudden change is delirium until proved otherwise, and it is a medical emergency. The error is common because the diagnosis is already on the file and because families present the change as deterioration.

Error 2 — Interpreting confusion without testing hearing. The cheapest test in this book and the most often skipped. A hearing loss produces social withdrawal, apparent paranoia, non-compliance and "difficulty", and every one of those is then treated as a symptom of dementia.

Error 3 — Meeting the family without the patient. The most common structural error in later-life work. A woman who is spoken over for ninety minutes will be described as difficult afterwards, and the description will be accurate.

Error 4 — Treating the daughter-in-law as a helper. She is frequently the most competent and least consulted member of the network, and a therapist who addresses the adult children and uses the carer as a source of information has reproduced the family's hierarchy rather than intervening in it.

Error 5 — Aiming at sibling reconciliation. The conflict is usually forty years old and is not the therapist's business. The aim is six weeks of concrete cooperation, which is achievable, and a therapist who aims at the former will lose the latter.

Error 6 — A plan with no fallback. Families implement plans that have a stated fallback and abandon plans that do not. The fallback is not pessimism; it is the condition under which the first plan survives its first bad week.

12.9a The phone call that is really a referral

A pattern worth naming separately, because it is how most later-life work actually begins.

The call comes from an adult daughter, usually in the evening, and it is framed as a request for information: "I just want to know whether we should be worried." Within three minutes the real request appears, and it is one of four.

"Tell me I am doing enough." The caller is asking for a verdict on her caregiving. The answer that works is behavioural rather than reassuring: "you are the one who telephoned. What are the three things you actually do each week?" Listing them is the intervention, because the caller has never counted.

"Tell me my brother should help." The caller wants an ally. The answer that avoids the trap: "I will not tell him that. I will ask him, in a meeting, what he can do — and I will ask you the same question." A therapist who supplies the ally is then blamed when the brother does not change.

"Tell me it is time for a home." The caller wants permission. Permission is not the therapist's to give, and the honest answer is the structure of the decision: "that is a decision for your family, and I can help you make it in a way you can all live with. What would have to be true for you to consider it?"

"Tell me she is not dying." The caller wants a prediction. Give the medical picture as far as it is known, name what is not known, and offer the one thing that can be offered: a plan for the next month rather than a prognosis for the next year.

The practical point. The first telephone call is the assessment and the engagement, and it is usually taken by the least experienced person in the service. The four framings above should be taught to whoever answers the telephone, because a call that is handled as a request for information and ends with "make an appointment" has lost the family's most motivated member.

12.10 Culture, faith and later life

Who cares for the elderly, and who is expected to. In most of the households this book is written for, care is a family obligation rather than a purchased service, and the obligation falls on a daughter-in-law or a daughter rather than on a son. The clinical consequence is that the person doing the work frequently has no authority, and the person with authority frequently does none of the work. This is the week's central finding, and it is a structural fact rather than a family pathology.

Respect and the impossibility of asking. In many households it is improper for a younger person to ask an older one what she wants, and the impropriety is felt by the family as a failure of respect rather than as an omission. The therapist can use the frame rather than fight it: "it is because we respect her that we are asking her" is a sentence that permits the question. Where the older person cannot be asked, the therapist asks the family to describe what she would say, which is a second-best procedure and is better than nothing.

Placement and its meanings. A care home carries, in many settings, the meaning of abandonment, and a family that is offered placement as an option may hear an accusation. The therapist's discipline is to keep four options on the page without recommending any, because the family's choice must be one the family can live with in front of its relatives, not one the therapist would make.

Faith, illness and the meaning of decline. Households may understand dementia as a spiritual trial, a punishment, or a season, and the understanding organises how much medical investigation is pursued. The clinical position: ask what the framework means for the family, and hold one non-negotiable — that a treatable infection should be treated. Framing the medical review as stewardship rather than as doubt is translation, not manipulation.

Mourning as a collective practice. Where grief is prescribed — forty days of visits, a year of black, a particular liturgy — the therapist's task is to support the rites and to watch for the period after them, when the company leaves and the bereavement becomes private. The dual-process model describes an individual oscillation; the culture supplies a collective one, and the clinical question is what happens when the collective one ends.

DEBATE — "The person who does the caring should make the decisions." Argue for: authority should follow knowledge and work, the distant decision-maker is making choices about a life they do not see, and the current arrangement is exploitative. Argue against: decisions about money require the person who has the money, medical decisions require medical knowledge, and a daughter-in-law with authority and no kinship has no standing to defend it against the family. Then state what you would actually have done in the Balogun meeting, and what you would have done if Bola had refused.

12.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. Structured life review and reminiscence reduce depressive symptoms in older adults, with modest but consistent effects. Caregiver burden interventions that combine psychoeducation with practical respite produce better outcomes than either alone, and the effect is larger when the carer is given specific tasks rather than general support. Hearing loss is independently associated with cognitive decline, social isolation and depression, and correction improves social participation. Delirium is common, frequently missed, and associated with poor outcomes, particularly when it is misattributed to dementia.

What is weaker. The evidence on family meetings is largely from medical and palliative settings and transfers imperfectly to a therapy clinic. The dual-process model is well supported descriptively and less well supported as a treatment target. Effects of grandparent involvement on child outcomes are confounded by selection and are difficult to interpret.

What is not supported. That "working through" grief requires the discharge of emotion — the evidence favours oscillation and continued bonds over severance. That cognitive decline is inevitable and untreatable in every case, which is the assumption behind the most damaging errors in this week's work. And that carers who are not relatives are less appropriate; the evidence on non-kin caregiving is thin and the clinical experience is that they are frequently the most reliable member of the network.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same dementia diagnosis produces, in one family, a coordinated network and, in another, a daughter-in-law doing forty hours a week in silence, and the difference is not in the diagnosis.

12.12 Key terms

Integrity versus despair — Erikson's final stage; the consolidation of a life into an account that can be held.

Delirium — an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness, usually caused by a treatable physical problem; a medical emergency.

Pseudodementia — the presentation of depression as apparent cognitive decline; treatable.

Dual-process model — the account of grief as oscillation between loss-oriented and restoration-oriented coping.

Caregiver burden — the objective and subjective cost of caregiving; measured, and frequently normalised by the family.

Unstated contract — the four unspoken clauses of a caregiving arrangement: responsibility, standard, entitlement, and the consequence of refusal.

Responsible child — the sibling appointed by proximity, gender or birth order, rarely by negotiation.

Life review — a structured reminiscence intervention aimed at coherence rather than discharge.

Legacy — what is handed on, and to whom; the developmental task that requires an audience.

Fallback — the pre-agreed response if the plan fails; the condition under which plans survive.

12.13 Self-test

  1. Give the four-way cognitive differential and the one question that best distinguishes each pair.
  2. Why is a change over two days a medical emergency, and what has usually happened when a family is told that "the dementia is progressing"?
  3. What is the inverse distribution, and why does it produce the referral?
  4. Name the four clauses of the unstated caregiving contract.
  5. Why does the therapist write the chair above the medical items on the rota?
  6. What are the four sibling patterns, and what is the move for the daughter-in-law?
  7. Why is sibling reconciliation the wrong aim, and what is the right one?
  8. In the Balogun case, what was the single most important fact, and how was it found?

Answers. (1) Delirium — "was he like this yesterday?"; depression — "when did you last enjoy something?"; dementia — "when did you last manage the money alone?"; hearing loss — "does he answer better when he can see your face?" (2) Because an acute change with fluctuation is the signature of delirium, usually from infection, constipation, dehydration, medication or pain, all treatable; the family has usually been given a diagnosis on the basis of a five-day event and has organised months of decisions around it. (3) The workload sits with the person who has least authority and the authority with those who do least work; the referral arrives when the worker's capacity fails. (4) Who is responsible; what counts as good care; what the carer is entitled to; what happens if the carer refuses. (5) Because the meeting is about Mama's life rather than her medication, and the order on the page is a statement about what the work is for. (6) The responsible child and the absent ones; the child who is not a child; placement versus refusal; the siblings not speaking — the move for the non-kin carer is to give them standing explicitly, in the meeting, by the family's agreement. (7) Because the conflict is decades old and is not the therapist's business; the right aim is six weeks of concrete cooperation on one practical matter. (8) That the three-month-old "progression" was probably a delirium — found by dating the change and asking what had been done about it.

12.14 Reflective task

Ask one person over seventy the six life review questions, and record the answers if they agree. Then answer three questions of your own: which question produced the most, which produced the least, and what did you have to stop yourself from doing while they spoke? Most students report that they wanted to reassure, to interpret, or to fill a silence, and that the silence was where the material was.

12.15 Further reading

  • Erikson, E., Erikson, J. and Kivnick, H. Vital Involvement in Old Age — the empirical follow-up to the final stage.
  • Stroebe, M. and Schut, H. "The dual-process model of coping with bereavement" — the source, and the evidence.
  • Zarit, S. et al. on caregiver burden — the instrument, and its limits in the room.
  • Butler, R. "The life review" — the original paper, still the best description of the task.
  • Livingston, G. et al. on dementia care and carer interventions — the best current summary of what works.

Next: Week 13 — Culture, poverty and development: the critique of the norms.

Week 13 — Culture, poverty and development: the critique of the norms

Development is not culture-free. Every norm in the preceding twelve weeks was measured somewhere, on somebody, and the somewhere and the somebody matter. This week supplies the critique, and the critique is not an optional add-on for students with an interest in culture; it is a correction to the instrument they have been learning to use.

The four critiques you must be able to state.

  1. The norms are Western. Attachment classifications, individuation timetables and "school readiness" were normed on particular populations. A child who sleeps in the parental bed until five is not necessarily enmeshed; in many cultures this is ordinary.
  2. Poverty changes development. Chronic stress, food insecurity, crowding and insecure housing affect attention, sleep, and the capacity for consistent parenting. Diagnosing "inconsistent parenting" without naming material conditions is a category error.
  3. Extended and distributed caregiving is normal. In much of the world, children are raised by networks — grandparents, aunts, older siblings, co-wives, foster kin. Attachment theory travels better when it is asked "who does this child go to when she is frightened?" rather than "is she securely attached to her mother?"
  4. The measurement is part of the finding. A test administered in a second language, by a stranger, in a clinic, to a child who has been told to be quiet, measures the test situation as well as the child. This is not a reason to abandon measurement; it is a reason to interpret it with the conditions attached.

Why this week is placed here and not earlier. Because a critique of the norms is only usable by someone who knows the norms. A student who has not spent twelve weeks learning what typical development looks like cannot evaluate the claim that the norms are parochial; they can only repeat it. By this point in the course the student has the instrument, and this week teaches them what it does not measure.

What the week is not. It is not a claim that development is entirely relative, that milestones are meaningless, or that any practice is defensible because it is cultural. Children die from practices that are cultural, and the therapist who cannot say so has abandoned the child. The position is narrower and harder: the norms describe one pathway in detail, other pathways exist and are not deficient, and the clinical question is always what this child, in this household, with these resources, needs in order to develop.

— A four-year-old sleeps in her parents' bed in a two-room flat shared with her father's brother. The nursery nurse who referred her has written "over-attached, does not self-soothe". The family is offended and has come only because the school insisted. Assessment finds: the child plays richly, separates without distress, has three adults who respond to her, and has no bedroom. The referral's central "finding" is a description of the housing.

13.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The problemWhy a norm is a measurement plus a population, and what happens when the population is missingWrite the population behind one milestone they know
8–22The Western normsAttachment, individuation, readiness — what was measured, on whom, and by whomInterrogate one classification system
22–36Poverty as a developmental variableThe five mechanisms, and the evidence for eachTrace one mechanism through a case they have seen
36–48Distributed caregivingThe network as the unit; the "who does she go to?" questionMap a child's caregiving network from a description
48–60The measurement problemLanguage, stranger, setting, instruction — four contaminantsDesign a fair assessment for a named child
60–72The interpreterWorking with a third person in the room, and the four rulesPractise a three-way conversation
72–82When to hold the lineThe practices a therapist must not relativise, and how to say soArgue one case both ways and then decide
82–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The risk in a week on culture is that it becomes either a sermon on sensitivity or a defence of the discipline. The lecture avoids both by making the class do measurement: give them a milestone chart and ask them to write down the population it was normed on. They cannot, and the inability is the lesson. The instrument they have been using all term is precise and its precision has a location.

The sentence the lecture must land. "Every norm is somebody's average. Your job is to know whose, and then to decide what this child needs."

13.2 The four critiques worked

1. The norms are Western, and more specifically they are from a handful of studies. The Strange Situation was developed in Baltimore in the 1970s on a sample of about a hundred infants; its classifications have been replicated in dozens of cultures, which is a genuine strength, and the distribution of classifications varies in ways that are informative. In several Northern German samples, avoidant classification was more common; in several Japanese samples, resistant. Neither distribution indicated a population of insecure children; it indicated a population of caregiving practices. The classification is a description of a relationship under a particular stressor, not a score of maternal quality.

The same applies to individuation. The timetable in which a child sleeps alone, weans early, and separates for nursery at eighteen months is a description of a particular household economy. A child who sleeps with her mother until five and is carried until three is not delayed; she is participating in a practice with its own developmental logic, and the evidence on later independence does not support the claim that early separation produces it.

2. Poverty is a developmental variable with mechanisms, not a background fact. The mechanisms are five, and each is worth knowing because each is addressable.

  • Chronic stress physiology. Poverty is associated with elevated cortisol and with alterations in the developing stress response, which affects attention, sleep and emotional regulation. The mechanism is biological and the cause is material.
  • Food insecurity. Both under-nutrition and the unpredictability of food affect growth, attention and behaviour. A child who has not eaten since the previous evening is not in a condition to learn, and the behaviour that results is routinely read as a disorder.
  • Crowding. Sleep disruption, no space for homework, no privacy for an adolescent, and no quiet for a child with a hearing loss. Crowding is measurable and is almost never measured.
  • Housing instability. Moves disrupt schooling, friendships and the continuity of care, and the disruption is frequently read as the child's difficulty with change.
  • Parental bandwidth. A parent managing money, transport, water, safety and a job has less attention for the ordinary work of parenting, not because they care less but because attention is finite. This is the finding that most changes a clinician's practice, because it converts "inconsistent parenting" from a character judgement into a resource calculation.

3. Distributed caregiving is the norm in most of the world. A child may be raised by a grandmother during the week and a mother at weekends, by an aunt in the city and parents in the village, by an older sister for most of the day. The clinical task is not to assess whether the arrangement is optimal by a Western standard; it is to ask whether the child has at least one adult who is reliably available and responsive, and whether the adults are coordinated.

The question that replaces the Strange Situation in practice: "when she is frightened or hurt, who does she go to?" Followed by: "and does that person know that they are the one?" The second question is the one that finds the problem, which is usually a coordination problem rather than a deficit of care.

4. The measurement is part of the finding. Four contaminants, all of which the therapist can control.

  • Language. A test in a second language measures proficiency, not ability. Assess in the stronger language, or use a nonverbal instrument, or bring an interpreter — and record which.
  • The stranger. A child who has been taught not to speak to unfamiliar adults will perform poorly with a clinician and well at home. Observe in the home, or with the caregiver present, or both.
  • The setting. A clinic room with a stranger and a parent who is anxious is a particular social situation, and the child's behaviour in it is a response to that situation.
  • The instruction. A child who has been told that children should be seen and not heard may answer "I do not know" to every question, accurately, as a matter of manners.

The correction that follows. Report the conditions with the finding. "Receptive language age equivalent three years six months, assessed in English, second language, with mother present, in clinic" is a substantially different statement from "language delay", and the difference is the whole of this week.

13.2a When the norms are right and the family is wrong

The week's hardest teaching point, and the one that separates a culturally competent clinician from a relativist.

The four categories. A practice may be different and equivalent (co-sleeping, early weaning, grandparent care), different and better (a caregiving network that gives a child four adults instead of two), different and harmful (female genital cutting, corporal punishment to the point of injury, withholding food as discipline, a child kept out of school to work), or not a practice at all but a consequence (crowding, hunger, an untreated infection).

The therapist's obligation in the third category. Name it, once, plainly, and then act according to the law and the safeguarding framework. The order matters: name it to the parents first, in the family's own terms, before any referral. A parent who learns from a social worker what the therapist thought has been humiliated, and the humiliation will be remembered by the whole extended family.

How to name it without a lecture. Three sentences. First, the practice, described factually: "you told me that when he is disobedient he is beaten with a cable." Second, the concern, in developmental terms: "I am worried about his body and about what it teaches him about being hit by people who love him." Third, the position: "I am not able to work with a plan that includes that, and I would like to find something else that works."

What not to do. Do not relativise harm ("in your culture that is normal"), which abandons the child. Do not moralise ("that is child abuse"), which ends the relationship before any change is possible. Do not report without telling the parents, which is sometimes legally required and is always clinically costly and must be explained as it happens.

The position in one sentence. Culture explains a practice; it does not decide whether the practice is good for this child. The therapist's obligation is to hold both halves of that sentence at once, which is what makes the work difficult and what makes it worth doing.

13.2b Three network configurations and what each needs

Distributed caregiving is not one arrangement. Three configurations account for most of the households in these clinics, and each has a different clinical question.

Configuration 1 — The grandmother as primary carer. The mother works, the grandmother raises the child from six months, and the mother resumes the primary role at school age. The child is well cared for and attached, and the transition at school age is the clinical event. The presenting difficulty is usually "he does not listen to me" — addressed to the mother, by a child who has had three years of a different authority. The question is not whether the arrangement was right; it is how the transfer of primary authority is conducted, and it is conducted best when it is explicit and gradual, with the grandmother's status preserved rather than retired.

Configuration 2 — The circulating child. A child who moves between households — school terms with an aunt in the city, holidays in the village, a period with a godmother. This arrangement is common, is often economically rational, and produces a specific clinical picture: a child who is competent, adaptable, and reluctant to attach quickly, and who is described by adults as "he does not settle". The clinical question is not whether circulation is harmful — the evidence is mixed and depends almost entirely on whether the moves were explained and whether one adult remained constant — but which adult has been constant, and whether the child knows that the constancy is intended to continue.

Configuration 3 — The compound. Several related households in one space, with children moving between them freely and discipline distributed across adults. The child has many caregivers and no privacy, and the developmental benefits are real: language exposure, multiple attachment figures, early social competence. The clinical difficulty is coordination: a child with a hearing loss in a compound is spoken to by eleven people in four languages and is described as "confused", and no single adult has the authority to change the arrangement.

The question for all three. "Who is the person this child would go to, and does that person know?" In configuration 1 the answer is usually the grandmother, and the mother does not know it. In configuration 2 the answer is usually an aunt, and the parents assume it is themselves. In configuration 3 the answer is frequently an older sibling, which nobody has noticed.

13.3 The method: a developmental cultural formulation

Six questions, adapted from the standard cultural formulation for use with a child and a family. Twenty minutes, and the answers go in the record because they change the interpretation of everything else.

Question 1 — Who is in this child's world, and what does each person do? Not the household roster. The functions: who feeds, who puts to bed, who takes to the clinic, who disciplines, who plays, who is trusted with a secret at eleven years old. The answer is the network, and it is frequently larger than the referral suggests.

Question 2 — What is this family's account of the problem, and what does it predict? Families hold theories — spiritual, developmental, characterological, medical, or a mixture — and the theory predicts what they will do. A family that believes the problem is spiritual will pursue prayer; a family that believes it is character will pursue discipline. The therapist who does not ask will recommend something the family cannot use.

Question 3 — What has the family already tried, and what did it cost them? The answer is the family's competence and its budget. A family that has spent four months' income on a treatment that failed is not unmotivated; it is exhausted and poorer.

Question 4 — What are the material conditions? Income, food, housing, water, transport, crowding, the number of people per room, the distance to the clinic. Asked plainly, as part of the history, not as a delicate enquiry. Families answer these questions readily when they are asked as facts rather than as suspicions.

Question 5 — Who has the authority to decide, and who has the authority to be believed? These are frequently different people. A grandmother may decide and a pastor may be believed. The plan must be addressed to both.

Question 6 — What does this family want for this child, in ten years? The question produces the family's own developmental goal, which is often more ambitious than the clinic's and always more motivating. It also frequently resolves an apparent cultural conflict: a family that wants its daughter to be a doctor will accept a good deal of developmental advice about her schooling, provided the advice is connected to that goal.

How to use the six. As the frame for a first session with any family whose background differs from the therapist's — and, since the therapist's own background is always particular, as a frame worth using with every family.

13.3a The interpreter

Many of the assessments in this week's work are conducted through a third person, and the third person changes the data.

Rule 1 — Brief the interpreter before the family arrives. Five minutes: what the assessment is for, what you will ask, and the two things you need — first person interpretation ("she says she cannot sleep", not "she says she cannot sleep and I think she is depressed") and no summarising. An interpreter who has not been briefed will summarise, edit, and add opinions, and all three are invisible in the transcript.

Rule 2 — Speak to the family, not to the interpreter. "How long has she not been sleeping?" is asked of the mother, with the eyes on the mother. The commonest error is the triangle in which the therapist and the interpreter talk to each other about a family sitting in the room.

Rule 3 — Build in double the time. Everything takes twice as long, and the assessment that is scheduled for forty minutes will take eighty. A therapist who runs out of time will cut the questions that are hardest to ask, which are the ones that matter.

Rule 4 — Do not use a family member. Not a child, not a cousin, not a neighbour. The information is distorted by loyalty, the family member learns things they should not, and the roles in the household are permanently altered. Where no interpreter is available, reschedule. This is the one rule in the section that should never be broken.

The fifth rule, which is not a rule but a caution. An interpreter from the same community may be known to the family, and the family may withhold material for that reason — a stigma, a relative, a dispute. Ask, at the start, whether they are comfortable, and offer a telephone interpreter from elsewhere if they are not.

13.3b The sentence that opens the conversation

One practical matter that determines whether the rest of the week's method is usable.

Families are frequently asked, by clinics, to explain their culture — "tell me about your customs" — and the request produces either a performance or silence, because nobody describes their own ordinary life as a custom. The question that works asks about a specific recent event and lets the practice appear inside the answer.

The four openings.

  • "Tell me about yesterday. Who was with her from morning until she slept?" — produces the network and the practices in one answer.
  • "When she was last ill, who decided what to do?" — produces the hierarchy of authority and the explanatory model.
  • "When she does something you do not want her to do, what happens in your house?" — produces the discipline practice without asking for it.
  • "What do you want for her that your parents did not have, and what would have to be different in your house for her to have it?" — produces the family's own developmental goal, and it is almost always the most useful sentence in the session, because it is the one that makes the family's own goal available to the plan.

What these four have in common. They ask about events rather than about identity. A family asked to describe its culture must decide what the therapist wants and then supply it; a family asked what happened yesterday simply reports, and the culture arrives in the report, where it can be worked with.

The mistake to avoid. Do not say "I understand your culture" — a claim no outsider can make and every family hears as a claim to authority. Say instead what you noticed: "in your house three people look after her and they all have a view. In mine it was one. Tell me how that works for you, and what you would change about it if you could." That sentence gives the family something to correct, and being corrected is how a therapist learns, which is a slower and more reliable method than being told about a culture by a textbook.

13.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The network map of a child. Draw the child in the centre, and around her every adult and older child who does something for her, with the function written next to each name. Then mark the one person she goes to when frightened. Then mark the person each of the others believes she goes to. The discrepancy between the last two markings is the finding, and it is frequently the whole of the case.

Technique 2 — The resource inventory. A list of what the household has, not what it lacks: adults, space, income, a church or mosque community, a neighbour, a skill, a relative in another city, a school with a good teacher. The inventory is written with the family, and it is read back to them. Its effect is not sentimental; it changes what the family believes is possible, which changes what they will attempt.

Technique 3 — The two-column budget. One column: what the plan requires in time and money. The other: what the household has. The technique prevents the therapist from writing plans that require a car, a morning off work, or a fee. It is unglamorous and it is the reason some plans are carried out.

13.4a The assessment kit for a household with nothing

A practical inventory of what a therapist can do when the household has no money, no car, no quiet room and no time.

What costs nothing and is worth doing. A hearing screen with a whispered instruction from behind the child, at a metre, in a quiet moment. A language sample — five minutes of the child talking about anything, recorded on a telephone, transcribed roughly, and counted for mean length of utterance by hand. A drawing. A three-step instruction, given in the corridor. An observation of the child with the person she goes to. An observation of the child with the person she does not go to. None of these requires a kit, a fee, or a second appointment.

What the clinic should supply because the family cannot. A referral letter typed and signed in the session, so that the family leaves with it rather than waiting for a post that does not arrive. A telephone number that is answered. A note for the school. A copy of the assessment in a form the family can keep.

What not to ask for. Do not ask a family to return on a day they cannot manage, to bring a document that does not exist, or to obtain a report from a school that is not answering. These requests are made in good faith and they are the mechanism by which families are lost: each one is a small obstacle, and the cumulative effect on a household operating at capacity is a decision not to come back.

The design principle. Every element of the assessment should be deliverable in one visit, by one person, with no equipment, and end with the family holding a piece of paper. That constraint produces a shorter assessment than the textbook describes and a more useful one, because it is completed.

13.4b The four-column plan

The plan for a household under material constraint is written in four columns, and the fourth is the one that keeps it alive.

Column 1 — What we are trying to change. One sentence. Not a list.

Column 2 — What it requires. In time, money, transport and space. Written honestly, including the invisible costs: a morning off work, a fare, a person to mind the other children.

Column 3 — What the household has. From the resource inventory. The column is frequently fuller than the family expected, which is part of its purpose.

Column 4 — What we drop if column 2 exceeds column 3. The reduction, specified in advance. Most plans fail because they were designed at full capacity and nobody decided what to cut when capacity fell. A plan with a stated reduction survives; a plan without one is abandoned whole.

The example. Column 1: "we want him to be understood at school." Column 2: a hearing test, an audiology appointment across the city, a school letter, and ten minutes a day of reading aloud. Column 3: the mother's mornings are free on Tuesdays; there is a bus; the school has a teacher who has been helpful. Column 4: if the audiology appointment cannot be reached, the school letter and the ten minutes continue and the appointment is rebooked; if the ten minutes cannot be managed, the school letter continues alone.

What the column prevents. The all-or-nothing plan, which is the characteristic failure of clinicians working with families in difficulty, and which is experienced by the family as another expectation they could not meet.

13.5 The first session when the family and the therapist disagree

Phase 1 — Ask for the family's account before offering any. The six questions, and particularly question two. A therapist who offers a developmental account in the first ten minutes has produced a competition between two theories, and the family will defend theirs.

Phase 2 — Find the point of agreement. It is almost always the goal: the family wants the child to do well at school, to be safe, to be respected. State it: "we want the same thing for her."

Phase 3 — Offer the second language, not a replacement. "Your grandmother is right that a child needs to be taught respect. I would add something from my side, which is that a child who is shouted at every morning finds it harder to concentrate at school, and concentration is what she needs for the exam." The move is additive: the family keeps its framework and gains a mechanism.

Phase 4 — Say clearly where you cannot go. If a practice is harmful, name it once, as in 13.2a, and hold the line. Ambiguity here is worse than refusal, because the family will hear permission.

Phase 5 — Agree one thing and a review. As in Week 11. One action, dated, with the exception named.

What to do when the family does not return. Assume the plan was unusable rather than that the family was unmotivated, and ask. A telephone call that says "I have been thinking about what we agreed and I wonder whether it was possible in your house" recovers a substantial minority of families who have dropped out, and no service does it.

13.5a When the therapist's own culture is the variable

The week's critique applies to the clinician, and the application is uncomfortable.

What the therapist brings. A household of origin with its own practices — sleeping arrangements, discipline, the meaning of respect, the proper distance between a mother and a grown son, the age at which a child is left alone. A training in a discipline that was normed somewhere. And an institution's assumptions, which are frequently the assumptions of the profession's country of origin rather than of the clinic's neighbourhood.

The three ways this shows up.

As an unexamined standard. The therapist who is irritated by a family and cannot say why is usually meeting a practice that violates a rule she has never stated. The correction is to state the rule: "I notice I am uncomfortable that the eight-year-old is still sleeping in the room with his parents. That is my household's rule, not a finding. What is the reason in yours?"

As a class position. A therapist from a professional household assessing a family in poverty may misread scarcity as disorganisation, or may over-correct and fail to name a real problem. Both errors come from the same place and both are corrected by asking about the material facts rather than inferring them.

As an alliance with the institution. The therapist is employed by a clinic, often by a school, sometimes by a court. Her assumptions drift toward her employer's, and the drift is invisible from inside. The correction is to ask, once per case, "whose interests does my formulation serve?" and to write the answer in the record.

The discipline that holds all three. State your own rule before you interpret the family's. The sentence "I was brought up to think X, and you are describing Y — tell me about Y" is not a confession; it is the technique. It removes the therapist's rule from the position of an unstated standard and puts it on the table where the family can examine it, which is the only position from which a family can disagree with a professional without feeling judged.

13.6 Case: the child with no bedroom

Referral. Morenike, four years and three months, referred by the nursery attached to a private school. The written note: "over-attached to mother, does not self-soothe, still in parents' bed, will not sleep alone. Family has been advised to implement a sleep programme."

The family. Yetunde, 29, a hairdresser who works from the flat. Bayo, 32, a dispatch rider. Morenike, 4. Bayo's younger brother, 24, a student, who sleeps in the sitting room. Total: four people in a flat of two rooms and a sitting room, in a compound of six flats. Languages: Yoruba at home, English at nursery.

Why they came. "The school said they will not promote her to the next class if she is not sleeping alone." Yetunde's tone, in the first minute: "they have told me I am spoiling her. My mother says the same. I want to hear it from a doctor."

What the assessment finds. Morenike plays richly, with a narrative and with other children; she separates from her mother at nursery without distress and greets her warmly on reunion; she has a vocabulary above age in Yoruba and age-appropriate in English; her hearing is normal; her sleep is unremarkable apart from its location — she goes to sleep at nine and wakes at six. When frightened, she goes to her mother. Her mother knows this. Her father believes she goes to her grandmother, who lives two compounds away.

The five findings.

1. There is no evidence of a sleep disorder and there is no spare room. The family occupies two rooms: one for Yetunde and Bayo, one for Morenike and her cousin when the cousin stays. The uncle sleeps in the sitting room. "Sleeping alone" would mean sleeping in a room with a twenty-four-year-old man, which the parents correctly regard as unacceptable. The clinic's recommendation was not a developmental intervention; it was a recommendation to rearrange a household that has no space, made by someone who did not ask.

2. The referral is a description of the housing written in the language of attachment. "Does not self-soothe" describes a child who has never been alone in a room. "Over-attached" describes a child in a two-room flat. Neither phrase is false and both are diagnoses of a circumstance.

3. The child's attachment behaviour is unremarkable. She separates, she explores, she returns. The Strange Situation, if it had been administered, would have had to be administered in a room she had never seen with a stranger she had never met, and her behaviour in that room would have described the room.

4. There is a real question, and it is about the school's requirement. The school's promotion criterion is being used as a developmental standard, and it is not one. The therapist's task is not to adjudicate between the family and the school on a matter of principle; it is to give the family a position and a sentence they can take to the school.

5. The family's own goal is specific and achievable. They want her to sleep in her own bed by the time she starts Primary 1, in fourteen months, so that the uncle can move out when he finishes his studies and the room can be rearranged. They have a plan and it is better than the clinic's.

The formulation. A well-developing four-year-old in a household with four people, two rooms and no spare bed, referred by an institution that has mistaken a housing constraint for a developmental deficit, and whose mother has been told by three authorities that she is spoiling her child. The intervention is three sentences to a school, one piece of information to a mother, and a plan that begins in fourteen months.

The intervention, in five parts.

Part 1 — Say the finding in one sentence, and say it early. "There is nothing wrong with your daughter's development, and there is nowhere in your flat for her to sleep alone. Those are two different things and the school has joined them together."

Part 2 — Give the mother the developmental fact she can use. "A four-year-old who sleeps in her parents' room in a two-room flat is doing what children in most of the world do. What matters for her development is that she is responded to when she calls, and she is."

Part 3 — The letter to the school, written in the session. Three paragraphs. It states that the child has been assessed, that no developmental concern was found, that the family's sleeping arrangement reflects their housing and not their parenting, that the assessment found no basis for a sleep programme, and that the clinic will review the arrangement at a stated date. It is signed by the therapist and copied to the parents. The therapist reads it aloud before anyone leaves.

Part 4 — A plan for fourteen months, in the family's own shape. A bed for Morenike, bought second-hand, in the room she shares; a routine at nine that is hers; and a target — by the time the uncle finishes, she sleeps in her own bed in the room, and the room is rearranged. Written on a page, with the date.

Part 5 — One sentence for the grandmother. "She is not spoiled; she is four, and we have no room." The sentence is rehearsed, because the grandmother's opinion is the one that reaches the mother every day and the mother needs something to say that is not a defence of herself.

13.6a Five months later

The letter to the school was effective in a way the therapist had not anticipated: the school removed the promotion condition and asked the clinic to speak to the nursery staff, which the therapist did, for forty minutes, on the difference between a developmental assessment and a housing description. Nine children in that class stopped being on a sleep programme.

Morenike has a bed. It is in the same room as her mother's, at right angles, and she sleeps in it most nights. The target date is still fourteen months away and the plan is ahead of schedule, which the family reports with pride.

Yetunde has stopped apologising in the first minute of a conversation, which the therapist regards as the case's most significant outcome and which is not measurable by any instrument in this book.

The honest limitation. The uncle still sleeps in the sitting room and the flat is still two rooms. The therapist has not changed the material condition and does not claim to have done so. What she has changed is that a family is no longer being told that its housing is a parenting failure, and that an institution has been corrected, which affects every family that institution sees afterwards.

13.7 Transcript: the letter, read aloud

Nine turns from the third session. The therapist has drafted the letter in front of the parents and is reading it back to them before it is sent.

T1 Therapist: (reading) "To the Head of Nursery. I am writing about Morenike, whom I assessed on the fourteenth. I found no developmental concern. Her play, her language, her separation and her relationships are all appropriate for her age." (pause) Is that right? — The check after the first paragraph. The letter is a clinical document and the family is the authority on its accuracy. T2 Yetunde: It is right. T3 Therapist: (reading) "The family's sleeping arrangement reflects the size of their home. It is not a sign of developmental difficulty, and I have not recommended a sleep programme." (pause) Yetunde, when you read that, what will you feel? — The question is asked before the letter is sent, because a letter the parent cannot bear to read is a letter that will not be used. T4 Yetunde: (quietly) That somebody said it. T5 Therapist: (reading) "I would be glad to speak to the nursery staff about the difference between a developmental assessment and a description of a family's housing." (pause) That last sentence is a criticism of the school. Do you want it in? — The choice is offered deliberately. A letter that criticises an institution on a family's behalf, without the family's agreement, converts the therapist into an advocate and the family into a party to a dispute they did not choose. T6 Bayo: (for the first time in the session) Leave it in. T7 Therapist: Then I will send it today and I will copy you both. (pause) And one more thing. (to Yetunde) Your mother will hear about this letter from the school, not from you. What will you say to her? — The household rehearsal from Week 11, applied. The plan will be tested at home before it is tested at the school. T8 Yetunde: (after a pause) That the doctor said she is not spoiled. That we have no room. T9 Therapist: Say it again, so that you have said it once before you say it to her.

13.8 Laboratory: assessing across a difference

Aim. To complete a developmental assessment with a family whose language, housing and explanatory model differ from the therapist's, and to report the finding with its conditions attached.

Procedure. Groups of four: therapist, mother, interpreter, observer. The trainer supplies a brief in which the family speaks mainly Yoruba, has no word for "self-soothe", believes the child's night waking is caused by a neighbour's envy, and has four people in two rooms. The therapist may use the interpreter and must produce a one-paragraph written finding in ten minutes at the end.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The six questionsTwo or fewer askedFour askedAll six, in order, with the answers in the family's words
The interpreterTreated as a participant; spoke to them not the familyUsed correctly but not briefedBriefed, addressed the family, first-person, no summarising
The material factsNot askedAsked, and not usedAsked, used, and reflected in the plan
The explanatory modelIgnored, or correctedAcknowledgedAcknowledged, incorporated, and one mechanism added alongside
The written finding"Language delay, insecure attachment"A description without conditionsA finding with the language, setting and conditions stated

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the written finding to state the conditions of the measurement. "Receptive language age equivalent three years, assessed in English as a second language, with the mother present, in a clinic room" is the standard; "language delayed" is a failure of the exercise however accurate the number.

13.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Treating a housing description as a developmental finding. The week's central error. "Does not self-soothe" and "no bedroom" are the same observation written by two different professions.

Error 2 — Relativism as kindness. "In your culture that is normal" is said to avoid a difficult conversation and it abandons the child. Culture explains a practice; it does not settle whether the practice is good for this child.

Error 3 — Importing the norms without their population. Every instrument in the course was normed somewhere. Using it elsewhere is legitimate; using it elsewhere as though it were normed there is not.

Error 4 — Ignoring material conditions in the formulation. A plan that requires a car, a morning off, a fee or a spare room will fail, and the family will be recorded as unmotivated. The two-column budget exists to prevent this.

Error 5 — Using a family member as interpreter. It distorts the information, alters the household's roles, and cannot be undone. Reschedule instead.

Error 6 — Fighting the institution in front of the family. The letter in the case is a clinical instrument; a therapist who becomes the family's advocate against a school has taken a side in a dispute she will not be present to finish, and the family will carry the consequences.

13.10 Culture, faith and the limits of the critique

The four critiques in this week do not license a fifth, which would be: nothing can be known. The position this course takes is stated here so that students can hold it under pressure.

What is universal enough to rely on. Children need at least one reliably responsive adult; they need food, sleep and safety; they need language input from people who speak to them; they need to be protected from violence; and they develop through play with other children. These are not Western values; they are findings that hold across the ethnographic record, and a therapist who cannot assert them cannot protect a child.

What varies and must not be pathologised. Sleeping arrangements, weaning, who provides care, the age of school entry, the amount of autonomy given to a child, the manner of discipline within limits, the expression of emotion, the meaning of a disability, the conduct of mourning, and the relationship between the family and institutions.

The test that distinguishes them. Ask of any practice: does it protect or damage the child's development — her body, her language, her capacity to relate, her access to learning? If it damages, it is not protected by culture. If it does not, the therapist has no business treating it as a problem, whatever her own household did.

Faith and the explanatory model. A family's spiritual account of a child's difficulty is not a competitor to the assessment and does not need to be displaced. It needs to be joined, and one mechanism added alongside. The pastor who is told that the clinic is not an enemy becomes an ally who can say, from the pulpit, that a hearing test is not a lack of faith — and that sentence reaches more families than the clinic will see in a year.

DEBATE — "A developmental assessment should not be conducted across a language barrier at all." Argue for: the instrument measures the language, the conditions invalidate the norm, and a wrong number is worse than no number because it becomes a label. Argue against: no assessment means no service, waiting lists are a form of harm, and a properly-reported assessment with its conditions stated is more useful than nothing. Then state what you would actually do for a five-year-old who speaks only Hausa in a clinic where nobody does, and what you would write in the record.

13.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The cross-cultural replication of attachment classifications is substantial: the categories appear in every studied population, and the distribution varies with caregiving practice, which supports the theory's universality and undermines any single distribution being treated as a standard. The evidence on poverty as a developmental variable is among the strongest in the field, with the mechanisms — stress physiology, nutrition, crowding, instability — demonstrated across cohorts. The association between hearing loss and apparent cognitive and social difficulty is robust and correction improves outcomes.

What is weaker. Norms for most developmental instruments are drawn from samples that are unrepresentative of the populations in which the instruments are used, and the published corrections are sparse. The evidence on culturally adapted parenting interventions is growing and remains thinner than the evidence for the unadapted versions, which is a problem of research funding rather than of principle.

What is not supported. That early independence training produces later independence. That co-sleeping produces enmeshment. That a child raised by a network is at risk by virtue of the network. And that cultural sensitivity requires suspending judgement — the evidence on child maltreatment across cultures is clear enough that a therapist who cannot name harm is not sensitive but negligent.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same sleeping arrangement is unremarkable in one household and a safeguarding concern in another, and what distinguishes them is not the arrangement but the responsiveness of the adults in it.

13.12 Key terms

Norm — a distribution of measurements in a population; a description, not a standard.

Explanatory model — the family's own account of what is wrong and why; it predicts what they will do.

Distributed caregiving — care provided by a network rather than a pair; the norm in most of the world.

Material conditions — income, food, housing, crowding, water, transport; developmental variables, not background.

Bandwidth — the finite attention a caregiver has for the work of parenting, reduced by scarcity.

Cultural formulation — a structured enquiry into the family's world, account, resources, authorities and goals.

Conditions of measurement — the language, setting, relationship and instruction under which a finding was obtained, which must be reported with it.

Relativism — the position that no practice can be judged from outside its culture; incompatible with child protection.

Network map — a diagram of who does what for a child and who the child goes to when frightened.

13.13 Self-test

  1. State the four critiques, and give one piece of evidence for each.
  2. What are the five mechanisms by which poverty affects development, and which one most changes clinical practice?
  3. Give the question that replaces the Strange Situation in a household with distributed caregiving, and the follow-up question that finds the problem.
  4. Name the four conditions of measurement and explain how each contaminates a finding.
  5. When is a practice harmful rather than different, and how do you say so in three sentences?
  6. Give the four rules for working with an interpreter, and the one that must never be broken.
  7. Why does the therapist ask the family to read the letter before it is sent?
  8. In the Morenike case, what was the referral actually describing?

Answers. (1) Western norms — the Strange Situation's distribution varies with caregiving practice across cultures; poverty — the stress-physiology and nutrition evidence; distributed caregiving — the ethnographic record and the network as the normal unit; measurement — the language and setting effects on test performance. (2) Chronic stress physiology, food insecurity, crowding, housing instability, and parental bandwidth — the last changes practice most, because it converts "inconsistent parenting" into a resource calculation. (3) "When she is frightened, who does she go to?" and "does that person know they are the one?" (4) Language, stranger, setting, instruction — each measures the situation as well as the child, so the finding must be reported with them attached. (5) When it damages the child's body, language, capacity to relate or access to learning; name the practice factually, name the concern developmentally, and state what you are not able to work with. (6) Brief beforehand; speak to the family; double the time; never use a family member — the last must never be broken. (7) Because a letter the parent cannot bear to read will not be used, and because the family is the authority on its own accuracy. (8) The family's housing.

13.14 Reflective task

Write down three things your own household did with children that a textbook would call a problem, and three that it did that the textbook would call correct. Then answer: which of the six did you assume was normal until this week? Most students find at least one in each column, and the discovery that the textbook's standards are also somebody's household practice is the week's actual learning.

13.15 Further reading

  • Henrich, J., Heine, S. and Norenzayan, A. "The weirdest people in the world?" — the sampling problem, in general.
  • Keller, H. Cultures of Infancy — the comparative evidence on caregiving practice.
  • McLoyd, V. "Socioeconomic disadvantage and child development" — the poverty mechanisms.
  • Kirmayer, L. on cultural formulation — the structured interview this week adapts.
  • Serpell, R. The Significance of Schooling — African evidence on what school readiness actually means.

Next: Week 14 — When development goes wrong: the differential habit.

Week 14 — When development goes wrong: the differential habit

The four categories. For any developmental concern, run the check before you form a hypothesis. Every presentation in this course is one of four things, and the four have different prognoses and different referrals.

CategoryWhat it meansExampleWhat it usually requires
VariationWithin the normal range, differently timed or differently shapedLate talking with good comprehension at 22 monthsInformation, review, no referral
DelayBehind the expected sequence, but progressingTwo-word combinations at three yearsAssessment and early intervention
RegressionLoss of a skill that was presentWords at two, silence at two and a halfUrgent medical assessment
SymptomA behaviour that expresses something elseNight waking after a house moveThe thing it expresses

The five systemic questions. Behind every presentation there is a family, and the family has five facts that predict what will happen next.

  1. When did it start, and what else changed then?
  2. Who noticed first, and who disagrees?
  3. What does the family do when it happens, and what happens next?
  4. What would improve if it stopped — and what would get worse?
  5. What has already been tried, and what did that teach us?

The differential table. The table below is the instrument, not a reference. It is used in the first session, from memory, for any presentation in the course.

PresentationDevelopmental differentialSystemic differential
Aggression at 5Language delay; impulsivity; traumaParental conflict; inconsistent limits; a new baby
Withdrawal at 12Depression; bullying; neurodivergenceFamily secrecy; a parent's illness; migration
Decline at 70Delirium; dementia; depression; hearing lossBereavement; relocation; loss of role
Non-attendance at 9Anxiety; learning difficulty; bullyingA function at home; an unresolved parental conflict
Clinging at 2Temperament; separation; illnessA caregiver's own anxiety; a recent change of carer

Why a "habit" and not a "skill". Because the differential is not something a clinician does once; it is something they do automatically, before the formulation forms. The difficulty is that formulations form in about four seconds, from the referral letter, before the family has sat down. This week's purpose is to install a pause between the referral and the hypothesis, and a pause is installed by repetition rather than by understanding.

— A boy of two and a half who had forty words at twenty months and has none now. His mother is certain. His father says he is lazy. The grandmother says he will talk when he is ready, as his uncle did. The nursery says he is fine. Regression is the one category in which the family's disagreement is not a family problem but a clinical emergency, and the therapist's first task is to establish the facts before anyone's theory is discussed.

14.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesStudent activity
0–8The four-second formulationHow a hypothesis forms from a referral letter, and why that is the problemRead a referral and write their hypothesis in one sentence
8–22The four categoriesVariation, delay, regression, symptom — with examples from earlier weeksSort twelve presentations into the four
22–34RegressionWhy it is the one urgent category, and what it demandsIdentify the three facts that establish it
34–48The five systemic questionsThe questions that predict what the family will doRun the five on a case, in pairs
48–62The differential tableWorked from memory, from four presentationsReproduce the table for two presentations
62–74The red flagsThe specific findings that require a same-week referralMatch eight red flags to their urgency
74–84Referral and its aftermathHow to refer well, and what to do while the family waitsWrite a referral letter
84–90The assessmentWhat the case analysis requiresDraft the outline of their case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The lecture opens by having every student write their hypothesis from a referral letter in one sentence, before any discussion. The sentences are read aloud, and they are almost always characterological ("anxious mother", "difficult child", "attention-seeking"). The class then works the same case properly and discovers how little the letter supported. The exercise is uncomfortable and it is remembered, which is what a habit requires.

The sentence the lecture must land. "The referral letter is a description of somebody's worst day, and your first job is to find out which of four things you are looking at."

14.2 The four categories worked

Variation. The child is developing along a recognisable path at a different rate or in a different shape. The wide range of normal at every milestone is the single most under-used fact in clinical practice: walking anywhere from nine to eighteen months, first words from nine to eighteen months, two-word combinations from eighteen to thirty months, bladder control from two to four years. A child at the far end of any of those ranges, with good comprehension, good play, good relating and no regression, is a child about whom the correct action is review.

The clinical discipline of variation is to say so plainly. Parents are frequently more reassured by a number than by a sentence — "the normal range for this is nine to eighteen months and he is fifteen" — and giving the range is not false reassurance; it is the finding.

Delay. The child is behind the expected sequence and progressing slowly. The distinguishing feature is not the gap but the trajectory: a child who is six months behind and gaining a month a month is closing; a child who is six months behind and gaining a month every three is falling further behind. The trajectory requires two measurements, which means the assessment is not complete in one visit, which is why the review date is part of the finding rather than a courtesy.

Regression. The child has lost a skill. It is the only category that is always urgent, for three reasons: some causes are treatable and time-critical (a space-occupying lesion, a metabolic disorder, epileptic encephalopathy, a hearing loss from infection, severe iron deficiency); some are progressive; and the family's account of a regression is frequently disbelieved by the first professional they see, which costs months. The therapist's obligation is narrow and absolute: establish whether it is real, and if it is, refer the same week.

Symptom. The behaviour is an expression of something else — a change, a loss, a conflict, a fear, an illness. This is the category the rest of the course has been teaching, and it is the one most often over-applied: a therapist trained in family systems will find a systemic explanation for a regression that needs an MRI. The four-category check exists precisely to prevent the reverse error, and the therapist's discipline is to run it first, even when the systemic explanation is obvious.

The fifth possibility that is not a category. Nothing. A substantial proportion of referrals to child services describe a child who is developing normally in a family that is under strain, and the correct formulation is "no developmental concern; a family that needs help". That formulation is not a failure to diagnose; it is a diagnosis, and it should be written down as clearly as any other.

14.2a The five systemic questions, worked

Each question has a purpose, a common wrong answer, and a follow-up that defeats it.

Question 1 — When did it start, and what else changed then? The purpose is to date the onset relative to events, which is the entire method of Week 1. The common wrong answer is a duration ("about a year"), which is unusable. The follow-up: "what was happening in the house that month — a move, a birth, a death, a new job, someone leaving?" Families can usually date an event even when they cannot date a behaviour, and the event dates the behaviour.

Question 2 — Who noticed first, and who disagrees? The purpose is to find the household's fault line. The disagreement is data: it tells you who has the child during the day, whose authority is contested, and which of the adults will not support the plan. The follow-up: "what does he say when you tell him?" The answer frequently reveals a second, older disagreement.

Question 3 — What does the family do when it happens, and what happens next? The purpose is the behavioural sequence, and it is the question that produces the intervention. The follow-up matters more than the question: keep asking "and then what?" until the sequence closes. A typical sequence: he cries, she shouts, he cries harder, she gives him the phone, he stops. The phone is the intervention's target, and nobody in the family has described the sequence as a sequence.

Question 4 — What would improve if it stopped, and what would get worse? The systemic question, and the one students forget. A symptom does something in a household, and its removal changes the household. The follow-up: "who would notice first?" This question identifies the person whose position depends on the problem, and that person is frequently the one who will not cooperate.

Question 5 — What has already been tried, and what did that teach us? The purpose is the family's competence and its budget. The follow-up: "what happened when you stopped?" A family that tried a plan and abandoned it has usually learned something about what is feasible, and the therapist who does not ask will recommend the same plan again.

Why the five are asked in this order. Question 1 establishes the timeline, which the other four depend on. Question 2 finds the fault line, which tells the therapist whom to address. Question 3 produces the behavioural sequence. Question 4 is asked only after the sequence is known, because it is a question about function and function cannot be guessed. Question 5 produces the plan's constraints, and asking it earlier tends to convert the interview into a negotiation.

14.2b Red flags: the findings that require a same-week referral

The list below is not exhaustive and is not a licence to practise medicine. It is the minimum that a family therapist must recognise in order to refer rather than to treat.

Regression of any kind. Loss of words, loss of walking, loss of bladder control after a year of dryness, loss of social smiling, loss of a skill that was established. Urgent.

Loss of vision or hearing, or a change in either. A child who stops turning to sound, or who begins to hold objects very close. Urgent.

A change in consciousness or awareness. Episodes of "switching off", staring that cannot be interrupted, a child who does not respond to pain. Urgent.

Headache that wakes the child, or vomiting on waking, or a new squint, or a change in gait. Urgent.

Growth failure. Weight loss, or a fall across two centile lines, or a child who is not gaining. Urgent.

Any symptom that is worse in the morning and better later, which is the pattern of raised intracranial pressure, and any symptom that is steadily progressive.

Seizures, or suspected seizures, including episodes the family describes as "he goes quiet and his eyes roll".

Self-harm, suicidal ideation, or a plan in any child old enough to hold one, at any age. Same day.

Sexual knowledge or behaviour beyond the child's developmental stage, or any disclosure of abuse. Same day, safeguarding framework.

A parent's account that does not fit the injury. The safeguarding question, asked every time, without exception, and without requiring certainty.

The discipline. The therapist does not need to know what the condition is. She needs to recognise the category, say so plainly to the family, and make the referral in writing before the family leaves the building. "I do not know what is causing this and it needs to be looked at this week" is a complete and honest clinical statement, and it is far better than a formulation that keeps the family in the therapy room for three months.

14.2c The child who is referred twice in a year

A configuration that tests the four-category habit more than any single presentation: the same child, returned to the clinic, with the same complaint, by a different professional.

What the second referral usually means. One of four things. The first assessment found nothing and nobody said so clearly. The first intervention was not implemented, for reasons that were material rather than motivational. The complaint has changed in a way nobody recorded. Or the first formulation was wrong.

The four questions that distinguish them, asked in this order.

  1. "What has changed since the last time he was here?" If nothing has changed in the child and something has changed in the setting — a new school, a new teacher, a new baby — the second referral is about the setting.
  2. "What were you told to do, and what happened when you did it?" If the family did the action and it failed, the formulation is probably wrong. If the family did not do it, the reason is the finding.
  3. "Who sent you this time, and what did they say?" The referral's source is information: a school that refers twice is telling you about the school's capacity, not only the child's difficulty.
  4. "What would have to change for you not to need to be here?" The family's answer is the outcome measure, and it is frequently something the first assessment never addressed.
