The trap. The second referral invites the therapist to work harder on the same formulation. The discipline is to work the four-category check again from the beginning, as though the child were new, because one time in five they are.

The record. A second referral should produce a note that says explicitly which of the four explanations applies and what the evidence is. A file that accumulates four assessments and no conclusion is a file that will produce a fifth, and the fifth will be the one in which somebody finally notices that the question itself was the wrong one.

14.5a The assessment the family can afford

A short section with a large practical consequence, placed here because this week generates more referrals than any other.

The arithmetic. A specialist appointment may cost a fare, a day's income, a night's accommodation and the care of other children. For a household with three children and a daily wage, the true cost of a referral is frequently more than a month's discretionary income, and the therapist who does not compute this will make referrals that are not attended and will conclude that the family is not motivated.

What to ask, every time, before the referral is written. "What would it take for you to get there?" Then listen for the four costs: money, time, distance, and the care of the other children, which is the cost most often omitted from a clinician's arithmetic and the one most often decisive. Then solve the ones that can be solved. A referral to a Thursday morning clinic for a mother who works Thursday mornings is not a referral; it is a suggestion.

The three practical moves. Ask the receiving service whether there is a cheaper or closer site, which there frequently is and which nobody has asked about. Write the letter in a way that lets the family be seen once rather than three times, by putting the trajectory and the red flags in the first paragraph. And give the family a single named contact at the clinic who will take a telephone call, which reduces the number of journeys and is the therapist's own job rather than a system's.

What cannot be solved, and must be said. Sometimes the appointment is unaffordable and the service is distant, and the honest response is to say so, to write to the service, and to be explicit with the family about what the clinic will do in the interval. A family told the truth about a difficulty can plan around it; a family told that help is coming cannot.

14.3 The method: a differential assessment in one session

Step 1 — Establish the facts of the trajectory, not the level. Two questions: "what could he do six months ago that he cannot do now?" and "what can he do now that he could not do six months ago?" Every assessment that opens with these two questions is better than one that opens with a milestone checklist, because it produces a direction.

Step 2 — Place the presentation in one of the four categories, provisionally, and say so. "On what you have told me this looks like a delay rather than a regression, and I will tell you if that changes." Families find provisional formulations more usable than certainty, because a provisional formulation leaves room for their knowledge.

Step 3 — Run the red-flag list. Thirty seconds. If any is positive, stop the assessment and refer.

Step 4 — Run the five systemic questions. The timeline, the fault line, the sequence, the function, the attempts.

Step 5 — Complete the four-category check with the evidence you have. If the evidence is insufficient, say which category is most likely and what would settle it — an audiology appointment, a developmental assessment, a second measurement in three months.

Step 6 — Write the differential in the record, in order of probability, with the reasoning. Not a diagnosis. A differential with reasons is what a clinician needs six months later, and it is what protects a family from being reassessed from scratch by every professional they meet.

Step 7 — Decide the referral, and make it in the room. Typed, signed, copied, with a telephone number and an expected waiting time. And say what happens if the waiting list is long: what the family will do in the meantime, and who will chase it.

Step 8 — Book the review before the family leaves. Even where a referral has been made. The review is the therapist's own second measurement, and it is the thing that catches the case in which the first formulation was wrong.

14.3a Referring well, and what to do while the family waits

The letter. Six elements: the reason for referral in one sentence; the trajectory, with dates; what the four-category check found; the red flags that are absent and the ones that are present; what has been tried; and what the referrer wants. One page. Addressed to a named service, with a named person if possible, and copied to the family.

The chase. A telephone call at two weeks and at six weeks. In most systems a referral that is not chased is a referral that is lost, and the loss is invisible to everyone except the family, who conclude that nothing can be done.

The waiting period. The family is waiting for a service that may take months. Three things can be done in the meantime and each is worth doing: protect sleep; give the child one domain of competence; and give the family one explanatory sentence they can use with relatives, which reduces the pressure that usually falls on the mother.

The wrong thing to do while waiting. Do not begin a treatment for the condition you suspect before it has been assessed, because the treatment will muddy the assessment. Do not tell the family that the referral is a formality. And do not stop seeing them: the referral is to a service, not away from the therapist.

14.3b Reading somebody else's assessment

Most children who arrive at a family therapist have been assessed before, and the previous report is frequently the most misleading document in the file.

What previous reports usually contain, and what each thing means. A score, without the conditions under which it was obtained. A diagnosis, frequently made at the end of a single appointment. A recommendation, frequently written for a service that no longer exists. And an absence — the things nobody asked, which are invisible because they are not written.

The four questions to ask of any previous assessment.

  1. Who did it, in what language, with whom present? Week 13's conditions of measurement, applied to a document rather than to a session.
  2. What was the child doing at the time? Children are assessed during illness, after a move, in the week a sibling is born. A score obtained in a bad month is a score of the month.
  3. What has changed since? A report is a photograph. The family's own account of the last year is the film, and it is usually more informative than the report.
  4. What did they not ask? The hearing. The sleep. The food. The school. The grandmother. These are the five omissions in most of the reports this book's students will read.

The rule for using a report. Quote it with its date and its conditions, and never inherit its conclusion without its evidence. "A developmental assessment in June last year found a language delay; I do not have the scores or the conditions, and I will ask for them" is a professional sentence. "He has a language delay" said on the basis of another clinician's paragraph is not.

When to ask for the original. Whenever a decision depends on it. A request for the full report, with the scores and the conditions, is a legitimate professional act and takes one telephone call.

The family's experience of repeated assessment. A child who has been assessed four times has usually been assessed well at least once, and the family has usually been told four different things. The therapist's question at the start is not "what did they find?" but "what were you told, and what did you think of it?" The second half is the part nobody asks, and it is where the family's real formulation lives, usually in a sentence the first clinician did not hear because the appointment had already run over its time.

14.4 Three techniques

Technique 1 — The four-category card. A card, carried, with the four categories on one side and the red-flag list on the other. Its purpose is not reference; it is to slow the four seconds between the referral letter and the hypothesis. Clinicians who carry it report that they use it for the first year and then no longer need it, which is the definition of a habit.

Technique 2 — The onset line. A single horizontal line from birth to today, with the onset of the concern marked, and every event the family can date marked above it. The line does two things: it dates the problem, and it makes visible the coincidence that the family has not noticed. It is the Week 1 timeline applied to a symptom rather than a life, and it takes four minutes.

Technique 3 — The two-measurement rule. Nothing that could be a delay is concluded from one measurement. The first assessment produces a level and a hypothesis; the review produces a trajectory. The rule is stated to the family as part of the finding — "I am going to measure this again in three months, and what I am looking for is not a different number but a different direction" — which converts the review from a courtesy into the assessment.

14.5 The first session with "something is wrong"

Phase 1 — Take the account without a hypothesis. Fifteen minutes in which the therapist asks only factual questions and writes down the family's words. The discipline is to say nothing interpretive in this phase, which is harder than it sounds when the account is vivid.

Phase 2 — The two trajectory questions. What could he do six months ago that he cannot now; what can he do now that he could not before.

Phase 3 — The red-flag list, quickly and out loud. "I am going to ask you some quick questions about his health. Most of them will be no." Families accept a list; they become frightened by a vague enquiry.

Phase 4 — The four-category placement, stated provisionally. "This looks like X to me at the moment, and here is what would change my mind."

Phase 5 — The referral, made in the room. Typed, signed, copied, explained.

Phase 6 — One thing for the wait, and the review date. Both written.

Why the hypothesis is withheld in phase 1. Because a family that hears a hypothesis in the first five minutes organises everything afterwards to support or refute it, and the assessment then measures the family's theory rather than the child.

14.6 Case: the boy who stopped talking

Referral. Mrs Okafor telephones about her son, Chuka, two years and seven months. "He has stopped talking. He used to say words. Now he says nothing. My husband says he is lazy. My mother says his uncle was the same and he talks now. The nursery says he is fine."

The household. Mrs Okafor, 31, a teacher. Mr Okafor, 36, a civil servant, posted to another state and home at weekends. Chuka, 2;7. A sister, 8 months. The paternal grandmother, 63, who lives with them and who raised Mr Okafor's younger brother, who "did not talk until four".

The trajectory questions. What could he do six months ago that he cannot now? "He had words. Maybe forty. He said 'mama', 'water', 'go', 'no', 'ball'. He would point at the fridge and say 'juice'." What can he do now that he could not before? "Nothing. He does not say anything now. He pulls my hand." Mother's voice, at this point: "I have been telling them for four months."

The onset line. Marked in the session: at 20 months, forty words and two-word combinations. At 22 months, the grandmother's sister died and the household travelled for the burial. At 23 months, the mother returned to work and the grandmother took over the days. At 24 months, the sister was born. At 25 months, the words began to reduce. At 27 months, silence. At 29 months, the referral.

The red-flag list. Regression — positive. Hearing change — uncertain; the mother says he does not turn when called, which she had attributed to stubbornness. Consciousness — no episodes reported. Headache and vomiting — no. Growth — he has lost weight since the sister was born, which the mother mentions only when asked. Seizures — "sometimes he goes still and his eyes go somewhere else", which the mother has mentioned to two people who told her it was normal. Safeguarding — nothing disclosed.

What the therapist does, in order.

First, she says the sentence. "A child who had forty words and now has none has lost something that was there, and that is not laziness and it is not the same as his uncle. It needs to be looked at this week, and I am going to write the letter now." The sentence is said to the mother, in the grandmother's hearing, and it is the sentence the mother has been waiting four months for.

Second, the referral, in the room. To a paediatrician with a neurology interest, marked urgent, with the trajectory, the onset line, the red flags (regression, possible hearing change, possible absence episodes, weight loss), and one request: assessment of a regression of language with possible seizures in a two-year-seven-month-old.

Third, the audiology referral, to run in parallel. Regression with a possible hearing change requires a hearing assessment regardless of what the paediatrician finds.

Fourth, the family work, which continues and is not the treatment. The grandmother's belief is not stupidity; it is the family's available explanation, and it is protecting everyone from a fear. The therapist does not argue with it. She asks the grandmother one question: "when your brother did not talk, did he lose words he had already said?" The grandmother is silent, and then says: "no. He never said any." The distinction has been made by the family's own authority, in the family's own terms, and it took one question.

Fifth, one thing for the wait, and one for the household. For the wait: a communication book, in which the mother writes what Chuka communicated today and how, which gives the paediatrician four weeks of data and gives the mother something to do. For the household: twenty minutes a day with the mother alone with Chuka, at a fixed time, doing whatever he chooses, which addresses the thing that is also true — that a boy of two who has lost his words has also lost his place, to a sister who is eight months old.

The formulation, written in the record. "Regression of expressive language from a 40-word vocabulary with two-word combinations at 20 months to no words at 27 months, in a child with possible hearing change, possible absence episodes, and weight loss. Not explained by the family's circumstances, which are relevant but not causal. Urgent paediatric referral made; audiology referral made in parallel; family work continues and is not the treatment of the regression."

The five systemic findings, which are real and are not the diagnosis.

  1. The mother noticed four months before anyone acted, and was disbelieved three times — by her husband, her mother-in-law, and the nursery. The disbelieving is a clinical event and it has cost four months.
  2. The father's absence at weekends means the household's authority is the grandmother's, and the grandmother's explanation is the family's explanation.
  3. The sister's birth and the mother's return to work are genuinely relevant to the household's capacity, and they are not the cause of a regression. The distinction matters because the family will otherwise spend a year believing that a return to work caused a neurological event.
  4. The weight loss is a second red flag that nobody had investigated, and it is mentioned almost by accident.
  5. The family's disagreement is not a communication problem to be worked on in therapy; it is a race against time, and the therapist's job is to end it by making the referral, not by facilitating an understanding.

14.6a Five months later

The paediatric assessment found epileptic activity on an EEG, with clinical absence seizures, and Chuka was started on treatment. His language has begun to return — twenty words at the last review, no combinations yet. The neurologist's view is that the outcome depends on the underlying cause, which is still being investigated, and the therapist records that view in the family's copy rather than translating it into hope.

The weight loss was investigated and attributed to a feeding difficulty that developed after the sister's birth, and it has resolved with a dietetic plan.

The grandmother has become the family member who telephones the clinic. This is the case's most instructive outcome: the person whose explanation was the obstacle became the person who chases appointments, and she changed not because she was argued out of her belief but because one question — "did he lose words he had already said?" — gave her a fact that her own experience could not explain.

The father has attended twice, on Saturdays, and the therapist has used both sessions on one question: what does he want his son's life to be like at twenty-five? It is a Week 13 question and it is doing more work than any explanation of the regression.

The honest limitation. Nobody knows yet what Chuka's language will be at six. The therapist's record says so, and the family has been told so plainly. What has changed is that the question is in the hands of the people who can answer it, and that four months of disbelief have ended.

What would have changed the plan. If the mother's account had been of a delay rather than a regression, the referral would have been routine rather than urgent, and the family work would have been the treatment rather than an adjunct. If the safeguarding question had produced a disclosure, everything would have stopped. And if the paediatric service had a twelve-month waiting list, the therapist's obligation would have been to say so, to write to the service, and to tell the family that she would chase it monthly — because a family told the truth about a waiting list can survive it, and a family told that help is coming cannot.

14.7 Transcript: one question to a grandmother

Eight turns from the second session. The grandmother has come because Mrs Okafor asked her to. She has been in the room for four minutes and has said that the boy will talk when he is ready.

T1 Therapist: Mama, I want to ask you about your brother. T2 Grandmother: He did not talk until he was four. Then he talked. Now he is a lecturer. — The family's evidence, offered confidently and in good faith. T3 Therapist: A lecturer. (pause) And before he talked — did he say any words at all? Even a few? T4 Grandmother: (thinking) No. He never said anything. He just pointed. T5 Therapist: (gently) That is the difference, then. Your brother never started. Chuka started, and then stopped. (pause) Those are not the same thing, and the second one needs a doctor this week. — The distinguishing fact, delivered in the family's own material rather than in the therapist's vocabulary. The therapist has not said "regression"; she has used the grandmother's own memory. T6 Grandmother: (long pause) I did not know that was different. T7 Therapist: Nobody explained it to you. That is not your fault. (pause) Will you help me? His mother says he does not turn when he is called. You are with him in the day. What do you see? — The grandmother is recruited as an observer, which converts her from the family's obstacle into its second witness. This is the turn the case needed, and it is not a technique for managing a difficult relative; it is the recognition that she is the person with the most data. T8 Grandmother: (after a pause) He turns if he sees me. If I call from behind, he does not.

14.8 Laboratory: the differential under time pressure

Aim. To place four presentations in the four categories, identify the red flags, and make the correct referral decision, in seven minutes each.

Procedure. The class is divided into pairs. Four briefs are distributed, one at a time, with seven minutes on the clock for each: read, run the four-category check, run the red-flag list, and write the referral decision and its urgency. No discussion is permitted between briefs. The marking is by the trainer, against a prepared answer key, and the scores are read out anonymously.

Marking rubric (20 marks over four briefs).

Criterion0–12–34–5
CategoryWrong categoryRight category, wrong reasoningRight category, with the distinguishing fact named
Red flagsMissed a positive flagFound the flags, misjudged urgencyFound the flags, judged urgency correctly, and named the referral
The referralNone, or vagueMade, without a named destinationMade in writing, to a named service, with a stated urgency and what is wanted
LanguageDiagnostic label without assessmentDescription without conditionsDescription with trajectory, conditions, and a provisional formulation

The four briefs, and the answer key.

Brief 1. A girl of three with no words, good comprehension, rich play, no regression, hearing tested normal. Answer: delay in expressive language with preserved comprehension — routine referral for speech and language assessment, review in three months, no red flags. The error the brief is designed to catch is the urgent referral, which is common among trainees who have just learned about regression.

Brief 2. A boy of four who could dress himself, count to ten and use sentences, and who now cannot, over six weeks, with new stumbling and a change in handwriting. Answer: regression with a motor component — urgent. The error is the family-systems formulation, which is elaborate and wrong.

Brief 3. A girl of nine, refusing school for three weeks, after her parents' separation, with no red flags, normal report card, one named friend. Answer: symptom — anxiety in the context of a family transition; no medical referral; family work is the treatment.

Brief 4. A boy of two who has never walked independently, is not yet pulling to stand, and whose mother says he "went backwards" at fourteen months after a fever. Answer: regression with motor involvement, plus a possible delay — urgent, with the fever named in the letter. The error is to accept the family's explanation ("he was ill") without asking what he could do before the illness.

14.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Forming the hypothesis from the referral letter. The letter is a description of somebody's worst day, written by someone with a different agenda. The four-second hypothesis is the thing this week exists to interrupt.

Error 2 — Finding a family explanation for a regression. The most dangerous error in the book. A regression has a medical differential until proved otherwise, and a systemic formulation that keeps the child in the therapy room delays the assessment.

Error 3 — Accepting a diagnosis of "dementia" or "delay" as an explanation for a change. Week 12's lesson applies at every age: a change is a new event, not a progression, until investigated.

Error 4 — Referring without a trajectory. A referral letter that says "language delay" without dates cannot be triaged, and will be placed at the bottom of a list. A letter that says "40 words at 20 months, none at 27" will be seen this week.

Error 5 — Referring and disappearing. The referral is not the end of the therapist's involvement; it is the point at which the chasing starts and the family work continues. A family that is referred and then not seen concludes that the referral was a dismissal.

Error 6 — Treating the family's disagreement as the problem. The Okafor family disagreed for four months, and the disagreement was real, and it was not the clinical problem. A therapist who spends the four months working on the disagreement has done harm with good intentions.

14.9a Saying "I do not know" well

This week produces more uncertainty than any other in the course, and the way a therapist handles not knowing determines whether a family trusts the rest.

The three bad ways to not know. The hedge — a formulation so vague that it commits to nothing ("there are some developmental concerns we should keep an eye on"), which leaves the family without an action. The false certainty — a diagnosis supplied to end the conversation, which is comfortable for the therapist and dangerous for the child. The deflection — "that is a question for the paediatrician", which is accurate and, said early and often, communicates that the therapist has nothing to offer.

The good way, in four moves.

Move 1 — Say what you do know, concretely. "I know he had forty words and now has none, and I know that is not laziness." Certainty about the facts is available even when certainty about the cause is not.

Move 2 — Say what you do not know, and why. "I do not know what is causing it. assessment this week rather than an opinion from me, which would cost him a month and tell you nothing."

Move 3 — Say what would tell you. "If the hearing test is normal and the paediatrician finds nothing, the next thing I would look at is X." This gives the family a map of the uncertainty, which is more useful than its removal.

Move 4 — Say what you will do while not knowing. "I will chase the appointment in two weeks, I will see you in a month, and we will work on the sleep and on his communication book in the meantime."

Why the fourth move matters most. A family can tolerate uncertainty about the future if they are certain about the therapist's next action. The families who leave and do not return are not the ones who were told "I do not know"; they are the ones who were told it and then given nothing to hold.

14.10 Culture, faith and the urgent referral

The cost of a referral. For many households an appointment across the city costs a fare, a day's income, and the loss of a job. The therapist who says "you must go to the teaching hospital on Tuesday" without asking what that costs has made a recommendation that will not be followed. The question that must precede every referral is "what would it take for you to get there?"

The family's explanation and the family's fear. The grandmother in this week's case is not obscurantist; she is holding the family's only available explanation, and it protects everyone from a fear that is worse. The therapist's discipline is to join the explanation and add a fact, not to displace it. One question, asked in the family's own material, did more than an hour of explanation would have.

Faith and the timing of medical care. Where a family believes that healing is a matter for prayer, the sequence that works is: pray, and go. The framing of the referral as stewardship — seeing what has been made — is not a manipulation; it is a translation, and Week 13 established that translation is part of the job. What cannot be conceded is the delay, because the conditions that present as regression are among the few in this book where a month changes the outcome.

The mother who is disbelieved. Across this week's cases the same figure appears: a mother who noticed first and was told she was anxious, over-attending, or projecting. The evidence on parental concern is that it is a reasonably sensitive marker and a poorly specific one — which means it should generate assessment, not reassurance. A therapist who says "mothers are usually right about something being wrong and rarely right about what it is" has given the family a usable position.

DEBATE — "A family therapist should not be assessing development at all; that is a paediatrician's job." Argue for: the scope of practice is real, an amateur assessment produces false reassurance and false alarm, and the therapist's proper role is to refer on the first concern. Argue against: the therapist is frequently the only professional in the room, no referral happens without someone deciding to make it, and the four-category check takes four minutes and prevents months of delay. Then state what you would do if you were the only clinician a family could reach, and where you would draw the line.

14.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. Parental concern is a useful screening signal: the literature on parental suspicion of developmental delay finds reasonable sensitivity and modest specificity, which supports using it as a trigger for assessment rather than as a diagnosis. Regression in language or motor function is associated with a higher probability of an identifiable neurological condition than delay without regression, which is the empirical basis for treating it as urgent. Early intervention for established delay produces measurable gains, and the gains are larger the earlier the intervention begins, which is the argument for the whole of this week.

What is weaker. Screening instruments perform differently in different populations and the norms are frequently unrepresentative (Week 13). The evidence on the harms of false-positive developmental screening is thin, and it is a real question: a family told to worry about a child who is developing normally pays a cost that no trial has measured well.

What is not supported. That a family explanation and a medical explanation are in competition — they answer different questions and both can be true. That watchful waiting is safe for a regression. And that a normal assessment at one point in time excludes a problem later; the trajectory, not the level, is the finding.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same forty-word vocabulary at twenty months predicts nothing at all; the loss of it predicts a great deal. Timing is the variable, and this week is about noticing it.

14.12 Key terms

Variation — development within the normal range, differently timed or shaped.

Delay — development behind the expected sequence but progressing.

Regression — loss of an established skill; always urgent.

Symptom — a behaviour expressing something else; the category the rest of the course teaches.

Trajectory — the direction of development over time, which requires two measurements.

Red flag — a finding that requires a same-week referral regardless of the formulation.

Provisional formulation — a stated hypothesis with the evidence that would change it.

Onset line — a timeline of the symptom against the events the family can date.

Watchful waiting — a legitimate strategy for variation and delay, and never for regression, where it is a decision to lose time.

Triage — the ranking of a referral by urgency, which is why a letter without dates is placed at the bottom of a list.

Two-measurement rule — no conclusion about delay from a single assessment.

14.13 Self-test

  1. Give the four categories with one example each, and say which is always urgent.
  2. Why is a regression urgent even when the child is functioning well otherwise?
  3. State the five systemic questions and the purpose of each.
  4. Name six red flags and the referral each requires.
  5. Why does the therapist withhold a hypothesis in the first fifteen minutes of the session?
  6. What did the therapist ask the grandmother, and why did it work?
  7. What is the two-measurement rule, and how is it explained to a family?
  8. Name three things a therapist can do for a family while they wait for a specialist appointment.

Answers. (1) Variation — a child walking at fifteen months; delay — two-word combinations at three years; regression — words lost at two and a half; symptom — night waking after a move; regression is always urgent. (2) Because regression has a medical differential that includes treatable and progressive conditions, and because delay in referral is the one error in this work that cannot be recovered. (3) Onset and co-occurring change (to date the problem); who noticed and who disagrees (to find the fault line); the behavioural sequence (to find the intervention); what would improve and what would worsen (to find the function); what has been tried (to find the constraints). (4) Regression — urgent paediatric; change in hearing or vision — urgent audiology or ophthalmology; seizures or episodes of unresponsiveness — urgent paediatric; headache on waking or vomiting on waking — urgent; growth failure — urgent; disclosure or sexual knowledge beyond stage — same-day safeguarding. (5) Because a family that hears a hypothesis early organises its account to support or refute it, and the assessment then measures the theory rather than the child. (6) Whether her brother, who talked late, had ever said words before he stopped: he had not, which distinguished delay from regression in the family's own material and made the grandmother a witness rather than an obstacle. (7) Nothing that could be a delay is concluded from one measurement; it is explained as "what I am looking for in three months is not a different number but a different direction". (8) Protect sleep; give the child one domain of competence; give the family one explanatory sentence for relatives — and chase the appointment.

14.14 Reflective task

Take a referral you have received or read, and write the hypothesis you formed on first reading it, in one sentence. Then write the four categories beside it, and the red flags, and note how much of your hypothesis the letter actually supported. Most clinicians find that the sentence they wrote was about a person and the letter was about a day.

14.15 Further reading

  • Lipkin, P. and Macias, M. on developmental screening — the sensitivity and specificity of parental concern.
  • Shevell, M. on developmental regression — the differential, and why it is a different problem from delay.
  • Rutter, M. on developmental trajectories — the conceptual basis of the two-measurement rule.
  • Emde, R. and Emde, on early intervention — the evidence that earlier is better.
  • Nelson, C. et al. on deprivation and recovery — the limits of catch-up, stated honestly.

Next: Week 15 — Integration: the developmental lens in practice.

Week 15 — Integration: the developmental lens in practice

The developmental lens does three things for clinical work: it calibrates expectations, it dates the problem, and it predicts what the family must renegotiate next. Carry three questions into every first session:

  • What is this person's developmental task right now?
  • What is the family's corresponding task?
  • And what would "on track" look like in six months?

Why a week on integration, and not just a summary. Because the course has taught fifteen separate instruments and a student who arrives at a family with all fifteen in hand will use the one they like. The work of this week is selection: given this family, at this moment, which lens, in what order, and why. Integration is not the accumulation of perspectives; it is the discipline of choosing between them and being able to say why.

What integration is not. It is not eclecticism — using a little of everything, which produces a treatment with no theory and no way to know whether it failed. It is not synthesis on the grand scale — a unified model of human development, which nobody has and which is not needed. And it is not the addition of a developmental paragraph to a formulation built on something else. Integration means the developmental account is doing work: it changes what you notice, what you ask, and what you do first.

The three functions, briefly stated, with an example each.

Calibration. A father describes his nine-year-old as "wilful and disobedient". The developmental lens establishes that at nine a child's moral reasoning is reciprocal and an argument about fairness is a developmental achievement, not a discipline problem. The intervention changes from a behaviour plan to a conversation about rules. Without calibration, the therapist treats a stage as a fault.

Dating. A couple present with "we have grown apart". The timeline establishes that the distance began eighteen months ago, in the same quarter as a bereavement, a job change and a child's departure. The formulation changes from a relationship deficit to three developmental events arriving at once. Without dating, the therapist treats a reaction as a character.

Prediction. A family with a fourteen-year-old is doing well and asks for nothing. The developmental lens establishes that the next task is the renegotiation of autonomy, and that the family's current method — close supervision with no negotiation — will produce a conflict within a year. The work becomes preventive, which is the rarest and most valuable thing a family therapist does. Without prediction, the therapist waits for the conflict and then treats it.

15.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesStudent activity
0–10Selection, not accumulationWhy a therapist with fifteen lenses uses their favourite oneRank their own three preferred instruments, and say why
10–24The three functionsCalibration, dating, prediction — three cases, one eachApply each function to a case of their own
24–36The one-page formulationThe six cells, worked on the capstone caseDraft six cells for the capstone
36–48The limitsWhat the lens cannot do, and the errors of over-applicationName a case where the lens would mislead
48–62The map of the programmeHow development enters 701–727Place three other courses on the map
62–74The vivaThe four questions and the standard of an answerAnswer one question aloud, in three minutes
74–84Six errors of the whole courseThe errors that recur across the fifteen weeksIdentify which they are most likely to commit
84–90The closeWhat to carry, in one pageWrite their own three sentences to carry

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The lecture does not review the weeks. It takes a family the class has never seen and works it in real time, with the class choosing which instrument to use next and being asked to justify each choice. The justification is the assessment: a student who says "I would use a genogram" without being able to say what it would tell them that the first ten minutes did not has learned the instruments and not the discipline.

The sentence the lecture must land. "You will not use most of what you have learned in any single case. Your competence is in knowing which part, and your integrity is in being able to say why."

15.2 The three questions, worked in detail

Question 1 — What is this person's developmental task right now? The question is asked of every person in the room, not only the identified patient, and the answers are frequently different and sometimes incompatible. A mother of a newborn is in the young-children task; her own mother, who has moved in, is in the later-life task of adapting to widowhood; the newborn's sibling is in the autonomy task; and the father is in the midlife reckoning. The family's difficulty is usually the incompatibility, not any one task.

Question 2 — What is the family's corresponding task? Every individual task has a family task attached. A toddler's autonomy requires a household that can hold limits consistently across its adults. An adolescent's identity requires a household that can tolerate being disagreed with. A grandmother's integrity requires a household that asks her questions. The family task is the thing the treatment usually addresses, because it is the thing that can be changed in a room with more than one person in it.

Question 3 — What would "on track" look like in six months? The question forces specificity and it is the only protection against a treatment that continues because it has not been evaluated. An answer is a set of observable changes: he attends on Mondays; she speaks to her mother once a week; the grandmother is asked one question a day; the couple has twenty minutes on Thursdays. Six months is long enough to be a real change and short enough to be checked.

The fourth question, which is the one that keeps a therapist honest. And what would I expect to see if I am wrong? Every one of the three questions produces a hypothesis, and a hypothesis that cannot fail is not a hypothesis. The discipline of naming the disconfirming evidence, in advance, in the record, is the single practice most strongly associated with not doing harm over a career.

15.2a What the developmental lens cannot do

An integration week that does not state the limits produces graduates who see development everywhere, which is a way of not seeing the family.

It cannot diagnose. Developmental formulations describe trajectories and tasks; they do not identify disorders. A diagnosis requires an assessment by someone trained and resourced to make it, and the developmental lens is the reason a referral gets made, not a substitute for it.

It cannot explain everything. Some family difficulties are not developmental. Violence, addiction, poverty, trauma, and injustice are not stages, and a formulation that renders them as stages is a miscarriage. Week 13's critiques apply to the lens itself: it is one account, from somewhere, and it does not have jurisdiction over the whole of a family's life.

It cannot supply a therapy. The lens is a way of understanding what is happening; it is not a treatment protocol. The treatment comes from the modalities taught in MFT 702 and 710 and their successors, and the integration of lens and modality is the practitioner's own work.

It cannot make a family change. The most common error of a well-trained developmental therapist is to believe that an accurate formulation is persuasive. It is not. Families change when the formulation is translated into something they can do, by someone they trust, at a time when they have the capacity — which is what Weeks 11 and 13 were about.

It cannot predict an individual. Every claim in this book is probabilistic. The child who is at risk is not the child who will suffer, and the family that matches a risk profile is not the family that will need a clinic. Using the lens to forecast an individual's fate is the error that turns a developmental account into a sentence.

15.2b The ten-item developmental history

The instrument a graduate will use most often, and the one worth memorising. Ten items, twenty minutes, and it fits any age from birth to eighty.

  1. The present concern, in the family's words. Verbatim, in the first sentence of the record.
  2. The dated trajectory. What could he do a year ago, six months ago, now; and what has been lost.
  3. The events, dated. Births, deaths, moves, separations, job changes, migrations, illnesses, in the same period as item 2. This is the item that produces the formulation.
  4. Who does what. The network, its functions and its hours. Not the household roster.
  5. The authority. Who decides what, who is believed, and who is not consulted. Usually including the patient.
  6. Health and the senses. Hearing, vision, sleep, food, medication, and any episode of unexplained change. Four of the six are routinely omitted by clinicians and all six are cheap to ask.
  7. The school or the work. Not performance; function. What the child or adult can do in that setting and what has changed there.
  8. What has been tried, by whom, and what it cost. The family's competence and its budget, in one answer.
  9. The explanatory model. What the family believes is happening, and what that belief predicts they will do.
  10. The family's own goal. What they want in ten years, in their words. This is the item that makes the plan possible, and it is the one most often left out.

Why these ten and not the standard checklist. A milestone checklist produces a level; these ten produce a trajectory, a network, a budget and a goal — which is what a family therapist can act on. The checklist belongs in the referral to the developmental service. The ten items belong in the family therapist's first session.

How to take it in twenty minutes. Items 1, 2 and 3 in the first eight minutes, with both parents present. Items 4, 5 and 9 with the child or the identified patient out of the room if they are old enough to be embarrassed. Items 6 and 7 as a rapid list, out loud, with the family told that most answers will be "no". Items 8 and 10 at the end, when the family has told you enough to trust the question.

15.3b Saying the formulation in the family's language

The last skill of the course, and the one that decides whether fifteen weeks of theory reach a household.

The rule. Every formulation has two versions: the one in the record and the one in the room. They contain the same claims. They do not contain the same words.

The translation, worked. The record says: "Regression of expressive language with possible absence seizures in a household under financial strain with an unassessed hearing loss in the paternal grandmother." The room says: "He had words and now he does not, and that is not the same as his uncle, and it needs a doctor this week. Your mother cannot hear properly, and that is why she seems to be quarrelling. And I know the money is short, so let us work out how to get to the hospital."

The four rules of translation.

  • Keep the verbs. "He had words and now he does not" is a sentence a family can repeat. "Expressive language regression" is not.
  • Name the person, not the category. "Your mother cannot hear" rather than "there is a hearing loss in the household".
  • Put the hardest fact in the middle. Beginning with it produces shock; ending with it produces avoidance. The middle is where a family can hear it.
  • End with an action and a date. Every translated formulation ends with something to do and a time to come back, or it is not a formulation but a verdict.

The test. Ask the family to say it back. If they cannot, the translation failed and the fault is the therapist's, which is the same test as Week 11 and it is the last thing this course asks of a graduate.

15.3 The one-page formulation

Six cells, one page, written in the first two sessions and revised when the evidence changes. It is the document the rest of the treatment argues with.

Cell 1 — What is happening? One sentence, in the family's terms and the clinician's. "A three-year-old who had forty words has none; his mother has been disbelieved four times; the household is under financial strain and his grandmother is the daytime authority."

Cell 2 — What is the developmental task, person by person? The toddler's language and autonomy; the mother's shift from full-time care to work; the grandmother's later-life role; the father's absence. Four tasks, one household.

Cell 3 — What is the family's corresponding task? To agree who is the authority for what, and to support one adult to carry the child's assessment. Stated as a task, not a deficit.

Cell 4 — What is the trajectory, with dates? The onset line, in three or four dated sentences. This is the cell that makes the formulation clinical rather than descriptive.

Cell 5 — What is the differential, and what would change the formulation? The four-category placement, the red flags excluded and included, and the one finding that would overturn the whole thing.

Cell 6 — What happens first, and what does "on track" look like in six months? One action, one referral, one date, and three observable outcomes.

How the page is used. It is given to the family. Not the clinician's version — a version in plain language, one page, with the six cells as headings. Families keep it, argue with it, and bring it back annotated, and the annotations are the most useful clinical material in the file. A formulation the family cannot read is a formulation that will not be implemented.

15.3a How development enters the other twenty-six courses

The purpose of this table is to make the lens portable, because the student will spend the rest of the programme in courses that do not mention development at all.
