The teaching point in the table. Development is not a topic among topics. It is the dimension in which every other topic occurs, and a graduate who can only see it when the course is called "human development" has not learned it.

15.4 Capstone case: the Eze family

An unseen family, worked through the whole course. The class is given the referral and nothing else.

The referral. From a primary school, about a nine-year-old: "Ada is not settling in Primary 4. She is withdrawn, does not complete work, and has twice been found in the toilet at break. The mother is unresponsive to letters."

The family, established in the first session. Mr Eze, 44, a bus company supervisor, away four nights a week. Mrs Eze, 39, a seamstress working from home. Ada, 9. Chidi, 16, in Senior Secondary 1. Ngozi, 3. Mrs Eze's mother, 79, who lives with them and who is "difficult". A cousin, 19, who helps in the shop and sleeps in the sitting room.

The facts established across three sessions.

  • Ada's developmental history is unremarkable except for a period at four when she stopped sleeping alone after a burglary in the compound; the family solved it by moving her into her brother's room, where she still sleeps.
  • Her Primary 3 report was good. The Primary 4 report shows a fall in every subject, with a note that she "does not hear instructions the first time".
  • Her hearing has never been tested.
  • Chidi is in SS1 and is doing badly; he has been caught twice with a group of older boys. Mr Eze's response was to beat him; Mrs Eze's was to say nothing.
  • Ngozi, 3, has about ten words and does not combine them. Nobody in the family is concerned, because "her father's sister was late".
  • The grandmother, 79, has become "difficult": she does not answer, she accuses people of stealing, and she has begun to wander out of the compound. She has been seen once by a doctor, two years ago, who said it was old age.
  • The household's income has fallen by a third since Mr Eze's company cut overtime. One meal a day is now normal for the adults.
  • Mrs Eze has not left the house socially in eight months.

The class's first error, and it is deliberate in the design of the exercise. Presented with a nine-year-old's school difficulty, the class formulates the case as Ada's, and works on her. The trainer allows this for twenty minutes and then asks the three questions. The answers reorganise everything.

The three questions, answered for the Eze family.

What is each person's developmental task? Ada's is industry: she needs to be competent at something and to be seen to be. Chidi's is identity, and he is in a moratorium being conducted in the wrong company. Ngozi's is autonomy and language. Mrs Eze's is the young-children task, and she is doing it alone four nights a week with a dependent adult in the house. Mr Eze's is generativity and a work role that is being eroded. The grandmother's is integrity, and she has no audience.

What is the family's corresponding task? To establish who is in charge of what, across three adults and a cousin, and to protect one adult — Mrs Eze — from being the only person doing any of it. The family's task is structural, and it is the same task the course has met in Weeks 3, 7, 10 and 12.

What would "on track" look like in six months? A hearing test done and acted on; Ada attending and speaking to one named adult at school; Ngozi assessed and a language plan begun; Chidi's peer group known to his parents and one adult engaged with him; the grandmother seen by a professional and a hearing test requested; one evening a week in which Mr Eze is at home; and Mrs Eze out of the house once a fortnight.

The formulation, in six cells.

  1. What is happening. A household of six in which the income has fallen, the father is absent four nights a week, the grandmother's dementia is unassessed, the three-year-old is not talking, the sixteen-year-old is in trouble, and the nine-year-old has stopped managing at school. The school has noticed the nine-year-old because the school sees her.
  2. Developmental tasks. Industry (Ada); identity (Chidi); language and autonomy (Ngozi); the young-children task done alone (Mrs Eze); generativity under threat (Mr Eze); integrity without an audience (the grandmother).
  3. The family task. Authority and load, defined and distributed. Nothing else will hold until it is.
  4. The trajectory. A fall in income nine months ago; Mr Eze's overtime cut at the same time; the grandmother's decline noticeable over about a year; Ada's school change at the start of this term; Ngozi's ten words at three, with no regression.
  5. The differential and the revision points. Ada — hearing loss is the leading hypothesis and must be excluded before any formulation about anxiety; if hearing is normal, the next is a learning difficulty, and bullying is screened in both cases. Ngozi — delay rather than regression, requiring an assessment and a hearing test. The grandmother — delirium, dementia, depression and hearing loss, with the hearing and the delirium excluded first, as Week 12 requires. If the grandmother's decline is over a year and steady, the working formulation is dementia with a hearing loss; if it is over two months, it is a delirium and a medical emergency.
  6. First actions and the six-month marker. The hearing tests, three of them, this month; one referral for Ngozi; one eye-level conversation with the grandmother; one evening a week reallocated; and the six outcomes listed above.

Why the case is a capstone. Because the correct first action is not psychotherapy. It is three hearing tests, one developmental referral, and one question to a seventy-nine-year-old. The course's argument is that a family therapist who can see this in twenty minutes is worth more to this family than one who can conduct twelve sessions of family work on a child who cannot hear.

15.4a The Eze case, worked forward

The capstone continues into what happens after the first actions, because the integration the week is teaching is not only diagnostic.

Weeks 1 to 4 of the treatment. The three hearing tests are booked: Ada's, Ngozi's, the grandmother's. Ada's is normal. Ngozi's is normal. The grandmother's shows a moderate loss in both ears, and she is fitted; her "accusations" reduce within a fortnight, because a woman who cannot hear what is said about her reasonably concludes that she is being discussed.

The grandmother. With the hearing corrected, the picture is reassessed. The decline over a year, steady, with recent memory most affected and the wandering new in the last three months, is consistent with dementia. The wandering is a safeguarding question: the therapist asks, in the family session, what happens when she leaves the compound, and discovers that nobody has followed her. The first intervention of the case is therefore not psychological: it is a lock, a neighbour informed, and a name and telephone number in her pocket.

Ada. Normal hearing; a normal neurological examination; a report showing a fall in every subject. The therapist asks the school for the observations, not the verdict, and gets them: she does not complete work, she is found in the toilet at break, and she has twice been seen giving her lunch to an older girl. The last observation reorganises the case. Ada is not withdrawing from school; she is being extorted, and the toilet is where she hides.

The family work that follows is the work of Week 6: the school is given one request (a named adult and a change of seating), the parents are given the facts, and Ada is given a sentence and an adult. The extortion ends within three weeks. Her schoolwork does not recover within six months, which the therapist records honestly; a child who has lost a term does not recover it in a term.

Chidi. The adolescent whose behaviour is the family's second emergency. The Week 7 and 8 material applies: the peer group is known, the risk is the group rather than the individual, and the father's response — beating — has produced a boy who conceals. The single most useful intervention is the one from Week 8: one adult, one hour a week, doing something, asking nothing. Mr Eze takes it, on the condition that it is football and not conversation, and the therapist agrees, because the condition is the point.

Mr Eze and Mrs Eze. The couple work is Week 10's, and it is minimal in this case and it is not optional. The twenty minutes on the one evening he is home; the division-of-labour audit, which shows that Mrs Eze carries thirty-one of thirty-four hours of household work; and one sentence from the therapist to Mr Eze about his mother's care, which he had assumed was his wife's duty and had never been asked about.

The grandmother's integrity. Week 12's life review, begun in month three, in the evenings, with Ada present because Ada is the person she talks to. Two sessions; the second is forty minutes and produces the story of a market she ran for twenty-two years. It is recorded on a telephone. It is the only part of this case that the family describes, a year later, as having changed anything.

The six-month review, honestly reported. Ada is attending and has one named adult; her work is recovering slowly. Ngozi has twenty words and two-word combinations after speech and language input. Chidi's group is known and one adult is engaged; he has not been in trouble for four months. The grandmother is assessed, supported, and heard. Mr Eze is at home one more evening a week. Mrs Eze has been out three times in six months, which is below the plan and is progress.

What the case demonstrates. That the correct intervention in a family presenting with a child's school difficulty was, in order: three hearing tests, a safeguarding observation from a school, an extortion disclosed by a lunch, a lock on a gate, and a recording of a market. None of that is psychotherapy, all of it required a family therapist's developmental knowledge, and the therapy — the couple work, the adolescent's one adult, the grandmother's review — happened because the first six things made it possible.

15.4b The family that has been to four clinics

The last configuration the course considers, because it is the one graduates will meet most often in the public sector and the one that most tests the integration.

What the file contains. Four assessments, three diagnoses, two conflicting recommendations, one prescription, and no single document that says what the family's situation is. The family's own account is that nobody has listened, which is usually accurate.

The first session, structured for this family. Do not begin with the history, which they have given four times. Begin with the file: put it on the table and say, "I have four reports here and I am going to read them in front of you, and I want you to correct them." The correcting is the session. It tells the therapist what each previous clinician missed, and it tells the family that this one is not going to ask them to start again.

The three questions specific to this configuration.

  1. "What were you told, in each of the four places?" Not what was found; what they were told. Families remember the sentence, not the finding, and the sentences are frequently the reason the family is now doing something that contradicts the assessment.
  2. "Which of the four did you believe?" The answer identifies the family's explanatory model, which is the only one that has been consistently present in the case.
  3. "What would have to happen for you to believe this clinic was different?" The answer is the engagement contract, and it is usually small: a telephone call that is answered, one person who remembers the name, a letter that arrives.

What the therapist does not do. Do not criticise the previous clinicians in front of the family, however justified. Do not add a fifth assessment without reading the four. And do not begin a treatment before the family's own account is on the page, because a family with four assessments and no narrative will accept a treatment and discontinue it at the first difficulty, as they have three times before.

The outcome that matters. Not a fifth diagnosis. A single page, agreed with the family, that says what is happening, what is being done, by whom, and when it will be reviewed — which is the one-page formulation, and which is the most useful thing this course teaches a graduate to write.

15.5 The viva: what you will be asked

The module's examination includes a fifteen-minute oral. The four questions below are the standard, and the standard of an answer is stated with each.

Question 1 — Take this family and tell me the developmental task of every person in it. The examiner is testing whether the student sees a household rather than a patient. A failing answer names the identified patient only. A good answer names four or five people, and notices when two of the tasks are incompatible.

Question 2 — What is your differential, and what would change your mind? The examiner is testing intellectual honesty. A failing answer gives a formulation with no alternatives. A good answer gives two or three possibilities in order of probability, and names the finding that would overturn the first.

Question 3 — What will you do first, and why not something else? The examiner is testing judgement. A failing answer describes a treatment. A good answer describes the one action that must happen before any treatment, and can defend the order.

Question 4 — What in this case might you be getting wrong because of who you are? The examiner is testing the Week 13 discipline. A failing answer says "I am aware of my biases". A good answer names a specific assumption, states where it came from, and says what would correct it.

How to prepare. The four questions are the four sections of the one-page formulation. A student who can write the page in twenty minutes can answer the viva, because the viva is the page, spoken, with an examiner asking why.

15.5a What to keep from this course

A short, practical instruction, because graduates lose in six months what they do not keep deliberately.

Keep the ten-item history. On one card, in your bag. It will be the most used object of your first year.

Keep the four-category check and the red-flag list. On the back of the same card. It costs nothing and it has prevented more harm than any technique in this book.

Keep the one-page formulation. The six cells. Write it for every family for your first two years, even when nobody asks for it, and give a plain-language copy to the family. The habit will leave you with a file that can be read by a colleague and a family that can read its own case.

Keep the four parts of parent feedback. They will be used weekly.

Keep one sentence of your own. The reflective task at the end of the week asks for three; one is enough, and it should be a sentence about enquiry rather than about knowledge, because enquiry is what fails.

Do not keep the fifteen weeks as a list. The course is not fifteen topics. It is one claim: that people develop, that families develop, and that the difficulties people bring to clinics are usually the point at which those two developments are out of step. Everything else in the book is an instrument for locating that point.

15.6 Laboratory: the capstone

Aim. To produce the six-cell formulation for an unseen family in fifty minutes, defend it against two alternative formulations, and state the first three actions with reasons.

Procedure. The class receives a two-page family description with no diagnosis and no formulation. Fifty minutes in groups of four, then a plenary in which each group presents the page and the trainer offers a rival formulation that the group must answer. The final twenty minutes is the viva, with the trainer asking question 4 of each group.

Marking rubric (25 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
Every person's taskIdentified patient onlyThree peopleEvery person, with the incompatibilities named
The family taskAbsent, or a description of deficitsNamed, but vagueNamed as a task, in one sentence, and structural
The trajectoryUndatedPartly datedEvery significant event dated, with the coincidence named
The differentialOne possibilityTwo, unorderedThree, ordered, with the disconfirming evidence stated
First actionsA treatmentOne good actionThree actions in the right order, with the reason for the order

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the group to name an action that is not psychotherapy and to defend it as the first thing. Groups reliably want to begin with engagement. The Eze case is designed so that engagement is not the answer, and the class discovers this by being wrong.

15.6a Marking your own formulation

Before the viva, and for the rest of a career, the same six checks. A formulation that fails any of them is not ready to be given to a family.

Check 1 — Is every person in the household named? Count them. If the page mentions only the identified patient and one parent, the formulation is about a case and not about a family.

Check 2 — Is anything dated? A formulation without dates cannot be checked, cannot be compared with a later one, and cannot be defended. Three dates is the minimum.

Check 3 — Is there a differential? If there is one possibility on the page, there is no thinking on the page. Two is the minimum; three is better; and each needs the finding that would exclude it.

Check 4 — Is the family task stated as a task, not a deficit? "The parents are inconsistent" is a deficit. "The household has three adults and no agreed authority" is a task, and it implies an intervention.

Check 5 — Is there one action, with a date, that the family agreed to? Not a programme. One action, in their words, with a review. If the page has five recommendations, it has none.

Check 6 — Could you say it aloud in the family's language? Try it. If the sentence cannot be spoken without jargon, it is not yet a formulation; it is a paraphrase of the literature.

The scoring, which is deliberately crude. Six checks, one mark each, and a formulation scoring four or more is usable. Most trainees score two on their first attempt, and the two that are missing are almost always the differential and the dated trajectory — which are the two that take time and cannot be produced by intuition.

Why this matters more than the mark. A formulation is the only artefact of the work that survives the session. The family will remember one sentence, the file will hold one page, and the next clinician will read that page and nothing else. The six checks are a standard for a document that will outlive the therapist's memory of the case, which is the definition of a professional record, and the reason the six checks are worth learning by heart rather than looking up at the end of a long clinic when the file is already open and the next family is in the corridor.

15.7 Six errors of the whole course

These are the errors that recur across the fifteen weeks, stated once, at the end, because they are the ones a graduate will commit.

Error 1 — Treating the referral letter as the case. The letter describes somebody's worst day, written by somebody with an agenda. Week 14.

Error 2 — Finding a family explanation for a medical event. Regression, delirium, hearing loss, and extortion have all, in this book, been formulated as family dynamics by a competent clinician. The four-category check exists to prevent it.

Error 3 — Confusing a stage with a fault. Autonomy at two, industry at nine, identity at fifteen, integrity at seventy-nine. A therapist who has not learned the stages treats each of them as a behaviour problem.

Error 4 — Working with the wrong unit. The couple when the network is the unit; the child when the household is; the household when the school is. Weeks 10, 12, 13.

Error 5 — Giving an accurate formulation the family cannot use. A formulation in clinical language, with five recommendations, no written page, and no review date. Week 11.

Error 6 — Not asking. The suicide question, the safeguarding question, the hearing, the money, the grandmother's opinion. Every one of the six is a question that costs eight seconds and that a therapist avoids because of their own discomfort.

The common thread. Five of the six are failures of enquiry rather than of technique. The course has taught fifteen weeks of knowledge and the thing that will most often fail in practice is the willingness to ask a plain question of a person who is frightened.

15.7a The first year: five things nobody teaches

A closing practical section, written from the reports of graduates.

The first thing: the room will be fuller than the training. Every case in this book has three to six people in it and a therapist with an hour. The habit that survives is not speed; it is deciding, in the first ten minutes, which two things you are doing today and letting the rest wait. A therapist who tries to do the whole formulation in the first session will do none of it, and the family will return to find the therapist more tired than they are.

The second thing: the family will not do what you agreed. Not because they are unmotivated; because the plan was designed at full capacity. The four-column plan from Week 13 is the protection, and the review is where the failure is discussed as information rather than as non-compliance.

The third thing: the referrals will not happen by themselves. Chase at two weeks, chase at six. Keep a list. A clinic that chases loses fewer families than a clinic that refers better, and this is the least glamorous finding of the course.

The fourth thing: someone will tell you something you cannot hold alone. A disclosure, a suicide risk, a child in danger. The requirement to take it to supervision within the week is not a formality; it is the only reliable protection against the two errors of over-reaction and under-reaction, and both are made by clinicians working alone.

The fifth thing: the work will change your own developmental account. You will notice your own family's practices, your own childhood's stage tasks, your own parents' midlife. That noticing is an instrument if it is examined in supervision and a distortion if it is not. Week 13's discipline — state your own rule before interpreting the family's — is the practice that keeps it an instrument.

The last sentence of the course. Fifteen weeks of development are worth nothing in a room with a frightened family unless the therapist can ask a plain question, write one page, and be wrong out loud.

15.8 Culture, faith and the course's own position

A course that spends a week on the critique of its own norms owes the student a statement of where it finally stands.

What this course asserts. That there are developmental regularities: children acquire language in a sequence, form relationships with the adults who care for them, learn through play with other children, and need protection from violence. That these regularities are findings, not Western preferences, and a therapist who cannot assert them cannot protect a child. That the timing and the form of every one of them varies, and variation is not deficiency.

What this course refuses. It refuses relativism, which abandons children in the name of respect. It refuses universalism, which measures every household against one population's averages and calls the difference a deficit. And it refuses the compromise that says all practices are equally valid but some families are better at them, which is universalism with better manners.

The position, in one sentence. There is a human developmental project, its shape is known well enough to guide practice, and its shape is realised differently in every household — so the clinical question is always what this child, in this family, with these resources, needs next.

Faith in the room. The course has treated religious frameworks as resources, as constraints, as explanations and as communities, and never as obstacles to be removed. That is a clinical position and not a theological one: a family's faith is usually the most reliable structure it has, and a therapist who tries to replace it will lose the family and keep the framework.

The therapist's own formation. Week 13's discipline — state your own rule before interpreting the family's — is the practice that holds the whole position. It is also the practice that protects the therapist from the two career-long risks of this work: certainty, and its opposite.

15.9 Evidence: what this course claims

Claims with strong support. The sequence of early language and motor milestones; the association between early responsive caregiving and later regulation; the developmental timing of theory of mind; the peer amplification of adolescent risk; the association between poverty and developmental outcomes, with mechanisms; the predictive value of early intervention for established delay; means restriction in suicide prevention.

Claims with moderate support. Attachment classifications and their stability in adverse conditions; the identity statuses and their correlates; the couple's cascade and repair behaviours; the effectiveness of structured parenting feedback; the dual-process model of grief.

Claims stated as heuristics, with the evidence thin. Levinson's seasons; the six-phase couple map; the "four parts" of parent feedback as a package; the developmental reading of the midlife reckoning. These are useful and they are not established, and a graduate should be able to say which is which.

The course's own bias, stated. The evidence base is heavily weighted toward North American and European samples, and the mechanisms are more portable than the norms. Week 13 is the corrective, and it is a partial one: the studies that would settle the questions have not been funded.

15.10 Key terms of the course

Developmental task — the psychosocial challenge characteristic of a stage; the organising concept of the course.

Calibration — adjusting expectations to the person's developmental capacity.

Dating — locating a problem's onset relative to events and transitions.

Prediction — anticipating the next task the family must renegotiate.

Trajectory — the direction of development over time; requires two measurements.

Differential — the ordered list of possibilities, with the evidence that would separate them.

Formulation — a provisional account of what is happening and why, stated so that it can be wrong.

Referral threshold — the point at which a finding requires another professional, regardless of the therapist's formulation.

Translation — rendering a clinical fact in the family's own framework; a skill, not a concession.

Scope — what a family therapist does and does not do; not a limitation of ambition but a condition of safety.

Developmental history — the ten-item instrument of 15.2b; trajectory, network, authority, senses, function, attempts, model and goal.

Translated formulation — the same claims as the record, in words the family can say back, ending with an action and a date.

15.11 Self-test: the whole course

  1. State the three functions of the developmental lens and give one example of each.
  2. Name the four categories of developmental concern, and say which is always urgent.
  3. A nine-year-old's school performance has fallen. Give the first four things you would establish, in order.
  4. What is the difference between a stage and a fault, and give one example from the course of each.
  5. Why does the developmental lens not supply a treatment, and what does?
  6. In the Eze case, what were the first three actions and why were they in that order?
  7. What are the four viva questions, and which one is hardest to answer honestly?
  8. What does this course assert, refuse, and refuse to refuse?
  9. Name three claims of the course that are heuristics rather than established findings.
  10. What is the single practice most strongly associated with not doing harm over a career?

Answers. (1) Calibration — treating a nine-year-old's argument about fairness as a stage rather than defiance; dating — locating a couple's distance to a bereavement, a job change and a departure in the same quarter; prediction — anticipating that close supervision without negotiation will produce conflict within a year at fourteen. (2) Variation, delay, regression, symptom; regression is always urgent. (3) The hearing; the dated trajectory; the school's observations rather than its verdict; and the child's own account, alone. (4) A stage is a capacity being acquired and a fault is a behaviour being judged; autonomy at two is a stage and "defiant" is the fault; the same child is described by both words. (5) Because the lens describes what is happening and the treatment comes from the modalities of MFT 702, 710 and their successors; the lens selects and sequences the treatment but does not constitute it. (6) Three hearing tests, one developmental referral, and a question to the grandmother — in that order, because each was a finding that would reorganise the rest, and because a formulation built before them would have been about the wrong person. (7) The task of every person; the differential and what would change your mind; what you will do first and why not something else; and what you might be getting wrong because of who you are — the fourth is hardest, because it requires naming an assumption rather than displaying an awareness. (8) It asserts that there are developmental regularities that can guide practice; it refuses relativism and universalism; and it refuses to refuse the possibility of judging a practice harmful. (9) Levinson's seasons, the six-phase couple map, and the four-part feedback package — the midlife reckoning and the play taxonomy are also worth naming. (10) Naming, in advance and in the record, the finding that would prove you wrong.

15.12 Final reflective task

Write, in one page, the three sentences you will carry into your first year of practice. three sentences, each of which you would be willing to be judged by in front of a family who has heard a great deal of professional language and has been helped by very little of it. — three sentences, each of which you would be willing to be judged by. Then write, underneath them, the case you have met in this book that you are most likely to get wrong, and why. Bring both to the viva. The examiners will read the second page, not the first.

15.13 Further reading

  • Bronfenbrenner, U. The Ecology of Human Development — the frame that makes the household, the school and the economy part of one developmental system.
  • Rutter, M. and Rutter, M. Developing Minds — the most careful account of what developmental research can and cannot support.
  • Sameroff, A. The Transactional Model — the theoretical basis for everything in this course about bidirectionality.
  • Gottlieb, G. on probabilistic epigenesis — why "the same input produces different outcomes" is a finding and not a hedge.
  • Kirmayer, L. and Ban, L. on cultural formulation — the corrective this course ends with.

Glossary

Attachment — a behavioural system organising proximity-seeking to a caregiver under threat. Co-regulation — the process by which a caregiver's calm and responsiveness regulate the child's arousal. Developmental task — the psychosocial challenge characteristic of a life stage. Disequilibrium — a period of developmental reorganisation marked by increased conflict or regression. Goodness of fit — the match between a child's temperament and the demands of the environment. Regression — loss of previously acquired function; a red flag. Scaffolding — temporary support that allows a child to do what they cannot yet do alone. Separation-individuation — the process of developing autonomy while remaining connected. Social clock — culturally shared expectations about the timing of life events. Theory of mind — the capacity to attribute mental states to others. Zone of proximal development — the gap between what a learner can do alone and with support.

Self-assessment

Multiple choice

1. A four-year-old who stops speaking after a family bereavement is best described as: (a) normal variation (b) delay (c) regression (d) attention-seeking

2. "Goodness of fit" refers to the match between: (a) child and school (b) temperament and environment (c) parents' goals (d) IQ and achievement

3. The most common developmental concern presented to clinics is: (a) motor delay (b) expressive language delay (c) sleep (d) feeding

4. Disorganised attachment is most associated with: (a) daycare (b) frightening or frightened caregiving (c) prematurity (d) bilingualism

5. A toddler's frequent "no" primarily reflects: (a) oppositional disorder (b) emerging autonomy (c) poor parenting (d) language delay

6. Which is not a feature of adolescent brain development? (a) heightened reward sensitivity (b) peer sensitivity (c) mature impulse control (d) later prefrontal maturation

7. A "social clock" refers to: (a) sleep rhythms (b) culturally shared timing of life events (c) school timetable (d) biological ageing

8. In the dual-process model, healthy grieving involves: (a) staying on the loss track (b) avoiding reminders (c) oscillating between loss and restoration (d) suppressing emotion

9. Apparent confusion in an older adult should first prompt assessment of: (a) personality (b) delirium, depression, hearing, dementia (c) family conflict (d) medication refusal only

10. Extended-family caregiving should be assessed by asking: (a) whether the mother is attuned (b) who the child seeks when frightened (c) the parents' marital status (d) the family's income

Answer key

1 c · 2 b · 3 b · 4 b · 5 b · 6 c · 7 b · 8 c · 9 b · 10 b

Short answer

  1. Distinguish delay from regression, with one clinical example of each.
  2. Name three developmental tasks of adolescence and the corresponding family tasks.
  3. Why is "let him cry it out" a relational intervention, not only a behavioural one?
  4. Give two cultural critiques of attachment theory and one way to adapt your assessment.
  5. What are the four parts of effective parent feedback?

Essay (choose one, 1,800 words)

  1. "Normal development is a cultural achievement, not a biological fact." Discuss with reference to attachment and independence.
  2. Using a disguised case, show how a developmental formulation changed your understanding of a presenting problem that had been framed as behavioural.
  3. Critically evaluate the evidence that couple satisfaction declines after the birth of a child, and discuss the clinical implications.
